### [1] [Error-Aware TF-IDF Retrieval-Augmented Generation for ASR Error Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24915)
*Mohammad Aref Jafari-Raddani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A lightweight, lexical error-aware retrieval framework for ASR that uses symmetric text normalization and an error-aware TF-IDF algorithm to correct phonetic hallucinations, achieving a 90.9% error-aware hit rate and reducing WER from 23.06% to 18.83% on Persian FLEURS with near-zero latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ASR systems frequently hallucinate rare entities and domain-specific terms in low-resource languages due to phonetic confusions. Existing retrieval-augmented generation approaches either use sparse retrieval (ignoring phonetic errors) or heavyweight cross-modal embeddings (high latency), creating a need for a lightweight, error-aware retrieval mechanism.

Method: The framework combines: (1) symmetric text normalization to standardize both ASR hypotheses and retrieval documents, and (2) a novel error-aware TF-IDF algorithm that constructs a sparse diagonal penalty matrix from historical error patterns. This matrix mathematically boosts corrective documents containing high-risk misrecognitions, enabling the retriever to prioritize relevant context despite phonetic errors.

Result: On the Persian FLEURS subset, the error-aware hit rate rose from 53.7% to 90.9%. End-to-end evaluation reduced word error rate from 23.06% to 18.83%, with near-zero inference latency overhead.

Conclusion: The proposed purely lexical, error-aware retrieval framework effectively resolves phonetic and loop hallucinations in low-resource ASR, delivering substantial accuracy gains while maintaining high efficiency, making it suitable for latency-sensitive deployment scenarios.

Abstract: End-to-end automatic speech recognition systems frequently hallucinate rare entities and domain-specific terms, especially in low-resource languages. While retrieval-augmented generation frameworks can mitigate these errors using large language models, current architectures face significant challenges. They either rely on standard sparse retrieval that ignores phonetic misrecognitions or utilize heavyweight cross-modal embeddings that introduce high latency. This letter proposes a highly efficient, purely lexical error-aware framework designed to explicitly resolve phonetic and loop hallucinations. Our approach integrates a symmetric text normalization module with a novel error-aware term frequency-inverse document frequency algorithm. By constructing a sparse diagonal penalty matrix based on historical errors, the retriever mathematically prioritizes corrective documents containing specific high-risk misrecognitions. Evaluated on the Persian subset of the FLEURS dataset, our method increased the error-aware hit rate from 53.7% to 90.9%. In end-to-end evaluations, the integrated framework reduced the final word error rate from 23.06% to 18.83%, achieving significant accuracy gains with near-zero inference latency.

</details>


### [2] [Automatic Generation of Highlights for Academic Paper Via Prompt-based Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25253)
*Yi Xiang,Chengzhi Zhang,Heng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a prompt-based learning approach for automatically generating academic paper highlights, evaluating GPT-2, T5, and ChatGPT. Using designed prompt templates, ChatGPT matches supervised methods without task-specific training and surpasses SOTA with few-shot examples, though performance is sensitive to prompt design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many journals do not provide highlights for academic papers, limiting their utility in literature retrieval, text mining, and bibliometric analysis. Existing supervised methods for automatic highlight extraction require large labeled datasets, which are costly and domain-restricted. The authors seek a method that bypasses the need for extensive task-specific training data.

Method: The authors design task-specific prompt templates combined with paper abstracts as inputs. They evaluate locally deployed pre-trained models (GPT-2, T5) and ChatGPT (via API) across three datasets. The study explores zero-shot, few-shot prompting and systematically analyzes how prompt design choices affect generation quality, supplemented by case studies.

Result: ChatGPT with prompt templates achieves performance comparable to prior supervised methods without task-specific training. With few-shot examples, it significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods on two of three datasets. Generated highlights are generally coherent, informative, and close to author-written ones. Performance is highly sensitive to prompt design.

Conclusion: Prompt-based learning, particularly with ChatGPT, is a viable and effective approach for automatic highlight generation without requiring domain-specific training corpora. The method can generate highlights for papers lacking them, supporting downstream text mining and bibliometric research. This is among the first studies to apply prompt-based learning to this task.

Abstract: Highlights provide a concise summary of the main contributions of an academic paper and help readers quickly understand its focus. However, many journals do not provide highlights, which limits their use in literature retrieval, text mining, and bibliometric analysis. Existing studies have explored supervised learning methods for automatic highlight extraction, but these methods usually require large amounts of labeled training data. This study investigates prompt-based learning for automatic highlight generation. We design task-specific prompt templates and combine them with paper abstracts as model inputs. Several language models are evaluated, including locally deployed pre-trained models such as GPT-2 and T5, as well as ChatGPT accessed through an API. Experiments on three datasets show that ChatGPT with prompt templates achieves performance comparable to previous supervised methods without using task-specific training samples. When a small number of examples are added to the prompts, the model significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods on two datasets. We further analyze how prompt design affects generation quality and find that, although ChatGPT has strong language modeling ability, its performance on this task is highly sensitive to the information provided in the prompt. Case studies also show that the generated highlights are generally coherent, informative, and close to author-written highlights. This study is among the first to apply prompt-based learning to academic highlight generation. The proposed method does not rely on domain-specific training corpora and can generate highlights for papers that lack such information, thereby supporting downstream text mining and bibliometric research.

</details>


### [3] [Memory Makes the Difference: Evaluating How Different Memory Roles Shape Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25361)
*Yuxin Wang,Paul Thomas,Zhiwei Yu,Yuan Gao,Saeed Hassanpour,Soroush Vosoughi,Robert Sim,Nick Craswell*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates how different functional types of retrieved memories in RAG-based conversational systems differentially impact response quality. The authors propose a fine-grained memory taxonomy, classify retrieved memories by role, and design a user-centric evaluation framework. Experiments reveal that clarifying memory enhances factual accuracy and personalization, while irrelevant memory degrades topic relevance and constraint adherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work focuses on memory storage/retrieval mechanisms but neglects how memories with different functional roles influence response quality under varying conversational contexts. Existing evaluations are reference-based and fail to capture nuanced, user-preference-aware response differences.

Method: The authors propose (1) a fine-grained taxonomy of conversational memory that classifies retrieved memories into distinct role types, and (2) a user-centric evaluation framework that simulates user perspectives to assess response quality beyond reference-based metrics. Comparative experiments are conducted on long-term conversational datasets using frontier LLMs.

Result: Different memory types produce substantively different response behaviors: clarifying memory improves factual accuracy and constraint awareness, yielding more correct and personalized responses; irrelevant memory reduces topic relevance and degrades constraint awareness. These effects persist even with frontier LLMs.

Conclusion: The findings demonstrate that leveraging specific memory types can produce more personalized responses, offering actionable insights for memory management in conversational systems. The work encourages further research into memory-type-aware response generation.

Abstract: Prior research on memory mechanism in RAG-based conversational system has emphasized how memory is stored and retrieved. However, far less is known about how memories with different functional roles influence response quality. Specifically, how they shape an agent's responses under varying conversational contexts and whether they lead to substantively different response behaviors. Existing evaluations in conversational system are also largely reference-based, insufficiently capturing the nuances in responses that may address users' preferences differently. In this work, we probe the impact of different memory types in shaping agents' responses. We present a fine-grained taxonomy of conversational memory, classify retrieved memories into different role types, and design a user-centric evaluation framework that simulates user perspectives. Through comparative experiments on long-term datasets and frontier LLMs, our analysis reveal many differentiated effects of memories: e.g., clarifying memory improves responses' factual accuracy and constraint awareness, making them more correct and personalized; irrelevant memory reduces topic relevance and degrades constraint awareness. Despite the power of frontier LLMs, these findings shed light on how different memory types can be leveraged to produce more personalized responses and inspire further research in this direction.

</details>


### [4] [Three Buddhist Vocabularies: Computational Stylometry of the English Pali Canon across Sutta, Vinaya, and Abhidhamma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25372)
*Joy Bose*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Stylometric analysis of English Tipitaka translations (Sutta, Vinaya, Abhidhamma) using Zipf distributions, MATTR-500, numeral density, and vocabulary overlap. Vinaya texts are closest to ideal Zipf slope; Abhidhamma compendium shows highest lexical diversity and numeral density; Vinaya translations across 88 years share only 24.2% vocabulary; cross-tradition Vinaya texts show 20% Jaccard similarity reflecting shared legal heritage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend prior computational stylometric analysis of the Sutta Pitaka to the entire Tipitaka (all three Pitakas) in English translation, quantifying lexical and distributional properties across canonical divisions and translation traditions.

Method: Corpus of 134,831 segments from multiple English translations: Sujato's Sutta Pitaka, Brahmali's Vinaya Pitaka, Horner's 1938 Vinaya, three Abhidhammattha Sangaha translations, and cross-tradition Vinaya texts (Dharmaguptaka, Mulasarvastivada). Methods include Zipf rank-frequency distributions with OLS-fitted exponents, Moving Average TTR (MATTR-500), numeral-word density, and vocabulary overlap coefficients (Jaccard, Szymkiewicz-Simpson). Size-controlled subsampling used for MATTR comparison.

Result: (1) All corpora show Zipf-consistent distributions (R² > 0.989); Vinaya closest to ideal slope −1; Sangaha deviates most with 'consciousness' displacing grammatical particles at rank 8. (2) MATTR-500: Sutta and Theravada Vinaya nearly identical (0.399 vs 0.400); Sangaha corpus significantly more diverse (0.560), confirmed by subsampling. (3) Sangaha has highest numeral-word density (3.26%). (4) Mulasarvastivada Vinaya shares 20.0% (Jaccard) and 49.1% (overlap) with Theravada Vinaya. (5) Two English Vinaya translations 88 years apart share only 24.2% vocabulary; key diagnostic shifts include 'musing'→'absorption' (jhana) and 'defeat'→'expulsion' (parajika).

Conclusion: The three Pitakas exhibit distinct stylometric profiles in English translation. The Vinaya is distributionally closest to ideal Zipfian behavior; the Abhidhamma Sangaha is lexically richer and more numeral-dense, reflecting its taxonomic nature. Translation choices introduce substantial diachronic vocabulary divergence, and cross-tradition legal texts retain modest lexical overlap indicative of shared doctrinal heritage. All results are point estimates without significance testing.

Abstract: We present a computational stylometric analysis of the Tipitaka across all three Pitakas in English translation, extending earlier work on the Sutta Pitaka alone. The corpus spans 134,831 segments from Bhikkhu Sujato's Sutta Pitaka (114,591 segments, CC0), Bhikkhu Brahmali's Vinaya Pitaka (7,923 segments, CC0 2026), I.B. Horner's 1938 Vinaya translation (2,826 segments), three English translations of the Abhidhammattha Sangaha compendium (2,077 segments), and cross-tradition Vinaya texts from the Dharmaguptaka and Mulasarvastivada schools. We compute Zipf rank-frequency distributions with OLS-fitted exponents, Moving Average TTR (MATTR-500), numeral-word density, and vocabulary overlap (Jaccard and Szymkiewicz-Simpson coefficients). Main findings: (1) all corpora show Zipf-consistent distributions (R2 > 0.989); the Vinaya is closest to ideal Zipf slope -1 and the Sangaha corpus deviates most, with 'consciousness' displacing grammatical particles at rank 8; (2) MATTR-500 shows the Sutta and Vinaya Theravada are nearly identical in lexical diversity (0.399 and 0.400), while the Sangaha corpus is genuinely more diverse (0.560), confirmed by size-controlled subsampling; (3) the Sangaha corpus has the highest numeral-word density (3.26%), consistent with its systematic enumeration of mental and material categories; (4) the Mulasarvastivada Vinaya shares 20.0% vocabulary (Jaccard) and 49.1% (overlap coefficient) with the Theravada Vinaya, reflecting shared legal heritage across two millennia; (5) two English translations of the same Vinaya source text share only 24.2% of their vocabulary across 88 years, with 'musing' versus 'absorption' for jhana and 'defeat' versus 'expulsion' for parajika as the most diagnostic shifts. All results are point estimates; no significance testing is conducted. Code and data are released as open-source extensions to the Darshana Graph corpus (arXiv:2606.18222).

</details>


### [5] [Is GraphRAG Needed? From Basic RAG to Graph-/Agentic Solutions with Context Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25656)
*Long Chen,Ryan Razkenari,Yuxuan Zhou,Yuan Tian,Rahul Ghosh,Venkatesh Pappakrishnan,Disha Ahuja,Vidya Sagar Ravipati*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a framework for evaluating and comparing RAG variants (regular RAG, GraphRAG, Modular RAG, Agentic RAG) on semi-structured knowledge bases, implements 9 standardized scenarios, proposes a context engineering method reducing token usage by 19-53%, and identifies a retrieval-generation gap where expanded retrieval doesn't proportionally improve generation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With the emergence of advanced RAG variants like GraphRAG and Agentic RAG, practitioners lack clear guidance on when and how to apply each variant. The paper aims to fill this gap by providing systematic, data-driven evaluation across diverse, realistic use cases.

Method: The authors propose a unified evaluation framework implementing 9 standardized RAG scenarios spanning simple document retrieval to hybrid text-graph retrieval, domain knowledge graph integration, agentic multi-step planning, and agent-graph integration. They introduce a context engineering method using new representations and an agentic loop design to manage text and graph retrievals efficiently. Comparisons are conducted via comprehensive experiments across these scenarios.

Result: The proposed context engineering method achieves a 19%-53% reduction in token usage for GraphRAG and Agentic RAG. A key finding is the retrieval-generation gap: expanded retrieval does not proportionally improve generation quality, indicating that retrieval-oriented metrics may overstate the benefits of advanced retrieval techniques.

Conclusion: The work delivers data-driven, actionable insights on selecting and deploying the appropriate RAG variant for production-ready intelligent RAG systems, cautioning that improved retrieval metrics do not necessarily translate to better generation outcomes.

Abstract: As advanced RAG variants like GraphRAG and Agentic RAG emerge, one leading question is when and how to use them. Here, we introduce a framework for different RAG scenarios evaluation and comparison on semi-structured knowledge bases, including regular RAG, GraphRAG, Modular RAG and Agentic RAG. We provide implementation for 9 standardized RAG scenarios, and conduct experiments for a comprehensive comparison. These scenarios are designed for real use cases regarding data and domain restrictions, spanning from simple document-based retrieval to advanced features such as hybrid text-graph retrieval, integration with computed or pre-defined domain knowledge graphs, agentic multi-step planning, and agent-graph integration. Besides, we present a novel context engineering method for GraphRAG and Agentic RAG, addressing the context/memory overflow issues, efficiently managing text and graph retrievals with new representations and agentic loop design, leading to 19%-53% reduction on token usage. Moreover, further analysis identifies a retrieval-generation gap where expanded retrieval does not proportionally improve generation quality, suggesting retrieval-oriented metrics overstate advanced retrieval benefits. This work provides data-driven insights on when and how to use them for building production-ready intelligent RAG systems.

</details>


### [6] [BitNet Text Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25674)
*Zhen Li,Xin Huang,Liang Wang,Nan Yang,Ting Song,Yan Xia,Xun Wu,Shaohan Huang,Huishuai Zhang,Furu Wei,Dongyan Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: BITEMBED is a framework that compresses LLM-based text embedding models using ternary weights and quantized activations (BitNet-style), combined with knowledge distillation, to enable efficient inference and flexible low-bit embedding storage while maintaining competitive retrieval performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying LLM-based text embedders is costly due to two bottlenecks: (1) large backbone models cause slow embedding inference, and (2) high-dimensional full-precision embeddings impose significant storage and bandwidth overhead on large-scale indexes. Existing solutions address these issues separately, but no unified framework jointly tackles both encoding efficiency and vector storage compression.

Method: BITEMBED operates in three stages: (1) converting pretrained LLM backbones into BitNet-style encoders with ternary weights {-1, 0, +1}, quantized activations, and lightweight normalization refinement; (2) adapting the quantized model via continual contrastive pre-training followed by supervised contrastive fine-tuning, using both similarity-distribution distillation and attention-relation distillation from a full-precision teacher; (3) training output embeddings to support multiple storage precisions (e.g., binary, 4-bit, 8-bit) for flexible storage-accuracy trade-offs across different deployment scenarios.

Result: Experiments on MMTEB (English, v2) using Qwen3-0.6B and Gemma3-270M backbones show that BITEMBED achieves performance largely comparable to full-precision teacher embedders, while flexibly producing text embeddings at various precision levels that balance retrieval quality against storage cost.

Conclusion: BITEMBED demonstrates that extreme low-bit quantization of LLM-based text embedders — jointly applied to both the encoder backbone and output embeddings — is viable without substantial performance degradation, offering a practical path to cost-efficient, large-scale embedding deployment.

Abstract: LLM-based text embedders have substantially improved retrieval and semantic representation quality, but their deployment remains costly: large backbone models slow down embedding inference, while high-dimensional full-precision embeddings impose substantial storage and bandwidth overhead on large-scale indexes. In this paper, we present BITEMBED, an extreme low-bit framework for LLM-based text embedding that jointly targets encoding efficiency and vector storage. BITEMBED converts pretrained LLM backbones into BitNet-style embedding encoders with ternary weights, quantized activations, and lightweight normalization refinement. The converted model is adapted to representation learning through continual contrastive pre-training, followed by supervised contrastive fine-tuning with both similarity-distribution distillation and attention-relation distillation from a full-precision teacher. Beyond quantizing the backbone, BITEMBED further trains output embeddings to support multiple storage precisions meeting different storage needs in various scenarios. Experiments on MMTEB (eng, v2) with Qwen3-0.6B and Gemma3-270M show that BITEMBED is largely comparable to full precision teacher embedders. Moreover, BITEMBED flexibly obtains text embeddings of various precisions, achieving a trade-off between performance and storage cost.

</details>


### [7] [Beyond Function Calling: Benchmarking Tool-Using Agents under Tool-Environment Unreliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25819)
*Yang Tian,Zhengpeng Shi,Bo Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ToolBench-X is a benchmark that evaluates LLM agents under recoverable reliability hazards in tool environments, revealing a substantial gap between performance with reliable vs. unreliable tools. Failures stem from poor hazard diagnosis and ineffective recovery rather than tool-use volume or inference budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tool-use benchmarks assume clean, stable, and trustworthy tool environments, neglecting the unreliability that agents face in real-world deployments. The authors aim to systematically examine how agents handle recoverable reliability hazards during task completion.

Method: ToolBench-X introduces executable multi-step tasks across diverse domains with sequential, parallel, and mixed workflows. Starting from clean environments, it injects five structured hazard types: Specification Drift, Invocation Error, Execution Failure, Output Drift, and Cross-source Conflict. Each hazard instance is designed to be solvable via at least one valid recovery path (retrying, fallback, verification, or cross-checking). Experiments compare agent performance with and without hazards, and analyze the effects of recovery hints and test-time scaling.

Result: Agents that perform well with reliable tools often fail under recoverable hazards. Failures are driven primarily by limited hazard diagnosis and ineffective recovery, not by tool-use volume or inference budget constraints. Targeted recovery hints rescue many failed tasks, while test-time scaling yields only limited improvement.

Conclusion: Tool-use evaluation should move beyond function-call accuracy toward task completion under unreliable environments. Robustness to tool-environment hazards is a critical and underexamined capability for LLM agents.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed as agents that solve tasks by interacting with external tool environments. Although recent tool-use benchmarks increasingly cover complex task settings, they still largely assume clean, stable, and trustworthy tool environments, leaving tool-environment unreliability insufficiently examined. We introduce ToolBench-X, a benchmark for evaluating agents under recoverable reliability hazards. ToolBench-X contains executable multi-step tasks across diverse domains and sequential, parallel, and mixed workflows, each paired with deterministic tools and a canonical final answer for automatic evaluation. Starting from clean tool environments, ToolBench-X injects five structured hazard types: Specification Drift, Invocation Error, Execution Failure, Output Drift, and Cross-source Conflict. Crucially, each injected instance remains solvable through at least one valid recovery path, such as retrying, fallback, verification, or cross-checking. Experiments reveal a substantial reliability gap: agents that perform well with reliable tools often fail under recoverable hazards. Further analysis shows that failures are driven less by tool-use volume or inference budget than by limited hazard diagnosis and ineffective recovery. Targeted recovery hints recover many failed tasks, while test-time scaling yields more limited gains. These results suggest that tool-use evaluation should move beyond function-call accuracy toward task completion under unreliable tool environments. The code and data is available at https://github.com/Foreverskyou/ToolBench-X.

</details>


### [8] [How Reliable Is Your Jailbreak Judge? Calibration and Adversarial Robustness of Automated ASR Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25487)
*Yang Gao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Automated judges used to evaluate LLM jailbreak success rates are unreliable: safety classifiers over-flag (high recall, low precision), while LLM-as-judges show erratic recall and are easily fooled by surface-level benign framing. Both judge types fail under adversarial pressure, undermining reported ASR metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most LLM jailbreak and prompt injection papers report attack-success rates (ASR) using automated judges (safety classifiers or LLM-as-judges) without verifying their reliability. The authors aim to audit these judges against human labels, exposing systematic failures that render many reported ASR numbers unreliable.

Method: Using 596 human-labeled completions from the HarmBench classifier validation set, the authors compare two judge families — dedicated safety classifiers and three LLM-as-judges — against human majority votes. They then attack both judge types: LLM-judges via benign framing wrappers and a single prepended refusal sentence; the dedicated classifier via a white-box GCG adversarial attack on its open weights. A two-annotator audit verifies that attacks leave harmful content intact.

Result: The dedicated classifier shows high recall (0.974) but low precision (0.835), over-flagging benign outputs. LLM-as-judges maintain high precision (0.81–0.94) but have erratic recall (0.06–0.65), causing ASR to vary widely by judge. Benign wrappers flip 57–100% of LLM-judge decisions; a single refusal sentence flips 39–88%. The dedicated classifier resists surface attacks (≤6.7% flip) but a white-box GCG attack flips 70% of confident true positives (21/30). Audits confirm all 80 sampled flips retained harmful content.

Conclusion: Reported ASR numbers based on automated judges are unreliable both on average and under adversarial pressure. The authors recommend that papers report judge precision/recall on human-labeled data, report ASR corrected for judge precision, and include adversarial robustness checks of the judge.

Abstract: Almost every paper on LLM jailbreaks and prompt injection reports an attack-success rate (ASR), and that number is assigned not by people but by an automated judge: either a safety classifier trained for the task, or a general chat model prompted to grade. The judge is rarely checked. We check it. Using 596 human-labeled completions from the HarmBench classifier validation set, we compare the two judge families against human majority votes and then attack them. The two families fail in opposite ways. The dedicated classifier over-flags (precision 0.835, recall 0.974); three different LLM-as-judges keep high precision (0.81 to 0.94) but show erratic recall (0.06 to 0.65), so the same responses produce very different ASR depending on which judge scores them. The two families also differ sharply in robustness. Wrappers that leave the harmful text untouched and only add benign framing flip every LLM-judge between 57% and 100% of the time, and a single prepended refusal sentence accounts for much of this (39% to 88%). The dedicated classifier resists these surface attacks (at most 6.7%), but a white-box GCG attack on its open weights flips 70% of confident true positives (21 of 30; 95% CI 54 to 86%) even at a small optimization budget. A two-annotator audit confirms the attacks leave the harm intact: every one of 80 sampled flips still contained the harmful content. Because a large and growing share of reported ASR comes from LLM-judges, many such numbers are unreliable both on average and under deliberate pressure. We recommend that papers report judge precision and recall on a human-labeled slice, report ASR corrected for judge precision, and include an adversarial check of the judge. Our code is released.

</details>


### [9] [Detect, Unlearn, Restore: Defending Text Summarization Models Against Data Poisoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26036)
*Poojitha Thota,Shirin Nilizadeh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper addresses training-time data poisoning attacks on LLMs fine-tuned for summarization. The authors propose a unified post-hoc defense framework combining influence-function analysis (white-box) and behavioral auditing via perturbation sensitivity (black-box) for detection, plus gradient-ascent unlearning for remediation. They also introduce novel poisoning attacks targeting factual distortion and representational bias. Across nine architectures and six benchmarks, defenses achieve 85-92% detection precision, with unlearning restoring up to 96% of original behavior at under 0.6% ROUGE degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuning-stage data poisoning poses a critical threat to LLM summarization systems because small task-specific datasets disproportionately influence model behavior. Adversaries can induce persistent failures (biased/harmful summaries) while evading standard evaluation metrics. The authors aim to address the lack of unified detection and remediation frameworks for such attacks across the ML supply chain.

Method: A unified post-hoc defense framework with two detection modalities: (1) White-box: influence-function analysis with semantic consistency checks, exploiting that poisoned pairs exhibit abnormally high training influence. (2) Black-box: behavioral auditing measuring sensitivity to semantics-preserving perturbations, where poisoned models show 2-3× greater sensitivity. Remediation uses gradient-ascent unlearning. The authors also introduce novel attacks targeting factual distortion and representational bias. Evaluation spans nine architectures, six benchmark datasets, under adaptive attacks.

Result: Defenses achieve 85-92% detection precision. Gradient-ascent unlearning restores up to 96% of original behavior with minimal utility loss (<0.6% ROUGE degradation). Poisoned models in black-box settings exhibit 2-3× greater sensitivity to semantics-preserving perturbations. Poisoning alters summarization behavior without triggering conventional alarms, but leaves persistent structural artifacts exploitable for detection.

Conclusion: Fine-tuning-time poisoning leaves persistent structural artifacts in summarization models, enabling practical detection and post-deployment recovery without full retraining. The framework provides actionable defenses across both white-box and black-box scenarios, substantially mitigating the threat of adversarial fine-tuning manipulation in LLM summarization pipelines.

Abstract: Training-time data poisoning during fine-tuning poses a significant threat to large language models (LLMs) deployed for abstractive text summarization, where small task-specific datasets exert disproportionate influence on model behavior. In this setting, adversaries manipulate fine-tuning data to induce persistent summarization failures, such as biased or harmful summaries, while preserving standard evaluation metrics. We present a unified post-hoc defense framework for detecting and remediating fine-tuning-stage poisoning in summarization models across the machine learning supply chain. Our experiments show that in white-box settings, poisoned document-summary pairs exhibit abnormally high training influence, enabling detection via influence-function analysis with semantic consistency checks. In black-box settings, poisoned models display two to three times greater sensitivity to semantics-preserving perturbations, enabling behavioral auditing without training data access. Beyond existing poisoning formulations, we introduce novel attacks targeting factual distortion and representational bias, showing that poisoning alters summarization behavior without triggering conventional alarms. Across nine architectures and six benchmark datasets under adaptive attacks, our defenses achieve 85-92% detection precision, while gradient-ascent unlearning restores up to 96% of original behavior with minimal utility loss (less than 0.6% ROUGE degradation). These results indicate that fine-tuning-time poisoning leaves persistent structural artifacts, enabling practical detection and post-deployment recovery without full retraining.

</details>


### [10] [Self-Recognition Finetuning can Prevent and Reverse Emergent Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23700)
*Arush Tagade,Shaoheng Zhou,Jiaxin Wen,Shi Feng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Emergent misalignment (EM) is reframed as destabilization of aligned character rather than adoption of a misaligned persona. Self-generated text recognition (SGTR) finetuning is shown to be an effective character-targeted defense, particularly for prevention, with supporting evidence from identity self-report diversity, self-recognition corruption, and system prompt removal experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EM has been linked to misaligned persona vectors and evil character traits, suggesting it operates through disruption of aligned character rather than direct harmful content learning. This motivates studying character-targeted interventions distinct from standard in-training defenses.

Method: Two-stage finetuning experiments across three models (GPT-4.1, Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct, Seed-OSS-36B-Instruct) and multiple EM datasets. SGTR finetuning is compared against benign baselines (domain-specific data, general knowledge, word counting) in both reversal and prevention settings. Additional experiments manipulate identity self-reports, corrupt self-recognition, and remove identity-bearing system prompts.

Result: All interventions produce comparable EM reversal but only when restoring degraded capabilities. For prevention, only SGTR finetuning consistently reduces misalignment without worsening any metric. EM finetuning induces diversity in identity self-reports; corrupting self-recognition exacerbates misalignment; removing the identity-bearing system prompt substantially reduces EM effects.

Conclusion: EM should be reframed not as the adoption of a coherent misaligned persona but as the destabilization of aligned character. SGTR finetuning fortifies character and serves as an effective prevention mechanism against EM.

Abstract: Emergent misalignment (EM) has been linked to the activation of misaligned persona vectors and evil character traits, suggesting that EM operates through disruption of the model's aligned character rather than direct learning of harmful content. Motivated by this connection, we study self-generated text recognition (SGTR) finetuning as a character-targeted intervention that is distinct from existing in-training defenses. We conduct two-stage finetuning experiments across three models (GPT-4.1, Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct, Seed-OSS-36B-Instruct) and multiple EM datasets to compare SGTR finetuning against benign finetuning baselines (correct domain-specific data, general knowledge, and word counting) to find it an effective defense in both reversal and prevention settings. We find that all interventions produce comparable EM reversal, but only when restoring capabilities that EM had degraded. For prevention, only SGTR finetuning consistently reduces misalignment without exacerbating any individual metric, suggesting that character fortification specifically drives prevention. We provide further evidence for EM's relation to the LLM's default character by showing that EM finetuning induces diversity into the LLM's identity self-reports, artificially corrupting self-recognition exacerbates misalignment caused by EM finetuning, and that removing the model's identity-bearing system prompt substantially reduces the effect of EM finetuning. Together, these findings reframe EM not as the adoption of a coherent misaligned persona but as the destabilization of aligned character.

</details>


### [11] [Evaluating LLM Usage for Efficient and Explainable Numerical and Classified Implicit Sentiment Analysis of Product Desirability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23701)
*Sherri Weitl-Harms,John Hastings*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes an LLM-based framework for quantifying product desirability from qualitative feedback, using zero-shot sentiment scoring and classification. Tested on two PDT datasets, GPT-4o-mini achieved Pearson correlations up to 0.97 and classification accuracy up to 94%, matching expert labels at low cost while outperforming lexicon and transformer baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Qualitative product feedback captures rich user experiences, but its implicit sentiment is challenging to quantify reliably. Existing lexicon-based and transformer methods underperform, and human annotation is expensive. The authors seek a scalable, interpretable, and cost-efficient alternative using LLMs.

Method: The framework applies zero-shot LLMs (including GPT-4o-mini and larger models) to two PDT datasets (ZORQ and CARMA, 106 respondent term groupings) for continuous numerical sentiment scoring and categorical sentiment classification. No explicit review scores are used. Outputs include confidence ratings and human-readable rationale explanations (xAI). Baselines include lexicon-based and transformer-based approaches.

Result: LLMs achieved Pearson correlations up to 0.97 with expert labels and classification accuracy up to 94%. Results were robust across multiple data presentation formats. Lexicon and transformer baselines failed to produce statistically significant results. GPT-4o-mini matched larger models' performance at 94% lower cost.

Conclusion: Combining the PDT survey method with cost-efficient LLMs enables rich, interpretable product evaluation — yielding numerical sentiment scores, categorical classifications, and high-level user impressions useful for product development, improvement, and marketing strategy.

Abstract: Qualitative product feedback can reveal nuanced user experiences, but its implicit sentiment is difficult to measure. This paper presents a scalable and interpretable framework that uses large language models (LLMs) to quantify product desirability from such data. Using two Product Desirability Toolkit (PDT) datasets from ZORQ and CARMA comprising 106 respondent term groupings with gold-standard human annotation, zero-shot continuous numerical sentiment scoring and categorical sentiment classification are evaluated without relying on explicit review scores. Across the datasets, LLMs generated numerical sentiment scores directly from qualitative responses and closely matched expert labels, achieving Pearson correlations up to 0.97 and classification accuracy up to 94%. LLMs maintained robustness even when handling data presented in multiple forms and consistently expressed high confidence. In contrast, lexicon-based and transformer baselines did not produce statistically significant results. Among the models tested, GPT-4o-mini achieved performance comparable to larger models at 94% lower cost, supporting scalable deployment.
  The framework also incorporates model confidence ratings and human-readable rationale explanations (xAI), improving interpretability, transparency, and trust while supporting practical use in product satisfaction assessment. In general, using the PDT tool as a survey method along with a cost efficient LLM for sentiment analysis has the potential to provide for product evaluation with results that are rich in terms of sentiment scores (both numerical and classified sentiment) and in terms of the high-level user impressions of the product that can be used to identify ideas for product development and improvement, as well as marketing ideas for target audiences.

</details>


### [12] [Do LLM Attribution Metrics Transfer? Auditing Retrieval-Augmented Generation Evaluation Across Datasets and Constructs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23915)
*Tianyu Ding,Aditya Nannapaneni,Juan Pablo De la Cruz Weinstein*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Audits eight automatic attribution metrics across three evaluation constructs, finding none transfers reliably across datasets within the same construct. Per-dataset metric rankings invert, and a "best-on-average" rule fails leave-one-out validation, showing metric choice must be validated per target dataset. LLM judges avoid chance-level collapses but are costly, non-deterministic, and not uniformly best.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic attribution metrics for LLM retrieval-augmented generation are often treated as interchangeable in practice, but their cross-dataset reliability has not been systematically audited. The authors seek to determine whether any scorer can transfer robustly across different datasets within the same evaluation construct.

Method: Audited eight automatic scorers (lexical, embedding, BERTScore baselines; entailment/NLI models including ANLI, FEVER NLI, MiniCheck; and a prompt-based LLM judge) across three evaluation constructs: provenance/topicality, generated-answer attribution, and fact-check entailment. Transferability was assessed by whether a scorer stays within the 95% CI of the best audited scorer on every dataset of a multi-dataset construct. Used AttributionBench (four source datasets, n=1,610) plus HAGRID (n=2,150) for generated-answer attribution; computed Kendall tau rank correlations and leave-one-dataset-out regret analysis.

Result: No scorer transfers reliably. Per-dataset metric rankings invert (Kendall tau = -0.64, p = 0.031 on AttributedQA vs. LFQA). An NLI scorer best on AttributedQA (AUROC 0.90) collapses to chance (AUROC 0.53) on LFQA, where BERTScore wins (0.91); this is not a length or truncation artifact. A naive "best-on-average" selection rule yields mean held-out regret of 0.172 AUROC, worse than fixing one scorer. The LLM judge avoids chance-level collapses but is ~100× costlier and non-deterministic.

Conclusion: Metric choice for attribution evaluation cannot be learned from other datasets; it must be validated on the target dataset. Using an LLM judge relocates rather than removes the validation burden, as it introduces cost, non-determinism, and is not uniformly superior.

Abstract: Practice often treats automatic metrics for attribution in LLM retrieval-augmented generation as interchangeable. We audit eight automatic scorers -- lexical, embedding, and BERTScore baselines alongside entailment/grounding-trained models (clean and FEVER NLI, the checker MiniCheck) -- across three evaluation constructs (provenance/topicality, generated-answer attribution, and fact-check entailment), asking whether any scorer transfers: stays within the 95% confidence interval of the best audited scorer on every dataset of a multi-dataset construct. In the construct with the most multi-dataset human-labeled coverage -- generated-answer attribution (AttributionBench's four source datasets, n = 1,610, with independent HAGRID, n = 2,150) -- none does: the per-dataset metric rankings invert (Kendall tau = -0.64, p = 0.031 on AttributedQA vs. LFQA), and an off-the-shelf NLI scorer that is best on short-claim AttributedQA (AUROC 0.90) collapses to AUROC 0.53 (chance) on long-form LFQA, where BERTScore wins (0.91); the flip is not a length or truncation artifact. This instability has a concrete decision cost: a naive "best-on-average" rule for choosing an evaluator fails leave-one-dataset-out (mean held-out regret 0.172 AUROC, worse than fixing one scorer), so metric choice must be validated on the target dataset rather than learned from others. A prompt-based LLM judge avoids the chance-level collapses the automatic scorers suffer (no LFQA collapse) but is not uniformly best, ~100x costlier, and non-deterministic -- relocating, not removing, the validation burden.

</details>


### [13] [When Retrieval Metrics Mislead: Measuring Policy Signal in Long-Horizon Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23937)
*Tianyu Ding,Juan Pablo De la Cruz Weinstein*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether exact-match retrieval recall is a reliable proxy for downstream policy classification performance. Using tau-bench with Qwen2.5 classifiers (3B/7B), the authors find that retrieved policy clauses yield macro-F1 scores close to those obtained with gold clauses, despite low exact-match recall (7%). This suggests exact-match recall underestimates the true utility of retrieved context for decision models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exact-match retrieval recall is commonly used as a proxy metric to evaluate whether a retriever provides useful policy context to downstream decision models. The authors question the validity of this proxy, hypothesizing that retrieved clauses that do not exactly match the gold policy may still contain sufficient semantic overlap to support correct downstream classification.

Method: The authors conduct experiments on tau-bench for pre-action policy classification using Qwen2.5-3B and Qwen2.5-7B classifiers. They first establish baselines with gold-policy conditioning and a compact structured state representation. They then replace gold policy clauses with top-ranked retrieved clauses from decision-time context. Performance is compared across three conditions: retrieved clauses, gold clauses, and two controls (mismatched-policy and no-policy). A second retriever and the 7B model are used for robustness, and results are disaggregated across fine-tuning configurations.

Result: Key findings: (1) Compact structured state improves macro-F1 over raw trajectories by 0.13–0.17 with gold clauses. (2) Exact-match recall at rank 1 is only 7% for airline states, yet the 3B classifier achieves macro-F1 0.58 with retrieved clauses vs. 0.60 with gold clauses (Δ = −0.02; 95% CI [−0.23, +0.21]). Controls score 0.32 (mismatched) and 0.21 (no-policy). (3) No statistically significant macro-F1 difference is detected between retrieved and gold clauses, though the confidence interval is too wide to establish non-inferiority. (4) The same qualitative pattern holds with a second retriever and at 7B, with variation across fine-tuning configurations.

Conclusion: Exact-match clause recall can substantially underestimate the downstream utility of retrieved policies in this benchmark setting. The authors recommend evaluating retrievers with retrieved policies integrated into the classification loop rather than relying on recall alone, as semantically relevant but non-exact matches may still enable effective decision-making.

Abstract: Exact-match retrieval recall is often used as a proxy for whether a retriever supplies useful policy context to a downstream decision model. We test this proxy for pre-action policy classification in tau-bench using Qwen2.5-3B/7B classifiers. Under gold-policy conditioning, a compact structured state improves macro-F1 over raw trajectories by 0.13-0.17 after tuning. We then replace the benchmark-designated policy clause with the top-ranked clause retrieved from decision-time context. Although the exact governing clause is retrieved at rank 1 for only 7% of airline states, the primary 3B classifier obtains macro-F1 0.58 with retrieved clauses versus 0.60 with gold clauses (Delta=-0.02, task-cluster 95% CI [-0.23,+0.21]); mismatched-policy and no-policy controls score 0.32 and 0.21. We do not detect a macro-F1 difference between retrieved and gold clauses in this configuration, although the interval remains too wide to establish non-inferiority. The same qualitative pattern appears with a second retriever and at 7B, while varying across fine-tuning configurations. These results indicate that exact-match clause recall can underestimate downstream policy utility in this benchmark setting, motivating evaluation with retrieved policies in the classification loop rather than recall alone.

</details>


### [14] [Does My Embedding Reflect That $A = B$? Evaluating Mathematical Equivalence in Embedding Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23959)
*Jiaying Ye,Samarth Rao,Leo Carlin,Kedar Chintalapati,Saharsh Bhargava,Rachit Jaiswal,Michael Zhou,Jared Darlington,Jarod Alper,Vasily Ilin,Henry Kvinge*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Current embedding models fail to capture mathematical equivalence across differently-worded statements. The authors introduce the MELD dataset to benchmark this, revealing that models group by terminology rather than underlying mathematics. They propose a contrastive learning approach aligning informal and formal mathematical expressions, improving retrieval on both cross-formal tasks and natural-language-only benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mathematics statements can be equivalent but expressed in vastly different languages across subfields. Breakthroughs often occur when researchers recognize these cross-domain equivalences. With growing formalization resources (e.g., Lean), there is a pressing need for tools that reliably navigate between mathematical "languages." The authors investigate whether current embedding models actually capture mathematical equivalence, as opposed to superficial lexical similarity.

Method: The authors construct the MELD (Mathematically Equivalent but Lexically Different Pairs) dataset, consisting of mathematically equivalent statement pairs expressed in dissimilar natural language. They benchmark state-of-the-art embedding models on MELD and find they cluster by terminology, not math. To address this, they propose a contrastive learning method that aligns embeddings of informal natural-language statements with their corresponding formalizations (e.g., in Lean), pulling equivalent statements together in the embedding space regardless of surface form.

Result: State-of-the-art embedding models perform poorly on MELD, grouping statements by shared terminology rather than mathematical content. The proposed contrastive approach yields improvements both on informal-formal retrieval tasks and on the MELD benchmark, which contains only natural language statement pairs.

Conclusion: Existing embedding models are inadequate for capturing deep mathematical equivalence. Contrastively aligning informal and formal mathematical text produces embeddings that better reflect the underlying mathematics, improving cross-lingual mathematical retrieval and robustness to lexical variation.

Abstract: Because mathematics is highly abstract, a single statement can take very different forms depending on what subfield it is framed in. There are many examples where breakthroughs occurred after researchers discovered that a question had already been answered in a different field. At the same time, the growth of new resources related to formalization has increased the need for tools that enable efficient and reliable navigation between mathematical 'languages' (e.g., from Lean to natural language). In this paper, we investigate whether current embedding models capture mathematical equivalence. To do this, we introduce the Mathematically Equivalent but Lexically Different Pairs (MELD) Dataset, a collection of mathematically equivalent statements that are expressed in very different language. We show that current state-of-the-art embedding models tend to group statements by the terminology used to make them instead of the underlying math. Motivated by this, we propose a contrastive approach to learning embeddings of mathematical text that focuses on aligning informal statements with different formalizations. Our experiments demonstrate that this leads to improvements not only on informal-formal retrieval tasks but also on MELD, which only contains natural language statements.

</details>


### [15] [RASC+: Retrieval-Constrained LLM Adjudication for Clinical Value Set Authoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23992)
*Sumit Mukherjee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Existing LLM zero-shot generation fails at clinical value set completion due to poor memorization of large, versioned code systems. This paper proposes a two-stage pipeline: optimized candidate-pool retrieval for recall, followed by constrained LLM adjudication for selection, achieving substantial gains in both recall (0.553→0.730) and macro F1 (0.287→0.549) while maintaining auditable code provenance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical value set completion—identifying standardized terminology codes for quality measures, phenotyping, and decision support—is critical but poorly handled by direct LLM generation, as code systems are too large and frequently updated for reliable memorization. The RASC benchmark demonstrated this limitation, motivating a stage-wise approach that separates broad candidate recall from precise code selection.

Method: A two-stage pipeline: (1) Candidate-pool construction using Qwen3-based retrieval enhanced with vocabulary-aware expansion and code-display rescue retrieval to maximize recall; (2) Constrained LLM adjudication using blinded GPT-5 as the selector, restricted to choosing only from the retrieved candidate pool to ensure auditability. Compared against the original SAPBert cross-encoder baseline for stage 2.

Result: On the full 3,744-value-set RASC test split: candidate-pool recall improved from 0.553 (original RASC baseline) to 0.730 (Qwen3 retrieval with expansions), with 0.655 on the held-out-publisher stratum. Using GPT-5 adjudication on the expanded pool yielded macro F1 of 0.549 (full test) and 0.533 (held-out-publisher), versus SAPBert's 0.287 and 0.233 respectively.

Conclusion: Retrieval-constrained LLM adjudication substantially improves value set completion over both direct generation and weaker cross-encoder selection, while maintaining the safety property that all returned codes originate from an auditable, human-reviewable candidate pool—a critical constraint for clinical applications.

Abstract: Clinical value sets define the standardized terminology codes used in quality measurement, phenotyping, cohort construction, and clinical decision support. The recently introduced Retrieval-Augmented Set Completion (RASC) benchmark showed that direct zero-shot large language model (LLM) generation is poorly suited to this task: clinical code systems are large, version-controlled, and not reliably memorized by language models. We study a stage-wise alternative in which candidate-pool construction is optimized for recall and a constrained LLM adjudicator is optimized for candidate selection. On the full 3,744-value-set RASC test split, Qwen3-based retrieval with vocabulary-aware expansion and code-display rescue retrieval increases candidate-pool recall from the original RASC retrieval baseline of 0.553 to 0.730; on the held-out-publisher stratum, pool recall is 0.655. The higher-recall pool alone is not sufficient: applying the original SAPBert cross-encoder to this expanded pool gives full-test macro F1 of 0.287 and held-out-publisher macro F1 of 0.233. Replacing the stage-2 selector with blinded GPT-5 adjudication over the same pool increases full-test macro F1 to 0.549 and held-out-publisher macro F1 to 0.533. These results show that retrieval-constrained LLM adjudication can substantially improve value set completion while preserving the safety constraint that all returned codes must come from an auditable candidate pool.

</details>


### [16] [CAVEWOMAN: How Large Language Models Behave Under Linguistic Input and Output Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24083)
*Morayo Danielle Adeyemi,Ryan A. Rossi,Franck Dernoncourt*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Cavewoman, a protocol evaluating whether "caveman style" compression of prompts or responses actually saves inference cost. Output compression saves 1.4–3× cost; input compression backfires, increasing net cost (1.15–2.7×) while degrading accuracy and causing semantic divergence from unconstrained baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The widespread practice of shortening prompts and dropping grammar to reduce LLM inference costs lacks rigorous empirical validation. It is unclear whether compressing the user prompt versus the model response actually yields savings, and what the downstream effects on accuracy and semantic fidelity are.

Method: Cavewoman is a two-channel evaluation protocol that separately measures four compressed prompt settings and one compressed output setting across five reduction levels. Eight models (API and open-weight) are evaluated on five datasets. Each generation is scored on task accuracy, realized per-item cost (using real pricing), and reference-text agreement against the model's own unconstrained generation, with robustness checks including length-controlled re-scoring, multiple-comparisons correction, and complementary semantic measures.

Result: Output compression reduces realized cost on most API models (1.4–2.4×, up to 3× best case) and all open-weight models under public-tier pricing. Input compression is strictly lose-lose: it raises net cost (~1.15× mean, up to 2.7×) because models respond with longer outputs even as accuracy drops. Approximately half of non-reasoning model generations are correct but no longer entail the unconstrained baseline in surface text, a divergence robust to length-controlled re-scoring and semantic measures.

Conclusion: Compressing the model's output genuinely saves cost; compressing the user's prompt does the opposite. The semantic drift between compressed and unconstrained generations—even when both are correct—suggests that cost-saving interventions alter model behavior in non-trivial ways beyond mere accuracy metrics.

Abstract: "Talk short. Drop grammar. Save token." This caveman style is widely promoted as a way to cut inference cost, but whether it actually saves anything depends on which channel (the user's prompt or the model's response) is being compressed. We present Cavewoman, a two-channel evaluation protocol that scores every generation on task accuracy, realized per-item cost, and reference-text agreement against the model's unconstrained reference. We evaluate eight models on five datasets at five reduction levels, with both channels measured on the same items. Output compression cuts realized cost on most API models (1.4-2.4x per model, up to 3x in the best case) and on all four open-weight models under public-tier pricing. Input compression has the opposite effect, a strict lose-lose: it raises net cost rather than lowering it (~1.15x on the five-benchmark mean, up to 1.8x on the worst dataset and 2.7x under stronger compression), because models compensate with longer responses even as accuracy collapses. Under the same setting, surface text diverges from the unconstrained reference: on the non-reasoning models, roughly half of all generations are correct yet their surface text no longer entails the model's own unconstrained baseline generation. The divergence survives length-controlled re-scoring, multiple-comparisons correction, and replication under complementary semantic measures. Code and data are available at https://github.com/danielle34/cavewoman.

</details>


### [17] [PORTER: Language-Grounded Event Representations for Portable Structured EHR Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24102)
*Lin Lawrence Guo,Adam Paul Yan,Emily Vettese,Lillian Sung*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PORTER is an EHR foundation model that represents clinical events via text descriptions and a dedicated numeric pathway rather than fixed-vocabulary tokens, enabling zero-shot cross-vocabulary transfer across institutions while matching in-domain performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing EHR foundation models encode clinical events as discrete tokens from a fixed vocabulary, making them unable to represent unseen concepts or new combinations of attributes (e.g., numeric values). This severely limits transfer across institutions or deployment pipelines where vocabularies differ. The authors aim to eliminate this vocabulary bottleneck.

Method: PORTER decouples event representation from fixed vocabularies by: (1) representing events via free-text descriptions encoded through a frozen text encoder (e.g., BioBERT), (2) integrating numeric values through a dedicated numeric encoding pathway, and (3) learning temporal clinical dynamics via an autoregressively pretrained temporal backbone. Cross-vocabulary transfer is evaluated by rendering the same patient timelines using event descriptions unseen during pretraining and testing on MIMIC.

Result: Across 74 pediatric clinical prediction tasks, PORTER matched the mean AUROC of a fixed-vocabulary model with identical backbone and pretraining objective. Under cross-vocabulary transfer (unseen event descriptions), PORTER recovered 97.1% of the mean AUROC of a model trained directly on the target vocabulary, without retraining. On MIMIC, PORTER outperformed a fixed-vocabulary model that lost 69% of events due to unseen tokens. Mechanistically, cross-vocabulary transfer performance was tied to preservation of patient-level representation geometry, not text encoder scale. The numeric pathway improved magnitude sensitivity without disrupting clinical concept identity. PORTER also surpassed a task-specific text serialization comparator with 329× lower amortized compute.

Conclusion: PORTER demonstrates that language-grounded EHR representations can achieve vocabulary-independent transfer without sacrificing in-domain performance, reducing the need for vocabulary harmonization and enabling efficient cross-task reuse.

Abstract: Most electronic health record (EHR) foundation models encode clinical events as discrete event tokens from a fixed vocabulary and therefore cannot directly represent events containing unseen concepts or new combinations of concepts and attributes such as numeric values. This limits transfer across institutions and even across deployment pipelines within the same institution. We introduce PORTER, a language-grounded structured EHR foundation model that decouples event representation from this fixed vocabulary. PORTER represents events through their descriptions using a frozen text encoder, integrates numeric values through a dedicated pathway, and learns clinical dynamics over patient timelines with an autoregressively pretrained temporal backbone. Across 74 clinical prediction tasks at a pediatric hospital, PORTER matched the mean AUROC of a fixed-vocabulary model with the same temporal backbone and pretraining objective. When the same patient timelines were rendered using event descriptions not seen during pretraining, PORTER transferred without retraining or vocabulary mapping, recovering 97.1% of the mean AUROC of a model trained directly on the target vocabulary. When transferred to MIMIC, PORTER outperformed the fixed-vocabulary model, which dropped 69% of events because their tokens were unseen. Mechanistic analyses showed cross-vocabulary transfer tracked preservation of patient-level representation geometry rather than the scale of the text encoder, and the numeric pathway improved sensitivity to magnitude without disrupting clinical concept identity. PORTER also achieved higher AUROC than a task-specific text serialization comparator, at 329-fold lower amortized compute. PORTER is a step toward vocabulary-independent EHR foundation models that reduce the need for vocabulary harmonization while preserving in-domain performance and enabling efficient cross-task reuse.

</details>


### [18] [BehaviorBench: Benchmarking Foundation Models for Behavioral Science Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24162)
*Jin Huang,Yutong Xie,Wanli Song,Xingjian Zhang,Walter Yuan,Matthew O. Jackson,Qiaozhu Mei*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: BehaviorBench is a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating foundation models on behavioral science tasks across four core capabilities. The authors also introduce Be.FM-1.5, a behavioral foundation model, and find that proprietary models excel at individual-level prediction while behaviorally fine-tuned models achieve stronger distributional alignment, underscoring the importance of distributional evaluation for behavioral validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation models are increasingly used in behavioral sciences (psychology, sociology, economics), but there is no systematic understanding of their performance across diverse behavioral tasks, contexts, and populations. Existing evaluations lack both individual-level and distributional-level assessment, the latter being essential for behavioral validity.

Method: The authors introduce BehaviorBench, a benchmark evaluating models on four capabilities: (1) behavior prediction and simulation, (2) strategic decision-making, (3) subject-trait inference, and (4) behavioral knowledge application. Evaluation is conducted at both individual and distributional levels. They also develop Be.FM-1.5 by fine-tuning on behavioral data, extending the Be.FM model family. Proprietary general-purpose models are compared against behaviorally fine-tuned models on both metric types.

Result: Proprietary general-purpose models outperform on individual-level prediction and knowledge-intensive tasks. In contrast, behavioral foundation models (particularly Be.FM-1.5) achieve substantially stronger distributional alignment. Be.FM-1.5 leads on distributional metrics while remaining competitive on individual-level metrics, demonstrating that targeted behavioral fine-tuning can bridge the gap between model types.

Conclusion: Distributional evaluation is critical for assessing behavioral validity in AI systems. BehaviorBench provides a foundational platform for developing and evaluating behaviorally aligned models. Be.FM-1.5 shows strong potential for broad application in behavioral science research, and proper behavioral adaptation can close the performance gap with proprietary general-purpose models.

Abstract: Foundation models have been increasingly applied to behavioral science domains such as psychology, sociology, and economics. While these models show promise in individual tasks such as survey response prediction and human-subject experiment simulation, there remains no systematic understanding of how well they perform across diverse behavioral science tasks, contexts, and populations. We introduce BehaviorBench, a comprehensive benchmark that evaluates foundation models along four core capabilities: (1) behavior prediction and simulation, (2) strategic decision-making, (3) subject-trait inference, and (4) behavioral knowledge application. Crucially, BehaviorBench evaluates model outputs at both the individual and distributional levels, capturing not only per-subject accuracy but also population-level alignment, an essential requirement for behavioral validity. Leveraging the tasks in BehaviorBench, we further develop Be.FM-1.5, extending the Be.FM family of behavioral foundation models fine-tuned on behavioral data. Our results reveal a considerable gap: proprietary general-purpose models excel at individual-level prediction and knowledge-intensive tasks, whereas behavioral foundation models, fine-tuned on behavioral data, achieve substantially stronger distributional alignment. Notably, Be.FM-1.5 leads on distributional metrics and remains competitive on individual-level metrics, suggesting that proper behavioral adaptation can close the gap. Our results highlight the importance of distributional evaluation, establish BehaviorBench as a foundation for developing and assessing behaviorally aligned AI systems, and demonstrate Be.FM-1.5's potential for a broad range of behavioral science studies. Our BehaviorBench and Be.FM-1.5 models can be accessed via https://umich-foreseer.github.io/behaviorbench/.

</details>


### [19] [A Pāninian Foundation for Indic Language Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24172)
*Ritwik Banerjee,Lav R. Varshney*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Indic languages share a deep morphosyntactic architecture rooted in Pānini's Sanskrit grammar (the Astādhyāyī), which cuts across genealogical lines. The authors argue this Pāninian framework provides a unifying computational architecture that can merge sparse Indic resources into a single high-resource metalanguage, and propose a four-part benchmark suite to make this shared architecture explicit and measurable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Over a billion people speak Indic languages, yet NLP infrastructure for them remains fragmented. The field organizes tools around individual languages or small families, building separate resources for each. This overlooks that Indic languages share a common morphosyntactic architecture through two millennia of convergence around Sanskrit, formalized in Pānini's Astādhyāyī.

Method: The paper proposes leveraging the Pāninian grammatical framework as a unifying computational architecture for Indic NLP. This involves designing a four-part benchmark suite grounded explicitly in Pāninian categories, rendering the shared morphosyntactic architecture explicit and measurable for practical applications.

Result: The paper is a position/vision paper proposing the benchmark suite; it does not report empirical results. It frames the expected outcomes as more accurate, data-efficient, and transferable Indic language systems by merging sparse resources into a single high-resource metalanguage bedrock.

Conclusion: Adopting a Pāninian-grounded benchmark suite can unify Indic NLP, improve data efficiency and cross-lingual transfer, and raises an interpretability question: whether neural models trained on these languages implicitly learn Pānini's categories.

Abstract: More than a billion people communicate in Indic languages, yet the natural language processing infrastructure serving them remains fragmented and underdeveloped. The cause is structural: the field organizes its tools and benchmarks around individual languages or small subsets of genealogical language families, building separate analyzers, parsers, and datasets for each language and starting over for the next. This overlooks a deep regularity. Through more than two millennia of convergence around Sanskrit, Indic languages came to share a morphosyntactic architecture formalized in Pānini's grammar, the Astādhyāyī. This cuts across genealogical lines, uniting languages through a common framework. We argue that this Pāninian framework supplies a unifying computational architecture the field has lacked, and that benchmarks grounded explicitly in it would make Indic language systems more accurate, more data-efficient, and more transferable, effectively merging many apparently disparate and sparse Indic language resources into a single high-resource metalanguage bedrock. We propose a four-part benchmark suite to render this shared architecture explicit, measurable, and ready to be leveraged for practical applications. Moreover, we underscore the question it raises for interpretability research: whether neural models trained on these languages come to represent Pānini's categories on their own.

</details>


### [20] [Harmonic: Hierarchical State Space Models for Efficient Long-Context Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24650)
*Petr Nyoma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Harmonic introduces a hierarchical state space model with three recurrent levels operating at different timescales, where each level is driven by the prediction error of the level below. It achieves strong language modeling performance, scales linearly with sequence length, and eliminates positional encoding limits when used as a drop-in replacement for attention in Transformer architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformers suffer from quadratic O(L²) complexity in sequence length and are bounded by positional encoding limits (e.g., RoPE). Recurrent models like Mamba offer linear complexity but struggle with very long contexts. The authors aim to design an architecture that achieves strong performance across a wide range of sequence lengths, scales linearly, and overcomes the context-length constraints imposed by positional encodings.

Method: Harmonic is a hierarchical state space model with three recurrent levels, each operating at progressively slower timescales. Critically, instead of passing raw hidden states upward, each higher level receives the prediction error (residual) of the level below. This predictive coding-inspired design forces each level to model what the lower level fails to capture. The authors also introduce HarmonicBlock, a module that can replace all attention layers in a standard Transformer (e.g., TinyLlama 1.1B), creating the "Hallamonic" model that does not require RoPE positional encoding.

Result: On enwiki8, Harmonic outperforms a comparable Transformer (28M params) by +1.4% at 1K, +6.7% at 8K, and +11.4% at 32K tokens (bpt). It beats Mamba by 0.7–1.8% at every tested length. At 64K tokens, only Harmonic trains successfully (6.169 bpt); both Transformer and Mamba OOM on an 80GB H100. At 1B scale, the Hallamonic variant (HarmonicBlock replacing attention in TinyLlama 1.1B) maintains stable loss from 1K–8K tokens on Lambada and fineweb-edu, while TinyLlama degrades catastrophically past its 2K RoPE limit (+9.4 bpt gap at 8K on Lambada).

Conclusion: Harmonic demonstrates that a hierarchical, prediction-error-driven state space model can match or exceed the long-context performance of Transformers and Mamba while maintaining O(L) complexity and eliminating positional encoding constraints. The architecture scales effectively from 28M to 1.1B parameters and offers a viable path toward arbitrarily long-context language models.

Abstract: We present Harmonic, a hierarchical state space model (SSM) for language modeling. The architecture stacks three recurrent levels at progressively slower timescales; each level receives the prediction error of the level below as input, rather than its raw hidden state. On enwiki8 with equal token budgets, Harmonic outperforms a comparable Transformer (28M params) by +1.4% at 1K tokens, +6.7% at 8K tokens, and +11.4% at 32K tokens (bpt, lower is better). It also outperforms Mamba at every tested length by 0.7--1.8%. At 64K tokens, both Mamba and Transformer run out of memory on an 80GB H100; Harmonic trains successfully, reaching 6.169 bpt. Results replicate on WikiText-103 (H-TF gap +1.7% to +7.2% across 1K--32K). At 1B parameter scale, replacing all attention layers in TinyLlama 1.1B with HarmonicBlock eliminates the RoPE positional encoding limit: the resulting Hallamonic model maintains stable loss across sequence lengths 1K--8K on two independent clean benchmarks (Lambada and fineweb-edu held-out), while TinyLlama degrades catastrophically past its 2K-token RoPE limit (gap: +9.4 bpt at seq=8K on Lambada). Compute is O(L) per forward pass vs. O(L^2) for attention.
  Logs: https://github.com/Omibranch/harmonic-logs.

</details>


### [21] [AI-PAVE-Br: Leveraging Large Language Models for Enhanced Product Attribute Value Extraction through a Golden Set Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24655)
*Murilo Gazzola,Hugo Gobato Souto,Samuel Silva,Júlia Schubert Peixoto,Felipe Siqueira,André Luis Pedroso de Morais,Caio Gomes*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces AI-PAVEBr, an LLM-based system for extracting product attribute values from Brazilian Portuguese e-commerce data, along with a new manually annotated benchmark dataset called the Golden Set. The system significantly outperforms traditional NER baselines using prompt engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Product Attribute Value Extraction (PAVE) methods struggle with the linguistic complexity and diversity of Portuguese product descriptions in Brazil's rapidly growing e-commerce sector. There is a lack of specialized systems and publicly available benchmark datasets for this language and domain.

Method: The authors developed AI-PAVEBr, a system built on Large Language Models with targeted prompt engineering for PAVE. They also created the Golden Set — a manually annotated dataset organized by Entity, Category, and Subcategories — to serve as a reproducible benchmark for Portuguese PAVE evaluation.

Result: AI-PAVEBr dramatically outperforms conventional Named Entity Recognition baselines, demonstrating that LLMs with tailored prompt engineering can effectively handle the linguistic nuances of Brazilian Portuguese product descriptions.

Conclusion: The work delivers a superior, scalable PAVE solution for the Brazilian e-commerce market and contributes a valuable publicly available resource (the Golden Set) to the NLP community for advancing non-English PAVE research.

Abstract: The explosive growth and complexity of product data within the dynamic Brazilian e-commerce landscape demand robust and specialized methods for structured information extraction. Traditional approaches to Product Attribute Value Extraction (PAVE) often struggle with the linguistic nuances and sheer diversity of product descriptions in Portuguese. To address this critical gap, this paper introduces two major contributions. First, we present AI-PAVEBr, a specialized system engineered with Large Language Models (LLMs) to perform high-accuracy PAVE specifically for Brazilian e-commerce catalogs. Second, to facilitate reproducible research and provide a definitive benchmark, we introduce and share the Golden Set, a new, meticulously curated, and manually annotated dataset for PAVE in Portuguese. We detail the creation process and structure (Entity, Category, Subcategories) of this high-quality reference set. Our experiments conclusively show that AI-PAVE-Br, leveraging targeted prompt engineering, dramatically outperforms conventional Named Entity Recognition (NER) baselines. This work not only delivers a superior, scalable solution for a major non-English market but also enriches the NLP community with a valuable, publicly available resource for future PAVE research.

</details>


### [22] [L3Cube-MahaPOS: A Marathi Part-of-Speech Tagging Dataset and BERT Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24825)
*Hariom Ingle,Ronit Ghode,Ishwari Gondkar,Jidnyasa Harad,Raviraj Joshi*

Main category: cs.CL
