TL;DR: The paper introduces L3Cube-MahaPOS, a gold-standard POS tagging dataset for Marathi with 32,354 manually annotated sentences. Six model families are benchmarked, with the best achieving 88.67% accuracy and 81.67% macro-F1. The dataset, guidelines, and models are publicly released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Marathi, spoken by 83+ million people, remains severely under-resourced in annotated corpora and evaluation benchmarks for POS tagging. Its rich morphology, free word order, lack of capitalisation, and pervasive Hindi-English code-mixing pose unique computational challenges that existing resources fail to address.

Method: Manual annotation of 32,354 news-domain sentences by Marathi-proficient annotators using a 16-tag Universal Dependencies-aligned scheme. A structured preprocessing pipeline applies Unicode normalisation, Devanagari-aware tokenisation, and noise filtering. Benchmarking spans six model families: HMM, CRF, BiLSTM, BiLSTM+CharCNN, MuRIL, and MahaBERT-v2.

Result: The best-performing system achieves 88.67% token-level accuracy and a macro-F1 of 81.67% across 15 evaluated tag classes.

Conclusion: The release of this gold-standard dataset, annotation guidelines, and trained checkpoints aims to spur further research in low-resource Marathi NLP, providing a robust benchmark for morphologically rich Indic languages.

Abstract: Part-of-Speech (POS) tagging is a foundational NLP task underpinning machine translation, information extraction, and syntactic parsing. Despite Marathi being spoken by over 83 million people and ranking among the top twenty most spoken languages worldwide, it remains severely under-resourced in annotated corpora and standardised evaluation benchmarks. Marathi presents unique challenges for computational modelling owing to its rich morphology, relatively free word order, lack of capitalisation conventions, and pervasive code-mixing with Hindi and English. We introduce L3Cube-MahaPOS, a gold-standard POS tagging dataset for Marathi comprising 32,354 manually annotated sentences drawn from news text. Annotation was performed entirely manually by a team of Marathi-proficient annotators following a 16-tag Universal Dependencies-aligned scheme. A structured preprocessing pipeline covering Unicode normalisation, Devanagari-aware tokenisation, and noise filtering ensures label consistency across all splits. We benchmark the dataset across six model families spanning HMM, CRF, BiLSTM, BiLSTM+CharCNN, MuRIL, and the Marathi-specific transformer MahaBERT-v2. The best system achieves 88.67\% token-level accuracy and a macro-F1 of 81.67% over 15 evaluated tag classes. We release the dataset, annotation guidelines, and trained model checkpoints to foster further research in Marathi NLP.

</details>

### [23] [Fifty Years of Specification Completeness: What Aviation Certification Tells AI Governance About Epoch Limits, Proof Surfaces, and the Structural Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25120)
*Christo Zietsman*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper argues that three structural governance requirements from aviation software certification (DO-178C/DO-330) — structured traceability, context-bounded revalidation triggers, and objective evidence architecture — are transferable to AI governance at the document level, even though they break down at the system level due to AI's non-determinism. It maps these aviation principles onto AI governance concepts (epoch limits, proof surfaces, structural completeness) and proposes a seven-principle framework (PromptQ) to operationalize them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aviation has enforced three structural governance requirements since 1992 that no existing AI governance framework requires as intrinsic properties of individual governance documents. System prompts, AGENTS.md files, and governance policies can be deployed without satisfying any of these requirements, despite aviation's standard-setting bodies acknowledging their frameworks break down for AI systems.

Method: The paper performs a conceptual mapping exercise: it takes DO-178C's traceability architecture, DO-330's requalification triggers, and DO-178C's objective evidence requirements and maps them onto three structural findings for AI governance — (1) epoch limits on governance document validity, (2) proof surfaces as a revalidation feedback mechanism, and (3) identification of absent structural completeness requirements in current AI governance instruments. An empirical companion paper provides quantitative evidence.

Result: The mapping yields three structural findings: epoch-bound validity for governance documents, proof surfaces as the feedback mechanism driving revalidation, and a demonstrated absence of structural completeness requirements in AI governance. The companion empirical study found 37% of AI governance documents fall below a structural quality threshold. The PromptQ seven-principle framework operationalizes the aviation-derived requirements at the governance document layer.

Conclusion: Aviation's structural requirements, while breaking down at the system level for non-deterministic AI, remain transferable at the static governance document level. The paper concludes that AI governance instruments should adopt epoch-limited validity, proof surfaces for revalidation, and explicit structural completeness requirements — principles operationalized through the proposed PromptQ framework.

Abstract: Aviation software certification has operationalised three structural requirements for governed software systems since 1992: structured governance linkage between governing specifications and operational evidence, context-bounded validity that triggers revalidation when operational context changes, and an objective evidence architecture that defines what proof means and what makes it sufficient. These requirements appear in DO-178C and DO-330 and are enforced through FAA and EASA certification.
  No existing framework requires these structural properties as intrinsic properties of individual AI governance documents. A system prompt, an AGENTS.md file, a governance policy, or a task envelope can be deployed without satisfying any of the three requirements aviation has enforced for three decades. Aviation is the most technically rigorous instance: its standard-setting bodies have acknowledged that their frameworks break down for AI systems, yet none requires these properties of individual governance documents.
  Aviation's structural requirements break down at the system level because AI systems are non-deterministic, but remain transferable at the document level: the governance artifact is a static artifact whose structural properties can be evaluated independently of the stochastic system it governs.
  The paper maps DO-178C's traceability architecture, DO-330's requalification triggers, and DO-178C's objective evidence requirements onto three structural findings: epoch limits on governance document validity, proof surfaces as the revalidation feedback mechanism, and the absence of structural completeness requirements in AI governance instruments. An empirical companion (arXiv:2604.21090) found that 37% of AI governance documents fall below the structural quality threshold. PromptQ's seven-principle framework operationalises these requirements at the governance document layer.

</details>


### [24] [LLM4MTLs: Automated Generation and Empirical Evaluation of Model Transformation Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25193)
*Bowen Jiang,Nathan Hagel,Haowei Cheng,Benedikt Jutz,Arne Lange,Weixing Zhang,Rahul Sharma,Ralf Reussner,Anne Koziolek*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper presents LLM4MTLs, a workflow for generating model transformation language (MTL) code using LLMs. It systematically evaluates prompting strategies (few-shot, grammar prompting, helper methods) across four MTLs and three LLMs, finding that few-shot prompting improves syntax more than semantics, grammar prompting works best alongside examples, and LLM choice has limited impact on semantic correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Developing correct model transformations is difficult due to the need for both language-specific and domain expertise. LLMs offer potential for automating MTL code generation, but limited training data and executable examples mean generated code is often syntactically invalid or semantically unusable. A systematic understanding of which prompting strategies work for MTL code generation is lacking.

Method: The authors design LLM4MTLs, an automated workflow that systematically constructs and compares prompting strategies for LLM-generated MTL code. The workflow combines few-shot prompting, grammar prompting, and helper method inclusion in various configurations. An evaluation suite is built spanning four MTLs (ATL, ETL, QVTo, Reactions) with executable reference scripts and manually written test suites. Evaluation is conducted across three LLMs using syntactic and semantic metrics.

Result: Few-shot prompting consistently improves syntactic quality across all four MTLs, but semantic correctness gains are uneven and language-dependent. For ATL, Pass@1 remains unchanged across all strategies and models. Grammar prompting stabilizes generation when combined with few-shot examples but can be ineffective or counterproductive in isolation. Helper methods act as a complementary amplifier. LLM choice influences syntactic correctness for ETL and QVTo but has limited effect on semantic correctness.

Conclusion: Few-shot prompting reliably improves surface-level syntax but not deep transformation semantics. Effective MTL code generation requires combining grammar prompting with few-shot examples rather than using either in isolation. The findings highlight a gap between syntactic and semantic correctness in LLM-generated MTL code, suggesting future work should target semantic reasoning in model transformations.

Abstract: Model transformation languages (MTLs) are domain-specific languages for transforming models conforming to a given metamodel into other models, including textual models such as source code. Developing correct model transformations is challenging, requiring both language-specific and domain knowledge, and motivating the use of large language models (LLMs) for MTL code generation. However, due to limited training data and executable examples, LLM-generated MTL code is often not syntactically valid or semantically usable out of the box. This paper presents LLM4MTLs, an automated workflow for constructing and comparing prompting strategies for LLM-generated MTL code, together with an evaluation suite and an empirical evaluation. The workflow systematically explores prompt constructions combining few-shot prompting, grammar prompting, and helper method inclusion, and evaluates them using syntactic and semantic metrics. We construct an evaluation suite spanning four MTLs (ATL, ETL, QVTo, and the Reactions language) with executable reference scripts and manually written test suites, and evaluate across three LLMs. We find that few-shot prompting consistently improves syntactic quality across all four MTLs while gains in semantic correctness are uneven and language-dependent. For ATL, Pass@1 remains unchanged across all strategies and models, indicating that few-shot prompting improves surface-level syntax more readily than deep transformation semantics. Grammar prompting stabilizes code generation when combined with few-shot examples, but in isolation it can be ineffective or even counterproductive for certain model-language combinations. Including helper methods as a complementary amplifier can also be beneficial. Finally, LLM choice influences syntactic correctness and similarity for certain MTLs, particularly ETL and QVTo, while its influence on semantic correctness remains limited.

</details>


### [25] [Tensor-Based Batch Fuzzing with Adaptive Perturbation Scaling for Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25239)
*Guanqin Zhang,Yulei Sui*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper proposes a specification-aware batch fuzzing framework for DNNs that replaces the traditional one-input-per-iteration sequential fuzzing with batched processing and introduces adaptive perturbation scaling based on specification-defined feasible ranges, achieving up to 40× higher throughput and 4× more violations than sequential baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DNN fuzzing frameworks suffer from two key limitations: (1) they inherit a one-input-per-iteration design from traditional software fuzzing, limiting testing throughput, and (2) they apply uniform perturbation budgets across all input dimensions, ignoring the heterogeneous feature scales of input spaces. Both issues hinder efficient and precise exploration of the input space for safety-critical DNN reliability assessment.

Method: The framework introduces two core innovations: (1) Adaptive perturbation scaling that derives per-dimension or global mutation step sizes from specification-defined feasible ranges (the gap between lower and upper bounds) using a shared scale factor, enabling effective exploration across heterogeneous feature scales. (2) Specification-aware batch fuzzing that embeds input constraints and output property checks directly into the network as non-trainable layers, creating a wrapped model that processes B specification instances in a single batched forward pass, dramatically improving fuzzing throughput.

Result: Evaluated on three benchmarks (TrafficSigns, Cifar100, TinyImageNet) covering six networks and over 400 specifications, the tensor-based fuzzing framework achieves up to 40× higher throughput and 4× more violations than the sequential baseline under the same time budget.

Conclusion: Batch fuzzing combined with adaptive, specification-aware perturbation scaling significantly outperforms traditional sequential fuzzing in both efficiency and effectiveness for DNN reliability testing. The approach is particularly well-suited for input spaces with heterogeneous feature scales and large specification sets.

Abstract: Deep neural networks are increasingly deployed in safety-critical domains such as autonomous driving and medical diagnosis, yet their opaque, high-dimensional parameter spaces make it difficult to systematically assess model reliability on unseen inputs. Existing coverage-guided sequential fuzzing frameworks for DNNs inherit a one-input-per-iteration design from traditional software fuzzing and apply uniform perturbation budgets across all input dimensions, limiting both testing throughput (i.e., inputs processed per unit time) and the precision of input-space exploration. We present a new specification-aware batch fuzzing framework with adaptive perturbation scaling that addresses both limitations. Rather than relying on a fixed global perturbation radius epsilon, our approach derives mutation step sizes from specification-defined feasible ranges (the gap between lower and upper bounds) using a shared scale factor. This scaling can be applied either as a global scalar (isotropic) or as per-dimension step sizes (anisotropic), keeping perturbations consistent with the underlying constraint structure. As a result, the fuzzer can explore input spaces with heterogeneous feature scales more effectively across all specifications in the batch. We embed input constraints and output property checks directly into the network as non-trainable layers, yielding a wrapped model that processes B specification instances in a single batched iteration, substantially improving fuzzing efficiency and counterexample exploration. We evaluate our framework extensively on three benchmarks, covering six networks and over 400 specifications across TrafficSigns, Cifar100, and TinyImageNet. Our tensor-based fuzzing achieves up to 40X higher throughput and 4X more violations than the sequential baseline under the same time budget, demonstrating significantly improved effectiveness in specification-guided fuzzing.

</details>


### [26] [How Do Developers Maintain and Evolve Their Agents' Instructions? An Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25257)
*Gianmario Voria,Alfonso Cannavale,Andrea De Lucia,Yutaro Kashiwa,Gemma Catolino,Fabio Palomba*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes a large-scale mining study to investigate how Agent Context Files (ACFs) evolve alongside code in agent-driven software development. The authors plan to classify ACF changes using a maintenance-theory-grounded taxonomy, analyze their relationship with code quality, and examine temporal patterns across the development lifecycle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous coding agents are increasingly integrated into software engineering workflows, with Agent Context Files (ACFs) serving as a key mechanism for guiding agent behavior. However, there is a gap in understanding how these ACFs are maintained, how they evolve, and what impact their evolution has on code quality and governance of agent-driven development.

Method: A large-scale mining study that combines repositories containing ACFs with agent-generated commits. ACF evolution is reconstructed at the commit level, changes are classified using a qualitative approach grounded in software maintenance theory, and their association with code quality metrics is analyzed using statistical methods and hypothesis testing.

Result: The abstract does not report empirical results, as this describes a planned study. The expected outcomes include a taxonomy of ACF changes, insights into associations between change types and code quality, and characterization of temporal patterns in ACF evolution.

Conclusion: The paper outlines a research plan to inform the future design of ACFs for governing autonomous coding agents, contributing both a classification framework and empirical evidence on how ACF maintenance practices relate to software quality outcomes.

Abstract: Context. Autonomous coding agents are increasingly used in software development, shifting parts of the engineering process to AI assistance. While this automation brings clear benefits, it introduces challenges in governance, traceability, and control over agent behavior. Agent Context Files (ACFs) have emerged as a practical mechanism to guide agents through structured instructions, yet little is known about how these artifacts are maintained and how their evolution relates to code development. Objective. This paper plans to investigate the evolution of ACFs and their role in agent-driven development. Specifically, we (1) classify ACF changes through a taxonomy grounded in software maintenance theory, (2) analyze how different types of changes are associated with code quality outcomes, and (3) examine their temporal patterns across the development lifecycle. Method. We conduct a large-scale mining study combining repositories with ACFs and agent-generated commits. We reconstruct ACF evolution at the commit level, classify changes using a qualitative approach, and analyze their association with code quality metrics. Statistical analyses and hypotheses are used to evaluate differences across maintenance categories, to inform future design of ACFs for governing autonomous coding agents.

</details>


### [27] [Semantic Code Clone Detection: Are We There Yet?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25272)
*Zhiwei Xu,Weixian Deng,Xuyang Liu,Xiaolin Peng,Jiabao Gao,Tian Qiu,Hai Wan,Xibin Zhao*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents the first systematic study evaluating the generalizability of state-of-the-art semantic (Type-4) code clone detectors. Using eight transformation operators derived from Type-2 and Type-3 clone variations, the authors construct distribution-shifted Type-4 clones and test 11 detectors on BigCloneBench. Results show substantial performance degradation, revealing that existing detectors rely on shortcut learning (lexical/structural cues) rather than true semantic understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite SOTA semantic clone detectors reporting remarkably high performance on established benchmarks, it is unclear whether these results reflect genuine semantic equivalence understanding or merely exploitation of dataset-specific patterns. The authors aim to rigorously test the generalizability of these detectors beyond standard benchmark evaluation settings.

Method: The authors propose a clone operator framework with eight transformation operators derived from Type-2 (systematic renaming) and Type-3 (statement insertion/deletion/ modification) clone variations. These operators generate distribution-shifted yet semantically equivalent Type-4 clone instances. They evaluate 11 representative detectors (token-based, tree-based, and graph-based) on the BigCloneBench dataset, measuring performance degradation between original and transformed test sets.

Result: All evaluated approaches exhibited substantial performance degradation when tested on the transformed, distribution-shifted Type-4 clones, despite their strong original benchmark performance. The degradation was consistent across token-based, tree-based, and graph-based paradigms. Further analysis confirmed that detectors heavily depend on shortcut learning — exploiting lexical and structural cues rather than developing robust semantic understanding of code.

Conclusion: Current SOTA semantic code clone detectors exhibit limited generalizability in real-world scenarios. The findings highlight that shortcut learning is pervasive across detection paradigms, and future research should focus on developing approaches capable of robust semantic reasoning rather than exploiting dataset-specific patterns.

Abstract: Code clone detection has been extensively studied for decades, and recent approaches have begun reporting remarkably high performance for semantic (Type-4) clones on benchmark datasets. However, it remains unclear whether these results reflect a genuine ability to capture semantic equivalence between programs, or simply an ability to exploit dataset-specific patterns.
  In this paper, we present the first systematic empirical study investigating the generalizability of state-of-the-art (SOTA) semantic code clone detectors beyond benchmark evaluation settings. Inspired by the inherent inclusion relationship among clone types, we propose a clone operator framework consisting of eight transformation operators derived from Type-2 and Type-3 clone variations. Using these operators, we construct distribution-shifted yet semantically equivalent Type-4 clone instances and evaluate 11 representative detectors spanning token-based, tree-based, and graph-based paradigms on the real-world BigCloneBench dataset. Our results reveal substantial performance degradation across all evaluated approaches, despite their strong benchmark performance. Further analyses show that existing detectors heavily rely on shortcut learning based on lexical and structural cues rather than robust semantic understanding. Our findings suggest that current SOTA semantic code clone detectors exhibit limited generalizability in real-world scenarios, highlighting important avenues for future research.

</details>


### [28] [LibEvoBench: Probing Temporal Knowledge Stratification in Code Generation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25402)
*Daniele Cipollone,Sergey Titov,Maliheh Izadi,Egor Bogomolov,Arie van Deursen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Current LLMs fail at version-specific API reasoning, making anachronistic errors when generating code for older library versions. The authors introduce LibEvoBench and the SEUS metric to measure this, finding that models are "version-oblivious" and only documentation — not version specification — improves accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large software projects often depend on older library versions with different APIs. LLMs, trained on temporally mixed data, lack mechanisms for version-specific reasoning, leading to anachronistic API calls. No systematic benchmark existed to quantify this failure mode.

Method: The authors introduce LibEvoBench, a multi-task benchmark spanning multiple versions of popular Python libraries (e.g., NumPy, pandas, TensorFlow), and the Software Evolution Understanding Score (SEUS), a metric that measures consistency when models work with evolving vs. stable APIs across versions. They evaluate state-of-the-art LLMs under varying conditions: no version info, version specified, and version-relevant documentation provided.

Result: State-of-the-art models are largely version-oblivious: accuracy degrades for evolving APIs across versions while remaining flat for stable APIs. Simply specifying the target version yields no benefit; however, providing relevant documentation significantly boosts accuracy.

Conclusion: Current training paradigms produce a systematic limitation in temporally grounded API knowledge. The findings motivate new approaches for version-aware code generation, suggesting that retrieval-augmented generation with versioned documentation may be a practical mitigation.

Abstract: Large software projects often depend on older versions of libraries, even as APIs continue to evolve across releases. This creates a challenge for LLMs: they must maintain knowledge of multiple API versions, not merely the latest or most common one. However, current LLMs are trained on temporally mixed corpora and lack explicit mechanisms for such version-specific reasoning, leading to anachronistic errors - calling APIs as they exist in a different library version. To systematically evaluate this phenomenon, we introduce LibEvoBench, a multi-task benchmark spanning multiple versions of widely used Python libraries, along with a new metric, the Software Evolution Understanding Score (SEUS), to measure models' consistency when working with evolving APIs. Our results show that state-of-the-art models are largely version-oblivious: performance degrades for evolving APIs, while for stable APIs it remains the same across versions. Moreover, simply specifying the target version provides no benefit, while relevant documentation significantly boosts models' accuracy. These findings highlight a systematic limitation of current training paradigms and motivate new approaches for temporally grounded knowledge in code generation.

</details>


### [29] [Project-wise Comparison of Software Birthmarks Using Weighted Partial Similarity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25418)
*Nikolay Fedorov,Akito Monden,Hiroki Inayoshi,Haruaki Tamada,Masateru Tsunoda*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a project-level software birthmark comparison framework that uses symmetric aggregation of module similarities, enhanced with a weighting scheme to mitigate noise from small modules and a partial similarity method to detect partial code reuse, outperforming existing approaches on 35 Java projects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing software birthmark similarity measures operate at the module level (source/class files), but real-world code reuse occurs at the project level where only a subset of modules may be reused. This creates two challenges: (1) partial reuse detection when reused modules form a small fraction of the project, and (2) false positives from incidental similarity among small, trivial modules.

Method: The authors establish a project-wise birthmark comparison framework using symmetric aggregation of module-level similarities. Two complementary mechanisms are proposed: (1) a weighting scheme that assigns higher importance to larger modules to suppress noise from small-module matches, and (2) a partial similarity method that focuses only on the top fraction of highly similar module pairs for robust partial reuse detection. Evaluation uses 35 open-source Java projects across ten categories, with different versions as reuse cases.

Result: The proposed method consistently outperforms existing approaches when evaluated using the harmonic mean of resilience and credibility (two core birthmark properties). It achieves robust and stable detection of partial code reuse at the project level.

Conclusion: The project-level birthmark comparison framework, augmented with size-based weighting and partial similarity focusing, effectively addresses the challenges of partial reuse and incidental small-module similarity, providing a more practical and reliable solution for detecting code plagiarism in real-world scenarios.

Abstract: Software birthmarks provide a robust approach to detecting code plagiarism even under substantial modifications, while distinguishing independently developed software. Existing similarity measures are typically applied at the module level (e.g., source or class files). However, in practice, software reuse often occurs at the project level, where only a subset of modules may be reused. This setting introduces two key challenges: (1) partial reuse, where reused modules constitute only a small fraction of the project, and (2) incidental similarity from small modules, which can lead to false positives.
  In this paper, we establish a framework for project-wise birthmark comparison based on a symmetric aggregation of module-level similarities. On top of this framework, we propose two complementary mechanisms to address the above challenges. First, we introduce a weighting scheme that assigns higher importance to larger modules, reducing the influence of noisy matches from small modules. Second, we propose a partial similarity method that focuses on the top fraction of highly similar module pairs, enabling robust detection of partial reuse.
  We evaluate the proposed approach on 35 open-source Java projects across ten categories, where different versions of the same project are treated as reuse cases. The dataset and experimental artifacts are made publicly available to support reproducibility. Performance is assessed using two complementary properties of software birthmarks, resilience and credibility, combined via their harmonic mean. The results show that the proposed method consistently outperforms existing approaches, achieving robust and stable detection of partial code reuse at the project level.

</details>


### [30] [Unlocking Model Potentials Through Adaptive Multi-Agent Scaffolding for Efficient Issue Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25514)
*Yang Chen,Aliya Ahmad,Yiheng Zhou,Reyhaneh Jabbarvand*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: icat-agent is a decentralized multi-agent system for automated bug fixing that replaces shared context with event-based message passing. It uses a rubric-based quality check to pivot between parallel patching (for clear issues) and exploratory workflows (for ambiguous ones). It outperforms existing baselines (SWE-agent, mini-SWE-agent, Claude Code) by 3.6–18.5% on SWE-bench benchmarks while reducing costs by $1.18 per instance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based bug-fixing agents rely on shared context, which leads to context degradation and context poisoning over long-horizon, complex debugging workflows—ultimately preventing successful resolution of ambiguous or incomplete bug reports.

Method: icat-agent employs a decentralized, multi-agent scaffolding architecture with synchronous, event-based message passing instead of shared context. It incorporates a rubric-based issue quality assessment that strategically routes workflows: high-quality issues trigger parallel patching and validation, while low-quality issues trigger preliminary exploration to gather missing information before attempting a fix.

Result: On SWE-bench Verified, icat-agent outperforms baselines by 3.6–8.4%; on SWE-bench Pro, by 6.3–18.5%. It reduces average cost by $1.18/instance versus multi-agent Claude Code. When paired with GPT-5.4-xhigh, it resolves 67.4% of SWE-bench Pro problems, surpassing the prior state-of-the-art (59.10%) by 8.3 percentage points.

Conclusion: A robust scaffolding architecture like icat-agent unlocks substantial latent capability in fixed models, enabling the same backbone LLM to resolve significantly more issues than under existing scaffolds. Decentralized, message-based coordination proves superior to shared-context approaches for long-horizon software engineering tasks.

Abstract: Resolving issues with ambiguous and incomplete descriptions, particularly concerning complex bugs, requires a sophisticated, long-horizon workflow. Agents must navigate codebases to locate the root cause, reproduce the failure, implement a fix, and validate the resulting patch. Inefficient context management, thereby, can lead to rapid context degradation and context poisoning, preventing successful resolution. We propose icat-agent, a decentralized, multi-agent scaffolding that replaces shared context with synchronous, event-based message passing. Utilizing a rubric-based issue quality check, icat-agent strategically pivots its workflow: it initiates parallel patching and validation for well-defined issues, while deploying preliminary exploration for low-quality ones. A comprehensive evaluation of icat-agent on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro demonstrates that it consistently outperforms prominent baselines across all difficulty levels, including SWE-agent, mini-SWE-agent, and Claude Code, while using the same underlying models, improving by 3.6-8.4% on SWE-bench Verified and 6.3-18.5% on SWE-bench Pro. icat-agent is also computationally efficient, reducing the average cost by $1.18 per instance compared with the multi-agent Claude Code baseline. Our findings reveal that a robust scaffold such as icat-agent unlocks substantial latent capability within a fixed model, with the same backbone resolving markedly more issues under icat-agent than under existing scaffolds. icat-agent +GPT-5.4-xhigh resolves 67.4% of SWE-bench Pro problems, outperforming the current best result on SWE-bench Pro (59.10%, mini-SWE-agent+GPT-5.4-xhigh) by 8.3 percentage points.

</details>


### [31] [The impact of artificial intelligence on enterprise software user roles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25525)
*Isabel Unger,Elizangela Valarini,Martin Schrepp,Nina Hollender,Gabriela Rocha,Erik Bertram*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This qualitative study (n=20 interviews, n=24 workshop) examines how AI is reshaping software development roles and workflows on SAP's BTP. Findings reveal automation of operational tasks, increased human-AI collaboration, and agentic AI reliance, necessitating revised role taxonomies, new governance functions, and updated design approaches for enterprise platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI is fundamentally transforming software development work, yet enterprise platforms lack updated frameworks to capture shifting professional responsibilities, workflows, and user roles. The study addresses the gap between existing static role taxonomies (like SAP's BTP User Type Matrix) and the rapidly evolving AI-driven work environment.

Method: A qualitative multi-method design combining 20 expert interviews and one participatory workshop with 24 participants, all situated within the context of SAP's Business Technology Platform (BTP). Thematic analysis was applied to identify patterns in how AI alters professional responsibilities and user-role frameworks.

Result: Three key shifts were identified: (1) increasing automation of operational tasks, (2) expanding human-AI collaboration, and (3) growing reliance on agentic AI systems. These shifts expose significant limitations in existing frameworks like the BTP User Type Matrix, revealing a workforce in transition requiring updated taxonomies and governance structures.

Conclusion: Enterprise software platforms must adapt through revised role taxonomies, new governance and oversight functions, and AI-native design approaches. The findings underscore that AI adoption is not merely a technical shift but demands socio-technical reconfiguration of professional roles and collaboration patterns.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) is rapidly reshaping the nature of work in software development, transforming user roles, workflows, and collaboration patterns across enterprise platforms. This qualitative study investigates how AI alters professional responsibilities within the context of SAP's Business Technology Platform (BTP), combining expert interviews (n=20) and a participatory workshop (n=24). The results reveal substantial shifts in day-to-day tasks and roles in the development domain, characterized by increasing automation of operational tasks, expanding human-AI collaboration, and growing reliance on agentic AI systems. The study further identifies significant implications for existing user-role frameworks, such as the BTP User Type Matrix, which requires adaptation as the workforce is undergoing significant role specific changes. Collectively, these findings highlight a workforce landscape in transition and underscore the need for revised role taxonomies, new governance and oversight functions, and updated design approaches for AI-native enterprise software systems.

</details>


### [32] [Evaluating LLMs on Real-World Software Performance Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25530)
*Ezgi Sarıkayak,Wenchao Gu,Hesham Ghonim,Chunyang Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SWE-Pro is a repository-level benchmark with 102 real-world optimization tasks, evaluating LLMs on runtime, peak memory, and TWMU under noise-aware conditions. Current LLMs fail to produce meaningful optimizations, while expert implementations achieve 15.5x speedup and 171.3x memory reduction, exposing a large capability gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM code optimization benchmarks oversimplify by focusing on isolated functions or single metrics, ignoring the trade-offs between execution time and memory, measurement noise, and input variability. There is no benchmark capturing how optimization actually occurs in real-world, multi-file codebases.

Method: The authors construct SWE-Pro, a benchmark derived from 102 expert-written performance optimizations mined from open-source projects. Each task includes parameterized tests that evaluate runtime, peak memory, and Time-Weighted Memory Usage (TWMU) across varying inputs and execution conditions, using noise-aware measurement to account for environmental variability.

Result: Current LLMs show negligible runtime improvements and near-zero memory optimization. In contrast, expert implementations achieve 15.5x aggregate speedup and 171.3x peak memory reduction. Expert-written improvements manifest in 91.2% of tasks for runtime and 65.7% for peak memory, revealing a substantial gap between LLM and expert performance.

Conclusion: SWE-Pro establishes that real-world, repository-level performance optimization remains far beyond current LLM capabilities, and the benchmark provides a rigorous platform for measuring progress toward expert-level automated code optimization.

Abstract: Software performance optimization is a notoriously complex and manual task. Despite the growing use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for code refinement, we still lack benchmarks that capture how optimization actually happens in real-world codebases. Existing frameworks often oversimplify the problem by focusing on isolated functions or a single performance metric, missing the critical trade-offs between execution time and memory footprint, the inherent noise of the measurement environment, and the variability introduced by different input data and execution conditions. We address this by introducing SWE-Pro, a repository-level benchmark derived from 102 expert-written optimizations from open-source projects. Unlike previous benchmarks, SWE-Pro pairs each task with parameterized tests to evaluate runtime, peak memory, and Time-Weighted Memory Usage (TWMU) across varying input data and execution conditions under noise-aware measurement conditions. Our evaluation shows that current LLMs struggle significantly: runtime gains are negligible, and memory optimizations are nearly non-existent. This stands in sharp contrast to expert implementations, which achieve an aggregate speedup of 15.5x and peak memory reduction of 171.3x over benchmark tasks. Expert-written improvements are observed in 91.2% of tasks for runtime and 65.7% for peak memory. Our findings expose a substantial gap between current LLM capabilities and the demands of expert-level engineering.

</details>


### [33] [On the Viability of Requirements Generation From Code: An Experience Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25550)
*Alexander Korn,Jone Bartel,Max Unterbusch,Andreas Vogelsang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This experience report evaluates an LLM-based, RAG-supported agentic approach for generating requirements from source code to create synthetic datasets for empirical RE research. The approach failed on three key hypotheses: LLMs could not reliably generate non-implemented requirements, produce high-quality requirements, or introduce synthetic requirements smells. The authors conclude that LLM-based code-to-requirements dataset generation is not yet viable without substantial human supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Empirical Requirements Engineering (RE) research suffers from a scarcity of datasets that pair source code with corresponding requirements. Synthesizing requirements from existing code using LLMs appears to be a promising solution to this data bottleneck, enabling broader and more robust empirical studies in RE.

Method: The authors employed an LLM-based, RAG-supported agentic pipeline that: (1) generates requirements from source code, (2) verifies implementation status via a human-in-the-loop, and (3) synthetically introduces requirements smells and non-implemented requirements. The approach was evaluated against its ability to produce realistic, high-quality datasets suitable for empirical RE research.

Result: All three core hypotheses were refuted: (i) LLMs failed to reliably generate non-implemented requirements; (ii) LLMs could not produce high-quality requirements; (iii) LLMs could not reliably introduce synthetic requirements smells. Additionally, neither an LLM nor a single human-in-the-loop alone could reliably detect requirements smells.

Conclusion: LLM-based generation of code-to-requirements datasets is not yet viable and demands human supervision, particularly for quality assurance. The paper provides critical reflections and actionable lessons for researchers and practitioners attempting similar synthesis approaches.

Abstract: Empirical research in Requirements Engineering is hampered by a lack of adequate datasets that pair source code with corresponding requirements. A tempting route to addressing this lack is the use of Large Language Models to synthesize requirements from existing code bases. We investigate this question by evaluating an LLM-based and RAG-supported agentic approach that generates requirements from source code, verifies their implementation status relying on a human-in-the-loop, and synthetically introduces requirements smells and non-implemented requirements. Our goal was to create datasets that mimic reality and foster empirical RE research. However, during the study, various problems arose, leading to this experience report. Contrary to our initial hypotheses, LLMs were unable to (i) generate non-implemented requirements reliably, (ii) generate high quality requirements, and (iii) reliably introduce synthetic requirements smells. Furthermore, neither an LLM nor a single human-in-the-loop suffices to detect requirements smells reliably. These findings suggest that the generation of code-to-requirements datasets using LLMs is not yet viable and requires human supervision, especially for quality assurance. We critically reflect on our lessons learned and draw relevant conclusions for both researchers and practitioners.

</details>


### [34] [IntentTester: Intent-Driven Multi-agent Framework for Cross-Library Test Migration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25588)
*Yi Gao,Ziyuan Zhang,Xing Hu,Xiaohu Yang,Xin Xia*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: IntentTester is a multi-agent LLM-based framework that abstracts unit tests into a language-agnostic Test Description Language (TDL), aligns them semantically with a repository graph, and synthesizes executable tests, enabling cross-library and cross-language test reuse with 85% correctness vs. 51% and 43% for baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unit tests encapsulate valuable domain knowledge, but existing migration approaches rely on structural code mappings (e.g., API signatures) that fail under divergent designs or cross-language settings, producing non-executable tests. There is a need to unlock and reuse this knowledge across libraries and languages through semantic rather than syntactic alignment.

Method: IntentTester uses a multi-agent framework that first abstracts source tests into a language-agnostic Test Description Language (TDL), then aligns these descriptions with semantically related entities in a repository graph of the target library, and finally synthesizes executable tests via LLM-guided reasoning with iterative validation.

Result: Evaluated on nine open-source projects across three domains (JSON, HTML, Time) and two languages (Java, Python), IntentTester generated 2,776 syntactically correct tests with 85% correctness (vs. 51% and 43% for two baselines). Of these, 2,410 executed successfully (74% effectiveness). It also uncovered previously unknown bugs including stack overflows, null dereferences, and parsing inconsistencies, several acknowledged or patched by maintainers.

Conclusion: Intent-driven migration shifts focus from code-level mappings to semantic alignment, making practical cross-library and cross-language test reuse feasible while simultaneously improving test quality and exposing latent implementation flaws.

Abstract: Unit tests capture both functional checks and domain-specific knowledge, but this knowledge remains locked within individual projects and is rarely reused across libraries with overlapping functionality. Existing migration techniques based on structural code mappings (e.g., API signatures) often break down under divergent designs or cross-language settings, resulting in non-executable migrated tests. In this paper, we present IntentTester, a multi-agent framework for intent-driven test reuse. Instead of translating raw code, IntentTester abstracts tests into a language-agnostic Test Description Language (TDL), aligns them with semantically related entities and dependencies in a repository graph, and synthesizes executable tests through LLM-guided reasoning and iterative validation. This design enables cross-library and cross-language migration without manual intervention, producing migrated tests that existing structure-mapping approaches cannot achieve. We evaluate IntentTester on nine open-source projects across three domains (JSON, HTML, and Time) and two languages (Java and Python). IntentTester generates 2,776 syntactically correct tests with 85\% correctness; in comparison, the two baselines achieve 51\% and 43\%. Among them, 2,410 tests executed successfully, yielding a 74\% effectiveness rate. Beyond higher success rates, IntentTester also surfaced previously unknown defects including stack overflows, null dereferences, and parsing inconsistencies, several of which have been acknowledged or patched by maintainers. Our results show that intent-driven migration shifts the focus from code mappings to semantic alignment, allowing practical cross-library and cross-language test reuse while improving test quality and exposing implementation flaws.

</details>


### [35] [CodeChat-Eval: Evaluating Large Language Models in Multi-Turn Code Refinement Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25747)
*Guoxiang,Guo,Kla Tantithamthavorn,Neelofar Neelofar,Yuanyuan Qi,Aldeida Aleti*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LLMs exhibit a statistically significant drop in functional correctness (19.2%–69.2%) across multi-turn code refinement dialogues, particularly during logic-level and additive changes, revealing a critical evaluation gap unaddressed by single-turn benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing code generation benchmarks evaluate only single-turn generation, ignoring real-world multi-turn refinement dialogues. This leaves a blind spot regarding whether LLMs can preserve functional correctness when iteratively modifying code in response to follow-up instructions.

Method: The authors introduce CodeChat-Eval, an evaluation framework that constructs multi-turn code refinement sessions using a dynamic instruction selection algorithm. They conduct an empirical study across both open-weight and proprietary LLMs, measuring functional correctness (pass@k on original test suites) after each refinement turn.

Result: Functional correctness degrades significantly across all models, with drops from 19.2% (GPT-5 Nano) to 69.2% (Llama 3.1 8B). The largest regressions occur with logic-level refinements and additive change requests, indicating LLMs fail to reliably preserve intended behavior during iterative editing.

Conclusion: LLMs struggle to maintain functional correctness in multi-turn refinement settings, underscoring the urgent need for benchmarks that evaluate functionality-preserving refinement rather than single-shot generation alone.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in software engineering to generate and refine code. In practice, developers often continue from an initial code generation request with follow-up refinement instructions, such as requests to improve style, restructure implementation, or change the execution strategy while preserving the intended behaviour. However, existing benchmarks generally omit this multi-turn code refinement dialogue setting and therefore cannot evaluate whether LLMs maintain functional correctness, i.e., whether the refined code still passes the test suite for the original task. To address this limitation, we introduce CodeChat-Eval, an evaluation framework that constructs evaluation sessions from multi-turn code refinement dialogues using a dynamic instruction selection algorithm. Our empirical study on open-weight and proprietary LLMs observes a statistically significant decrease ranging from 19.2% (GPT-5 Nano) to 69.2% (Llama 3.1 8B) in functional correctness over multi-turn refinement. The largest correctness drops are associated with logic-level refinements and additive change requests. These findings indicate that LLMs struggle to maintain functional correctness during multi-turn code refinement dialogues, and highlight the need for benchmarks that evaluate functionality-preserving refinement beyond single-turn generation.

</details>


### [36] [Automated Detection of Configuration-Specific Security Vulnerabilities via Patch Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25863)
*Felipe de Sant'Anna Paixão,Joanna C. S. Santos,Paulo Anselmo da Mota Silveira Neto,Daniel Sadoc Menasche,Gustavo Bittencourt Figueiredo,Eduardo Santana de Almeida*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: PatchLens is a static analysis technique that automatically computes Vulnerability Impact Conditions (VICs) — Boolean predicates over compile-time configuration options identifying which software variants contain a given vulnerability. Evaluated on 1,192 Linux kernel, 289 FFmpeg, and 100 PHP patches, it produces compact, human-readable predicates without compilation, revealing that most vulnerabilities affect only specific configurations and that CVE descriptions rarely encode configuration information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Highly configurable C/C++ systems (like the Linux kernel) have compile-time options that determine which code is included in a given build. Security patches fix vulnerabilities, but it is unknown which specific configurations (variants) are affected. Current CVE descriptions almost never encode this information (~1% recall), and existing approaches require compilation or manual analysis. There is a need for automated, static, and scalable identification of which compile-time variants contain a vulnerability.

Method: PatchLens formalizes the Vulnerability Impact Condition (VIC) as a Boolean predicate over configuration options. It works purely statically in three steps: (1) aligning AST-level patch hunks (from git diffs) with source-level presence conditions (from the code's conditional compilation structure, e.g., #ifdef blocks), (2) resolving file inclusion via lightweight build system analysis (Kbuild for Linux, custom for FFmpeg/PHP) to propagate conditions across compilation units, and (3) computing the VIC as the weakest condition under which the patched code is included, representing all affected variants.

Result: On 1,192 Linux kernel patches, VICs average 1.84 variables; on 289 FFmpeg patches, 3.23; on 100 PHP patches, 1.04 — showing compact, interpretable predicates. Only a small fraction of vulnerabilities are system-wide (unconditionally compiled); these carry higher CVSS scores. Analysis of CVE texts shows ~1% average recall for configuration option mentions, confirming near-total absence of variant information in vulnerability disclosures. The approach requires no compilation and produces human-readable Boolean expressions.

Conclusion: PatchLens enables automated, scalable, and explainable variant-aware vulnerability assessment for highly configurable software. The computed VICs support CI applications (variant-aware triage, test selection), targeted sampling and fuzzing, and feature risk scoring. The authors advocate for enriching CVE descriptions with VICs to improve vulnerability management in configurable systems.

Abstract: We study how security patches in highly configurable C/C++ systems map onto the space of compile-time variants. We formalize the Vulnerability Impact Condition (VIC) - a Boolean predicate over configuration options that denotes all variants that contained the original flaw - and introduce PatchLens, a purely static technique that recovers VICs by aligning AST-level patch hunks with source-level presence conditions and resolving file inclusion via lightweight build system analysis. Evaluating PatchLens on 1,192 Linux kernel, 289 FFmpeg, and 100 PHP patches, we compute precise, human-readable VICs without the need to compile any system variant. The resulting predicates are compact (avg. 1.84 variables for Linux, 3.23 for FFmpeg, 1.04 for PHP) and show that only a small fraction of vulnerabilities are system-wide, which carry higher CVSS scores; meanwhile, CVE texts almost never encode the required options ($\approx$ 1% average recall), motivating automated enrichment of CVE descriptions with VICs. PatchLens and the accompanying dataset enable immediate applications in CI (variant-aware triage and test selection), targeted sampling and fuzzing, and feature risk scoring, offering a scalable, explainable path to vulnerability assessment in highly configurable software.

</details>


### [37] [LLM-Based Discovery of Latent Requirements from Stakeholder Conversations: Preliminary Results from Industry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25867)
*Mithila Sivakumar,Martin Lochner,Shiva Nejati,Mehrdad Sabetzadeh*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LENS is a tool that uses LLMs to analyze stakeholder interview transcripts, extracting explicit requirements and inferring latent (implicit) requirements as user stories with traceability links. Evaluated on 12 cybersecurity interview transcripts, it achieved 84.4% F1 for explicit extraction and 75% of inferred latent requirements were deemed useful by domain experts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stakeholder interviews are critical for requirements elicitation, but many relevant requirements remain implicit — stakeholders describe workflows and challenges without articulating software capabilities. Existing LLM-based approaches focus only on explicitly stated requirements, leaving latent opportunities unexplored.

Method: LENS analyzes stakeholder interview transcripts using LLMs to (1) extract explicitly stated requirements and (2) infer latent requirements by reasoning over stakeholder statements combined with contextual information about organizational tools and infrastructure. Outputs are formatted as user stories and linked to transcript excerpts for traceability.

Result: In a preliminary evaluation using 12 cybersecurity interview transcripts from an industrial setting: LENS achieved an average F1-score of 84.4% for explicit requirement extraction, and 75% of the latent requirements inferred by LENS were perceived by domain experts as providing useful automation or time-saving potential.

Conclusion: LENS demonstrates that LLMs can effectively go beyond extracting explicit requirements to infer latent needs from stakeholder interviews, with strong performance and practical utility validated by domain experts in a cybersecurity context.

Abstract: Stakeholder interviews are an important source of information for requirements elicitation, yet many relevant requirements remain implicit in such conversations. Stakeholders frequently describe workflows, challenges, and operational practices without explicitly articulating the software capabilities that could address them. Recent work has considered the use of LLMs to analyze conversational data and extract requirements from stakeholder interviews. Existing approaches, however, primarily focus on identifying explicitly stated requirements, leaving implicit opportunities largely unexplored. In this paper, we present LENS (LLM-Enabled Needs Discovery from Stakeholder Interviews), an approach that analyzes stakeholder interview transcripts to both extract explicit requirements and infer additional latent requirements. LENS performs this inference by reasoning over stakeholder statements together with contextual information about organizational tools and infrastructure. Both extracted and inferred requirements are represented as user stories and linked to transcript excerpts to ensure traceability. We conduct a preliminary evaluation of LENS using twelve stakeholder interview transcripts collected in an industrial setting involving cybersecurity operations. We show that LENS achieves an average F1-score of 84.4% for extracting explicit requirements, while, on average, 75% of the latent requirements identified by LENS were perceived as providing useful automation or time-saving potential by domain experts.

</details>


### [38] [The Web4 Agent Economy: A Large-Scale Empirical Study of the Landscape, Challenges, and Opportunities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25876)
*Yuhan Jin,Shuohan Wu,Chong Chen,Lingfeng Bao,Xiaohu Yang,Jiachi Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: First large-scale empirical study of the emerging Web4 autonomous agent ecosystem. Analyzes 99K+ identity registrations, 317M+ transactions, 341 MCP repositories, and 349 GitHub issues to reveal a booming machine-to-machine payment economy built on fragile infrastructure, with payment interoperability as the most persistent bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite growing industrial interest in Web4 agent protocols (MCP, x402, EIP-8004), the real-world Web4 agent ecosystem remains largely underexplored. There is a critical need to empirically characterize how agents are deployed, what challenges developers face, and how communities respond.

Method: Large-scale empirical study across four data sources: (1) 99,448 multi-chain identity registrations, (2) 317,596,323 on-chain transaction logs, (3) source code of 341 MCP (Model Context Protocol) projects, and (4) 349 filtered GitHub issues. Analysis structured around three research questions concerning deployment patterns, engineering challenges, and community responses.

Result: Key findings: (1) Autonomous agents have established a highly active machine-to-machine payment economy with millions of daily transactions; (2) This growth rests on immature infrastructure across three dimensions: identity/authorization practices, cross-environment operation, and payment interoperability; (3) Community responses to these challenges are visible but unevenly distributed across repositories, with payment interoperability remaining the most persistent unresolved bottleneck.

Conclusion: There is a critical gap between the rapid growth of the Web4 agent economy and its fragile underlying infrastructure. The paper highlights future research directions for building a more secure Web4 agent ecosystem, particularly around payment interoperability, robust identity/authorization, and cross-environment operational standards.

Abstract: The Internet is transitioning from Web3 toward Web4, where autonomous agents serve as independent economic actors. These agents can now hold crypto wallets, execute on-chain trades, and pay for external API calls. This transition calls for a new infrastructure stack capable of supporting key agent operations, including agent-to-tool interaction, agent-to-agent payments, and verifiable agent identity, represented by emerging protocols such as the Model Context Protocol, x402, and EIP-8004. Despite growing industrial interest in these protocols, the real-world Web4 agent ecosystem remains largely underexplored. To bridge this gap, we conduct the first large-scale empirical study of the Web4 ecosystem. Specifically, our study targets three interconnected questions: how Web4 agents are deployed and used in practice; what engineering challenges developers face when building Web4 agents; how current project communities respond to these challenges. To answer these questions, we analyze 99,448 multi-chain identity registrations, 317,596,323 transaction logs, the source code of 341 MCP projects, and 349 filtered GitHub issues. Our findings reveal that autonomous agents have established a highly active machine-to-machine payment economy, processing millions of daily transactions. However, this growth is built on immature infrastructure, including identity/authorization practice, cross-environment operation, and payment interoperability. Our follow-up analysis shows that community responses are visible but unevenly distributed across repositories, and payment interoperability remains the most persistent unresolved bottleneck. Overall, this study reveals a critical gap between the rapid growth of the Web4 agent economy and its fragile underlying infrastructure, highlighting future directions for building a more secure Web4 agent ecosystem.

</details>


### [39] [The Dependency Black Hole](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25949)
*Ehsan Zabardast,Bhuwan Paudel,Javier Gonzalez-Huerta*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: An industrial case study of 267 microservices reveals a recurring "black hole" dependency pattern — a dense core of dependency magnets, a transitional region, and a lightly connected outer region. The authors propose the Dependency Black Hole theory, mapping network anatomy to singularity, event horizon, and accretion disk, with three hypotheses on how dependency concentration emerges and evolves at scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Microservice architectures promise independent evolution via loose coupling, yet large-scale systems often exhibit strong dependency concentration around a small set of services. The authors seek to understand, characterize, and theorize about this emergent phenomenon to enable reasoning about dependency growth and guiding governance interventions.

Method: An exploratory industrial case study of a 267-microservice product, triangulating three dependency signals (compile-time, run-time, and task dependencies). Findings were iteratively validated with practitioners. The observed macro-structure was mapped metaphorically to black hole anatomy (singularity, event horizon, accretion disk), yielding a grounded theory with three testable hypotheses.

Result: The dependency network exhibited a consistent three-zone structure: (1) a dense core of "dependency magnets," (2) a transitional region of services increasingly entangled with the core, and (3) an outer region of lightly connected services. This led to the Dependency Black Hole theory and three hypotheses about how dependency concentration emerges and evolves.

Conclusion: The Dependency Black Hole theory offers an explanatory lens for reasoning about dependency growth, identifying services at risk of becoming dependency magnets, and motivating governance interventions. The authors outline practical implications and call for longitudinal, multi-case validation to strengthen the theory.

Abstract: Microservice architectures promise independent evolution through loose coupling, yet large systems often exhibit strong dependency concentration around a small set of services. In an exploratory industrial case study of a product composed of 267 microservices, we triangulated multiple dependency signals -- compile-time, run-time, and task dependencies -- and iteratively validated our interpretations with practitioners. We observed a recurring macro-structure in the dependency network that resembles a black hole: a dense core of dependency magnets, a transitional region of services increasingly entangled with the core, and an outer region of lightly connected services. Based on these observations, we propose the dependency black hole theory, mapping the network to the black hole anatomy of a singularity, an event horizon, and an accretion disk, and formulating three hypotheses about how dependency concentration emerges and evolves at scale. The theory provides an explanatory lens for reasoning about dependency growth, identifying services at risk of becoming dependency magnets, and motivating governance interventions. We outline practical implications and directions for longitudinal and multi-case validation.

</details>


### [40] [Helpful or Harmful? Evaluating LLM-Assisted Vulnerability Patching via a Human Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25973)
*Giulian Biolo,Michael Tezza,Yuanjun Gong,Fabio Massacci*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes a controlled experiment using a Balanced Crossover design to empirically compare LLM-assisted vulnerability patching against manual debugging, employing hidden Ghost Tests to assess both functional and security patch integrity beyond superficial repairs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General developers often lack the specialized security expertise required for software vulnerability remediation. While LLM-assisted tools show promise in vulnerability detection and repair, they risk introducing hallucinations or insecure code that passes functional checks but fails security validation. There is a need for rigorous empirical evidence comparing LLM-assisted versus manual patching in real-world scenarios.

Method: A controlled experiment using a Balanced Crossover design, implemented via a custom WebApp for code execution. Hidden Ghost Tests are integrated to verify patch integrity beyond visible functional requirements. The experiment includes both training and evaluation scenarios, measuring remediation speed, remediation efficacy (for standard functionality tests and security tests), and participant perception.

Result: A pilot experiment with a small sample of participants has been conducted, providing preliminary insights for the subsequent full-scale study. Specific quantitative results from the pilot are not detailed in this abstract.

Conclusion: The paper presents the experimental design and pilot study results as groundwork for a larger empirical investigation. The full experiment aims to unveil the true capability and limitations of LLM-assisted vulnerability patching compared to manual debugging, particularly regarding the risk of superficial repairs that evade security validation.

Abstract: Software vulnerability remediation is a cognitively demanding task that requires specialized security expertise often lacking in general developers. In the meantime, Large Language Models (LLMs) assisted tools show potential in vulnerability detection, location, and repair tasks. [Hypothesis:] While LLM-assistance is hypothesized to accelerate patching, it also risks introducing hallucinations or insecure code, leading to a higher likelihood of generating superficial repairs that bypass the standard functionality checks but fail the security validation. [Objective:] We aim to present an empirical experiment, unveiling the capability of LLM-assisted vulnerability patching compared to manual debugging on human participants in real-world scenarios. [Method:] We plan to conduct a controlled experiment using a Balanced Crossover design. For that, we have developed a WebApp for code execution and integrated hidden Ghost Tests to verify patch integrity beyond visible functional requirements. The experiment involves training and evaluation scenarios. The remediation speed, remediation efficacy for both standard functionality tests and security tests, and participant perception will be evaluated. [Pilot Study:] A pilot experiment with a small sample of participants has been conducted, providing insights for the following study.

</details>


### [41] [EnerInfer: Energy-Aware On-Device LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23001)
*Bohua Zou,Nian Liu,Binqi Sun,Matteo Mascherin,Debayan Roy,Yutao Liu,Yu Peng,Ning Jia,Haibo Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: EnerInfer is an on-device LLM inference framework that improves energy efficiency by exploiting configuration slack — lowering NPU/DDR frequencies without harming quality of experience — using model-structure-aware prediction and thermal-aware dynamic switching, achieving up to 65% energy savings on phones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device LLM inference faces critical energy and thermal bottlenecks. Existing systems optimize only for decoding speed under the assumption that faster is always better. The authors identify that exploitable configuration slack exists: modest frequency reductions preserve QoE while significantly improving energy efficiency and reducing heat. However, realizing this in production is difficult because the optimal NPU/DDR settings vary across models, engines, platforms, and runtime conditions, and commercial devices lack component-level power sensing and face complex thermal dynamics.

Method: EnerInfer replaces traditional per-model profiling and sensor-heavy control with three key components: (1) disaggregated, model-structure-aware prediction that estimates throughput and power for unseen LLMs across NPU/DDR frequency settings; (2) ranking-driven online feedback that selects QoE-satisfying, energy-efficient configurations under runtime interference; and (3) lightweight limited-horizon thermal prediction that dynamically switches between energy-optimized and thermally-constrained inference modes based on request arrivals, response lengths, and thermal history.

Result: Evaluations on real-world LLMs demonstrate that EnerInfer improves energy efficiency by up to 65% on phones, 12% on a laptop, and 24% on a development board, all without violating quality of experience constraints.

Conclusion: Exploiting configuration slack via intelligent frequency scaling is a viable and effective strategy for on-device LLM inference. EnerInfer demonstrates that joint management of energy, throughput, and thermal comfort can yield substantial energy savings across diverse hardware platforms without degrading user experience, overcoming the limitations of static profiling and sensor-dependent approaches.

Abstract: On-device LLM inference is increasingly attractive for privacy-preserving, reliable, and cost-effective deployment, yet its energy and thermal costs remain a critical bottleneck. Existing systems primarily optimize for decoding speed, implicitly assuming that faster execution is always preferable. We show instead that on-device LLM inference often has exploitable configuration slack: modestly lowering NPU and memory frequencies preserves quality of experience (QoE) while substantially improving energy efficiency and reducing heat.
  Realizing this opportunity in production is challenging. The most energy-efficient NPU/DDR setting varies with the model, inference engine, platform, and runtime conditions, with no stable ranking across configurations. Commercial devices further lack component-level power sensing, and shell temperature evolves with request arrivals, response lengths, and thermal history. To address these challenges, we propose EnerInfer, the first on-device LLM inference framework that jointly manages energy efficiency, throughput, and thermal comfort for LLM workloads. EnerInfer replaces per-model profiling and sensor-heavy control with disaggregated, model-structure-aware prediction and ranking-driven online feedback. It predicts throughput and power for unseen LLMs across NPU/DDR frequency settings, selects QoE-satisfying efficient configurations under runtime interference, and uses lightweight limited-horizon thermal prediction to dynamically switch between energy-optimized and thermally constrained inference. Evaluations on real-world LLMs show that EnerInfer improves energy efficiency by up to 65%, 12%, and 24% on phones, a laptop, and a development board, respectively, without QoE violation.

</details>


### [42] [Toward Self-Evolution-Ready Workflow Harnesses: A Reversible Migration Path and Convertibility Taxonomy for Expert LLM Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24598)
*Yimo Lin,Zhen Zhang,Yibin Li*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Presents a Strangler-Fig migration framework that refactors static, expert-validated "LLM + script" workflows into composable, typed, auditable stages using a three-tier convertibility taxonomy (A/B/C) for routing and gradual adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Expert-validated "LLM + script" workflows encode valuable domain knowledge but are static—they cannot adapt execution based on feedback. Existing agent research focuses on greenfield agents and synthetic benchmarks, leaving the practical problem of migrating active legacy workflows to adaptive, agent-based systems entirely unaddressed.

Method: The authors propose a reversible Strangler-Fig migration pattern that incrementally refactors legacy workflows into composable, typed, and auditable stages. The core mechanism is a three-tier convertibility taxonomy (categories A, B, C), implemented as a routing stage within a system harness. This routing stage diagnoses each workflow component's readiness for conversion and dynamically routes execution accordingly, enabling gradual, risk-managed migration.

Result: The paper delivers a structured migration framework rather than empirical benchmarks: (1) a taxonomy classifying workflow stages by their readiness for agent-based refactoring, (2) a routing harness that operationalizes this taxonomy, and (3) a reversible migration path ensuring legacy workflows remain operational throughout transition.

Conclusion: The Strangler-Fig pattern, combined with a convertibility taxonomy and routing harness, provides a viable, reversible path for migrating static legacy LLM workflows into adaptive, composable agent architectures—bridging the gap between production-proven scripts and the flexibility promised by agent-based systems.

Abstract: While expert-validated "LLM + script" workflows deliver significant value, they remain static: they encode hard-won domain knowledge yet fail to adapt execution based on feedback. Existing agent research predominantly targets greenfield agents and synthetic benchmarks, leaving the migration of active legacy workflows unresolved. To bridge this gap, we present a reversible, Strangler-Fig migration path that refactors legacy workflows into composable, typed, and auditable stages. Central to this framework is a three-tier convertibility taxonomy (A/B/C), implemented as a routing stage within the system harness, which diagnoses a workflow's readiness and routes it accordingly.

</details>
