### [43] [TokenMinds: Pretrained User Tokens and Embeddings for User Understanding in Large Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25147)
*Qingyun Liu,Bo Yan,Yang Liu,Yuji Roh,Ekansh Sharma,Likang Yin,Emma Olowo,Min-hsuan Tsai,Yuxuan Li,Diego Uribe,Saksham Aggarwal,Siqi Wu,Yuan Hao,Vikas Kedigehalli,Lukasz Heldt,Lichan Hong,Li Wei,Xinyang Yi*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: TokenMinds extends the PLUM framework to user modeling, generating both discrete Semantic ID (SID) user tokens and dense embeddings via an encoder-decoder adapted from pre-trained LLMs. This dual-output design combines the complementary benefits of discrete, semantically grounded representations with compatibility for existing dense-embedding pipelines, while unified cross-scenario modeling reduces training/serving costs. Validated on full YouTube traffic (billions of users), the system demonstrates that SID-based user tokens and dense embeddings provide complementary value in production ranking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense user embeddings suffer from fixed-dimensional representational constraints. While LLM-generated text-based user tokens capture topical co-occurrences, they fail to model deep sequential behavior dynamics and are difficult to ground to item attributes. SID-based item tokenization has proven effective for generative recommendation, but SID-based user representations remain unexplored. The authors aim to bridge this gap by bringing discrete, semantically grounded SID representations to user modeling at industrial scale.

Method: The authors propose TokenMinds, which adapts the PLUM framework from item retrieval to user modeling. It employs an encoder-decoder architecture derived from pre-trained LLMs to jointly produce: (1) discrete SID-based user tokens and (2) dense user embeddings in a dual-output design. The shared SID vocabulary enables cross-scenario modeling, unifying long-form and short-form video behaviors into a single model. An asynchronous serving infrastructure decouples representation generation from downstream scoring, serving full user traffic (billions of users) on multiple YouTube surfaces.

Result: Extensive offline experiments and live A/B launches on multiple YouTube surfaces at full traffic scale validate the approach. Results confirm the practical viability of SID-based user tokens at industrial scale and demonstrate that discrete tokens and dense embeddings provide complementary value across different production ranking systems — the primary downstream use case evaluated.

Conclusion: SID-based discrete user tokens are practically viable at industrial scale and complement dense embeddings in production ranking systems. The dual-output TokenMinds design leverages pre-trained LLMs and shared SID vocabularies to unify cross-scenario user modeling while reducing training and serving costs, establishing a new direction for discrete user representation in large-scale recommender systems.

Abstract: User modeling in industrial recommender systems typically produces dense embeddings, which suffer from representational constraints inherent to fixed-dimensional vectors. An emerging alternative for discrete user representation -- using LLMs to generate text-based user tokens -- captures topical co-occurrences rather than deep sequential behavior dynamics and produces outputs that are difficult to ground to item attributes. Meanwhile, Semantic ID (SID) based item tokenization has proven effective for improving generalization in generative recommendation, yet discrete SID-based representations for users remain largely unexplored. We propose TokenMinds, an industrial-scale system that extends the PLUM framework from item retrieval to user modeling, generating both discrete SID-based user tokens and dense user embeddings via an encoder-decoder architecture adapted from pre-trained LLMs. This dual-output design provides the complementary benefits of discrete, semantically grounded user representations while maintaining compatibility with existing downstream models that rely on dense embeddings. Additionally, the shared SID vocabulary naturally extends to cross-scenario modeling: by unifying long-form and short-form video behaviors into a single model, we substantially reduce training and serving costs. We validate TokenMinds through extensive offline experiments and live launches on multiple YouTube surfaces, served on full user traffic (billions of users) via an asynchronous infrastructure that decouples representation generation from downstream scoring. Focusing on ranking as the primary downstream use case, our results confirm the practical viability of SID-based user tokens at industrial scale and demonstrate that tokens and dense embeddings provide complementary value across different production ranking systems.

</details>


### [44] [Extreme Meta-Classification for Large-Scale Zero-Shot Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25237)
*Sachin Yadav,Deepak Saini,Anirudh Buvanesh,Bhawna Paliwal,Kunal Dahiya,Siddarth Asokan,Yashoteja Prabhu,Jian Jiao,Manik Varma*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Proposes EMMETT, a framework that synthesizes classifiers for novel (zero-shot) items on-the-fly using existing classifiers, and IRENE, a scalable instance of EMMETT. IRENE achieves up to 15% Recall@10 improvement over leading encoders and 4.2% CTR lift in a live ad retrieval A/B test.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Siamese embedding methods allow easy addition of novel items but lack representational capacity; extreme classification methods have high capacity but can't handle zero-shot items. There is a gap for a method that combines the capacity of classifier-based approaches with the zero-shot capability of Siamese approaches.

Method: EMMETT framework synthesizes classifiers for novel items by leveraging existing classifiers of observed items. IRENE is a lightweight, scalable instance of EMMETT designed for large-scale deployment. A theoretical framework for zero-shot retrieval generalization is also developed to guide algorithmic and training decisions.

Result: IRENE improves zero-shot Recall@10 by up to 15 percentage points over leading encoders across diverse retrieval tasks. In a large-scale online A/B test on a major search engine's ad retrieval system, IRENE yielded a 4.2% increase in ad click-through rate. Ablation studies validate design choices.

Conclusion: EMMETT and IRENE effectively bridge the gap between Siamese and extreme classification methods, delivering strong zero-shot retrieval performance with practical scalability. The theoretical framework provides principled guidance for training and algorithm design in zero-shot retrieval settings.

Abstract: We develop accurate and efficient solutions for large-scale retrieval tasks where novel (zero-shot) items can arrive continuously at a rapid pace. Conventional Siamese-style approaches embed both queries and items through a small encoder and retrieve the items lying closest to the query. While this approach allows efficient addition and retrieval of novel items, the small encoder lacks sufficient capacity for the necessary world knowledge in complex retrieval tasks. The extreme classification approaches have addressed this by learning a separate classifier for each item observed in the training set which significantly increases the representation capacity of the model. Such classifiers outperform Siamese approaches on observed items, but cannot be trained for novel items due to data and latency constraints. To bridge these gaps, this paper develops: (1) A new algorithmic framework, EMMETT, which efficiently synthesizes classifiers on-the-fly for novel items, by relying on the readily available classifiers for observed items; (2) A new algorithm, IRENE, which is a simple and effective instance of EMMETT that is specifically suited for large-scale deployments, and (3) A new theoretical framework for analyzing the generalization performance in large-scale zero-shot retrieval which guides our algorithm and training related design decisions.
  Comprehensive experiments are conducted on a wide range of retrieval tasks which demonstrate that IRENE improves the zero-shot retrieval accuracy by up to 15% points in Recall@10 when added on top of leading encoders. Additionally, on an online A/B test in a large-scale ad retrieval task in a major search engine, IRENE improved the ad click-through rate by 4.2%. Lastly, we validate our design choices through extensive ablative experiments. The source code for IRENE is available at https://aka.ms/irene.

</details>


### [45] [Adaptive Re-Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25249)
*Ata Cinar Genc,Emir Kaan Korukluoglu,James Allan*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Proposes Adaptive Re-Ranking, a cost-aware query routing framework that selectively applies light or heavy re-rankers based on a utility function, achieving 1.15–53× lower latency while maintaining competitive nDCG@10.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard "retrieve-then-rerank" pipelines waste computation by applying heavy cross-encoders uniformly to all queries, regardless of complexity. There is a need for query-adaptive routing to balance cost and effectiveness.

Method: An utility-based labeling framework for cost-aware routing, with a trained classifier that routes each query to one of three strategies: BM25 (sparse retrieval), MiniLM-L6-v2 (dense re-ranking), or BGE-v2-m3 (heavy neural re-ranking). Oracle analysis validates the potential of per-query routing.

Result: Compared to BGE-only, 1.15–53× lower median latency and 1.11–5.22× lower mean latency across tested datasets, with nDCG@10 ranging from –17.5% to +4.0%.

Conclusion: Utility-based query routing offers a scalable, cost-efficient solution for IR systems, enabling significant latency reduction with competitive ranking quality.

Abstract: Modern Information Retrieval (IR) systems typically use a "retrieve-then-rerank" pipeline, where a computationally expensive, pre-determined cross-encoder re-ranks the top results from a fast initial retriever. While effective, this approach often applies heavy re-ranking models regardless of query complexity, resulting in high latency and wasted computational resources on simple queries. We propose Adaptive Re-Ranking, an utility-based labeling framework for cost-aware routing and present empirical evidence (via oracle analysis and a trained baseline router) that per-query routing offers large potential gains but is non-trivial to learn from limited supervision. We train a routing classifier with 3 strategies: sparse retrieval (BM25), dense re-ranking (MiniLM-L6-v2), and heavy neural re-ranking (BGE-v2-m3). Compared to BGE our method achieves 1.15-53x lower median latency and 1.11-5.22x lower mean latency across all datasets we have tested, while delivering -17.5% to +4.0% nDCG@10, which is competitive in some datasets. Our findings show that routing queries based on our novel utility function offers a scalable solution for reducing computational costs and latency in a variety of IR systems.

</details>


### [46] [TheoremGraph: Bridging Formal and Informal Mathematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25363)
*Simon Kurgan,Evan Wang,Eric Leonen,Sophie Szeto,Luke Alexander,Artemii Remizov,Jarod Alper,Giovanni Inchiostro,Vasily Ilin*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: TheoremGraph is a unified statement-level dependency graph bridging informal (arXiv) and formal (Lean 4) mathematics, linking 11.7M informal theorem environments with 388K formal declarations via semantic embeddings and releasing infrastructure for mathematical search and retrieval-augmented reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mathematical knowledge is structured around statement dependencies, but existing representations are fragmented: informal papers cite at the coarse document level, while formal libraries capture fine-grained dependencies over a smaller corpus. No unified, statement-level dependency graph spans both domains, limiting cross-domain search, attribution, and retrieval.

Method: The authors (1) parse 11.7M theorem-like environments from arXiv, recovering 18.3M candidate directed dependencies using multiple extractors; (2) build LeanGraph, an elaborator-level extractor for Lean 4 producing 388,105 declaration nodes and 11.3M typed edges; and (3) bridge both graphs by embedding natural-language slogans into a shared semantic space, employing an LLM judge to validate cross-domain matches at varying cosine thresholds. They also evaluate formal concept retrieval using name-and-signature representation with graph expansion.

Result: The LLM judge affirms 47,952 cross-domain matches above a 0.8 cosine floor, with judge-acceptance rising from 48% (0.8 tier) to 87% (≥0.9 tier). On formal concept retrieval, their method achieves Recall@10 of 0.775, within 0.5pp of LeanSearch v2's 0.780, without using an LM reranker.

Conclusion: TheoremGraph provides a novel unified dependency graph across informal and formal mathematics, enabling improved mathematical search, attribution, and retrieval-augmented reasoning. The dataset, extractors, API, and MCP interface are publicly released as research infrastructure.

Abstract: Mathematical knowledge is organized around statements and their dependencies, but this structure is exposed unevenly: informal papers cite mostly at the document level, while formal libraries record fine-grained dependencies over a much smaller body of mathematics. We introduce TheoremGraph, a unified statement-level dependency graph spanning both informal and formal mathematics. On the informal side, we parse 11.7M theorem-like environments from mathematics arXiv and recover 18.3M candidate directed dependencies, each labeled by the extractor that proposed it so downstream users can trade coverage for precision. On the formal side, we release LeanGraph, a Lean 4 elaborator-level extractor producing 388,105 declaration nodes and 11.3M typed edges across 25 Lean projects. We bridge the two graphs by embedding generated natural-language slogans into a shared semantic space, linking related statements across papers and across the informal/formal divide; an LLM judge affirms 47,952 such matches above a 0.8 cosine floor, with the judge-acceptance rate rising from 48% across the floor to 87% in the >=0.9 tier. On formal concept retrieval, our name-and-signature representation with graph expansion comes within 0.5pp of LeanSearch v2's reranked Recall@10 (0.775 vs. 0.780) without an LM reranker. We release the dataset, extractors, HTTP API, and MCP interface as infrastructure for mathematical search, attribution, and retrieval-augmented reasoning, available at theoremsearch.com and huggingface.co/datasets/uw-math-ai/theorem-matching.

</details>


### [47] [S2-CAR: Segmentation-Supervised Complexity-Adaptive Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25415)
*Linjiang Guo,Nitin Bisht,Shiqing Wu,Xianzhi Wang,Guandong Xu*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: S2-CAR proposes a segmentation-supervised framework that models user intent as a continuous latent energy state, using a Soft Temporal Point Process for energy-decay-based boundary segmentation and a Segment-Count-Adaptive module for multi-intent extraction, outperforming SOTA on three benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sequential recommendation models treat interaction histories as homogeneous or rely on rigid time-window segmentation, causing cross-intent interference and over-reliance on short-term signals when user interests shift latently over time.

Method: S2-CAR uses: (1) Context-Aware Soft Temporal Point Process (Soft-TPP) that segments boundaries based on natural decay of latent-state energy rather than fixed intervals; (2) Segment-Count-Adaptive Multi-Intent Extraction module that hierarchically aggregates intent-coherent segments into compact multi-interest representations.

Result: S2-CAR consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods across all datasets and metrics on 3 public benchmarks (movie, e-commerce, gaming) against 13 baselines. The energy-based segmentation also works as a plug-and-play module providing consistent gains when integrated into existing backbones.

Conclusion: Modeling user intent as a continuous latent energy state with soft, decay-driven segmentation effectively addresses the heterogeneity problem in sequential recommendation, and the approach generalizes well as a plug-and-play component.

Abstract: Sequential recommendation aims to predict user preferences from interaction histories, yet existing models often struggle when behavior patterns become complex and heterogeneous. A key reason is that interaction histories are rarely uniform: users' interests shift in a latent way over time, yet existing models either treat the full sequence as a homogeneous context or rely on rigid time-window segmentation that misaligns with true intent boundaries. This mis-segmentation not only introduces cross-intent interference at intermediate sequence positions but also leads to over-reliance on short-term interest signals. To address this, we propose S2-CAR, a segmentation-supervised and complexity-adaptive framework for sequential recommendation that models user intent as a continuous latent energy state. Specifically, it uses the Context-Aware Soft Temporal Point Process (Soft-TPP) to segment boundaries triggered by the natural decay of latent-state energy rather than fixed intervals, enabling intent segmentation without fixed time-gap rules. Next, upon this segmentation, a Segment-Count-Adaptive Multi-Intent Extraction module hierarchically aggregates intent-coherent segments into a compact set of multi-interest representations. Extensive experiments on 3 representative public benchmark datasets spanning movie, e-commerce, and gaming domains across 13 baselines demonstrate that S2-CAR consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods across all datasets and metrics. Further analysis shows that the proposed energy-based segmentation serves as a plug-and-play module, yielding consistent improvements when integrated into existing sequential recommendation backbones.

</details>


### [48] [Recommendation as Generation: Unifying Personalized Video Generation and Recommendation at Industrial Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25496)
*Yanhua Cheng,Bo Wang,Haotian Zhang,Xinyuan Gao,Zhihui Yin,Ben Xue,Yongzhi Li,Jieting Xue,Ye Ma,Minquan Wang,Jiahui Li,Tianyu Xu,Zhiqiang Liu,Xiao Lin,Shiyang Wen,Changcheng Li,Liu Liu,Quan Chen,Peng Jiang,Kun Gai*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: RaG proposes a new paradigm that generates personalized short-videos on demand by unifying recommendation and video generation through shared semantic IDs, achieving 1.87% ad revenue improvement in deployment with 400M+ DAU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional short-video recommendation systems are constrained to matching users with pre-produced videos from a fixed pool, limiting their ability to capture fine-grained, dynamic user preferences. The authors aim to overcome this by generating personalized videos tailored to inferred user interests in real-time.

Method: RaG introduces shared Semantic IDs (SIDs) that disentangle video representation into content semantics and creative style semantics, unifying generative recommendation with video generation. Video Generation Agents (VGAs) conditioned on inferred SIDs drive hierarchical planning and refinement across visual composition, audio alignment, and artistic effects. A synergistic cross-domain reward learning mechanism jointly optimizes interest alignment, user feedback, and video quality assessment.

Result: Deployed on an industrial-scale platform with over 400 million daily active users, RaG achieved up to 1.87% improvement in ad revenue over a strong production Generative Recommendation Model (GRM) baseline in online A/B tests.

Conclusion: A closed-loop generative system that integrates personalized video generation into recommendation represents a promising new paradigm, demonstrating tangible business value beyond what generative recommendation alone can achieve.

Abstract: Traditional short-video recommendation systems match user interest to a fixed pool of pre-produced videos, which limits their ability to capture fine-grained and dynamic preferences. We propose Recommendation-as-Generation (RaG), a new paradigm that generates personalized videos on demand from inferred user interest. Our framework unifies generative recommendation and video generation through shared semantic IDs (SIDs), which disentangle video representation into content semantics and creative style semantics, enabling both fine-grained modeling of user interest and controllable generation of interest-aligned videos. We further develop Video Generation Agents (VGAs) that are conditioned on inferred SIDs to drive hierarchical planning and refinement for video creation, including visual composition, audio alignment, and artistic effect enhancement. To optimize the framework, we effectively introduce a synergistic cross-domain reward learning mechanism that jointly enforces interest alignment, user feedback, and video quality assessment.
  We deploy RaG on an industrial-scale platform with over 400 million daily active users and evaluate it in a revenue-critical advertising scenario. Online A/B tests show up to 1.87% ad revenue improvement compared to a strong production GRM baseline, demonstrating its effectiveness in driving further revenue gains beyond generative recommendation. Our results highlight a closed-loop generative system as a promising paradigm for integrating personalized video generation into recommendation.

</details>


### [49] [A Stochastic Epidemiological Model of Latent Tuberculosis in a Radiation Exposed Mars Colony](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25728)
*Teddy Lazebnik*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: A stochastic model simulates latent TB reactivation in a Mars colony under chronic radiation, linking immune impairment to airborne transmission. Adaptive AI-driven countermeasure policies outperform fixed protocols, reducing disease burden and mortality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As Mars colonization moves toward reality, the biological risks of a small, closed population exposed to chronic galactic cosmic radiation, constrained medical resources, and engineered habitats remain poorly quantified. Latent infections like tuberculosis pose a unique threat: clinically silent carriers can become infectious when radiation-induced immune decline triggers reactivation, creating an endogenous outbreak with no external exposure.

Method: A stochastic host-radiation-pathogen-habitat model couples four components: (1) galactic cosmic radiation exposure, (2) radiation-driven immune competence degradation, (3) latent TB reactivation probability as a function of immune status, and (4) airborne transmission dynamics within a closed-loop habitat. Countermeasure allocation (isolation and medication) is framed as a partially observable sequential decision problem, solved via both fixed baseline policies and a proximal policy optimization (PPO) reinforcement learning agent trained on an agent-based simulator.

Result: Active TB emerged endogenously despite zero initial infectious cases. Outbreak risk was most sensitive to three parameters: latent reservoir size, the radiation-immune coupling coefficient, and reactivation sensitivity to immune decline. The PPO-based adaptive control policy reduced infectious burden and mortality while minimizing unnecessary interventions compared to fixed baselines.

Conclusion: The framework enables mission-specific stress testing of screening, monitoring, shielding, and treatment protocols before launch, supporting evidence-based medical planning for long-duration planetary settlement.

Abstract: Plans to establish a sustained human presence on Mars have moved from speculative ambition toward concrete engineering programmes, making the biological consequences of settlement an increasingly practical question. A Mars colony would place a small, closed population in an environment combining chronic radiation, altered immunity, constrained medical autonomy, and engineered indoor air. Latent infections are especially important because clinically silent carriers may become sources of transmissible disease when host control deteriorates. In this study, we develop a stochastic host-radiation-pathogen-habitat model of latent tuberculosis reactivation in a Mars colony. The model links galactic cosmic radiation to immune competence, immune competence to latent-tuberculosis reactivation, and reactivation to airborne transmission in a closed habitat. We also formulate countermeasure allocation as a partially observable sequential decision problem in which isolation and medication are selected by fixed baselines or by a proximal policy optimization policy trained on an agent-based simulator. Our simulations show that active tuberculosis can emerge endogenously despite no initial infectious cases, and that risk is most sensitive to latent reservoir size, radiation-immune coupling and reactivation sensitivity. Adaptive control reduced infectious burden and mortality while limiting unnecessary intervention. This framework supports mission-specific stress testing of screening, monitoring, shielding and treatment strategies before launch.

</details>


### [50] [How Large Language Models Source Brand Reputation Across Languages and Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25787)
*Dmitrij Zatuchin*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This study examines 167,551 URL-grounded citations across 128 brands, 12 markets, and 13 languages to understand where LLMs retrieve brand information. Key findings: 85.7% of citations are third-party (not brand-owned), citation distribution follows Zipf's law with ~18% of domains supplying 80% of citations, Wikipedia dominates across nearly all languages, and source mixes vary by market at the margin.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing analyses of AI brand visibility focus exclusively on what LLMs say in their answer text. The authors shift attention one step upstream — to the citations and sources that ground those answers — arguing that retrieved sources fundamentally determine model output and that citation-level analysis is an underexplored dimension of AI-mediated brand representation.

Method: The study merges three Rankfor.AI datasets covering 128 brands across 12 home markets and 13 languages. It analyzes 167,551 URL-grounded citations (189,974 total attribution rows). Each citation is classified by domain and source type (owned vs. third-party). Distributional analysis includes Zipf-law fitting (alpha = 0.86, R² = 0.983) to characterize citation concentration, alongside cross-linguistic and cross-market comparisons of top-cited domains.

Result: Four empirical patterns emerge: (1) 85.7% of citations are third-party vs. 14.3% brand-owned; (2) citation sources are highly concentrated — ~18% of domains account for 80% of citations, fitting Zipf's law; (3) Wikipedia is the most-cited domain in 11 of 12 languages, with the sole exception of Lithuanian (vz.lt edges it at 4.38%); (4) source mix is market-specific — e.g., for 46 Polish brands, YouTube is most-cited, and four HR/careers portals supply 637 citations vs. 297 for Polish Wikipedia.

Conclusion: LLM-generated brand answers are overwhelmingly shaped by third-party, concentrated, and platform-dependent citation ecosystems. Brand-owned sources play a minor role. Wikipedia's near-universal dominance as a citation source underscores its outsized influence on AI-mediated brand perception, though market-level exceptions reveal that local media and platform dynamics can modulate the source mix at the margin.

Abstract: When a large language model (LLM) answers a question about a company, it grounds the answer in retrieved web sources, and those sources decide what the model says. Most analysis of AI brand visibility looks at the answer text. This study looks one step earlier, at the citations. We merge three Rankfor.AI datasets covering 128 brands across 12 home markets and 13 languages, and analyse 167,551 URL-grounded citations (189,974 total attribution rows). We classify each citation by domain and source type and measure where AI gets its brand information, by language and by market. Four patterns hold. First, AI grounds brand answers overwhelmingly in third-party sources: 85.7% of citations point to sites the brand does not own, against 14.3% owned. Second, the source base is concentrated and long-tailed: 80% of citations come from about 18% of domains, fitting a Zipf law (alpha = 0.86, R^2 = 0.983). Third, one reference site dominates almost everywhere: Wikipedia is the most-cited domain in 11 of 12 languages, the exception being Lithuanian, where the business daily vz.lt edges it (4.38%). Fourth, the source mix is market-specific at the margin: for 46 Polish national brands the most-cited domain is YouTube, and four HR and careers portals supply 637 citations against 297 for Polish Wikipedia, about twice as many.

</details>


### [51] [AutoRelAnnotator: Calibrated Model Cascades for Cost-Efficient Relevance Evaluation in Sponsored Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25871)
*Md Omar Faruk Rokon,Shasvat Desai,Hong Yao,Kuang-chih Lee*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: The paper proposes a calibrated model cascade for cost-efficient offline relevance annotation, combining domain-specific fine-tuning for accuracy, cascading progressively larger models to halve compute costs, and per-class isotonic calibration for a small additional gain (+0.6 points), validated on 150M+ annotations in production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relevance annotations are critical for search ranking (training, NDCG evaluation, root cause analysis), but human labeling is slow and expensive, while off-the-shelf LLMs lack accuracy on domain-specific tasks. There is a need for high-quality, scalable, and cost-efficient annotation at production scale.

Method: A calibrated model cascade that routes queries through progressively larger fine-tuned classifiers. Three orthogonal components are combined: (1) domain-specific fine-tuning of classifiers for accuracy; (2) a cascading architecture where smaller, cheaper models handle easy queries and larger models handle harder ones, reducing compute cost; and (3) per-class isotonic calibration applied on top to yield a small but reliable accuracy gain.

Result: Fine-tuning contributes approximately 20 accuracy points. Cascading is roughly accuracy-neutral while halving compute cost. Per-class isotonic calibration adds a statistically significant gain of +0.6 points over the strongest calibration baseline. The system was validated in production across six offline use cases, processing over 150 million annotations and enabling faster experimentation cycles.

Conclusion: The calibrated model cascade is a practical building block for scalable, high-quality offline annotation pipelines in search and advertising systems, demonstrating that accuracy and cost can be orthogonally optimized through the combination of fine-tuning, cascading, and isotonic calibration.

Abstract: How can we generate high-quality relevance annotations at scale without the cost and delays of human labeling? Relevance annotations are the backbone of search ranking systems which is needed for training data preparation, NDCG evaluation, and root cause analysis. However, human annotation is slow and off-the-shelf LLMs suffer from accuracy on domain-specific tasks. We propose a calibrated model cascade, a systematic approach for cost-efficient offline relevance annotation by routing queries through progressively larger fine-tuned classifiers. Our central insight is that accuracy and cost are orthogonal optimizations: domain-specific fine-tuning drives accuracy, cascading drives cost, and per-class isotonic calibration adds a small but reliable gain on top. Our contribution is threefold: (a) we decompose the gains and show that fine-tuning contributes 20 accuracy points while cascading is approximately accuracy-neutral but halves compute cost, (b) we introduce per-class isotonic calibration as one component of the cascade, contributing a small but statistically significant gain (+0.6 points over the strongest calibration baseline), and (c) we validate the system in production across six offline use cases, processing 150M+ annotations and enabling faster experimentation cycles. Our work is a building block for scalable, high-quality offline annotation pipelines in search and advertising systems.

</details>

### [52] [Proactive Systems in HCI and AI: Concepts, Challenges, and Opportunities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25149)
*Nima Zargham,Sharon Ferguson,Jaisie Sin,Cosmin Munteanu,Anastasia Kuzminykh*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This workshop paper identifies conceptual ambiguity in how "proactivity" is defined across research and practice, arguing that current design/evaluation methods rooted in reactive paradigms are inadequate for proactive systems, and proposes a multidisciplinary workshop to establish shared definitions, identify methodological gaps, and co-create human-centered guidelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The term "proactivity" is loosely defined and inconsistently applied, conflating fundamentally different system behaviors (e.g., reminders vs. assistive robots). This conceptual ambiguity hinders systematic design, comparison, and evaluation. Furthermore, existing methodologies are rooted in reactive interaction paradigms and fail to address unique proactive challenges: timing, appropriateness, user control, transparency, and trust.

Method: This is a workshop proposal paper. The authors organize a multidisciplinary workshop convening researchers and practitioners from Human-Computer Interaction, AI, and related fields. The workshop employs interactive discussions and collaborative activities structured around three goals: (1) developing a shared conceptualization of proactivity, (2) identifying gaps in current design and evaluation approaches, and (3) co-creating human-centered guidelines and research directions.

Result: As a workshop proposal, the paper does not present empirical results. The expected outcomes include: a clearer conceptual foundation for proactive systems, identification of key challenges and opportunities, and robust frameworks for designing and evaluating proactive technologies.

Conclusion: Establishing a rigorous, shared understanding of proactivity is essential for advancing proactive systems. The workshop aims to bridge disciplinary divides and produce human-centered guidelines that address the unique demands of proactive behavior, ultimately enabling more systematic and trustworthy proactive technology development.

Abstract: The last few years have seen a significant rise in interest in highly autonomous and proactive systems, fueled by advances in AI. Systems that anticipate user needs, take initiative, and act without explicit user input. Such systems span a wide range of applications, from smart lighting that adapts to user activity to assistive robots that plan actions in advance to intelligent thermostats that learn routines and adjust environments proactively. Despite this breadth, the concept of proactivity remains loosely defined and inconsistently applied across research and practice. Current usage of the term often conflates fundamentally different system behaviors. For instance, simple reminders or recommendation systems are frequently labeled as proactive, even though underlying mechanisms and intentions differ significantly. This conceptual ambiguity limits our ability to systematically design, compare, and evaluate proactive systems. Moreover, existing methodologies for design and evaluation are largely rooted in reactive interaction paradigms, failing to address the unique challenges posed by proactive behavior, including timing, appropriateness, user control, transparency, and trust. This multidisciplinary workshop aims to establish a clearer and more rigorous foundation for understanding proactive systems. We bring together researchers and practitioners from Human-Computer Interaction, AI, and related fields to (1) develop a shared conceptualization of proactivity, (2) identify gaps and limitations in current design and evaluation approaches, and (3) co-create human-centered guidelines and research directions for future systems. Through interactive discussions and collaborative activities, the workshop seeks to map key challenges and opportunities, ultimately advancing robust and consistent frameworks for designing and evaluating proactive technologies.

</details>


### [53] [ARTOO-DARTU: Studying AR-HRC With AR Obstruction Mitigation During a Warehouse Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25202)
*Christian Fronk,Hanting Ye,Zhehan Qu,Maria Gorlatova*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: ARTOO-DARTU is an AR system for warehouse human-robot collaboration that provides real-time robot situated analytics while using an obstruction detection and mitigation pipeline to prevent AR content from blocking critical real-world elements. A 34-participant study showed 46% overall task efficiency gains and 61% faster performance on visibility-dependent subtasks when the obstruction mitigation was active.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In warehouse HRC scenarios, AR situated analytics can convey robot intentions to users but risk obstructing important real-world elements, creating safety and usability hazards — especially when AR content moves dynamically with mobile robots. Existing approaches lack mechanisms to balance information delivery with real-world visibility preservation.

Method: The authors developed ARTOO-DARTU, an AR system featuring an obstruction detection and mitigation (ODM) pipeline tailored for AR-HRC. They also created Pocket MonstARs, a gamified abstraction of warehouse inventory picking where virtual monsters represent pick targets and labeled/object-marked boxes maintain real-world identification demands. A controlled user study (N=34) compared task efficiency and subtask speed with ODM active versus inactive.

Result: AR situated analytics improved overall HRC task efficiency by 46%, but only when the ODM was active. Participants with ODM active completed visibility-dependent subtasks 61% faster. The ODM was essential for realizing the efficiency benefits of the situated analytics.

Conclusion: Situated analytics in AR-HRC can significantly enhance efficiency and user experience in warehouse scenarios, but only when paired with an active obstruction detection and mitigation pipeline that preserves real-world visibility.

Abstract: Human-robot collaboration (HRC) often requires robot intentions and internal states to be conveyed to users for task efficiency and safety. Recently, augmented reality (AR) situated analytics provide such real-time robot feedback in HRC contexts. However, AR situated analytics can obstruct important real-world elements, posing safety and usability risks, especially when content is dynamically positioned relative to movements of mobile robots in a warehouse HRC scenario. In this paper, we introduce the Augmented Reality Technique Of Obstruction Deterrence while Aiding Robotic Teaming for Users (ARTOO-DARTU), an AR system tailored specifically for warehouse HRC that enables real-time robot situated analytics and control while preserving visibility of the real world through an obstruction detection and mitigation pipeline (ODM) that is uniquely suited for AR-HRC. To evaluate ARTOO-DARTU, we developed Pocket MonstARs, a controlled gamified abstraction of HRC warehouse inventory picking in which virtual monsters serve as proxies for pick targets, while labeled and object-marked boxes preserve the real-world identification demands of the picking task. In a 34-participant user study, we found that our designed AR situated analytics yielded a 46% increase in efficiency on the overall HRC task, but only when the ODM was active. Participants with the ODM active were also 61% faster on subtasks requiring visibility of the real world. Our findings demonstrate that, when paired with our developed ODM to prevent real-world obstructions, the situated analytics in ARTOO-DARTU can significantly enhance efficiency and user experience in AR-HRC warehouse scenarios.

</details>


### [54] [FUTO Swipe: Layout-Agnostic Neural Swipe Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25247)
*David Lee Miller,Aleksandras Kostarevas*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A neural swipe decoder is trained to be layout-agnostic by predicting character locations from gesture dynamics, using keyboard layouts supplied only at inference. Geometric augmentations during training enable generalization to unseen layouts, supported by a newly released 1M+ swipe dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional neural swipe decoders are locked to a specific keyboard layout, requiring costly retraining for each new layout. Public swipe data is scarce, especially for non-QWERTY layouts, limiting research and development.

Method: An encoder predicts, at each point along a swipe trajectory, whether a character is being indicated and its spatial coordinates. At inference, the target keyboard layout is supplied and used to map these spatiotemporal predictions to per-key logits, decoupling layout from training. Geometric augmentations are applied to both swipe trajectories and keyboard layouts at every training step to force the model to rely on gesture characteristics rather than memorized layout positions. A new publicly released corpus (swipe.futo.org) with 1M+ MIT-licensed swipes from 12k+ donor sessions supports training.

Result: The model successfully generalizes to keyboard layouts never seen during training, in some cases surpassing accuracy on the original training layout. This matches the flexibility of algorithmic decoders while retaining neural-level accuracy.

Conclusion: Layout-flexible neural swipe decoding is achievable through inference-time layout injection and geometric data augmentation, eliminating per-layout retraining. The release of a large public swipe corpus and trained models supports further research.

Abstract: Neural swipe decoders are typically tied to the keyboard they were trained on, requiring a new corpus and training run for each layout. In this report, we document our approach toward training models that can function on any contiguous mobile keyboard layout. At each point along the swipe, our encoder predicts whether the user is indicating a character and where on the keyboard that character lies. The keyboard layout is supplied at inference time and used to map the spatial and temporal prediction to a logit at each key, rather than being learned during training.
  Training neural models requires substantial data, but public swipe data is limited, particularly for non-QWERTY layouts. We release swipe.futo.org, the largest MIT-licensed swipe corpus we are aware of, containing over 1M donated swipes from more than 12k donor sessions. To generalize beyond the English QWERTY layout, we apply geometric augmentations to both the swipe trajectory and the keyboard layout at every training step, forcing the model to make predictions based on characteristics of the swipe gesture rather than the training layout. The model generalizes to layouts absent from training, in some cases more accurately than the layout it was trained on. This combines the layout-flexibility of an algorithmic decoder with the accuracy of a neural model. Trained models are publicly available.

</details>


### [55] [Co-designing a Preliminary Repository of Augmented Reality Concepts for Real-Time Emotion Regulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25271)
*Graciela Camacho-Fidalgo,Edgar Rojas-Muñoz*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A two-phase participatory design study involving 40 anxiety-prone individuals and 10 mental health professionals produced a co-designed AR design repository containing 8 thematic clusters and 106 design ideas for real-time emotion regulation interventions, establishing an expert-informed, user-centered foundation for AR-based mental health support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AR techniques for therapeutic support lack a user-centered, expert-informed understanding of how real-time AR can assist people in emotional distress without disrupting their ongoing activities. The absence of reusable design resources hinders broader adoption of AR for mental health.

Method: The authors conducted a two-phase participatory design process. Phase 1 recruited 40 anxiety-prone individuals who used the Nominal Group Technique (NGT) to generate ideas about how AR affordances could support emotion regulation. Phase 2 recruited 10 mental health professionals who organized these ideas into thematic clusters and assessed their clinical feasibility.

Result: The process yielded a co-designed AR design repository comprising 8 thematic clusters and 106 design ideas, grounded in both user perspectives and clinical expertise.

Conclusion: This work constitutes an initial step toward developing seamless, real-time AR interventions for mental health, providing a reusable design resource that bridges the gap between user needs and clinical feasibility.

Abstract: Augmented Reality (AR) can be a positive therapeutic approach to support mental health and emotion regulation. Although AR techniques for therapeutic support exist, there is no user-centered, expert-informed understanding of how real-time AR designs can support people in emotional distress without disengaging them from their ongoing activities. This lack of reusable design resources hinders the adoption of AR for mental health support. This paper addresses this gap by introducing a co-designed collection of AR interventions describing how this technique can support real-time emotion regulation. The repository was created following a two-phase participatory design process. Phase 1 recruited 40 anxiety-prone individuals and used the Nominal Group Technique to list ideas on how AR affordances could support emotion regulation. Phase 2 recruited 10 mental health professionals to organize these ideas into thematic clusters and assess their clinical feasibility. The resulting AR design repository, grounded in user perspective and clinical expertise, identifies eight thematic clusters and 106 design ideas. This work represents a first step towards the development of seamless real-time AR interventions for mental health.

</details>


### [56] [The Digital Pirahã Condition: Ecological Mismatch and the Reconstruction of Recursive Cognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25287)
*Dhushy Thillaivasana,Samar Shailendrab,Kristina Nichollsc,Deepani Guruged*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper proposes the "Digital Pirahã Condition" — a cultural-ecological model explaining how AI-mediated digital environments foster adaptive but shallow cognitive patterns (epistemic flattening, reduced recursion, external scaffolding dependence), creating an ecological mismatch with the recursive reasoning demands of academia. It advocates for intentional cognitive niche construction in education, positioning lecturers as cultural entrepreneurs who build analog sanctuaries, metacognitive scaffolds, and recursive curricula.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To theorize how contemporary digital and AI-mediated environments reshape human cognitive ecologies, producing adaptive yet shallow cognitive habits that conflict with the deep, recursive reasoning required in academic and institutional settings — and to frame this not as a psychological deficit but as an ecological outcome requiring structural intervention.

Method: The paper employs theoretical synthesis and cultural-ecological modeling, drawing analogies from the Pirahã linguistic/cognitive case (Everett, 2005) to construct the "Digital Pirahã Condition" framework. It integrates concepts from cognitive ecology, niche construction theory, distributed cognition, and activity theory to diagnose the mismatch between digitally-adapted cognition and academic demands, then proposes design principles for educational intervention.

Result: The Digital Pirahã Condition model posits that digital environments cultivate: (1) epistemic flattening — reduced depth of understanding; (2) diminished recursive capacity — impaired hierarchical, embedded reasoning; (3) over-reliance on external cognitive scaffolds — algorithms, notifications, search. These adaptations are functional within digital systems but produce an ecological mismatch with academic activity systems requiring integrative, recursive thought. The lecturer's role is reconceptualized as that of a cultural entrepreneur who constructs cognitive niches through analog sanctuaries, AI-supported metacognitive tools, and recursive curriculum architectures.

Conclusion: The Digital Pirahã Condition offers both a diagnostic theoretical lens for understanding contemporary cognitive change and a prescriptive framework for ecological redesign in education. Addressing the mismatch requires intentional cognitive niche construction rather than blaming individual deficits, with educational institutions serving as sites of cognitive-ecological intervention in AI-mediated societies.

Abstract: Contemporary digital and AI-mediated environments are reshaping the cognitive ecologies within which human reasoning develops. As everyday activity becomes embedded in datafied infrastructures, cognitive habits adapt to conditions of immediacy, fragmentation, externalisation, and algorithmic filtering. This paper introduces the Digital Pirahã Condition, a cultural ecological model explaining how these environments cultivate adaptive but shallow cognitive patterns, epistemic flattening, reduced recursive capacity, and heightened reliance on external scaffolds. While functional within digital systems, these adaptations create an ecological mismatch with the recursive, integrative reasoning required in academic and institutional activity systems. The paper argues that this mismatch is an ecological outcome rather than a psychological deficit, and that addressing it requires intentional cognitive niche construction within educational institutions. The lecturer is conceptualised as a cultural entrepreneur who reconstructs the cognitive ecology of learning through analog sanctuaries, AI-supported metacognitive scaffolds, and recursive curriculum architectures. The Digital Pirahã Condition thus provides a theoretical lens for understanding contemporary cognitive change and a framework for ecological redesign in AI-mediated societies.

</details>


### [57] [When LLM Rationales Become User-Facing: Effects on Trust Perception, Decision-Making, and Gaze Behaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25489)
*Xin Sun,Ting Pan,Yajing Wang,Shu Wei,Jos A. Bosch,Isao Echizen,Abdallah El Ali,Saku Sugawara*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether LLM step-by-step rationales genuinely support user trust calibration or merely persuade. Through two studies, it finds that rationale correctness and certainty framing influence trust, while incorrect rationales increase cognitive effort and reduce system trust. The work advocates for selective, evidence-linked, verifiable rationale designs over unrestrained reasoning output.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs increasingly surface step-by-step reasoning to users, but it is unknown whether these rationales help users appropriately calibrate trust (i.e., judge when an answer is warranted) or simply persuade through fluent, plausible-sounding text. This gap motivates an investigation into auditable trust calibration — the idea that rationales should enable users to inspect whether evidence supports an answer.

Method: Two linked studies were conducted in a factual verification task. Study 1 (N=68) was an online experiment manipulating rationale presentation format (instant, delayed, on-demand), rationale correctness (correct, incorrect), and certainty framing (none, certain, uncertain). Study 2 (N=54) was a controlled eye-tracking experiment comparing no-rationale, correct-rationale, and incorrect-rationale conditions, measuring trust judgments, decision-making, and eye-movement patterns (including a post-hoc predictive modeling analysis of gaze features).

Result: Study 1 found no reliable effect of presentation format; instead, rationale correctness and certainty framing significantly influenced trust in information, trust in the LLM system, and decision confidence. Study 2 revealed that incorrect rationales attracted more visual attention to supporting evidence and elicited larger pupil diameter (indicating greater cognitive effort), and lowered trust in the LLM system compared to no-rationale, though the effect on trust in information was weaker. Gaze features carried predictive signal for trust and decision states.

Conclusion: The findings challenge the assumption that more reasoning is always beneficial. The authors recommend rationale designs that are selective, explicitly linked to evidence, calibrated in certainty expression, and structured for easy verification, rather than simply displaying all reasoning by default.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly show step-by-step reasoning rationales alongside their answers, turning reasoning from an internal model capability into a user-facing interface feature. Yet it is unclear whether such rationales help users judge when trust is warranted or merely persuade through fluent reasoning. We address this gap through the lens of auditable trust calibration: user-facing rationales should help people inspect whether an answer is warranted by evidence. We test this framing in factual verification through two linked studies. Study 1, an online experiment (N=68), manipulated rationale presentation format (instant, delayed, on demand), rationale correctness (correct, incorrect), and certainty framing (none, certain, uncertain). Study 2, a controlled eye-tracking study (N=54), examined how no-, correct-, and incorrect-rationale conditions were associated with users' trust, decision-making, and eye-movement patterns. Study 1 showed no reliable presentation-format effects; instead, rationale correctness and certainty framing influenced the trust in the information, trust in the LLM system, and decision confidence. In Study 2, incorrect rationales drew more attention to the supporting evidence and larger pupil diameter while the rationale was viewed, consistent with greater cognitive effort. Incorrect rationales also lowered trust in LLM system relative to showing no rationale, whereas the no-rationale difference was weaker for trust in information. A post-hoc predictive modeling analysis of gaze data from Study 2 further showed that gaze features carried predictive signal for trust- and decision-related user states. This work challenges the assumption that more reasoning is always better and supports rationale designs that are selective, linked to evidence, calibrated in how they express certainty, and easier to verify.

</details>


### [58] [Dissociable Spatial and Temporal Effects of Interaction Latency in Virtual Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25681)
*Xiaoye Michael Wang,Catherine M. Sabiston,Timothy N. Welsh*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This study investigated how interaction latency (delay between hand movement and virtual hand rendering) affects spatial, temporal, and efficiency measures in VR pointing tasks. Results showed dissociable effects: endpoint accuracy degraded at even short delays, while movement time only increased at longer delays. This demonstrates that latency-sensitive VR evaluations must assess both spatial and temporal performance, not just movement time or throughput.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While motion-to-photon latency is a known issue in VR systems, prior work has not clarified whether common performance measures (spatial accuracy, movement time, throughput/efficiency) reveal the same or different patterns of latency-related disruption. Understanding this is critical for proper evaluation of VR interaction quality.

Method: Participants performed manual pointing tasks on a horizontal surface under two conditions: a physical environment baseline and a VR environment with a motion-captured virtual hand avatar. In VR, artificial delays (0–500 ms) were systematically imposed between the participant's actual hand movement and the rendered virtual hand movement. Performance was measured using endpoint error, endpoint variability, movement time, and throughput (Fitts'-style efficiency).

Result: Compared to the physical baseline, VR performance showed worse endpoint error, longer movement time, greater endpoint variability, and lower throughput. Within VR, added latency further degraded endpoint error, variability, and throughput, and altered movement time — but with distinct profiles: endpoint error worsened at even the shortest delays, while movement time remained stable at short delays and only increased at longer delays.

Conclusion: Interaction latency produces dissociable spatial and temporal consequences. Endpoint accuracy reveals disruption before movement time or throughput. Therefore, HCI evaluations of latency-sensitive VR interactions must incorporate both spatial and temporal measures, especially for visually guided manual actions.

Abstract: Motion-to-photon latency is inherent in immersive virtual reality (VR) systems and can arise from multiple sensorimotor loops, including view-contingent latency between head movement and display update and interaction latency between hand movement and the virtual effector. Although prior work shows that interaction latency can impair VR performance, it remains unclear whether common spatial, temporal, and efficiency measures reveal the same latency-related disruption. This study addressed this question by experimentally imposing delays between the physical and virtual hands during manual pointing in VR. Participants pointed to targets on a horizontal surface in VR and in the physical environment as an unmediated baseline. In VR, pointing was performed with a virtual hand avatar controlled by a motion capture pipeline, and additional delays (0-500 ms) were imposed between the participant's hand movement and the rendered movement of the virtual hand. Relative to the baseline, performance in VR showed greater endpoint error, longer movement time, greater endpoint variability, and lower throughput. Within VR, added interaction latency further increased endpoint error and variability, reduced throughput, and altered movement time, but these effects followed different profiles: endpoint error increased even at the shortest delays, whereas movement time remained stable at short delays and increased primarily at longer delays. These findings show that interaction latency produces dissociable spatial and temporal consequences in immersive VR, such that endpoint accuracy revealed disruption before movement time or throughput. Thus, latency-sensitive VR interactions cannot be fully evaluated using movement time or efficiency measures alone. Instead, HCI evaluations should assess both spatial and temporal performance, particularly when VR tasks involve visually guided manual actions.

</details>


### [59] [Designing Trustworthy LLM-based Wellbeing Recommendation through Controllable Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25809)
*Alan Said,Alexandra Weilenmann*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper proposes a system-level framework for LLM-based wellbeing recommender systems that replaces implicit adaptation via prompting with explicit interaction constraints—covering guidance strategies, explanation styles, directness, and user control—to improve trust calibration, intent alignment, and consequence awareness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed for personalized wellbeing guidance (physical activity, stress, mental health), but current approaches rely on implicit mechanisms (prompting, alignment) that offer limited control over how recommendations are framed, adapted, and attributed. In wellbeing contexts, where recommendations have real consequences for users' actions and long-term health outcomes, this lack of explicit control raises serious concerns about responsibility, user agency, and appropriate reliance.

Method: The authors propose a system-level perspective that structures conversational behavior through explicit interaction constraints: guidance strategies, explanation styles, degrees of directness, and mechanisms for user control. They build on prior work on tangible recommendations and outline a modular architecture for controllable LLM-based recommendation, where different constraint configurations can be systematically designed and evaluated against user-centered outcomes.

Result: The paper is primarily a conceptual/design contribution. It maps how explicit interaction constraints address three key challenges in wellbeing recommendation: trust calibration, intent alignment, and consequence awareness. It further connects constraint configurations to measurable user-centered outcomes such as self-efficacy, perceived agency, and appropriate reliance, providing a framework for systematic evaluation.

Conclusion: The paper contributes a system-level framework that enables LLM-based recommender systems to be adaptive while remaining transparent, controllable, and aligned with human wellbeing—shifting the design paradigm from implicit model-internal alignment to explicit, evaluable interaction design.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate personalized guidance in wellbeing contexts such as physical activity, stress management, and mental health support, enabling fluent and context-aware interaction but relying on largely implicit mechanisms that shape how recommendations are expressed and adapted. We argue that this reliance on implicit adaptation through prompting and alignment limits control over guidance, responsibility framing, and user influence, which is particularly problematic in wellbeing settings where recommendations affect users' actions and long-term outcomes. We propose a system-level perspective in which conversational behavior is structured through explicit interaction constraints, including guidance strategies, explanation styles, degrees of directness, and mechanisms for user control. Building on prior work on tangible recommendations, we show how these constraints address key challenges in wellbeing-oriented recommendation, namely trust calibration, intent alignment, and consequence awareness. We outline a modular architecture for controllable LLM-based recommendation and discuss how different configurations can be systematically designed and evaluated in relation to user-centered outcomes such as self-efficacy, perceived agency, and appropriate reliance. This paper contributes a system-level framework for designing LLM-based recommender systems that are adaptive while remaining transparent, controllable, and aligned with human wellbeing.

</details>


### [60] [Explainable Control Framework (XCF) based on Fuzzy Model-Agnostic Explanation and LLM Agent-Supported Interface](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25941)
*Faliang Yin,Hak-Keung Lam,David Watson*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Proposes an explainable control framework (XCF) with a hierarchical fuzzy model-agnostic explanation method (HFMAE-C) and an LLM agent-supported user interface, providing multi-level IF-THEN rule explanations and salience values for controller behavior in closed-loop systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Increasingly sophisticated controllers (data-driven black-box models, complex mathematically rigorous designs) create a pressing need for explainable control that provides human-understandable insights into how controllers determine their actions and underlying working mechanisms.

Method: The XCF provides model-agnostic explanations for closed-loop controllers, with optional refinement by system response dynamics. HFMAE-C uses a fuzzy logic system to approximate controller behavior and system dynamics, generating four levels of explanations (sample, local, domain, universe) via IF-THEN rules and salience values. An LLM-agent interface automates requirement analysis, algorithm selection, explanation interpretation into natural language reports, and interactive consultation.

Result: Case studies on an inverted pendulum system and Turtlebot obstacle avoidance demonstrate effectiveness through simulated user experiments and quantitative comparisons with mainstream explainable control approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed XCF with HFMAE-C and LLM-supported interface effectively explains controller decision logic across multiple abstraction levels, validated through case studies and comparative experiments.

Abstract: Increasing demand for precise and reliable control in complex scenarios has led to the development of increasingly sophisticated controllers, including data-driven approaches employing closed box models and mathematically rigorous yet complex designs. This complexity highlights the needs for explainable control that can provide human-understandable insights into controller behavior. In this paper, an explainable control framework (XCF) along with supporting algorithms and user interface are proposed to explain how controllers determine their control actions and their underlying working mechanism. The novel contributions of this work are threefold: First, the XCF is designed to provide model-agnostic explanations for controllers in closed-loop systems and can optionally refine local explanations by system response dynamics. Second, a novel explanation method, hierarchical fuzzy model-agnostic explanation for control systems (HFMAE-C), is proposed based on the designed framework. The HFMAE-C employs a fuzzy logic system to approximate the controller's behavior and system dynamics, providing sample, local, domain and universe level explanations via IF-THEN rules revealing the controller's decision logic and salience values quantifying the contribution of system states to control actions. Third, a large language model agent-supported user interface is developed to automatically analyze user requirements, select appropriate algorithms, interpret the generated explanations to a natural language report, and provide interactive consultation. Case studies on inverted pendulum system and Turtlebot obstacle avoidance demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method through simulated user experiments and quantitative comparisons with mainstream explainable control approaches.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [61] [Systematic Exploration of 4-Expert Heterogeneous Mixture-of-Experts via Automated Pipeline Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23739)
*Yashkumar R Lukhi,Harsh Rameshbhai Moradiya,Radu Timofte,Dmitry Ignatov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An automated pipeline searched heterogeneous 4-expert MoE architectures from the LEMUR dataset, generating 4,463 candidates (1,021 evaluated). A critical coverage bias was discovered: alphabetical enumeration anchored the entire search to the AirNet family, covering only 4.8% of the theoretical space. ShuffleNet+MobileNetV3 ensembles yielded highest accuracy (~0.632); FractalNet and MNASNet were low-yield. The authors propose a stratified random sampling fix and release the corrected pipeline as open-source.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual design of heterogeneous Mixture-of-Experts architectures is labour-intensive and non-scalable. The authors aim to automate the systematic exploration of 4-expert MoE combinations from the LEMUR model database to discover high-performing ensembles without human bias, while characterising the limitations of naïve combinatorial search strategies.

Method: A deterministic code-assembly generator combines base architecture families from LEMUR into MoE4 ensembles using itertools.combinations (alphabetical enumeration). Each ensemble employs a convolutional gating network with temperature scaling, mixup augmentation, and cosine-annealed learning rate scheduling. The pipeline ran for 28 days on an NVIDIA RTX 4090, producing 4,463 candidates across 197 batches, with 1,021 successfully evaluated.

Result: Only 4.8% of the theoretical 23,751 possible 4-family combinations were explored, with the entire space anchored to AirNet due to alphabetical enumeration bias. Within this constrained space, ShuffleNet + MobileNetV3 ensembles achieved the highest mean accuracy (up to 0.632). FractalNet and MNASNet were identified as consistently low-yield families. The coverage bias was precisely characterised and traced to the itertools.combinations generator.

Conclusion: Naïve combinatorial enumeration introduces severe coverage bias in architecture search. A stratified random sampling strategy is proposed to ensure unbiased exploration. Low-yield families (FractalNet, MNASNet) should be excluded from future campaigns. All artefacts and the corrected generator are released open-source in the NNGPT project.

Abstract: We present an automated large-scale search pipeline for heterogeneous 4-Expert Mixture-of-Experts (MoE4) architectures within the LEMUR neural network dataset ecosystem. Building on a hand-crafted heterogeneous MoE reference model, we replace manual design with a deterministic code-assembly generator that systematically combines base architecture families drawn from the LEMUR database into MoE4 ensembles, each governed by a convolutional gating network with temperature scaling, mixup augmentation, and cosine-annealed learning rate scheduling. Over a 28-day campaign on an NVIDIA RTX 4090, the pipeline generated 4,463 candidate models across 197 batches, of which 1,021 were evaluated successfully. A critical finding emerged from the campaign: due to alphabetical enumeration via itertools.combinations, the entire explored search space (4.8% of the theoretical 23,751 possible 4-family combinations) is anchored to a single family, AirNet. We characterise this coverage bias precisely, identify the root cause in the generator, and propose a stratified random sampling fix. Within the AirNet anchored scope, ShuffleNet and MobileNetV3 consistently co-produce the highest-accuracy ensembles (mean accuracy up to 0.632), while FractalNet and MNASNet are identified as low-yield families warranting exclusion in future campaigns. The pipeline, analysis artefacts, and corrected generator are released as part of the open-source NNGPT project at https://github.com/ABrain-One/nn-gpt

</details>


### [62] [Weight-Space Geometry of Offline Reasoning Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23740)
*Aleksandr Nikolich,Igor Kiselev,Vladimir Platonov,Karina Romanova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether various offline reinforcement-learning losses used for knowledge distillation (RFT, RIFT, DFT, Offline GRPO, DPO) produce mechanistically distinct weight updates or converge to similar solutions. Using identical data and a shared base model with attention-only LoRA, the authors find SFT/RFT/RIFT yield nearly colinear weight deltas, while DFT diverges more, GRPO adds orthogonal components, and DPO occupies a near-orthogonal subspace with distinct geometry — yet DPO achieves the highest downstream accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline RL-style losses are widely used to distill reasoning capabilities from large teacher models into smaller student models, but they are typically compared only on downstream accuracy. The authors aim to determine whether these methods are mechanistically distinct or whether they converge to fundamentally similar weight updates, providing insight into whether the choice of loss function matters beyond final performance metrics.

Method: Six methods (SFT, RFT, DFT, RIFT, Offline GRPO, DPO) are trained on identical math rollouts from a single base model (Qwen3-4B) using attention-only LoRA. The resulting weight deltas are analyzed via: (1) cosine similarity, (2) principal-angle subspace analysis across 144 modules, (3) linear mode connectivity (loss barrier measurements), and (4) Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA). Downstream evaluation uses GSM8K (n=1319) and AIME26, with pairwise McNemar tests for statistical significance. DPO uses a 10× smaller learning rate per standard convention.

Result: Key findings: (i) SFT, RFT, and RIFT produce nearly colinear weight deltas (cosine ≥ 0.97, top-1 principal angle ~7° median) and comparable GSM8K accuracy (87-88%, McNemar p ≥ 0.15). (ii) DFT diverges more in direction than any reward-weighted method despite identical data. (iii) Offline GRPO adds a substantial orthogonal component to the SFT direction (~67% globally, up to ~86% in late layers) while remaining in the SFT loss basin. (iv) DPO sits in a near-orthogonal subspace, shows a mode-connectivity barrier, and collapses late-layer CKA to ~0.46. DPO achieves highest GSM8K (93.5%, p < 10⁻⁹) and AIME26 (30.0% vs. 3.3–10.0%) accuracy, though learning-rate differences preclude pure loss-function comparisons.

Conclusion: Loss functions produce mechanistically distinct weight updates despite identical training data: SFT/RFT/RIFT are functionally near-equivalent; DFT diverges counterintuitively; GRPO adds orthogonal structure; DPO learns a fundamentally different solution in a near-orthogonal subspace. DPO's superior accuracy may stem from both its loss function and its conventionally smaller learning rate. A learning-rate-matched DPO comparison is identified as important future work to disentangle these factors.

Abstract: Offline reinforcement-learning losses (RFT, RIFT, DFT, Offline GRPO, DPO) are widely used to distill reasoning from large teachers into smaller students, and are typically compared on downstream accuracy alone. We ask whether they are mechanistically distinct or converge to a similar weight update. Training six methods (SFT, RFT, DFT, RIFT, Offline GRPO, DPO) on identical math rollouts from a single base model (Qwen3-4B) with attention-only LoRA, we analyze the resulting deltas via cosine similarity, principal-angle subspace analysis, linear mode connectivity, and CKA. We observe: (i) SFT, RFT, and RIFT have nearly colinear weight deltas (cosine >= 0.97, top-1 principal angle ~7 deg median over 144 modules) and comparable GSM8K accuracy (87-88%, n=1319; pairwise McNemar p >= 0.15); (ii) DFT diverges further in direction than any reward-weighted method despite using the same data; (iii) Offline GRPO adds a substantial component orthogonal to the SFT direction (~67% globally, up to ~86% in late layers) while staying in the SFT loss basin; (iv) DPO sits in a near-orthogonal subspace, shows a mode-connectivity barrier, and collapses late-layer CKA to ~0.46. DPO also reaches the highest accuracy in our protocol on both GSM8K (93.5%, McNemar p < 10^-9 vs. each other method) and AIME26 (30.0% vs. 3.3-10.0%); its training uses a 10x smaller learning rate than the others (the standard convention), so the update-norm and accuracy gaps reflect loss-function and optimizer choices jointly, and a learning-rate-matched DPO comparison is left for future work.

</details>


### [63] [A Survey on Federated Causal Discovery and Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23741)
*Xianjie Guo,Yuwei Wang,Guodu Xiang,Xiaoli Tang,Kui Yu,Han Yu,Qiang Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A systematic survey unifying federated causal discovery (FCD) and federated causal inference (FCI) into a single pipeline. FCD is organized by methodological paradigm, federation topology, and structural scope, while FCI is categorized by target estimand and estimation strategy. The paper bridges a gap by providing multi-dimensional taxonomies and identifying shared concerns and open challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Causal reasoning is critical for data-driven decisions, yet data are often siloed across institutions due to privacy and communication constraints. Federated learning offers a solution, spawning the fields of FCD and FCI, but the field lacks a comprehensive survey, creating barriers to entry given its interdisciplinary nature.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic literature review, constructing multi-dimensional taxonomies: FCD is organized along three axes—methodological paradigm (how structures are learned), federation topology (how data are partitioned), and structural scope (what knowledge each party obtains). FCI is categorized by target estimand (ATE vs. CATE/ITE) and estimation strategy (classical weighting to deep generative models). The review also examines temporal dynamics, data heterogeneity, missing data, and non-identical variable sets. Crucially, FCD and FCI are formalized as complementary stages of a unified pipeline.

Result: The paper delineates a clear taxonomy of existing FCD and FCI methods, establishes their formal connection as sequential stages in a federated causal reasoning pipeline, and identifies shared concerns including privacy, communication efficiency, theoretical guarantees, and real-world application domains.

Conclusion: FCD and FCI should be viewed as complementary rather than isolated tasks. Key open challenges remain, including tighter privacy guarantees, improved communication efficiency, stronger theoretical foundations, and broader real-world deployment across diverse application domains.

Abstract: Causal reasoning, which encompasses the discovery of causal structures and the inference of causal effects, is fundamental to data-driven decision making. In practice, data for reliable causal analysis are often distributed across institutions and cannot be centralized due to privacy regulations or communication constraints. Federated learning (FL) addresses this by enabling collaborative analysis without raw data sharing, giving rise to the rapidly growing field of federated causal discovery (FCD) and inference (FCI). However, the interdisciplinary nature of this field and the absence of a comprehensive survey present barriers to entry for researchers. This paper bridges that gap by providing a systematic review through multi-dimensional taxonomies. Grounded in the three core design decisions underlying any FCD solution, namely how structures are learned, how data are partitioned, and what structural knowledge each party obtains, we organize FCD along three axes: methodological paradigm, federation topology, and structural scope. We further examine key practical dimensions, including temporal dynamics, data heterogeneity, missing data, and non-identical variable sets. For FCI, we categorize methods by target estimand (average versus individualized/conditional treatment effects) and by estimation strategy, from classical weighting methods to modern deep generative architectures. Unlike prior works that treat FCD and FCI separately, we formalize their connection as complementary stages of a unified federated causal reasoning pipeline, where FCD supplies the structural knowledge required for valid effect estimation in FCI. Finally, we highlight their shared concerns regarding privacy, communication efficiency, theoretical guarantees, and application domains, and conclude by identifying open challenges for future research.

</details>
