### [64] [Low-power analogue neural networks with trainable nonlinear connections for continuous control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23742)
*Ian T. Vidamour,Fernando Aguirre,Thomas J. Hayward,Matthew O. A. Ellis,Charles Swindells,Alexander McDonnell,Martin Trefzer,Finley Robins,Luca Manneschi,Susan Stepney,Tony Kenyon,Oliver J. Sutton,Jack C. Gartside,Ivan Y. Tyukin,Adnan Mehonic,Eleni Vasilaki*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Physical neural networks with trainable nonlinear functions placed on connections (inspired by Kolmogorov-Arnold networks) achieve superior parameter efficiency for smooth, continuous tasks compared to MLPs, but offer no advantage on classification boundaries. Demonstrated on analogue FPAA hardware with ~35k connections and projected ~30 µW CMOS power.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional physical neural networks force nonlinear device responses to act only as scalar weights, underutilising the computational potential of physical substrates. The authors seek to exploit nonlinear device physics more fully by making each physical connection a learnable computational element, rather than merely a weighted edge.

Method: Inspired by Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs), trainable nonlinear functions are placed on network connections instead of nodes. These functions are physically realised as analogue band-pass filters on field-programmable analogue arrays (FPAAs). The approach is validated experimentally on hardware and extended via simulation to memristive implementations.

Result: The architecture achieves far fewer nodes and connections than MLPs on smooth, continuously valued regression tasks (robotic kinematics, continuous control, photovoltaic MPPT). However, no parameter-efficiency gain is observed on classification-like decision boundaries. Trained networks transfer to hardware across ~35,000 connections with quantified fidelity. A dedicated CMOS implementation is projected at ~30 µW. Memristive simulation reproduces the same behaviour, confirming the advantage stems from connection-level nonlinearity, not device specifics.

Conclusion: Placing trainable nonlinearity on connections yields significant parameter-efficiency gains for smooth regression tasks in physical neural networks, independent of the physical substrate. This suggests a task-dependent design principle: connection-level nonlinearity is advantageous for continuous mappings but not for classification boundaries.

Abstract: Physical neural networks promise low-power machine learning by computing directly with analogue device physics, but most architectures force nonlinear device responses to act as scalar weights. Inspired by Kolmogorov-Arnold networks, we place trainable nonlinear functions on the connections, making each physical connection a learnable computational element. Realising these functions as analogue band-pass filters on field-programmable analogue arrays, we find that the benefit is task-dependent and follows from the smoothness of the physical basis: the networks represent smooth, continuously valued targets, including robotic kinematics, continuous control, and photovoltaic maximum-power-point tracking, with far fewer nodes and connections than multilayer perceptrons, but offer no parameter-efficiency advantage on classification-like decision boundaries. Trained networks transfer to hardware across approximately 35,000 connections with quantified fidelity, and a dedicated CMOS implementation is projected to operate at approximately 30 microwatts. A memristive realisation reproduces the same behaviour in simulation, indicating that the advantage comes from placing trainable nonlinearity on connections, rather than from a particular device.

</details>


### [65] [GCT-MARL: Graph-Based Contrastive Transfer for Sample-Efficient Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25073)
*Animesh Animesh,Satheesh K Perepu,Kaushik Dey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GCT-MARL proposes a graph contrastive transfer learning framework for cooperative MARL that uses adaptively weighted alignment loss and a two-phase training protocol to enable effective policy transfer across populations of varying sizes and compositions, while also supporting continual learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training MARL agents from scratch for each new environment or task is expensive and impractical from a deployment perspective. Existing transfer methods in MARL have key limitations, particularly in handling populations with varying sizes and compositions (both homogeneous and heterogeneous).

Method: GCT-MARL builds on the multi-view graph contrastive backbone of MAIL. It introduces (1) a per-view, adaptively weighted alignment loss and (2) a two-phase training protocol designed for transfer across populations of varying sizes and compositions. The framework also supports continual learning via sequential chaining of the two-phase protocol across related tasks.

Result: The framework markedly accelerates convergence on target tasks compared to training from scratch. This holds across homogeneous (within-faction, varying N) and heterogeneous (cross-faction and mixed unit-type) transfer scenarios.

Conclusion: GCT-MARL provides a unified approach that mitigates key limitations in current MARL transfer methods, offering both methodological and empirical contributions for scalable multi-agent transfer and continual learning.

Abstract: In cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL), from a deployment perspective, it is challenging and expensive to train agents from scratch for each new environment or task. In this work, we propose GCT-MARL, a transfer learning framework that builds on the multi-view graph contrastive backbone of MAIL and augments it with a per-view, adaptively weighted alignment loss and a two-phase training protocol specifically designed for transfer across populations of varying sizes and compositions. We empirically demonstrate that the proposed framework markedly accelerates convergence on the target task relative to from-scratch training, in both homogeneous (within-faction, varying N) and heterogeneous (cross-faction and mixed unit-type) transfer scenarios. Furthermore, we show that the framework naturally supports continual learning by sequentially chaining the two-phase transfer protocol across a series of related tasks. Overall, this work provides a unified approach to mitigating key limitations in current MARL transfer methods with new insights at both methodological and empirical levels.

</details>


### [66] [Synergizing Physically Constrained MCMC and Chemical-Informed Gaussian Processes for Reaction Network Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23757)
*Runzhe Liu,Zihao Wang,Wenbo Yang,Shengyang Tao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PC-MCMC-CIGP is a gray-box workflow that integrates spike-and-slab topology sampling, conservation/thermodynamic constraints, and a Chemistry-Informed Gaussian Process for parameter calibration. It distinguishes radical pathways from phenomenological fits on H₂+Br₂ and improves styrene epoxidation yield by 12.5% over GP-BO, with PC-EI reducing low-yield suggestions and EI-style criteria delivering best final yields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extracting interpretable kinetic equations from sparse, noisy chemical time-series data is fundamentally difficult because discrete reaction topology and continuous kinetic parameters are tightly coupled. Existing methods struggle to disentangle these, and there is a need for a reproducible, physically constrained workflow that handles uncertainty-aware experimental design.

Method: PC-MCMC-CIGP integrates three components: (1) spike-and-slab topology sampling via MCMC for reaction network inference, (2) hard conservation laws and thermodynamic screening as physical constraints, and (3) a Chemical-Informed Gaussian Process (CIGP) residual model for parameter calibration and uncertainty-aware acquisition. Five acquisition functions (EI, GWU, PC-EI, uncertainty sampling, discrepancy hunting) plus random search are compared in a 10-seed study.

Result: On the H₂+Br₂ benchmark, the constrained sampler successfully distinguishes elementary radical pathways from deceptive phenomenological fits. On styrene epoxidation, the CIGP optimization loop achieves a 12.5% improvement in final yield over the reported GP-BO baseline. The 10-seed acquisition study reveals that PC-EI substantially reduces low-yield BO suggestions, while EI-style criteria yield the strongest final-yield performance.

Conclusion: The integration of physically constrained topology sampling with a chemistry-informed surrogate model provides a robust, interpretable, and reproducible framework for kinetics discovery and experimental optimization. The choice of acquisition function involves distinct trade-offs between avoiding poor suggestions and maximizing final yield, with PC-EI and EI offering complementary strengths.

Abstract: Extracting interpretable governing equations from sparse, noisy chemical time-series data remains difficult because discrete reaction topology and continuous kinetic parameters are tightly coupled. We present PC-MCMC-CIGP, a reproducible gray-box workflow that combines spike-and-slab topology sampling, hard conservation and thermodynamic screening, and a Chemical-Informed Gaussian Process (CIGP) residual model for parameter calibration and experimental design. The methodological contribution is not a new MCMC or GP family in isolation; rather, it is the integration of these components into a physically constrained workflow with explicit uncertainty-aware acquisition choices. On the H2 + Br2 benchmark, the constrained sampler distinguishes elementary radical pathways from deceptive phenomenological fits in our experiments. On styrene epoxidation, the CIGP optimization loop improves final yield by 12.5% over the reported GP-BO baseline. A new 10-seed acquisition study shows that EI, GWU, PC-EI, uncertainty sampling, discrepancy hunting, and random search have different trade-offs: PC-EI substantially reduces low-yield BO suggestions, while EI-style criteria give the strongest final-yield performance.

</details>


### [67] [Exploring Dualistic Meta-Learning to Enhance Domain Generalization in Open Set Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23758)
*Xiran Wang,Jian Zhang,Lei Qi,Yang Gao,Yinghuan Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MEDIC is a meta-learning strategy that jointly optimizes domain and class matching to address open set domain generalization, where the model must recognize unseen classes in unseen domains while avoiding over-rejection of known-class samples due to class imbalance in one-vs-all training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing open set domain generalization methods using one-vs-all classifiers suffer from severe positive-negative imbalance: few positive samples versus many negatives cause the decision boundary to skew toward positives, leading to over-rejection of out-of-distribution data belonging to known classes from unseen domains. A balanced boundary for both domain shift and class novelty is needed.

Method: MEDIC employs dualistic meta-learning with joint domain-class matching. It performs implicit gradient matching across two simultaneous task-split dimensions: inter-domain splits (matching across source domains) and inter-class splits (matching across known vs. unknown classes), enabling the model to learn decision boundaries that are balanced for both domain generalization and open class detection.

Result: MEDIC outperforms prior methods in open set domain generalization scenarios while maintaining competitive performance on standard close set generalization tasks.

Conclusion: Jointly optimizing for domain and class matching via dualistic meta-learning effectively mitigates the over-rejection problem in open set domain generalization, yielding robust recognition of both known and unknown classes across unseen domains.

Abstract: Domain generalization learns from multiple source domains to generalize to unseen target domains. However, it often neglects the realistic case of label mismatch between source and target. Open set domain generalization is then proposed to recognize unseen classes in unseen domains. A simple approach trains one-vs-all classifiers to separate each class and detect outliers as unknown. Yet, the imbalance between few positive samples and many negative samples skews the decision boundary towards the positive ones, leading the model to over-reject out-of-distribution data, even from known classes in unseen domains. In this paper, we propose a novel meta-learning stategy called dualistic MEta-learning with joint DomaIn-Class matching (MEDIC), which considers implicit gradient matching towards inter-domain and inter-class task splits simultaneously to find optimal boundaries balanced for both domains and classes. Experimental results show that MEDIC not only outperforms prior methods in open set scenarios, but also maintains competitive close set generalization ability.

</details>


### [68] [Low Variance Trust Region Optimization with Independent Actors and Sequential Updates in Cooperative Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25526)
*Bang Giang Le,Viet Cuong Ta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the high-variance problem in sequential-update multi-agent trust region methods by introducing a clipping objective that bounds advantage fluctuation, providing monotonic convergence guarantees to ε-Nash Equilibria, and demonstrating superior empirical performance on standard benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning with independent actors and sequential policy updates, the joint advantage function must be re-estimated after each agent's update. Importance sampling introduces exponentially growing variance, leading to unstable convergence. The authors aim to formally analyze and mitigate this variance problem.

Method: The authors first empirically and theoretically analyze the high-variance advantage estimation problem. They then introduce a clipping objective that caps the upper bound of advantage fluctuations during sequential updates. Using this objective, they prove a monotonic improvement bound with sub-linear convergence to ε-Nash Equilibria. Two practical algorithms are derived from the clipping objective.

Result: Experiments on three popular multi-agent RL benchmarks show the proposed method outperforms baselines in most environments. The method exhibits stable convergence and low-variance advantage estimation across different training settings.

Conclusion: The clipping objective effectively controls advantage variance in sequential-update trust region methods for cooperative MARL, yielding both theoretical guarantees (monotonic bound, sub-linear convergence) and practical performance improvements. Code is publicly released.

Abstract: Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning assumes each agent shares the same reward function and can be trained effectively using the Trust Region framework of single-agent. Instead of relying on other agents' actions, the independent actors setting considers each agent to act based only on its local information, thus having more flexible applications. However, in the sequential update framework, it is required to re-estimate the joint advantage function after each individual agent's policy step. Despite the practical success of importance sampling, the updated advantage function suffers from exponentially high variance problems, which likely result in unstable convergence. In this work, we first analyze the high variance advantage both empirically and theoretically. To overcome this limitation, we introduce a clipping objective to control the upper bounds of the advantage fluctuation in sequential updates. With the proposed objective, we provide a monotonic bound with sub-linear convergence to $ε$-Nash Equilibria. We further derive two new practical algorithms using our clipping objective. The experiment results on three popular multi-agent reinforcement learning benchmarks show that our proposed method outperforms the tested baselines in most environments. By carefully analyzing different training settings, our proposed method is highlighted with both stable convergence properties and the desired low advantage variance estimation. For reproducibility purposes, our source code is publicly available at https://github.com/giangbang/Low-Variance-Trust-Region-MARL.

</details>


### [69] [One Ruler: A Same-Hands Re-Evaluation of Bivariate Causal Direction on Tuebingen, with a Parameter-Free Compression Baseline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23767)
*Wietse Stienstra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper argues that comparing headline accuracies on the Tübingen cause-effect pairs is invalid due to differing evaluation protocols. The authors conduct a standardized re-evaluation of all methods on the same 102 pairs with forced decisions and no tuning. Their zero-parameter compression baseline achieves 74.7%, tying with the best methods. Published figures are inflated by test-set model selection and significance-gated abstention. Under a common ruler, all methods cluster in the low-to-mid 70s.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Tübingen cause-effect pairs benchmark lacks standardized evaluation. Each method reports accuracy under its own protocol (different subsets, weightings, model selection, decision rates), making cross-paper comparisons misleading. The authors aim to establish a fair, common-ruler comparison and expose mechanisms that inflate published accuracies.

Method: The authors re-implement and re-evaluate every method on the identical set of 102 pairs under a strict protocol: no hyperparameter tuning and a forced decision on every pair. They introduce a minimal baseline (sorted-conditional compression) that quantizes, sorts, and first-differences data before feeding to bz2, with zero fitted parameters. They use McNemar's test for statistical comparisons, document mechanisms like test-set model selection and significance-gated abstention, and conduct a pre-registered falsification test.

Result: The zero-parameter compression baseline reaches 74.7% weighted accuracy (p=3.7e-7). On the 100 pairs SLOPE is evaluated on, it scores 76.0% vs. SLOPE's 77.2% (McNemar p=0.39, not significant). A faithful re-run of RECI yields 70.7%, not the often-quoted 77.5% (traced to a mis-copied cell). SLOPE's published 82.4% is a decided-subset figure; reproducing the authors' stored output on decided pairs yields 81.7%. Under the common ruler, all methods cluster in the low-to-mid 70s. Compression score magnitude is a model-free confounding flag (p=2.8e-68).

Conclusion: Cross-paper accuracy comparisons on the Tübingen benchmark are unreliable due to protocol variance. Under standardized conditions, methods perform similarly (low-to-mid 70s), and a simple zero-parameter compression baseline matches the strongest. Published figures are systematically inflated by post-hoc pair selection and abstention mechanisms. The community should adopt standardized evaluation protocols and treat the benchmark as saturated until harder pairs are introduced.

Abstract: Headline accuracies on the Tuebingen cause-effect pairs are routinely compared across papers even though each is measured under its authors' own protocol -- different pair subsets, weightings, model-selection, and decision rates. We argue this is the wrong comparison and run the right one: a same-hands re-evaluation in which every method is run by us on the identical 102 pairs, with one strict rule -- no tuning and a decision forced on every pair. As a clean reference point we introduce a deliberately minimal baseline: sorted-conditional compression, which feeds quantized, sorted, first-differenced data to an off-the-shelf compressor (bz2) and has zero fitted parameters. Under the common ruler the ranking differs sharply from the literature. Our baseline reaches 74.7% weighted accuracy (p = 3.7e-7); on the same 100 pairs that SLOPE is evaluated on it scores 76.0%, a 1.2-point gap below the authors' own forced-decision SLOPE (77.2%) that is well inside noise (McNemar p = 0.39). A faithful re-run of RECI lands at 70.7% -- inside the original authors' reported error bar, not the 77.5% often quoted (which we trace to a mis-copied cell). SLOPE's published 82.4% is a decided-subset figure: scoring the authors' own stored output only on the pairs its significance test chose to answer reproduces 81.7%. Under the common ruler the methods cluster in the low-to-mid 70s and the zero-parameter compressor ties the strongest of them. We document the mechanisms that inflate published figures (test-set model selection, significance-gated abstention) and contribute two further results: compression score magnitude is a model-free confounding flag (p = 2.8e-68), and a pre-registered falsification test fails in an instructive way that bounds the method's theoretical interpretation. Code, pre-registrations, and per-pair outputs are released.

</details>


### [70] [Deciphering Fingerprints of 3D Molecular Surfaces for Accurate Epitope Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23830)
*Fang Wu,Weihao Xuan,Jure Leskovec,Yejin Choi,Li Erran Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SurfBind is a surface-centric Transformer-based framework for antibody-antigen epitope prediction that operates directly on molecular surface representations, outperforming sequence- and backbone-based methods on discontinuous epitopes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing epitope prediction methods rely on sequences or backbone structures and fail to capture discontinuous, surface-driven epitopes that are key to antibody-antigen recognition.

Method: SurfBind uses a Transformer architecture with patch-level molecular surface modeling, binder-aware cross-attention, and a hierarchical coarse-to-fine prediction paradigm that integrates geometric and physicochemical surface cues.

Result: On SAbDab and DB5.5 benchmarks, SurfBind achieves state-of-the-art epitope prediction performance with strong generalization across unseen antibodies and conformational states.

Conclusion: Interaction-aware surface modeling is highly valuable for understanding protein-protein interaction mechanisms, and SurfBind demonstrates the effectiveness of this approach for epitope prediction.

Abstract: Molecular surfaces encode the geometric and physicochemical patterns that determine antibody-antigen recognition, central to epitope prediction. However, existing methods rely on sequences or backbone structures and struggle to capture discontinuous, surface-driven epitopes. This study presents SurfBind, a surface-centric learning framework for epitope prediction that operates directly on molecular surface representations. SurfBind integrates geometric and physicochemical cues through a Transformer-based architecture with patch-level surface modeling, binder-aware cross-attention, and a hierarchical coarse-to-fine prediction paradigm. Experiments on challenging epitope identification benchmarks, including SAbDab and DB5.5, demonstrate that SurfBind achieves state-of-the-art performance and strong generalization across unseen antibodies and conformational states, highlighting the value of interaction-aware surface modeling for understanding the crucial mechanisms of protein-protein interactions.

</details>


### [71] [Reconstructing GRACE Terrestrial Water Storage with Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Networks: An Application to South America](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23833)
*Lukas Arzoumanidis,Lara Johannsen,Klara Middendorf,Annette Eicker,Youness Dehbi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A graph neural network (MTGNN) reconstructs global GRACE-like terrestrial water storage anomalies back to 1940 using only three ERA5 meteorological variables, achieving competitive basin-scale performance (r=0.94) compared to established methods while using far fewer predictors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRACE/GRACE-FO satellite gravimetry provides the only direct global TWS observations, but the record starts only in 2002 — too short for climate-scale analyses. There is a need to extend TWS anomaly estimates further back in time to study multi-decadal hydrological variability and climate teleconnections.

Method: The authors adapt the Multi-variate Time series Graph Neural Network (MTGNN) — originally for urban traffic forecasting — to reconstruct monthly TWSA from 1940–present. The model learns a mapping from daily ERA5 forcings (precipitation, evapotranspiration, runoff) to monthly GRACE TWSA. Spatial dependencies are encoded in a static hybrid adjacency matrix combining geodesic distance with lagged climatic correlations, capturing both local coupling and large-scale teleconnections. The model is systematically compared against GTWS-MLrec, RM-REC, and GRAiCE baselines.

Result: The reconstruction achieves a grid-cell Pearson correlation of 0.69, a basin-mean correlation of 0.94, and near-zero bias. It successfully reproduces spatial fingerprints of the 2015/16 El Niño and 2020/21 La Niña events. Compared to baselines, the MTGNN approach is within 0.025 correlation of the best method at basin scale while using only half to one-tenth the number of predictors. All models exhibit characteristic weaknesses in arid regions.

Conclusion: Graph neural networks with interpretable hybrid adjacency matrices offer a data-efficient and statistically competitive alternative to existing GRACE reconstruction methods, enabling robust TWS anomaly hindcasting for climate-scale hydrological analyses.

Abstract: Terrestrial water storage (TWS) integrates snow, soil moisture, surface water, and groundwater and is a key indicator of how climate variability and human activity reshape the global water cycle. The GRACE and GRACE-FO satellite missions provide the only direct, globally consistent observations of TWS change, but their record only begins in 2002 which is too short for many climate-scale analyses. We present a deep learning application that reconstructs monthly GRACE-like TWS anomalies (TWSA) back to 1940 by learning the relationship between daily ERA5 meteorological forcing (precipitation, evapotranspiration, runoff) and monthly GRACE observations. In contrast to prior reconstruction approaches based on grid-cell-wise regression, CNNs, or LSTMs, we adapt a multi-variate time series graph neural network (MTGNN) architecture, which was originally developed for mobility and traffic forecasting on urban sensor networks to this satellite-geodesy task. Spatial dependencies are encoded in a static, interpretable hybrid adjacency matrix that combines geodesic proximity with lagged correlations of climatic time series, capturing both local hydrological coupling and large-scale teleconnections. The reconstruction achieves a grid-cell Pearson correlation of 0.69, a basin-mean correlation of 0.94, and a near-zero bias, and it reproduces the spatial fingerprints of the 2015/16 El Niño and 2020/21 La Niña events. A systematic comparison with established reconstruction approaches (GTWS-MLrec, RM-REC, GRAiCE) shows that the graph-based model is statistically competitive at basin scale, reaching a correlation within 0.025 of the best baseline while using only roughly half to a tenth of the predictors the other models require and revealing characteristic weaknesses in arid regions in all models. The complete implementation is publicly available at github.com/hcu-cml/MTGNN-TWS-Reconstruction-GRACE

</details>


### [72] [The Degeneracy Distillery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23838)
*T. Lucas Makinen,Deaglan J. Bartlett,Niall Jeffrey,Benjamin D. Wandelt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The degeneracy distillery automatically detects and symbolically resolves degenerate parameter combinations in physical models by estimating and flattening the Fisher information matrix, discovering coordinate transformations that yield independent parameter effects on data. This global flattening reduces simulation budgets for neural posterior estimation by up to 10× while providing physical insight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Degenerate parameters—those producing indistinguishable effects on data—undermine both machine learning and probabilistic inference. Manual identification of degeneracies is labor-intensive and model-specific. An automated, symbolic method is needed to detect and resolve degeneracies directly from parameter-data pairs, improving downstream inference efficiency and yielding scientific understanding.

Method: The method operates in three stages: (1) estimating the Fisher information matrix (FIM) from parameter-simulation pairs, (2) analyzing the FIM's information geometry to detect degenerate directions (near-zero eigenvalues), and (3) discovering symbolic coordinate transformations that globally flatten the FIM—rendering parameters statistically independent. Unlike posterior-based local methods, this approach flattens the Fisher information in expectation globally, requiring no observed data.

Result: On synthetic and real-world problems, the method successfully discovers symbolic transformations identifying independent parameter combinations. The resulting coordinates globally flatten the Fisher information, in contrast to single-point posterior flattening. Downstream neural posterior estimation required up to 10× fewer simulations at matched validation calibration, while simultaneously yielding physical insight.

Conclusion: The degeneracy distillery provides an automated, symbolic approach to detecting and resolving parameter degeneracies via global Fisher information flattening. It substantially reduces simulation budgets for neural posterior estimation and simultaneously delivers interpretable physical insights about the system under study.

Abstract: When two or more parameters or labels produce similar data, they are degenerate, or hard to distinguish. Degeneracies render both label prediction and inverse problems difficult, since both machine learning algorithms and probabilistic samplers rely on the distinguishability of data and its gradients with respect to parameters. However, identifying degeneracies in physical models or real-world datasets can be elucidating about the choice of model or the underlying process that produces the data. We present the degeneracy distillery, a method that (1) detects and (2) resolves degenerate parameter combinations (a) automatically and (b) symbolically, from parameter-data (or parameter-simulation) pairs alone, through estimation and flattening of the Fisher information matrix. By exploring the information geometry of the likelihood, we characterize degeneracies as an intrinsic property of the physical model, requiring no realised data observation. We demonstrate our approach on a range of synthetic and real-world problems, discovering symbolic coordinate transformations that identify the combinations of parameters of a model which yield independent effects on the data. The resulting coordinates flatten the Fisher information in expectation globally, in contrast to posterior-based methods that flatten only at a single point, and substantially reduce the simulation budget required for downstream neural posterior estimation. In test cases we require up to $10\times$ fewer simulations for posterior estimation at matched validation calibration whilst simultaneously gaining physical insight on the system.

</details>


### [73] [EveLoad: Cognitive Workload Recognition from Event-Based Eye Movements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25177)
*Guorui Lu,Shaohua Guan,Zhen Xu,Qinyu Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces EveLoad, the first event-camera-based eye-movement dataset for graded cognitive workload recognition (6 levels), collected from 20 participants using an N-back-guided fixation paradigm. A spatiotemporal event representation learning framework achieves ~96% accuracy, demonstrating event-based eye tracking as a promising modality for workload-aware adaptive rehabilitation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing eye-movement-based cognitive workload recognition relies on frame-based eye trackers with limited temporal resolution and degraded performance under rapid eye movements. Furthermore, prior studies using free-viewing paradigms risk models learning gaze-location distributions rather than genuine workload-related ocular dynamics. Event cameras offer microsecond resolution, high dynamic range, and low latency, but no event-based eye-movement dataset with graded workload annotations existed for controlled, task-driven settings.

Method: The authors collect EveLoad, an event-based eye-movement dataset from 20 healthy participants performing a controlled N-back-guided fixation paradigm that spatially constrains gaze to isolate workload-related ocular dynamics. They annotate six graded cognitive workload levels. A benchmark is established, and a learning framework is proposed that encodes spatiotemporal event representations for workload classification, evaluated under subject-specific and mixed random split protocols.

Result: The proposed spatiotemporal event representation learning approach achieves an average subject-specific accuracy of 96.36% and 96.13% under mixed random split evaluation for six-level cognitive workload classification.

Conclusion: Event-based eye movements provide a useful sensing pathway for cognitive workload recognition, offering fine-grained ocular dynamics that can inform future workload-aware rehabilitation systems in extended reality and robot-assisted environments.

Abstract: Cognitive workload monitoring is important for adaptive rehabilitation and assistive interfaces, where task difficulty, pacing, and feedback should be adjusted according to the user's cognitive state to avoid overload and under-challenge. Emerging extended reality and robot-assisted rehabilitation environments provide controllable training tasks, but they require unobtrusive sensing methods that can capture rapid ocular dynamics during interaction. Existing eye-movement-based cognitive workload recognition methods mainly rely on frame-based eye trackers, which often suffer from limited temporal resolution and degraded robustness under rapid eye movements. In contrast, event cameras provide microsecond-level temporal resolution, high dynamic range and low latency, making them suitable for capturing fine-grained ocular dynamics. Many previous studies rely on free-viewing or similar paradigms, where gaze locations can vary across tasks. As a result, models may learn associations between gaze-location distributions and cognitive workload, rather than workload-related eye movement characteristics themselves. In this work, we introduce EveLoad, which, to the best of our knowledge, is the first event-based eye-movement dataset with graded cognitive workload annotations, collected from 20 healthy participants under spatially constrained and task-driven conditions using a controlled N-back-guided fixation paradigm. Based on this dataset, we establish a benchmark for cognitive workload recognition with six workload levels and propose a learning framework that encodes spatiotemporal event representations. Experimental results show that our approach achieves an average subject-specific accuracy of 96.36% and 96.13% under mixed random split evaluation. These results suggest that event-based eye movements may provide a useful sensing pathway for future workload-aware rehabilitation.

</details>


### [74] [Machine Learning Modeling for Real-Time Melt Pool Monitoring in Laser Powder Bed Fusion Additive Manufacturing: A Hybrid Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23851)
*Inioluwa Emmanuel,Zhuo Yang,Ho Yeung,Xinyao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A hybrid approach combining EfficientNetB0 feature extraction with Random Forest classification achieves superior melt pool anomaly detection (F1=0.9451, AUC=0.9904) with sub-millisecond inference (1.15 ms), outperforming pure deep learning models for real-time LPBF monitoring under data-limited conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time melt pool monitoring in laser powder bed fusion (LPBF) additive manufacturing demands both high accuracy and low-latency inference under hardware constraints of open-architecture machines. Existing approaches may prioritize one over the other, and there is a need to systematically evaluate AI/ML methods that balance classification performance with deployability on the factory floor.

Method: A binary image classification framework was developed using 1,200 balanced melt pool images (Nickel superalloy 625, NIST AMMT platform). Three transfer learning architectures (ResNet50, EfficientNetB0, MobileNetV2) were benchmarked against two Random Forest variants: one trained on EfficientNetB0 feature embeddings (hybrid) and one on raw pixels (baseline). Stratified 80/20 train-test splits with 90/10 validation splits were used, with standardized resizing, normalization, and label-preserving augmentation. Evaluation covered accuracy, precision, recall, F1, AUC, training time, inference latency, and CPU/GPU usage.

Result: The hybrid EfficientNetB0 + Random Forest achieved top performance: F1=0.9451, accuracy=0.9458, AUC=0.9904, with 1.15 ms per-image inference. Pure deep learning models showed higher inference times with lower accuracy, demonstrating the hybrid approach's superiority for real-time, data-limited additive manufacturing monitoring.

Conclusion: Combining pre-trained convolutional features with classical ensemble methods provides a robust, computationally efficient solution for real-time melt pool anomaly detection. The hybrid architecture is well-suited for deployment on resource-constrained, open-architecture LPBF systems where both accuracy and low latency are critical.

Abstract: This work investigates the implementation of artificial intelligence and machine learning (AI/ML) for real-time monitoring in laser powder bed fusion (LPBF) additive manufacturing. We developed a binary image classification framework for distinguishing normal and abnormal melt pool images using a balanced dataset of 1,200 images collected from Nickel superalloy 625 on the NIST AMMT platform. The study evaluates accuracy and inference time based on control requirements and hardware limitations of open-architecture LPBF machines. We benchmark three transfer learning architectures (ResNet50, EfficientNetB0, and MobileNetV2) against two Random Forest approaches: one trained on EfficientNetB0 feature embeddings (hybrid) and one trained on raw pixel features (baseline). Images are stratified into 80/20 train-test splits, with a further 90/10 validation split on the training set, and undergo standardized resizing, normalization, and label-preserving data augmentation to emulate realistic process variability. Each model is evaluated using accuracy, precision, recall, F1 score, and area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC), along with training time, inference latency, and CPU & GPU usage to capture deployability constraints relevant to factory-floor monitoring. The hybrid EfficientNetB0-plus-Random Forest approach achieves the best performance on the held-out test set, with an F1 score of 0.9451, accuracy of 0.9458, and AUC of 0.9904, while maintaining sub-millisecond per-image inference (1.15 ms). In contrast, purely deep learning models exhibit significantly higher inference times with lower accuracy. These results demonstrate that combining pre-trained convolutional features with classical ensemble methods provides a robust, computationally efficient route to real-time melt pool anomaly detection in data-limited additive manufacturing environments.

</details>


### [75] [Sesame: Structure-Aware Molecular Generation via Spatial Density-Map Conditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23856)
*Konstantin Yatsenko,Arvind Thiagarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sesame is a diffusion-based molecular generation model that uses a spatial pairformer to condition on continuous spatial density maps of both protein pockets and partial molecular scaffolds. It unifies de novo generation and fragment-conditioned lead optimization, with joint denoising of atom types, bond types, and positions, plus trajectory finetuning for improved sample quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative molecular models for drug design often treat de novo generation and scaffold-based lead optimization as separate tasks. The authors argue that a unified conditioning mechanism — one that can handle both empty (de novo) and partial (scaffold/fragment) starting points while incorporating protein pocket context — is essential for practical computational drug design pipelines. Existing methods lack a single framework that cleanly supports both modes with spatial awareness.

Method: Sesame employs a diffusion framework operating on atom types, bond types, and 3D positions jointly. The key architectural contribution is a spatial pairformer module that conditions generation on continuous spatial density maps representing both the protein pocket and any partial molecular structure. This allows a single model to handle de novo generation (empty molecular map) and fragment-conditioned growth. Additionally, a trajectory finetuning scheme is introduced, where the model is further trained on its own sampling rollouts to improve generation quality. The model is trained on large-scale ligand-only and protein-ligand datasets.

Result: The paper reports that Sesame supports both de novo molecular generation and scaffold-based lead optimization within a unified framework. The trajectory finetuning scheme demonstrably improves generation quality. Quantitative benchmarking results are not detailed in the abstract, but the architectural contributions are presented as enabling productive scaffold growth conditioned on protein pocket context.

Conclusion: Sesame demonstrates that a single, spatially-aware diffusion model with density-map conditioning can effectively unify de novo drug design and fragment-based lead optimization. The spatial pairformer and trajectory finetuning represent meaningful advances toward practical generative tools for medicinal chemists.

Abstract: Generative molecular models for drug design are a promising direction with much active research. In the next phase of computational drug design, such models will need to understand small molecule structure and protein-ligand interactions, and they will need to possess the machinery to generate molecules de novo. Incorporating each feature poses a critical challenge. Equally important, yet often treated as secondary, is the ability to grow a molecule from a partial starting point -- a scaffold or fragment supplied by a chemist -- which is the central operation of lead optimization. We present Sesame (Spatial Evoformer for a Structure-Aware Molecular Engine), a diffusion-based molecular generation model that leverages a novel spatial pairformer module to condition on partial molecular structure and the surrounding protein pocket, both expressed as continuous spatial density maps. This single conditioning mechanism supports both de novo generation and fragment-conditioned lead optimization, letting a medicinal chemist prune a hit to a scaffold and have Sesame grow it in productive ways. In addition to this module, we also introduce a diffusion framework for joint denoising of atom types, bond types, and positions, along with a trajectory finetuning scheme that trains on the model's own sampling rollouts to improve generation quality. Sesame is trained on a large corpus of ligand-only and protein-ligand datasets.

</details>


### [76] [Are Safety Guarantees in Neural Networks Safe? How to Compute Trustworthy Robustness Certifications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23858)
*Merkouris Papamichail,Konstantinos Varsos,Giorgos Flouris,João Marques-Silva*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses the intractability of computing volume-optimal robustness certifications for neural networks by introducing the apothem measure. It presents an algorithm to compute apothem-optimal certifications with linear oracle complexity, proves the impossibility of efficient volume-optimal oracle-based algorithms, introduces dual certifications for class-wise upper bounds, and demonstrates significant improvements over prior work on MNIST benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing robustness certification methods aim to maximize the volume of axis-aligned hyper-rectangles representing safe input regions, but recent intractability results show volume-optimal certifications cannot be computed in reasonable time. This motivates the search for an alternative, efficiently computable optimality measure.

Method: The authors introduce the apothem measure (minimum edge half-length) as an alternative to volume for certifying robustness. They develop an oracle-based algorithm that computes apothem-optimal certifications using a linear number of calls to a NN verifier relative to the input domain's diameter. They also introduce dual certifications that provide apothem-minimum upper bounds encompassing all instances of a class. The ParallelepipedoNN system implements these ideas and is evaluated on MNIST and Fashion MNIST.

Result: The apothem-optimal certification algorithm requires only a linear number of oracle calls. The authors formally prove that no volume-optimal oracle-based algorithm can exist, even ignoring oracle costs. Empirical evaluation on MNIST and Fashion MNIST shows at least a two-fold improvement in minimum edge length compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: The apothem measure is a practical and theoretically sound alternative to volume-based robustness certification. It enables efficient computation of optimal certifications while circumventing known intractability barriers, offering both tighter certifications and improved computational feasibility.

Abstract: A primary challenge in AI safety is the existence of adversarial examples -- slightly distorted inputs that cause a neural network (NN) to misclassify. To mitigate this problem, recent research focuses on the computation of robustness certifications, which, for a given input, determine the largest distortion the input may receive without breaking the network's prediction. Robustness certifications can be interpreted as an axis-aligned hyper-rectangle (multi-dimensional intervals). Most existing approaches focus on maximizing the certification's volume, but recent intractability results prohibit the computation of volume-optimal certifications in reasonable time. We introduce the apothem measure and show how to compute apothem-optimal certifications in a linear number of calls to a NN verifier (oracle) w.r.t. the input domain's diameter. Moreover, we prove that we cannot have a volume-optimal, oracle-based algorithm, even if we discard the oracle costs. Also, we introduce dual certifications -- an interval including all instances of a class -- thus providing apothem-minimum upper bounds to a robustness certification. Further, we present the ParallelepipedoNN system, which we evaluate on the standard MNIST and Fashion MNIST benchmarks. A preliminary comparison with existing work on the same datasets reveals at least two-fold improvement w.r.t. the minimum edge length.

</details>


### [77] [Exact Schur-Sylvester Dimensionality Reductions for Non-Smooth Stochastic Complexity and Manifold Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23867)
*Trenton Lau,Gary P. T. Choi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Exact NML codelength computation for non-smooth estimators like Lasso has been bottlenecked by O(N³) projection and volume operations. This paper uses block Schur complement and Sylvester's determinant identity to collapse complexity to O(k³ + N²k) per step, achieving >14,100× speedup while maintaining double-precision equivalence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exact Normalized Maximum Likelihood (NML) codelength computation for regular non-smooth estimators (Lasso, sparse SVMs, Elastic Net, Group Lasso) is computationally prohibitive due to cubic scaling in the sample size N. Specifically, each step of the Propose-and-Project Metropolis–Hastings (PPMH) sampler requires: (1) inverting an (N+k)×(N+k) generalized KKT system for the projection operator, and (2) computing the determinant of an (N−k)×(N−k) Gram matrix for the volume factor — both O(N³). This has historically rendered exact non-smooth NML estimation intractable for large-scale problems.

Method: The authors derive a mathematically equivalent reformulation of both bottleneck operations using: (a) the block Schur complement to reduce the KKT matrix inversion, and (b) Sylvester's determinant identity to simplify the volume factor computation. This reduces per-step complexity from O(N³) to O(k³ + N²k), where k is the model sparsity (number of active predictors). The reduction is generalized to Sparse SVMs, Elastic Net, and Group Lasso. A rigorous numerical stability analysis is provided, and sampler efficiency is evaluated via Effective Sample Size (ESS) per second.

Result: Empirical benchmarks on high-dimensional datasets demonstrate a constant speedup exceeding 14,100× compared to the baseline exact methods, while maintaining double-precision numerical equivalence (i.e., no approximation error). This renders exact non-smooth NML estimation practically tractable for large-scale statistical inference.

Conclusion: The proposed Schur-complement/Sylvester-identity reformulation eliminates the cubic scaling bottleneck in exact NML computation for non-smooth estimators. The massive speedup (>14,100×) with no loss of numerical precision makes exact large-scale NML inference feasible, opening the door to rigorous minimum description length (MDL) model selection for high-dimensional sparse models without resorting to approximations.

Abstract: The exact computation of the Normalized Maximum Likelihood (NML) codelength for regular non-smooth estimators (e.g., Lasso) has been historically limited by the cubic scaling walls of manifold-constrained projection and volume integration. At each step of the geometric Propose-and-Project Metropolis--Hastings (PPMH) sampler, evaluating the projection operator requires inverting an $(N+k) \times (N+k)$ generalized KKT matrix, while calculating the volume factor requires the determinant of an $(N-k) \times (N-k)$ Gram matrix. This paper presents an exact, mathematically equivalent formulation that bypasses both bottlenecks by utilizing the block Schur complement and Sylvester's determinant identity. We prove that the computational complexity of both operations collapses from $\mathcal{O}(N^3)$ to $\mathcal{O}(k^3 + N^2 k)$ per step. We generalize this reduction to Sparse Support Vector Machines (SVMs), Elastic Net, and Group Lasso. Finally, we provide a rigorous numerical stability analysis and evaluate the sampler's efficiency using the Effective Sample Size (ESS) per second. Our empirical benchmarks on high-dimensional datasets confirm a constant speedup exceeding $14{,}100\times$ while maintaining double-precision numerical equivalence, rendering exact non-smooth NML estimation highly tractable for large-scale statistical inference.

</details>


### [78] [Federated Survival Analysis in Healthcare: A Multi-Model Evaluation on Cross-Institutional Heterogeneous Breast Cancer Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23871)
*Natalia Moreno-Blasco,Anusha Ihalapathirana,Pekka Siirtola,Miguel Fernandez-de-Retana*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A systematic evaluation of federated survival analysis across three models (Cox PH, DeepSurv, RSF) and three federated optimizers (FedAvg, FedProx, FedAdam) on real breast cancer data, showing FL outperforms local training and can match centralized performance, with RSF proving most robust and FedAvg/FedProx most stable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Survival analysis requires large, diverse cohorts rarely available at single institutions, and privacy regulations prevent data centralization. While federated learning offers a privacy-preserving alternative, its effectiveness for survival modeling under realistic, heterogeneous conditions remains insufficiently understood.

Method: Cross-institutional breast cancer cohort with naturally heterogeneous client distributions is used to compare centralized, local, and federated training of three survival models: Cox Proportional Hazards, DeepSurv, and Random Survival Forest. For gradient-based models, three FL optimization strategies are evaluated: FedAvg, FedProx, and FedAdam.

Result: FL consistently outperforms local training and approaches, occasionally exceeds, centralized performance. RSF offers the best overall balance of discrimination, calibration, and robustness. Performance depends on client distribution diversity. FedAvg and FedProx are stronger and more stable than FedAdam.

Conclusion: Practical, decision-oriented guidelines are derived, mapping data, privacy, interpretability, and resource constraints to recommended model and training-paradigm choices for federated survival modeling in healthcare.

Abstract: Survival analysis is central to clinical decision-making, yet reliable time-to-event models require large, diverse cohorts that are rarely available at a single institution, while privacy regulations restrict the centralization of patient data. Federated learning (FL) offers a privacy-preserving alternative by training shared models without exchanging raw data, but its effectiveness for survival modeling under realistic, heterogeneous conditions remains insufficiently understood. This paper presents a systematic, multi-model evaluation of federated survival analysis on a cross-institutional breast cancer cohort with naturally heterogeneous distributed clients. Three representative survival models, the Cox Proportional Hazards model, DeepSurv, and Random Survival Forest (RSF), are compared across centralized, local, and federated training, and three federated optimization strategies (FedAvg, FedProx, and FedAdam) are assessed for the gradient-based models. Results show that FL consistently outperforms local training and approaches, and occasionally exceeds, centralized performance, while RSF offers the best overall balance of discrimination, calibration, and robustness across heterogeneous clients. We further find that performance depends on the diversity of client distributions, and that FedAvg and FedProx are stronger and more stable than FedAdam. Based on these findings, we derive practical, decision-oriented guidelines mapping data, privacy, interpretability, and resource constraints to recommended model and training-paradigm choices for federated survival modeling in healthcare.

</details>


### [79] [MGI: Member vs Generated Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23872)
*Bihe Zhao,Michel Meintz,Juangui Xu,Franziska Boenisch,Adam Dziedzic*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper formalizes "Member vs Generated Inference" (MGI) — distinguishing training members from model-generated samples — and proposes Data Circuit Breaker (DCB), a three-stage method leveraging autoencoder and latent generator signals, outperforming existing membership inference and attribution methods across generative models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing membership inference methods misclassify generated samples as training members, and attribution methods misclassify training members as generated, because both rely on likelihood-related signals that are similarly elevated for training data and model outputs. A dedicated framework is needed for the MGI problem.

Method: Data Circuit Breaker (DCB) is a three-stage approach: (1) autoencoder reconstruction error, (2) latent-space density estimation, and (3) a combined classifier that integrates complementary signals from the generative model's autoencoder and latent generator to distinguish training members from generated samples.

Result: DCB consistently outperforms membership inference and attribution methods across image autoregressive and diffusion models, remains effective when models reproduce near-duplicates of training samples, and generalizes to model derivative settings where new models are trained on generated data.

Conclusion: MGI is a distinct and practically important challenge that existing methods fail to address; DCB provides a robust solution by combining complementary signals from different model components, with strong generalization even under challenging distributional shifts.

Abstract: As generative models increasingly produce samples that are indistinguishable from human-created content, it becomes difficult to determine whether a given data point was part of a model's natural training set or was generated by the model itself, especially when models memorize and reproduce training data. We formalize this challenge as Member vs Generated Inference (MGI): given a sample and a target generative model, infer whether the sample is a true training member or a generated output of that model. Focusing on image generation, we show that existing membership inference methods systematically misclassify generated samples as training members, while attribution-based methods often misclassify true members as generated. This failure arises because both approaches rely on likelihood-related signals that are similarly elevated for training examples and for the model's own outputs. To address MGI, we propose Data Circuit Breaker (DCB), a three-stage method that combines complementary signals from a generative model's autoencoder and latent generator to distinguish training members from generated samples. Across multiple generative models, including image autoregressive and diffusion models, DCB consistently addresses the shortcomings of membership inference and attribution methods, remains effective even when models reproduce near-duplicates of training samples, and generalizes to challenging model derivative settings in which new models are trained on generated data.

</details>


### [80] [GRACE: Gated Refinement for Accurate Causal Edge Discovery in High-Dimensional Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23880)
*Mohammad Fesanghary,Abhinav Havaldar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GRACE proposes a gated refinement approach for causal discovery that combines a fast linear CI skeleton with L0-regularized Hard Concrete gates to prune false positives, achieving high precision and significant speed gains over nonlinear CI tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constraint-based causal discovery methods are statistically rigorous but their nonlinear conditional independence tests don't scale to high-dimensional datasets. Score-based methods avoid CI testing but require arbitrary thresholds to binarize continuous edge scores, producing unreliable binary decisions due to overlapping score distributions.

Method: GRACE uses a two-stage pipeline: (1) a fast linear conditional independence skeleton provides high-recall candidate edges; (2) a single gated model with Hard Concrete gates and L0 regularization learns which edges genuinely improve prediction, automatically adapting regularization to problem dimensions and skeleton density, producing a clean bimodal separation of gate values near 0 or 1 for robust binarization.

Result: On synthetic benchmarks with diverse topologies (scale-free, Erdős-Rényi, small-world) up to d=100, GRACE substantially improves F1 over its base CI method while maintaining high precision, outperforms attention-based and score-based alternatives, and matches or exceeds expensive nonlinear CI tests at 75× speedup. On real-world Elbe River flow data with confounders and distributional shifts, a temporal bootstrap variant recovers 9/11 causal edges with 1 false positive (F1=0.86, AUROC=0.99), reducing the skeleton's 106 false positives by 99%.

Conclusion: GRACE offers a scalable, statistically grounded causal discovery method that bridges the gap between constraint-based rigor and score-based scalability, delivering robust edge selection without arbitrary thresholds through L0-regularized gating with natural bimodal separation.

Abstract: From climate teleconnections to gene regulation, modern time-series datasets encompass tens or hundreds of interacting variables, making causal discovery increasingly challenging. Constraint-based methods offer statistical rigor but their nonlinear CI tests are infeasible at scale, while score-based alternatives avoid CI testing but require arbitrary thresholds to binarize continuous edge scores. We propose GRACE ($\textbf{G}$ated $\textbf{R}$efinement for $\textbf{A}$ccurate $\textbf{C}$ausal $\textbf{E}$dge discovery), which refines constraint-based discovery using Hard Concrete gates with $L_0$ regularization: each candidate edge has an independent gate whose values concentrate near 0 or 1, yielding a clean bimodal separation that makes the binary decision robust, unlike the narrow, overlapping score distributions produced by $L_1$ and attention-based methods. A fast linear CI skeleton provides high-recall candidates; a single gated model then prunes false positives by learning which edges genuinely improve prediction, with automatic regularization adapted to problem dimensions and skeleton density. Systematic experiments on synthetic benchmarks, spanning diverse graph topologies (scale-free, Erdős-R'enyi, small-world) and dimensionalities up to $d=100$, show that GRACE substantially improves F1 over its base CI method while maintaining high precision, and outperforms attention-based and score-based alternatives. GRACE matches or exceeds expensive nonlinear CI tests at a fraction of the cost ($75\times$ faster). On a real-world river flow dataset, where rainfall confounders, variable propagation lags, and distributional shifts violate standard assumptions, a temporal bootstrap variant of GRACE recovers 9 of 11 causal edges along the Elbe River with only 1 false positive ($F_1 = 0.86$, AUROC${} = 0.99$), reducing the skeleton's 106 false positives by 99%.

</details>


### [81] [ARIA: Adaptive Region-Based Importance Allocation for Conditional Diffusion Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23898)
*Loay Mualem,Vinh Tong,Samir Darouich,Mathias Niepert*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ARIA is a framework that adaptively allocates training effort across regions of the conditioning space during conditional diffusion distillation, by tracking teacher-student discrepancy online. It improves upon Random Conditioning (RC), especially in unseen/underrepresented regimes, with theoretical guarantees on discrepancy tracking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge distillation in conditional diffusion models requires exploring a large conditioning space, but practical bottlenecks arise from limited paired image-condition data and computational infeasibility of synthetic image generation. While recent work (Random Conditioning, RC) addresses this by switching conditions during training, it leaves open the question: given a large conditioning corpus, how should training effort be optimally allocated across conditioning regions to maximize distillation quality?

Method: ARIA maintains online estimates of teacher-student discrepancy at the region level of the conditioning space. During training, it adaptively allocates update effort toward regions where misalignment persists, while keeping the original distillation objective unchanged. The method includes a tracking mechanism that follows the evolving discrepancy, supported by theoretical analysis under bounded variance and drift assumptions.

Result: ARIA outperforms Random Conditioning (RC) across most architectures and settings, with the most pronounced improvements in unseen and underrepresented conditioning regimes. The theoretical analysis confirms the tracking mechanism reliably follows the evolving discrepancy during training.

Conclusion: Adaptive allocation of training effort across conditioning regions, as implemented by ARIA, is an effective and principled strategy for improving conditional diffusion model distillation, particularly for broadening generalization to underexplored parts of the conditioning space.

Abstract: Distilling conditional diffusion models aims to transfer the behavior of a large teacher to a smaller student while preserving alignment across conditioning inputs. Unlike recognition tasks, knowledge distillation in conditional diffusion often struggles to transfer knowledge beyond the training distribution, since the predicted noise strongly depends on the conditioning signal. As a result, effective distillation requires exploring a large conditioning space. In practical settings, this creates a major bottleneck. Paired image-condition data may be limited, and generating synthetic images for every available condition is often computationally infeasible, while the pool of conditions, such as text prompts, can be extremely large. Recent work addresses this issue by switching conditions during training, exposing the student to a broader conditioning space without changing the distillation objective. Yet this raises a complementary question: once a large conditioning corpus is available, how should the training effort be allocated? In this work, we introduce ARIA, a framework that adaptively allocates training effort across coarse regions of the conditioning space. By maintaining online estimates of teacher-student discrepancy at the region level, ARIA focuses updates where misalignment persists while preserving the original distillation objective. Empirically, ARIA improves over RC across most architectures and settings, with the clearest gains observed in unseen and underrepresented regimes. We also provide a theoretical analysis showing that the proposed tracking mechanism follows the evolving discrepancy during training under bounded variance and drift assumptions.

</details>


### [82] [Closing the Loop: Formally Verified Law as a Reward Signal for Self-Improving Legal AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23913)
*Armin Heydari,Torben Leowald*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A formally verifiable architecture for legal AI is proposed, adapting the "LLM proposes, verifier disposes" paradigm. It combines LLM-driven autoformalization into an extended Catala calculus, a verification kernel, and proof-trace-based explanations, demonstrated on German procedural deadlines, U.S. Commerce Clause analysis, and cross-jurisdictional sanction proportionality, with a structural advantage for closing the RL loop in legal AI training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Legal AI lacks formally verifiable reward signals, making reliable training and correctness guarantees difficult. The mathematical AI paradigm of separating proposal (LLM) from verification (deterministic verifier) has not been adapted to the distinctive demands of law, where both computational rules and open-textured reasoning must be accommodated.

Method: The architecture has three components: (1) LLM-driven autoformalization into a formal legal calculus that extends Catala, (2) a verification kernel that deterministically checks formalized arguments, and (3) explanation generation grounded in formal proof traces. For computational law, it yields provable correctness; for open-textured analysis, it provides structural guarantees ensuring all argument stages are addressed, argumentation occurs at correct stages, and deductive links between steps are valid. The deterministic external verifier also supplies verifiable outcomes to close the reinforcement-learning training loop.

Result: The architecture was demonstrated on three tasks: procedural deadline calculations in German law, Commerce Clause analysis in U.S. constitutional law, and cross-jurisdictional sanction proportionality. The deterministic verifier successfully provided verifiable outcomes, closing the traditional RL training loop gap in law.

Conclusion: The proposed architecture effectively adapts formal verification to legal AI, offering provable correctness for computational legal tasks and structural guarantees for open-textured reasoning, while also enabling more reliable reinforcement learning through verifiable reward signals.

Abstract: This article develops an architecture that creates a formally verifiable reward signal to train legal AI, adapting the LLM proposes, verifier disposes paradigm from mathematical AI to the distinctive demands of law. We present an architecture comprising LLM-driven autoformalization into a formal legal calculus extending Catala, a verification kernel, and explanation generation grounded in formal proof traces. For the computational components of law, the architecture provides provable correctness. For open-textured legal analysis, it provides structural guarantees: every required stage of the legal argument is addressed, argumentation is exercised at the correct stages and not omitted, and the deductive links between steps are valid. We demonstrate the architecture on procedural deadline calculations in German law, Commerce Clause analysis in U.S. constitutional law, and cross-jurisdictional sanction proportionality. We further show that the same architecture has a structural advantage for legal AI training: a deterministic external verifier supplies verifiable outcomes for legal problems and thereby closes the traditional reinforcement-learning loop gap in law.

</details>


### [83] [Catastrophic Compositional Generation: Why Vanilla Diffusion Models Fail to Extrapolate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23920)
*Duncan Soiffer,Chandler Squires,Yuan Guan,Jason Hartford,Pradeep Ravikumar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper argues that vanilla conditional diffusion models are fundamentally incapable of compositional generation when target distributions lie out-of-distribution relative to training sources, and that score estimation error—not merely inference-time approximation error—is the catastrophic bottleneck, challenging recent methods like Feynman-Kac correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compositional generation—combining conditionally trained generative models to sample from novel, geometrically combined target distributions—is a desirable capability for flexible and reusable generative systems. The authors question whether this is even feasible with standard conditional diffusion models, suspecting a fundamental limitation rather than a mere algorithmic shortcoming.

Method: The authors employ theory-guided generalization arguments to conjecture infeasibility. They support this with carefully designed experiments on both synthetic and realistic data, comparing performance under conditions where the target is in-distribution versus out-of-distribution with respect to the source training distributions.

Result: Score estimation error has a catastrophic effect on sample quality when the target distribution is out-of-distribution relative to the sources. While methods like Feynman-Kac correction reduce inference-time approximation error, they do not address the more fundamental score estimation error, which dominates performance degradation in the OOD compositional setting.

Conclusion: Compositional generation with vanilla conditional diffusion models is often infeasible under well-motivated settings, and a fundamentally different approach beyond inference-time correction techniques is needed to address the core problem of score estimation error in out-of-distribution compositional targets.

Abstract: The task of compositional generation involves using a conditional generative model, trained only on a subset of the possible conditions, to produce samples from compositionally-defined target distributions such as a geometric combination of the source distributions. In this work, we argue that this task is often infeasible for vanilla conditional diffusion models: we conjecture that no inference-time technique can efficiently produce samples from the target distribution in certain well-motivated settings. This idea is supported by theory-guided generalization arguments and carefully-designed experiments on both synthetic and realistic data. In particular, while recent methods such as Feynman-Kac correction reduce inference-time approximation error, our results show that score estimation error has a more catastrophic effect on performance when the target distribution is out-of-distribution with respect to the sources, highlighting the need for a different approach to this task.

</details>


### [84] [KLip-PPO: A per-sample KL perspective on PPO-Clip](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23932)
*Riccardo Colletti,Robin Holzinger*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The authors prove that PPO-Clip and PPO-KL are mathematically equivalent: the clipped surrogate's gradient can be exactly reproduced by a KL-penalized objective with per-sample, advantage-dependent coefficients. This identity holds at every minibatch step, and the two losses produce indistinguishable training curves on MuJoCo benchmarks. The reformulation reveals PPO-Clip's implicit per-sample penalty as a step function at the trust-region boundary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The literature treats PPO-Clip (clipped importance ratio) and PPO-KL (KL penalty) as distinct algorithms with separate gradients, hyperparameters, and implementations, spawning a large body of empirical comparisons. The authors aim to unify these two seemingly disparate formulations and expose the hidden structural properties of the clipped surrogate.

Method: The authors analytically derive the gradient of the PPO clipped surrogate objective and show it matches the gradient of a KL-penalized objective where the KL coefficient is a per-sample function of the importance ratio and advantage. This is a mathematical identity (not an approximation) holding at every minibatch step and throughout the inner optimization loop. They validate the equivalence empirically on five MuJoCo continuous-control tasks.

Result: The gradient identity holds exactly. On five MuJoCo benchmarks, PPO-Clip and the equivalent KL formulation produce indistinguishable training curves. The analysis reveals that PPO-Clip's implicit per-sample penalty takes the form of a step function at the trust-region boundary (i.e., at clipping thresholds 1±ε).

Conclusion: PPO-Clip and PPO-KL are not distinct algorithms but two views of the same underlying mechanism. The clipped surrogate's implicit step-function penalty suggests that the shape of the per-sample coefficient function (currently a step) is the natural axis for generalizing PPO, opening follow-up design directions.

Abstract: Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) is the standard policy-gradient algorithm for on-policy reinforcement learning. The literature presents it in two forms, a clipped surrogate that bounds the importance ratio between successive policies and a Kullback-Leibler penalty between them. These forms are treated as separate algorithms with their own gradients, their own hyperparameters, and their own reference implementations, and a sizeable body of empirical work compares them. We show that the gradient of the clipped surrogate is reproduced exactly by a Kullback-Leibler surrogate whose coefficient varies per sample, with closed-form dependence on the importance ratio and the advantage. The identity holds at every minibatch step and across the entire inner loop, and on five MuJoCo continuous-control benchmarks the two losses produce indistinguishable training curves. The reformulation exposes a structural feature of the clipped surrogate that the min notation hides. PPO-Clip's implicit per-sample penalty is a step function at the boundary of the trust region, and the shape of this coefficient is the natural design axis for generalising the algorithm. We sketch the resulting follow-up directions in the discussion.

</details>


### [85] [DREG: A Layer-Wise Jacobian Regularization as a General-Purpose Penalty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23942)
*Rowan Martnishn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A large-scale empirical study (960 experiments) finds that Derivative Regularization (DREG) achieves the highest clean accuracy among six regularizers, ranks second in noise robustness behind Spectral Normalization, performs best under GELU activation and data scarcity, and works effectively as a plug-and-play method with a single fixed hyperparameter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To rigorously isolate and characterize when, where, and why the Derivative Regularization (DREG) penalty works, given the lack of systematic empirical understanding of its behavior across diverse architectures, datasets, and training regimes.

Method: A fully-crossed factorial design with 960 experiments sweeping 4 activations (including GELU), 6 regularizers (DREG, Weight Decay, IGPen, Spectral Normalization, and others), 8 datasets (spanning vision and NLP, clean and noisy/messy benchmarks), and 5 random seeds. DREG is applied with a single fixed hyperparameter λ=10^{-2.5} without per-dataset tuning. Comparisons use Wilcoxon signed-rank tests.

Result: DREG achieves the highest overall clean-regime accuracy, significantly outperforming the unregularized baseline, Weight Decay, and IGPen (p ≤ 0.031). It ranks second in noise robustness behind Spectral Normalization — notably the only two layer-wise regularizers studied. DREG is globally best under GELU activation on messy vision and NLP benchmarks. Its advantage is most pronounced under data scarcity, consistent with its role as a geometric inductive bias.

Conclusion: DREG is a robust plug-and-play regularizer for neural networks with nontrivial Jacobian structure, concentrating regularization pressure on high-derivative layers rather than uniformly constraining the network. It is particularly well-suited to modern transformer settings (GELU activation) and data-scarce regimes.

Abstract: We present a large-scale empirical study isolating the contributions of the Derivative Regularization penalty (DREG). Across a fully-crossed factorial sweep of 960 experiments spanning 4 activations, 6 regularizers, 8 datasets, and 5 random seeds, we ask: when, where, and why does DREG work? Our results establish three principal findings. First, DREG achieves the highest overall and clean-regime accuracy among all regularizers evaluated (significantly so against the unregularized baseline, Weight Decay, and IGPen; Wilcoxon $p \leq 0.031$). It ranks second in noise robustness behind Spectral Normalization (SN) - the only two layer-wise regularizers in the study. Second, DREG is globally the best-performing regularizer under GELU, the default activation in modern transformer architectures, particularly on both messy vision and messy NLP benchmarks, suggesting direct applicability to frontier deep learning settings. Third, DREG's advantage over competing regularizers is most pronounced under data scarcity, consistent with its role as a geometric inductive bias that substitutes for the regularizing effect of data volume. Throughout, DREG is applied with a single fixed hyperparameter $λ= 10^{-2.5}$ and no per-dataset tuning, supporting its characterization as a plug-and-play regularizer for neural networks with nontrivial Jacobian structure.
