These findings are consistent with DREG's design: concentrating regularization pressure on layers where the activation derivative is largest, rather than constraining the network uniformly.

</details>


### [86] [Certification of Machine Learning Models via Directional Sharpness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25004)
*Gefei Tan,Adria Gascon,Sarah Meiklejohn,Mariana Raykova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Directional sharpness is a new, efficiently computable metric that more reliably correlates with generalization than existing proxies like test accuracy or standard sharpness, and it enables model quality certification even under training deviations, including via zero-knowledge proofs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generalization proxies (test accuracy, sharpness) are unreliable when training is perturbed — test accuracy can be misleading, and sharpness is both computationally expensive and fragile to training deviations. A robust, efficiently verifiable metric is needed for trustworthy model certification, especially in auditing scenarios.

Method: The authors propose directional sharpness, which measures loss landscape curvature specifically along directions relevant to the training data (rather than worst-case directions as in standard sharpness). It is computed using the training data available to an auditor, and can be integrated into zero-knowledge proof frameworks to certify model quality without revealing sensitive training data.

Result: Directional sharpness (1) correlates more strongly with generalization than existing metrics, and (2) identifies poorly generalizing models more reliably than existing metrics. Both claims are supported with empirical and analytical evidence.

Conclusion: Directional sharpness provides a practical, robust, and verifiable signal for model generalization, making it well-suited for certification and auditing pipelines where training integrity cannot be assumed.

Abstract: In machine learning, model certification has been identified as an important method for gaining assurance about a model's trustworthiness and quality. A model's quality is largely determined by its ability to generalize, i.e., to perform well on data beyond what it was trained on. It is not possible to certify generalization directly, however, as it depends on unknown data and is not directly measurable. Proxies such as test accuracy can be misleading when the training process is perturbed (intentionally or accidentally), and metrics such as sharpness -- which has an empirically supported link to generalization -- are computationally expensive and can also serve as unreliable signals when training deviates from a prescribed procedure. In this work, we propose directional sharpness, a metric designed to efficiently and reliably indicate generalization despite potential training deviations. We provide empirical and analytical evidence that directional sharpness (1) correlates more strongly with generalization than existing metrics and (2) identifies models with poor generalization more reliably than existing metrics. Furthermore, directional sharpness is efficiently computable in model auditing settings, where the verifier has access to training data, and via zero-knowledge proofs that certify quality without revealing training data.

</details>


### [87] [Learning the Koopman Operator using Attention Free Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23957)
*Mohammed Nagdi,Evangelos-Marios Nikolados,Alexey Yermakov,Mars Gao,Nathan Kutz,Filippo Menolascina*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Koopman autoencoders enable linear latent-space prediction but suffer from long-horizon drift. The authors propose two lightweight remedies: (1) an Attention-Free Transformer (AFT) memory block that corrects latents using a short past window with linear-time complexity, and (2) dynamic re-encoding via online change-point detection that projects drifting predictions back onto the learned manifold. The combined approach outperforms Koopman AE, multi-head attention, GRU, and Transformer baselines on Duffing, Repressilator, and IRMA benchmarks, achieving lower long-horizon error and faster inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Koopman operator learning with autoencoders promises linear prediction in latent space, but long-horizon rollouts progressively drift off the data manifold, causing phase/amplitude errors—especially on systems with switching dynamics, continuous spectra, or strong transients. Existing remedies (multi-head attention, RNNs) add substantial parameters or inference cost without fully solving the problem.

Method: Two components: (1) An Attention-Free Transformer (AFT) latent memory block that aggregates a short window of past latents using linear-time operations (~30k additional parameters, O(3d²+T²)), producing a corrected latent before each Koopman step. (2) Dynamic re-encoding via lightweight online change-point triggers (EWMA, CUSUM, sequential two-sample tests) that detect when predictions have drifted and project them back through the autoencoder decoder-encoder loop onto the manifold. Evaluated against Koopman AE, multi-head attention, GRU, and Transformer autoencoders across three benchmarks with rollouts up to 1000 steps.

Result: Koopman+AFT (with optional re-encoding) consistently reduces error accumulation across all three benchmark systems (Duffing oscillator, Repressilator, IRMA) compared to baseline Koopman autoencoder and matched-capacity multi-head attention. The method also surpasses GRU and Transformer autoencoders in long-horizon predictive accuracy while maintaining lower inference latency. Improvements are demonstrated over horizons up to 1000 steps, with ablation studies confirming the efficacy of different trigger policies.

Conclusion: The proposed Koopman+AFT predictor with dynamic re-encoding provides a fast, compact, and robust approach for long-horizon prediction that stays on the learned manifold. The lightweight AFT memory block effectively captures local temporal context to suppress error divergence without the quadratic cost of attention, while change-point-triggered re-encoding serves as an efficient corrective mechanism against manifold drift.

Abstract: Learning Koopman operators with autoencoders enables linear prediction in a latent space, but long-horizon rollouts often drift off the learned manifold, leading to phase and amplitude errors on systems with switching, continuous spectra, or strong transients. We introduce two complementary components that make Koopman predictors more robust. First, we add an attention-free latent memory (AFT) block that aggregates a short window of past latents to produce a corrected latent before each Koopman update. Unlike multi-head attention, AFT operates in linear time and adds only $\approx$30k parameters ($3d^2 + T^2$, fewer than matched multi-head attention), yet captures the local temporal context needed to suppress error divergence. Second, we propose dynamic re-encoding: lightweight, online change-point triggers (EWMA, CUSUM, and sequential two-sample tests) that detect latent drift and project predictions back onto the autoencoder manifold. Across three benchmark systems -- Duffing oscillator, Repressilator, IRMA -- our model consistently reduces error accumulation compared to a Koopman autoencoder and matched-capacity multi-head attention. We also compare against GRU and Transformer autoencoders, evaluated both from initial conditions and with a 50-step context, and find that Koopman+AFT (with optional re-encoding) attains markedly lower long-horizon error while maintaining lower inference latency. We report improvements over horizons up to 1000 steps, together with ablations over trigger policies. The result is a fast, compact predictor that stays on the learned manifold over long horizons.

</details>


### [88] [Speculative Decoding at Temperature Zero: A Scoped Safety-Invariance Screen with a 48,072-Sample Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25097)
*Sahil Kadadekar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TAIS, a rigorous behavioral-equivalence screen, shows that temperature-zero speculative decoding introduces no detectable safety divergence across extensive benchmarks, with byte-identical or statistically equivalent outputs between target-only and speculative pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speculative decoding raises a critical safety concern: draft model behaviors could leak into verified outputs. The authors aim to rigorously test whether temperature-zero speculative decoding preserves safety-scored outputs compared to target-only inference.

Method: TAIS (Typical-Acceptance Invariance Screen) enforces three criteria: (1) byte-identity evidence, (2) TOST equivalence within ±3 percentage points, and (3) per-task Cohen's h below |h| < 0.1. Applied across 16,783 confirmatory + 44,066 expansion samples under diverse conditions (fp16/bf16, canonical and DPO-adversarial drafts, GPTQ-4bit quantization, two seeds, four safety benchmarks).

Result: No detectable safety divergence found: max |Cohen's h| = 0.024 (~10× below trivial-effect floor); 25/27 TOST contrasts pass at ±3pp margin (two edge cases are ceiling-rate artifacts, not genuine non-equivalence); DPO-adversarial draft yields byte-identical output across 4,006 samples; bf16 alters 36–53% of bytes but zero safety-rate shifts outside equivalence. A 70B production probe (no matched target-only arm, thus not a TAIS pass) shows AdvBench refusal 0.839 [95% CI: 0.809–0.864].

Conclusion: At temperature zero, the tested vLLM speculative decoding stacks exhibit no detectable safety divergence under TAIS. Claims are explicitly limited to these conditions and do not extend to sampling temperatures, untested frameworks/models, or tree-speculation variants.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates inference by letting a draft model propose tokens for a target model to verify, raising a concrete safety question: at temperature zero, can draft-side behavior leak into safety-scored outputs? We answer with Typical-Acceptance Invariance Screen (TAIS), a behavioral-equivalence screen that pairs target-only and speculative outputs on the same safety battery and requires byte-identity evidence, TOST equivalence at +/-3pp, and per-task Cohen's h below a calibrated null cutoff of |h| < 0.1. Applied to a 16,783-sample confirmatory core plus 44,066 matched expansion samples (fp16/bf16 execution, canonical and DPO-adversarial drafts, GPTQ-4bit drafts, two seeds, and four safety benchmarks), the tested temperature-zero vLLM stacks show no detectable safety divergence under TAIS. The largest absolute Cohen's h on matched target-only versus speculative refusal is 0.024, roughly an order of magnitude below the conventional trivial-effect floor; 25 of 27 per-task TOST contrasts pass at the +/-3pp margin (the two non-pass contrasts are capability-domain Wald-CI edge cases at identical ceiling rates, not genuine non-equivalence); the DPO-adversarial draft produces byte-identical output to the canonical draft across 4,006 samples; and bf16 changes 36%-53% of output bytes without moving any per-task safety rate outside equivalence. A separate 4,006-sample 70B production-scale probe, which lacks a matched 70B target-only arm and is therefore not counted as a TAIS pass, produces AdvBench refusal 0.839 over 700 AdvBench completions with 95% Wilson CI [0.809, 0.864]. We make no claim about sampling temperatures, untested frameworks, untested model families, or tree-speculation variants such as EAGLE and Medusa.

</details>


### [89] [Forget Without Compromise: Nexus Sampling for Streaming KV-Cache Eviction Under Fixed Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23961)
*Duc Duong,Hoang Anh Duy Le,Jianwen Xie,Anshumali Shrivastava,Zhaozhuo Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Nexus Sampling introduces a training-free KV cache eviction method that combines iterative "bridge token" scoring with weighted reservoir sampling, replacing irreversible deterministic top-K selection to better preserve subtly important tokens under aggressive cache budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context and agentic LLM workloads exceed fixed KV cache budgets, forcing per-step token eviction. Existing methods rely on a single direct-attention score followed by deterministic top-K selection, which creates an irreversible verdict that permanently discards subtly important tokens that direct attention fails to distinguish from noise.

Method: Nexus Sampling is a training-free eviction method with two components: (1) Nexus scoring — an iterative walk over the direct-attention graph that surfaces "bridge tokens" (tokens important as intermediaries between high-attention tokens), and (2) weighted reservoir sampling — which retains tokens probabilistically with inclusion probability proportional to Nexus scores, replacing deterministic top-K selection.

Result: At 80% KV cache eviction, Nexus Sampling matches dense attention within 1% on LongBench and outperforms top-K baselines on retrieval-heavy tasks, achieving up to 10× smaller per-sequence cache memory.

Conclusion: Nexus Sampling theoretically dominates deterministic top-K in long-run survival probability for subtly important tokens and empirically provides a superior, training-free solution for aggressive KV cache compression in long-context and agentic LLM workloads.

Abstract: Long-context and agentic LLM workloads push the KV cache past any fixed memory budget, forcing the inference stack to permanently evict tokens at every step of a continuous-inference stream. Existing methods all share the same template, a per-step direct-attention score followed by deterministic top-$K$ selection, which converts a single below-cutoff step into an irreversible verdict and permanently erases any subtly important token that direct attention cannot single out from noise. To address this challenge, we propose Nexus Sampling, a training-free eviction method that pairs Nexus scoring, an iterative walk over direct attention that surfaces bridge tokens, with weighted reservoir sampling, which retains tokens with inclusion probability in place of deterministic top-$K$. Theoretically, we show that Nexus Sampling dominates deterministic top-$K$ in long-run survival of subtly important tokens. Empirically, at 80% KV cache eviction, Nexus Sampling matches dense attention within 1% on LongBench while outperforming top-$K$ baselines on retrieval-heavy tasks, with up to 10x smaller per-sequence cache memory.

</details>


### [90] [3D Masked Autoencoders are Robust Learners of Volumetric and Multimodal Cellular Representations for Microscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23964)
*Amirhossein Kardoost,Lion Gleiter,Tingying Peng,Carsten Marr*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper systematically compares 2D and 3D masked autoencoders for self-supervised learning on volumetric fluorescence microscopy data, demonstrating that native 3D modeling (MAE-3D) consistently outperforms 2D projection-based approaches, with further gains from cross-modal alignment with protein language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite cells being inherently three-dimensional, self-supervised learning in fluorescence microscopy predominantly relies on 2D projections, which discard valuable volumetric spatial context. The authors aim to quantify the benefits of native 3D representation learning and multimodal alignment.

Method: The authors compare MAE-2D (max-projection and slice-based variants) against MAE-3D under matched architectures and training protocols. They introduce cross-modal supervision by aligning visual representations with a pretrained protein language model (ESM2), and incorporate channel cross-attention and frequency-domain regularization to leverage 3D spatial context. Evaluation is performed on protein–protein interaction and protein localization downstream tasks.

Result: MAE-3D consistently outperforms all 2D variants. Cross-modal alignment with ESM2 yields larger gains for volumetric models. On protein–protein interaction, MAE-3D achieves ROC-AUC of 0.865 (+0.025 over prior methods). On protein localization, the best 3D model attains state-of-the-art AUC_micro (0.952) and F1_micro (0.742), with absolute improvements of +0.003 and +0.010 respectively.

Conclusion: Native 3D modeling combined with multimodal alignment offers clear advantages for representation learning in single-cell microscopy, outperforming 2D projection-based methods across downstream tasks and establishing new state-of-the-art results for protein localization and protein–protein interaction prediction.

Abstract: Self-supervised learning in fluorescence microscopy often relies on 2D projections, despite the inherently three-dimensional nature of cells. We present a systematic comparison of 2D and 3D masked autoencoders (MAE-2D vs. MAE-3D) on volumetric microscopy data. Under matched architectures and training protocols, MAE-3D consistently outperforms 2D max-projection and slice-based variants on downstream single-cell tasks. We further align visual representations with a pretrained protein language model (ESM2) and show that cross-modal supervision yields larger gains for volumetric models. Channel cross-attention and frequency-domain regularization are critical for leveraging 3D spatial context. On a protein--protein interaction task, MAE-3D achieves a ROC--AUC of 0.865, outperforming prior methods by up to +0.025. For protein localization, our best 3D model attains state-of-the-art AUC$_{\text{micro}}$ (0.952) and F1$_{\text{micro}}$ (0.742), improving over previous approaches by +0.003 and +0.010 absolute, respectively. Overall, these results demonstrate the advantages of native 3D modeling and multimodal alignment for representation learning in single-cell microscopy.

</details>


### [91] [Leaking Circuit Secrets: Gradient Leakage Attacks on Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25589)
*Rupesh Raj Karn,Johann Knechtel,Ozgur Sinanoglu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First comprehensive evaluation of gradient leakage attacks on GNNs used in circuit design and hardware security, analyzing vulnerabilities across GraphSAGE, GCN, GIN, and GAT on standard netlist benchmarks, and assessing defense mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GNNs are increasingly deployed for critical circuit design and hardware security tasks (e.g., Trojan detection, logic locking analysis), yet their vulnerability to gradient leakage attacks—which can expose sensitive design information—has been largely overlooked, creating unaddressed security and privacy risks.

Method: The authors evaluate gradient leakage attacks against four SOTA GNN architectures (GraphSAGE, GCN, GIN, GAT) on three standard netlist benchmarks (ISCAS'85, EPFL, TrustHub). They quantify information leakage (gate types, hardware Trojan properties) and assess architectural susceptibility. They further benchmark five defense techniques: differential privacy, gradient clipping, secure aggregation, quantization-based model compression, and adversarial training, measuring both resilience improvement and performance trade-offs.

Result: GLAs successfully expose sensitive netlist information including gate types and hardware Trojan signatures. GAT's attention mechanisms amplify leakage, while GIN's injective aggregation offers stronger inherent resilience. All evaluated defenses improve resilience only conditionally and often degrade model performance, with no single technique providing robust, generalizable protection.

Conclusion: Privacy-preserving GNN design for hardware security remains an open challenge. Current defenses are context-dependent and entail performance costs, underscoring the need for more robust, efficient, and generalizable privacy mechanisms tailored to this domain.

Abstract: As graph neural networks (GNNs) become standard tools for critical tasks in circuit design and analysis, their security and privacy risks require careful attention. Here, we present the first comprehensive evaluation of gradient leakage attacks (GLAs) on GNNs in circuit-design and hardware-security tasks, a practical threat that has been largely overlooked. We assess state-of-the-art (SOTA) GNNs, including GraphSAGE, GCN, GIN, and GAT, trained on standard netlist benchmarks (ISCAS'85, EPFL, and TrustHub), for their fundamental vulnerability to GLAs. We find that GLAs can expose sensitive information, such as gate types and distinctive properties of hardware Trojans, which may assist adversaries in analyzing logic locking schemes or evading Trojan detection mechanisms. Our analysis shows that these risks are influenced by architectural features, with attention mechanisms (GAT) exacerbating leakage, while injective aggregation (GIN) provides comparatively stronger resilience. We further evaluate several SOTA defense techniques, including differential privacy, gradient clipping, secure aggregation, model compression with quantization, and adversarial training. We find that these techniques improve resilience only in specific settings and can also compromise model performance. Overall, our work provides key insights toward privacy-preserving GNNs and highlights the need for more robust and efficient defenses. We release our full methodology and artifacts.

</details>


### [92] [A Comparative Study of Bayesian Contextual Bandits for Real-Time Warehouse Sorter Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23977)
*Tina Dongxu Li,Mouhacine Benosman,Ken Meszaros,Trevor Dardik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares three hybrid ML frameworks (LR+GDO, XGB+BO, BCB) for real-time sorter diversion control in e-commerce warehouses, finding that the Bayesian Contextual Bandits framework outperforms others with a 2.03% reward uplift over the heuristic baseline, while offering faster inference, online learning capability, and theoretically-grounded optimal control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sorter diversion systems in large-scale warehouses rely on static weight configurations in cost functions that cannot adapt to highly dynamic contexts such as volume mode, congestion level, equipment status, and upstream/downstream dependencies, leading to suboptimal operational efficiency.

Method: The authors conduct a comparative study of three hybrid ML frameworks: Linear Regression with Gradient Descent Optimization, XGBoost with Bayesian Optimization, and Bayesian Contextual Bandits. A high-fidelity physics-aware emulator is used to overcome the cold-start problem and enable safe offline-to-online transition. Evaluation metrics include reward model predictive accuracy, contextual sensitivity, action distribution, and projected reward uplift.

Result: Tree-based reward models (XGB+BO) achieved slightly better predictive power, but the BCB framework delivered the highest overall performance with a 2.03% reward uplift over the heuristic baseline. BCB also demonstrated a decisive time-optimal policy (aligned with Bang-Bang control theory), continuous online learning, exploration-exploitation balance, and significantly shorter inference latency.

Conclusion: The BCB framework shows strong potential for real-time sorter diversion control in large-scale warehouses and warrants further investigation toward operational deployment.

Abstract: Efficient sorter diversion control of automated material handling systems (MHS) is critical for optimizing operational efficiency in large-scale warehouse environments. In this study, we use an inbound receiving sorter at a high-volume e-commerce warehouse as our primary use case, where the sorter diversion system relies on cost functions with static weight configurations that fail to adapt to highly dynamic system contexts, such as volume mode, congestion level, equipment physical status, and upstream/downstream dependencies. To address this real-time sorter diversion optimization challenge, we conducted a comparative study of three candidate hybrid machine learning frameworks: Linear Regression with Gradient Descent Optimization (LR+GDO), XGBoost with Bayesian Optimization (XGB+BO), and Bayesian Contextual Bandits (BCB). Model training and evaluation were enabled by leveraging a high-fidelity physics-aware emulator to overcome the cold-start problem and allow a safe transition from offline to online learning. We performed comprehensive evaluations including reward model predictive accuracy, contextual sensitivity, action distribution, and projected reward uplift. Our results demonstrate that while tree-based reward models offer slightly better predictive power, the BCB framework achieved overall higher performance with 2.03% reward uplift over the heuristic baseline. Furthermore, BCB exhibits several superior characteristics, such as its decisive time-optimal policy backed by Bang-Bang control theory, continuous online learning capability, strategic balance between exploration and exploitation, and significantly shorter inference latency. These results demonstrate the potential of the BCB framework for real-time control optimization in large-scale warehouse environments, motivating further investigation toward operational deployment.

</details>


### [93] [TL++: Accuracy and Privacy Preserving Traversal Learning for Distributed Intelligent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25627)
*Erdenebileg Batbaatar,Young Yoon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TL++ is a two-mode federated/split learning framework that recovers centralized mini-batch gradient behavior via virtual batch construction across nodes. It offers a base mode for communication efficiency and a secure mode using two-server secret sharing of cut-layer activations/gradients. Evaluated on CIFAR-10 and BioGPT/PubMedQA, it achieves near-centralized accuracy while reducing per-step communication up to 13.1×.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distributed training across data silos faces two key challenges: (1) federated learning suffers under heterogeneous data partitions and incurs high communication from full-model exchange; (2) split learning reduces communication but fails to replicate centralized mini-batch gradient dynamics and exposes intermediate activations/gradients in plaintext. There is a need for a method that simultaneously achieves communication efficiency, centralized-equivalent optimization behavior, and privacy protection of intermediate tensors.

Method: TL++ introduces a traversal-learning framework with virtual batch construction across nodes under explicit synchronization, operating in two modes: (a) Base mode exchanges cut-layer activations and gradients instead of full models; (b) Secure mode uses additive secret sharing of cut-layer activations and gradients between an orchestrator and a non-colluding helper, preventing either from observing plaintext tensors. The secure path requires a linear/affine server path for exactness; nonlinear operations demand nonlinear MPC or approximation. The framework is formalized with communication/computation cost analysis and evaluated with full fine-tuning and LoRA.

Result: On CIFAR-10, TL++ base cut 1 achieves 91.41% (SD 0.19) and exact secure cut 3 achieves 90.93% (SD 0.17), exceeding the strongest non-TL++ baseline by over 12 percentage points. Base cut 1 reduces per-step communication 13.1-fold versus full-model synchronization. PubMedQA results similarly favor TL++. Overall, the method approaches centralized-training performance with reduced communication and activation-level secret sharing.

Conclusion: TL++ successfully bridges the gap between communication-efficient split learning and centralized optimization quality, while adding a practical two-server secret-sharing privacy layer for cut-layer tensors. Its limitations include semi-honest threat model scope, visible labels/loss to the orchestrator, and the linearity constraint in the secure path. The framework demonstrates strong empirical performance across vision and biomedical NLP tasks.

Abstract: Distributed intelligent systems increasingly need to train across data silos without centralizing raw data. Federated learning keeps data local but can suffer under heterogeneous partitions and requires repeated full-model exchange. Split learning reduces communication through cut-layer activations, but standard protocols generally do not recover centralized mini-batch gradient behavior and may expose activations and gradients in plaintext. We present TL++, a two-mode traversal-learning framework that constructs virtual batches across nodes to recover centralized mini-batch gradient behavior under explicit synchronization assumptions. Base mode exchanges cut-layer activations and gradients rather than full models. Secure mode secret-shares each cut-layer activation and gradient between an orchestrator and a non-colluding helper, preventing either server from observing plaintext cut-layer tensors. This protection is limited to a semi-honest two-server setting; labels and loss-related outputs remain visible to the orchestrator. In the lightweight secure path evaluated here, exactness requires a linear or affine server path, while nonlinear operations require nonlinear MPC or approximation. We formalize TL++, analyze communication and computation costs, and evaluate it against federated and split-learning baselines on CIFAR-10 and BioGPT/PubMedQA using full fine-tuning and LoRA. On CIFAR-10, TL++ base cut 1 and exact secure cut 3 achieve accuracies of 91.41% (SD 0.19) and 90.93% (SD 0.17), respectively, exceeding the strongest measured non-TL++ baseline by more than 12 percentage points. TL++ base cut 1 also reduces per-step communication by 13.1-fold relative to full-model synchronization. PubMedQA results similarly favor TL++. Overall, TL++ approaches centralized-training performance while reducing communication and providing activation-level secret sharing.

</details>


### [94] [Offline Reinforcement Learning for Warehouse SLAM Throughput Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23978)
*Tina Dongxu Li,Mouhacine Benosman,Rajat Kumar,Kevin Tan,Ken Meszaros,Trevor Dardik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes an offline RL framework for optimizing SLAM throughput control in warehouses, using history-informed states and delayed-impact actions. CQL outperforms baselines with 22.97% system health improvement and 3.18% reduction in throttling duration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SLAM throughput directly affects warehouse congestion and operational efficiency. Existing approaches lack adaptive, data-driven control that balances throughput maximization with downstream stability. Safe online exploration is infeasible in production warehouses, motivating an offline RL solution trained on historical logs.

Method: An algorithm-agnostic offline RL framework featuring: (1) history-informed state representation capturing temporal operational context; (2) action space abstraction for delayed-impact throttling control; (3) a reward function encoding both upstream and downstream metrics. Three offline RL algorithms (including CQL) are instantiated and trained on de-identified historical logs. Evaluation uses model-free methods (immediate reward regression, long-horizon FQE) and model-based Deep Koopman dynamics evaluation.

Result: CQL policy consistently outperforms alternatives, achieving a 22.97% improvement in system health and a 3.18% reduction in average throttling duration compared to baseline policies.

Conclusion: Offline RL is a viable, safe, and scalable approach for warehouse throughput control optimization, with CQL demonstrating the strongest performance. The framework supports plug-and-play integration of different offline RL algorithms.

Abstract: We present an offline reinforcement learning (RL) framework for optimizing SLAM throughput control in a warehouse fulfillment environment. SLAM (Scan/Label/Apply/Manifest) throughput directly influences system congestion and operational efficiency. Our RL-based control approach dynamically recommends SLAM throughput settings that adaptively balance throughput maximization with downstream stability through intelligent adjustment of throttling behavior. We include a history-informed state representation, action space abstraction for delayed-impact control, and a reward function that captures both upstream and downstream operational metrics. Our approach is algorithm-agnostic, enabling integration of multiple offline RL methods under a unified architecture. We instantiate our framework with three state-of-the-art offline RL algorithms, and trained the models offline using de-identified historical operational logs from a large-scale warehouse. Policy performance is evaluated using a comprehensive multi-method strategy. These include model-free approaches including immediate reward estimation via regression models and long-horizon Fitted Q Evaluation (FQE), as well as model-based Deep Koopman dynamics evaluation. Empirical results reveal that the CQL policy consistently outperforms alternatives, improving system health by 22.97% and reducing average throttling duration by 3.18%. These findings demonstrate the potential of offline RL for safe and scalable warehouse throughput control optimization.

</details>


### [95] [Learning to Trigger: Reinforcement Learning at the Large Hadron Collider](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23993)
*Zixin Ding,Shaghayegh Emami,Giovanna Salvi,Cecilia Tosciri,Abhijith Gandrakota,Jennifer Ngadiuba,Nhan Tran,Christian Herwig,David W. Miller,Yuxin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents the first reinforcement learning (RL)-based trigger threshold control system applied to real Large Hadron Collider data. Using Group-Filtered Policy Optimization variants (GFPO-F, GFPO-FR), the agent dynamically adjusts trigger thresholds online to maximize signal efficiency while maintaining background rates within a tolerance band, achieving significant improvements over static hand-tuned menus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trigger menus in high-throughput scientific facilities like the LHC are largely static and hand-tuned, becoming suboptimal as detector conditions, pileup, and background composition drift over time. There is a need for online, adaptive threshold tuning that can respond to changing conditions while meeting strict bandwidth, latency, and storage constraints.

Method: The authors cast online trigger threshold tuning as a sequential decision-making problem using reinforcement learning. They adapt Group-Filtered Policy Optimization (GFPO) to the streaming control setting and introduce two variants: GFPO-F (feasibility-filtered) and GFPO-FR (feasibility-filtered with recovery), which enforce background rate feasibility during training. The agent ingests streaming summaries of recent rates and signal-sensitive features. The approach is evaluated on two trigger types: a total transverse energy (H_T) trigger and an anomaly-detection (AD) trigger based on reconstruction loss, using both Monte Carlo simulation and real CMS collision data (Run 283408).

Result: On Monte Carlo streams, the agent improved the fraction of in-tolerance time intervals by 48% (H_T) and 28% (AD), with up to 2% cumulative gain in signal efficiency on in-tolerance intervals. When transferred zero-shot to real CMS collision data (Run 283408), the agent achieved 56% (H_T) and 28% (AD) in-tolerance improvements over baselines, with further signal-efficiency gains on both triggers.

Conclusion: This work demonstrates the first successful application of RL-based trigger control on real LHC collision data. The GFPO variants provide robust, adaptive threshold tuning that outperforms static hand-tuned menus, showing promise for operational deployment in high-energy physics trigger systems and potentially other streaming scientific instrumentation.

Abstract: High-throughput scientific facilities such as the Large Hadron Collider depend on real-time event filtering (\textit{triggering}) under tight constraints on bandwidth, latency, and storage. In practice, trigger menus are largely static and hand-tuned and can become suboptimal as detector conditions, pileup, and background composition drift over time. We cast online threshold tuning as a sequential decision-making problem: a reinforcement learning agent ingests streaming summaries of recent rates and signal-sensitive features and updates trigger thresholds to maximize signal efficiency while tracking a target background rate within a tolerance band. We adapt Group-Filtered Policy Optimization (GFPO) to streaming control and introduce two variants (GFPO-F, GFPO-FR) that enforce background rate feasibility during training. On a benchmark that emulates realistic collider operation, we study two representative triggers: a total transverse energy ($H_{T}$) trigger sensitive to pileup variation, and an anomaly-detection (AD) trigger based on reconstruction loss for rare or non-standard signatures. On Monte Carlo streams, our agent increases the fraction of in-tolerance time intervals by 48\% ($H_T$) and 28\% (AD), with a cumulative gain of up to 2\% in signal efficiency on those in-tolerance intervals. Transferring from simulation to \emph{real} collision data (CMS Run 283408), the same agent, without fine-tuning, achieves a 56\% ($H_T$) and 28\% (AD) in-tolerance improvement over baselines, with further signal-efficiency gain on both triggers. To our knowledge, this is the \emph{first} demonstration of RL-based trigger control on real Large Hadron Collider collision data. Code is available at https://github.com/Zixind/GFPO\_LHC.

</details>


### [96] [EMAgnet: Parameter-Space EMA Regularization for Policy Gradient Self-Play in Large Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23995)
*Tristan Maidment,JB Lanier,Chase McDonald,Nathan Tsang,Eugene Vinitsky,Roy Fox,Albert Wang,Wesley N. Kerr*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EMAgnet proposes adaptive regularization toward an exponential moving average of the policy network's parameters, rather than a uniform distribution, in PPO self-play for zero-sum imperfect-information games. It outperforms uniform-magnet regularization, especially in games with strictly dominated strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uniform-magnet regularization in self-play PPO treats all actions equally, which is suboptimal when many actions are unviable or strictly dominated. An adaptive regularization target that evolves with policy improvement should yield better performance, particularly in environments with exploration challenges and dominated strategies.

Method: EMAgnet replaces the uniform distribution regularization target in magnetic policy gradient self-play with an exponential moving average (EMA) of the last-iterate policy network's parameters. This creates an adaptive, evolving regularization anchor that tracks the agent's improving strategy rather than pulling toward a fixed uniform prior.

Result: EMAgnet achieves lower exploitability than PPO self-play with uniform-magnet regularization (under both linear and power-law annealing schedules) in the majority of tested two-player zero-sum benchmarks, with consistent gains in games containing many strictly dominated strategies.

Conclusion: Adaptive regularization via parameter-space EMA provides a simple yet effective alternative to uniform-magnet regularization in self-play PPO, particularly beneficial in domains with exploration challenges and dominated actions.

Abstract: Recent work has established that regularized policy gradient methods such as PPO, when used in self-play, can match or exceed specialized game-theoretic algorithms for solving two-player zero-sum imperfect-information games. The uniform distribution has emerged as a strong policy regularization target for this purpose, but it regularizes equally toward all actions regardless of their viability. We introduce EMAgnet, which instead regularizes toward an exponential moving average (EMA) of the last-iterate policy's parameters, providing an adaptive regularization target that evolves with the agent's improving strategy. We evaluate EMAgnet on both standard two-player zero-sum benchmarks and modified benchmarks with exploration challenges and large numbers of strictly dominated strategies. Relative to PPO self-play with uniform-magnet regularization under both linear and power-law annealing schedules, EMAgnet achieves lower exploitability in the majority of tested environments, with consistent performance gains across games containing strictly dominated strategies.

</details>


### [97] [Cyclic Denoising Reveals Ultrastable Memories in Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24000)
*Rishabh Sharma,Stefano Martiniani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cyclic denoising—repeated forward and reverse diffusion at controlled noise levels—is introduced as a novel extraction attack that reveals memorized training images in diffusion models. The method exposes ultrastable attractors (many corresponding to memorized data like stock photos and watermarks) using only sampler-level control, requiring no gradients, prompts, or prior knowledge of training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To probe the generative landscape of diffusion models and uncover memorized training data through a physics-inspired mechanism (random organization in disordered solids), with practical applications in privacy auditing, copyright compliance, and model fingerprinting.

Method: Cyclic denoising: repeatedly applying forward diffusion (noising) and reverse diffusion (denoising) at controlled noise amplitudes. This drives samples toward attractor basins in the learned distribution. The protocol is fully unconditioned, requires only sampler-level control (no gradients, weights, prompts, or captions), and was tested on Stable Diffusion v1.4 (latent-space) and a pixel-space DDPM.

Result: The method reveals attractors with a broad stability spectrum. The deepest attractors are ultrastable—regenerating after near-total corruption and persisting through thousands of cycles. Many correspond to memorized training images (stock photographs, watermarks, web-crawl artifacts). A yielding-like transition is observed: low-amplitude cycling yields trivial fixed points/limit cycles, while larger amplitudes induce rearrangements, basin hopping, and trapping in memorized attractor basins. Additional phenomena include hierarchical partial absorption, prompt-stabilized basins, and cross-initial-condition universality of recovered attractors.

Conclusion: Cyclic denoising serves as both a physics-inspired probe of generative model landscapes and a practical, accessible tool for memorization auditing with significant implications for privacy, copyright compliance, and model fingerprinting.

Abstract: We introduce cyclic denoising -- repeated forward and reverse diffusion at controlled noise amplitudes -- as an extraction attack for image diffusion models. Inspired by random organization in disordered solids, cyclic denoising exposes regions of the learned distribution that are largely inaccessible to standard sampling. The dynamics drive samples toward attractors with a broad stability spectrum. The deepest attractors are ultrastable: they regenerate after near-total corruption and persist through thousands of noising-denoising cycles. Many of these attractors correspond to memorized training images, including stock photographs, brand watermarks, and web-crawl artifacts. The attack requires only sampler-level control, with no gradients, weight inspection, prompts, captions, or prior knowledge of the training data. Unlike generate-and-filter attacks, which rely on large-scale prompted generation and post-hoc similarity or membership-inference filtering, our main protocol is fully unconditioned. We demonstrate the phenomenon in Stable Diffusion v1.4 and in a pixel-space DDPM, showing consistent behavior across latent- and pixel-space diffusion models. Across noise amplitudes, we observe a yielding-like transition: low-amplitude cycling produces trivial absorbing fixed points or limit cycles, while larger amplitudes induce rearrangements, basin hopping, and long-lived trapping in structured memorized attractor basins. We also observe hierarchical partial absorption, prompt-stabilized basins, and cross-initial-condition universality of the recovered attractor set. Our results therefore show that cyclic denoising is both a physics-inspired probe of generative landscapes and a practical tool for memorization auditing, with implications for privacy, copyright compliance, and model fingerprinting.

</details>


### [98] [Fast and Slow Variational Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24007)
*Subarnaduti Paul,Yohan Jung,Mohammad Emtiyaz Khan,Siddharth Swaroop,Thomas Möllenhoff,Martin Mundt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Continual IVON (CoVON), an optimizer that integrates fast-slow adaptation into the VCL framework by merging past posteriors to slow knowledge drift, then using the merged posterior as a prior for fast-weight updates. Implemented in the IVON optimizer at near-Adam cost, CoVON outperforms existing VCL and weight-regularization methods across domain-incremental learning, continual pre-training, and LLM fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard deep learning optimizers lack built-in mechanisms for continual adaptation. Fast-and-slow adaptation, inspired by neuroscience and biology, offers a natural way to balance stability (retaining old knowledge) and plasticity (learning new tasks), yet no consensus exists on how to integrate this into common optimizers.

Method: The authors propose incorporating slow adaptation by merging past posterior distributions to decelerate knowledge drift over time. This merged posterior serves as the prior in a Variational Continual Learning (VCL) update, enabling fast-weight adaptation. The entire mechanism is realized within the IVON optimizer, maintaining computational form and cost nearly identical to Adam. The resulting optimizer is named Continual IVON (CoVON).

Result: CoVON consistently improves over existing VCL-based optimizers and surpasses other weight-regularization strategies. Strong empirical performance is demonstrated across three settings: domain-incremental learning, continual pre-training, and fine-tuning of large language models.

Conclusion: Fast-and-slow adaptation can be effectively and efficiently implemented in a modern optimizer via posterior merging within the VCL framework. CoVON provides a practical, high-performing solution for continual learning with negligible overhead compared to Adam.

Abstract: Continual learning remains a major challenge for modern deep networks, partly because commonly used optimizers lack inherent mechanisms for continual adaptation. One such natural mechanism is fast and slow adaptation to balance stability and plasticity. This mechanism has deep roots in neuroscience and biology, but there is no consensus on how to best incorporate it in commonly used optimizers. Here, we show that this can be easily done via the VCL framework, where past posteriors are used as priors in the future. Our key idea is to incorporate slow adaptation via merging of past posteriors to slow down the drift in the knowledge as learning progresses. The merged posterior is then used as the prior in the VCL update to implement the fast-weight updates. These steps can be seamlessly implemented in the IVON optimizer, whose form and costs are nearly identical to that of Adam. We call this new optimizer the Continual IVON (CoVON) optimizer and show that it not only consistently improves over existing VCL optimizers, but also performs better than other weight-regularization strategies across domain-incremental learning, continual pre-training, and fine-tuning of large language models.

</details>


### [99] [You Don't Need to Run Every Eval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24020)
*Yuchen Zeng,Dimitris Papailiopoulos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A public score matrix of 84 frontier models across 133 benchmarks reveals an approximately rank-2 structure, meaning a model's performance can be summarized by just two latent factors. The authors develop BenchPress, a matrix completion method that recovers held-out scores within 4.6 points, and identify small benchmark subsets (as few as five) that can predict the rest of a model's scorecard with similar accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The frontier model evaluation landscape has become bloated, with releases reporting scores on 40+ benchmarks and many more evaluations run during development. This raises the question: do we truly need to run every evaluation, or is there significant redundancy that can be exploited to reduce computational cost and streamline model assessment?

Method: The authors compile a score matrix of 84 frontier models × 133 benchmarks (2,604 filled cells, 23.3% density). They demonstrate the matrix is approximately rank-2 via two validation approaches: (1) held-out scores are best recovered using exactly two latent factors, and (2) two factors explain over 90% of variance among models on shared benchmarks. They then develop BenchPress, a logit-space rank-2 matrix completion method with a confidence layer that indicates prediction reliability. Using BenchPress, they search for minimal benchmark subsets that can accurately recover the full scorecard, identifying both a high-accuracy five-benchmark set and a cheaper alternative set.

Result: The score matrix is approximately rank-2. BenchPress recovers held-out scores to within 4.6 points on average. A subset of five benchmarks — {GPQA-D, HLE, Codeforces, MMLU-Pro, ARC-AGI-1} — recovers the full public scorecard within 3.93 points. A cheaper set — {GPQA-D, MMLU-Pro, Aider Polyglot, MATH-500, AIME 2026} — achieves 4.55-point recovery.

Conclusion: Model evaluation across diverse benchmarks is highly redundant; two latent factors capture the vast majority of variance. This implies that most benchmarks measure largely the same underlying capability dimensions. BenchPress enables efficient, parsimonious model evaluation, and the authors release the score matrix, code, and an interactive prediction tool to the community.

Abstract: A modern model release reports scores on 40+ benchmarks and the same evaluations were run many more times before it: to track training progress, compare design choices, and select the checkpoint for the release. But do we need to run every eval? We compile a public score matrix of 84 frontier models on 133 benchmarks (2,604 cells, 23.3% filled) and find it is approximately rank-2: a model's scores across all 133 benchmarks are largely determined by just two numbers. We confirm this in two ways: scores hidden from the matrix are best recovered using two factors, and two factors already explain over 90% of the variation among models on the benchmarks they share. Building on this, we design BenchPress: a logit-space rank-2 matrix completion method that recovers held-out scores to within 4.6 points, and a confidence layer that says when each prediction can be trusted. Using BenchPress, we find a subset of five benchmarks {GPQA-D, HLE, Codeforces, MMLU-Pro, ARC-AGI-1} that can recover the rest of a model's public scorecard to within 3.93 points. For a tighter inference budget, a cheaper set {GPQA-D, MMLU-Pro, Aider Polyglot, MATH-500, AIME 2026} can predict a model's evals to within 4.55. We release the score matrix, the BenchPress code, and an interactive tool that predicts any model's score on any benchmark.

</details>


### [100] [Information-Theoretic Classifier-Free Guidance with Adaptive Schedule Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24025)
*Haobo Chen,Xiangxiang Xu,Yuheng Bu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes an information-theoretic framework to optimize classifier-free guidance (CFG) schedules across the reverse diffusion trajectory, addressing the consistency-coverage trade-off by matching the sampler's induced distribution to a clean endpoint reference, achieving improved trade-offs on ImageNet-512 and COCO benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CFG improves condition consistency but stronger guidance reduces diversity and distributional coverage. The consistency-coverage trade-off varies across noise levels in the reverse trajectory, yet existing approaches use fixed guidance weights. The distribution induced by CFG is not simply a fixed-time tilted distribution, making it unclear how to optimally allocate guidance strength across the trajectory.

Method: The authors propose an information-theoretic optimization framework that: (1) uses a clean endpoint reference distribution to specify the desired consistency-coverage trade-off; (2) optimizes the actual distribution induced by the guided sampler toward this reference; and (3) derives trajectory-level formulas to estimate the optimization objective from samples and score evaluations, circumventing explicit density estimation.

Result: On ImageNet-512 with EDM-XXL and COCO with SD-XL, the learned schedules achieve competitive or improved consistency-coverage trade-offs over constant guidance baselines. The optimized schedules allocate guidance strength selectively across noise levels rather than uniformly.

Conclusion: Optimizing CFG schedules across the reverse trajectory, rather than using constant guidance, yields better consistency-coverage trade-offs. The information-theoretic framework provides a principled way to learn these schedules without requiring explicit density estimation.

Abstract: Diffusion models have achieved strong performance in image, text-to-image, and video generation, where conditional generation is often controlled by classifier-free guidance (CFG). CFG improves condition consistency by increasing a guidance weight, but stronger guidance typically reduces diversity and distributional coverage. It remains unclear how this consistency-coverage trade-off should be controlled across the reverse trajectory, since the distribution induced by CFG is not simply the fixed-time tilted distribution given by the guided score field. To address this issue, we propose an information-theoretic framework for CFG schedule optimization. Our approach uses a clean endpoint reference to specify the desired consistency-coverage trade-off, while optimizing the actual distribution induced by the guided sampler toward this reference. We derive trajectory-level formulas to estimate the objective from samples and score evaluations, avoiding explicit density estimation. On ImageNet-512 with EDM-XXL and COCO with SD-XL, the learned schedules achieve competitive or improved trade-offs over constant guidance and allocate guidance selectively across noise levels.

</details>


### [101] [RoPE-Aware Bit Allocation for KV-Cache Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24033)
*Fengfeng Liang,Yuechen Zhang,Jiaya Jia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Block-GTQ introduces RoPE-aware block-wise bit allocation for KV-cache quantization, assigning more bits to high-energy frequency blocks under RoPE. It significantly outperforms uniform quantization, achieving near-fp16 quality at 3-bit keys, enabling 3.24x compression with faster inference on long contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KV-cache quantizers treat keys as flat vectors, ignoring that under RoPE, a key's contribution to attention logits decomposes position-dependently into 2D frequency blocks. High-energy RoPE blocks are more quantization-sensitive, motivating block-wise bit allocation rather than uniform quantization.

Method: Block-GTQ builds on TurboQuant-MSE (TQ-MSE). For each layer and KV head, it computes a label-free energy score per RoPE block, then greedily allocates integer bit widths by marginal gain under a total bit budget. A packed-cache serving path is implemented for deployment efficiency.

Result: Block-GTQ reduces per-layer MAE by 32-80% vs. uniform TQ-MSE at 2-3 b/dim K-only quantization, winning all 367/367 layer comparisons. At K2V2 on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, NIAH 6-task average rises from 70.6→97.4; LongBench-EN from 36.87→53.31. On AIME 2024/2025 with DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B at K3V2, scores 51.7/37.5 vs. fp16's 54.2/37.9, while uniform collapses to 0.0/0.0. Packed K3V3 achieves 3.24x compression, 1.34x faster than fp16 FlashAttention2 at 128K, peak memory reduction from 56.31GB→19.85GB, and runs at 256K/512K where fp16 OOMs.

Conclusion: RoPE-aware block-wise bit allocation is critical for low-bit KV-cache quantization. Block-GTQ enables near-lossless compression at 3-bit, making long-context inference feasible on memory-constrained GPUs with minimal quality degradation.

Abstract: Existing low-bit KV-cache quantizers often treat each cached key as a flat vector. Under RoPE, however, a key's contribution to a future attention logit decomposes into a position-dependent sum over two-dimensional frequency blocks. This makes key-cache quantization a block-wise bit-allocation problem: high-energy RoPE blocks are more sensitive to quantization error and should receive more bits. We introduce Block-GTQ, a RoPE-aware bit allocator for key-cache quantization built on TurboQuant-MSE(TQ-MSE). For each layer and KV head, Block-GTQ computes a label-free energy score for each RoPE block and greedily allocates integer bit widths by marginal gain. Under matched K/V bit budgets, Block-GTQ better preserves RoPE query-key logits on a ten-model diagnostic panel, cutting per-layer MAE by 32-80% at 2 and 3 b/dim K-only quantization and winning all 367/367 layer comparisons against uniform TQ-MSE. These fidelity gains translate to stronger downstream long-context retrieval, understanding, and reasoning. At K2V2 on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Block-GTQ raises the six-task NIAH average from 70.6 to 97.4, and the LongBench-EN average from 36.87 to 53.31. On AIME 2024/2025 with DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, without an fp16 recent-key buffer, Block-GTQ at K3V2 scores 51.7/37.5, close to fp16's 54.2/37.9, whereas uniform TQ-MSE collapses to 0.0/0.0. We further implement a packed-cache serving path. On a single H800 GPU with Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, packed K3V3 achieves 3.24x KV-cache compression with fp16-comparable quality, runs 1.34x faster than fp16 FlashAttention2 at 128K context, reduces peak memory from 56.31 GB to 19.85 GB, and remains feasible at 256K and 512K where fp16 OOMs. Code is available at https://github.com/JIA-Lab-research/blockgtq.

</details>


### [102] [Rapid FinFET Modelling Using an Autoencoder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24046)
*Amit Sarkar Suman Sau,Swagata Mandal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine learning framework using an autoencoder is proposed for efficient FinFET modeling, where I-V curves are compressed into a low-dimensional latent space. By incorporating VDS as an input feature, the model captures bias-dependent variations and directly extracts key device metrics (VTH, SS, gm) with high accuracy using minimal training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional compact models like BSIM-CMG require complex parameter extraction and calibration. The authors aim to develop a data-driven alternative that can rapidly model FinFET behavior, capture bias-dependent variations, and extract critical device metrics directly from measured or simulated I-V data with minimal training overhead.

Method: A BSIM-CMG model is first calibrated to generate a synthetic dataset of ID-VG characteristics. An autoencoder is then trained on these I-V curves, compressing them into a low-dimensional latent space that encodes device physics. A key architectural choice is the explicit inclusion of VDS as an input feature, enabling the model to capture bias-dependent variation. The decoder reconstructs full I-V curves, and critical metrics (VTH, SS, peak gm) are directly extracted from the model.

Result: The trained autoencoder successfully reconstructs full I-V curves and directly extracts threshold voltage (VTH), subthreshold slope (SS), and peak transconductance (gm). The approach achieves high accuracy while requiring only minimal training data, demonstrating effectiveness as a compact modeling tool.

Conclusion: Data-driven compact models built from actual characterization data using autoencoders can serve as powerful tools for rapid device characterization, modeling, and circuit-level simulation, offering a viable alternative to traditional physics-based compact models.

Abstract: This work presents a machine learning framework that leverages an autoencoder (AE) for the efficient modeling of FinFET. We first calibrated a BSIM-CMG model to generate a dataset of current-voltage (ID-VG) characteristics. This data was used to train an autoencoder that compresses full I-V curves into a low-dimensional latent space, which intrinsically encodes key device physics. A key innovation is the explicit incorporation of parameter such as drain to source voltage (VDS) as an input feature, enhancing the model ability to capture bias dependent variation. The trained model successfully reconstructs full I-V curves and directly extracts critical device metrics including threshold voltage (VTH), subthreshold slope (SS), and peak transconductance (gm). This approach demonstrates that data driven compact models, built from actual characterization data, can achieve high accuracy with minimal training data, providing a powerful tool for rapid device characterization, modelling and circuit level simulation.

</details>


### [103] [RAVEN: A Regime-Aware Variable-context Expert Network for Financial Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24062)
*Cheng He,Zhenyu Guan,Xijie Liang,Defu Lian,Jiajia Li,Enhong Chen,Patrick P. C. Lee,Geng Hu,Zehao Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RAVEN is a Mixture-of-Experts framework that adaptively selects temporal context windows for non-stationary financial time series, using Cumulative Importance Thresholding (CIT) for nested window construction, a Global Compressed Representation (GCR) branch for coherence, and Correlation-Aware Weighting (CAW) for expert output fusion, achieving SOTA on multiple financial and traffic benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial time series suffer from non-stationarity, extremely low SNR, and regime-dependent temporal dependencies. Existing SOTA time series models use fixed context windows, which are fundamentally mismatched to the time-varying optimal look-back horizon required by non-stationary price processes. This structural mismatch limits predictive performance in financial settings.

Method: RAVEN proposes three key innovations: (1) Cumulative Importance Thresholding (CIT) — patches are scored by learned importance in reverse chronological order, and cumulative importance is thresholded to derive nested contiguous prefix windows; (2) a scale-specialized Mixture-of-Experts where each nested window is routed to an expert specializing in that temporal scale; (3) a Global Compressed Representation (GCR) branch operating over the full context in parallel to preserve global temporal coherence; and (4) Correlation-Aware Weighting (CAW), which aligns variable-length expert outputs via interpolation and penalizes pairwise cosine similarity among expert outputs before aggregation to mitigate redundancy from overlapping inputs.

Result: RAVEN achieves SOTA performance across multiple domains: improves Pearson correlation by 9.2% on HS300 and 20.2% on S&P500 for cumulative log-return prediction; reduces MSE by 18.2% on fund sales forecasting; and achieves the best results in 14 out of 16 metrics across four PEMS traffic benchmarks.

Conclusion: Adaptive temporal context determination via learned importance scoring and nested expert routing effectively addresses the fixed-window limitation of existing time series models in non-stationary financial settings. The combination of CIT, GCR, and CAW provides a robust framework that generalizes beyond finance to other time series domains like traffic forecasting.

Abstract: Financial time series forecasting presents structural challenges absent from standard benchmarks. Log-returns are non-stationary, exhibit exceptionally low signal-to-noise (SNR) ratios, and are governed by regime-dependent temporal dependencies. We identify a key limitation of state-of-the-art (SOTA) time series models in financial settings. A fixed context window is mismatched to the time-varying optimal look-back of non-stationary price processes. We propose the Regime-Aware Variable-context Expert Network (RAVEN), a Mixture-of-Experts framework designed to adaptively determine the temporal context for each input sample. Instead of relying on a fixed look-back horizon, RAVEN constructs a hierarchy of nested contiguous windows whose lengths are determined by the data itself. Specifically, RAVEN scores patches by learned importance in reverse chronological order and applies the Cumulative Importance Thresholding (CIT) mechanism to derive nested prefix windows, each routed to a scale-specialized expert. A Global Compressed Representation (GCR) branch runs in parallel over the full context, preserving global temporal coherence that local experts cannot guarantee. Because the nested routing induces structured overlap among expert inputs, we introduce a Correlation-Aware Weighting (CAW) to align variable-length expert outputs and penalize pairwise cosine similarity prior to aggregation. Experiments on cumulative log-return prediction (HS300, S&P500) and fund sales forecasting demonstrate that RAVEN achieves SOTA performances, improves Pearson correlation by 9.2% on HS300 and 20.2% on S&P500, and reduces MSE by 18.2% on fund sales forecasting, while achieving the best results in 14 of 16 metrics on four PEMS traffic benchmarks.

</details>


### [104] [Blockwise Policy-Drift Gating for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24084)
*Liwen Zheng,Haiyun Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight, student-only blockwise gating mechanism that controls for policy drift between old (behavior) and current student policies improves the robustness of on-policy distillation with rollout reuse on math reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampled-token on-policy distillation (OPD) is fragile on long-horizon reasoning tasks. While local teacher-support matching helps, there remains an unaddressed problem: when rollouts are reused, the behavior policy that generated them drifts from the current student policy, degrading training signal quality. The authors seek a lightweight control signal to mitigate this drift without altering teacher targets or the rollout policy.

Method: The authors propose blockwise policy-drift gating: (1) compute log-probability shifts between the behavior student (policy that generated the rollout) and the current student at each token; (2) aggregate these shifts over fixed-size blocks (e.g., 64 tokens); (3) apply detached, mean-normalized gates to reweight OPD position losses. Crucially, this method does not modify teacher targets, teacher top-K supports, or the rollout policy. Experiments use a uniform 200-step training budget across all variants, evaluated with pass@8 on a Qwen3 math reasoning benchmark suite.

Result: Fixed 64-token block gating improves sampled-token OPD mean pass@8 from 0.4978 to 0.5160 across AIME24, AIME25, MATH500, and AMC23. On Teacher-TopK/LSM, Block64 achieves the best four-benchmark mean pass@8 among all trained student variants.

Conclusion: Local old-current policy drift is a practical and effective control signal for reused OPD rollouts. Block-level gating is recommended as a simple default for improving solve-rate robustness in math reasoning distillation.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student policy using teacher signals computed on trajectories sampled by the student itself. Recent work shows that sampled-token OPD can be fragile on long-horizon reasoning tasks and that local teacher-support matching is a simple and effective repair. This paper introduces blockwise policy-drift gating, a lightweight student-only old-current drift controller for OPD under rollout reuse. The method computes log-probability shifts between the behavior student and the current student on the sampled token path, aggregates these shifts over fixed blocks or spans, and uses the resulting detached, mean-normalized gates to reweight OPD position losses. It does not change teacher targets, teacher top-K supports, or the rollout policy. In a six-variant Qwen3 math reasoning benchmark with a uniform 200-step training budget for all trained variants, we use pass@8 as the primary problem-level solve-rate metric. Fixed 64-token block gating improves sampled-token OPD mean pass@8 from 0.4978 to 0.5160 across AIME24, AIME25, MATH500, and AMC23. On Teacher-TopK/LSM, Block64 gives the best four-benchmark mean pass@8 among trained students. The results identify local old-current policy drift as a practical control signal for reused OPD rollouts and motivate block-level gating as a simple default for improving solve-rate robustness.

</details>


### [105] [NeuroSonic: Conditional Flow Matching for EEG-to-Speech Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24087)
*Wenhao Gao,Yifan Wang,Yijia Ma,Carl Yang,Wen Li,Chenyu You*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NeuroSonic introduces a conditional flow-matching framework for EEG-to-speech reconstruction that uses a deterministic probability-flow ODE instead of stochastic sampling, achieving up to 26.3% improvement in perceptual quality over GAN, diffusion, and mean-flow baselines on CineBrain and EAV benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG signals are weak, spatially diffuse, and highly variable, while speech has strong harmonic and temporal structure. This mismatch renders direct waveform regression unstable and makes stochastic multi-step generation sensitive to artifacts and subject variability. A deterministic transport-based formulation is proposed to overcome these limitations.

Method: NeuroSonic employs a conditional flow-matching framework where EEG and audio are embedded into a shared token space and processed by a time-conditioned gated Transformer. The model learns a deterministic probability-flow velocity field that transports noise-corrupted acoustic states to clean speech via an ordinary differential equation (ODE), avoiding iterative stochastic sampling. Evaluation uses cross-subject protocols on CineBrain and EAV benchmarks.

Result: NeuroSonic outperforms GAN-, diffusion-, and mean-flow baselines in distributional realism, spectral fidelity, and perceptual quality, achieving up to a 26.3% gain in overall perceptual quality. The performance advantage is most pronounced in artifact-heavy segments where conditioning variability is greatest.

Conclusion: Deterministic conditional transport via flow matching provides a stable and effective formulation for EEG-driven speech reconstruction, particularly robust under high conditioning variability, suggesting a promising direction for neural speech decoding from non-invasive brain recordings.

Abstract: Reconstructing continuous speech from scalp electroencephalography (EEG) remains fundamentally challenging. EEG provides a weak, spatially diffuse, and highly variable measurement of distributed cortical activity, whereas speech is organized as a coherent acoustic trajectory with strong harmonic and temporal structure. The resulting mismatch makes waveform regression unstable and causes stochastic multi-step generation to be sensitive to artifact-dependent conditioning and subject variability. We introduce NeuroSonic, a conditional flow-matching framework for EEG-to-speech reconstruction. Instead of predicting waveforms directly or refining them through stochastic denoising, NeuroSonic learns a deterministic probability-flow velocity field that transports a noise-corrupted acoustic state toward clean speech under EEG conditioning. EEG and audio are embedded into a shared token space and processed by a time-conditioned gated Transformer that parameterizes the transport ordinary differential equation. This formulation models trajectory evolution explicitly while avoiding iterative stochastic sampling. We evaluate NeuroSonic on the CineBrain and EAV benchmarks under cross-subject evaluation. Across both datasets, the proposed method improves distributional realism, spectral fidelity, and perceptual quality over representative GAN-, diffusion-, and mean-flow baselines, with up to a 26.3\% gain in overall perceptual quality. The performance gap is most evident in artifact-heavy segments, where conditioning variability is strongest. These findings indicate that deterministic conditional transport provides a stable and effective formulation for EEG-driven speech reconstruction. Code is available at https://github.com/Y-Research-SBU/NeuroSonic/ .

</details>


### [106] [FedUP: One-Shot Federated Unlearning via Centroid-Guided Plug-in Filters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24113)
*Feihong Nan,Zhengyi Zhong,Pan Wang,Weidong Bao,Xiongtao Zhang,Quan Wen,Ji Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedUP proposes a one-shot federated unlearning framework using server-side pluggable filters trained on DP-protected class centroids, reducing unlearning latency from minutes to seconds while preserving non-target knowledge and enabling reversibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current federated unlearning methods face a dilemma between non-target knowledge loss (catastrophic forgetting of retained data) and high request latency (multi-round communication and retraining). There is a need for efficient, precise unlearning that complies with "right to be forgotten" mandates without degrading the model on non-target tasks.

Method: FedUP freezes the original model parameters and introduces lightweight pluggable filters trained only at the server side. These filters act as a "knowledge funnel" to screen out target data. Training uses differentially private (DP)-protected class centroid samples, eliminating multi-round client-server communication. Unlearning is achieved in one shot, and the pluggable architecture allows easy reversal by removing filters.

Result: Extensive experiments on image and text tasks show FedUP effectively reduces non-target knowledge loss while achieving superior unlearning precision and efficiency. Unlearning latency is reduced from minutes to seconds compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: FedUP resolves the core dilemma in federated unlearning by decoupling forgetting from retraining. Its server-side, one-shot, DP-protected filter approach delivers fast, precise, and reversible unlearning, making it suitable for real-world decentralized ML systems subject to data deletion requests.

Abstract: Federated unlearning (FU) is critical for complying with legal mandates like the right to be forgotten in decentralized systems, yet current methods face a persistent dilemma between non-target knowledge loss and high request latency. To resolve these issues, we propose FedUP, a one-shot federated unlearning framework utilizing lightweight pluggable filters that act as a "knowledge funnel" to screen out target data while preserving original model performance. By freezing original model parameters and training filters at the server side using differentially private (DP)-protected class centroid samples, FedUP bypasses the need for multi-round client-server communication and complex retraining, reducing unlearning latency from minutes to mere seconds. Additionally, the framework's pluggable architecture ensures inherent reversibility, enabling the seamless restoration of forgotten knowledge by simply removing the filters. Extensive experiments on diverse image and text tasks demonstrate that FedUP effectively reduces non-target knowledge loss and achieves superior unlearning precision and efficiency across various scenarios. Code is available at: https://github.com/suows/FedUP-code.

</details>


### [107] [When Top-1 Fails: Calibrating LoRA Monitors for Masked Diffusion LMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24119)
*Lucky Verma,Pratik Yadav*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Top-1 argmax concentration fails as a PEFT collapse detector for discrete diffusion LMs (zero precision over 816 runs), despite firing universally. The cause is pre-equilibrium saturation: top-1 concentration is already high before optimization begins. Max LoRA gradient norm instead achieves 0.68 precision and 0.79 F1 for identifying poor final-loss configurations on held-out LLaDA-family data. Recommendation: replace top-1 monitoring with early-training max-gradient logging and family-specific threshold calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diagnosing training collapse in discrete diffusion language models (DLMs) under parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT/LoRA) is critical but lacks validated early-warning signals. Existing denoising-time confidence metrics (top-1 argmax concentration) provide cheap diagnostics but have never been empirically tested for their predictive value in PEFT regimes.

Method: The authors systematically test two candidate collapse-warning signals across 816 LoRA/PEFT configurations from three DLM families (including LLaDA): (1) top-1 argmax concentration (denoising-side) and (2) max LoRA gradient norm (parameter-side). They evaluate precision, recall, and F1 for predicting final-loss degradation at a 200-step horizon. A train-optimized threshold is calibrated on a pooled held-out LLaDA-family split. Autoregressive controls and cross-family tests are used to bound generalizability.

Result: Top-1 argmax concentration fires for all 816 configurations while 0/816 actual collapses occur, yielding zero precision — explained by pre-equilibrium saturation where top-1 is already near-ceiling before fine-tuning. Max LoRA gradient norm, with a calibrated threshold, identifies top-decile final-loss configurations at precision 0.68 and F1=0.79 on held-out LLaDA data, surpassing the all-positive baseline even at the lower bootstrap confidence bound. Cross-family threshold failures and autoregressive controls demonstrate the method is specific to short-horizon DLM-LoRA inspection, not a universal collapse detector.

Conclusion: Top-1 argmax concentration should be abandoned as a PEFT collapse alarm. Practitioners should instead log max LoRA gradient norm early in training and calibrate detection thresholds per DLM family. The proposed workflow enables routing of unstable runs for inspection before resources are wasted.

Abstract: Discrete diffusion language model (DLM) fine-tuning inherits inexpensive diagnostics from denoising-time confidence monitors, but their PEFT-training meaning is untested. We test top-1 argmax concentration as a collapse warning. Across 816 LoRA/PEFT configurations from three DLM families, the warning fires for every configuration while logs record 0/816 actual collapses at the 200 step horizon, giving zero precision. The cause is pre-equilibrium saturation: top-1 concentration is already high before optimization and quickly becomes insensitive to final training stability. We then evaluate max LoRA gradient norm, a parameter-side signal that samples gradient routing rather than token concentration. On a pooled held-out LLaDA-family split, a train-optimized threshold identifies top-decile final-loss configurations with precision 0.68 and F1=0.79, above the all-positive top-1 baseline even at the lower split-bootstrap confidence bound.
