Autoregressive controls and cross-family threshold failures bound the result to short-horizon DLM-LoRA inspection rather than a universal collapse detector. Workflow: drop top-1 as a PEFT alarm, log max-gradient early in training, and calibrate thresholds per DLM family before routing runs for inspection.

</details>


### [108] [Holistic Data Scheduler for LLM Pre-training via Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24133)
*Chenhao Dang,Jing Ma,Mingjie Liao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HDS frames online data mixing for LLM pre-training as a reinforcement learning problem using Soft Actor-Critic, with a holistic multi-objective reward combining data quality, inter-domain loss, and weight norms. It achieves the same perplexity as the next best method with 44% fewer iterations and yields a 7.2% MMLU improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing online data mixing methods rely on a single optimization perspective, failing to capture the multi-dimensional nature of dynamic data composition needed for effective LLM pre-training.

Method: HDS formulates data scheduling as a continuous-control RL problem solved via Soft Actor-Critic (SAC). Its novel holistic reward function integrates three components: (1) a data-driven reward for quality, (2) a loss-driven reward capturing inter-domain influence, and (3) a model-driven reward based on weight norms.

Result: On The Pile benchmark, HDS matches the final validation perplexity of the next best method while using 44% fewer training iterations. It achieves a 7.2% improvement on MMLU 0-shot and consistent gains across other benchmarks.

Conclusion: Multi-dimensional, holistic data scheduling via RL significantly improves both LLM pre-training efficiency and downstream capability, outperforming single-perspective online mixing approaches.

Abstract: The composition of training data, governed by the diversity of sources and their mixing strategy, is a cornerstone of Large Language Model (LLM) pre-training. Online Data Mixing (ODM), the technique of adaptively adjusting data mixtures during training, has emerged as a promising direction to improve efficiency. However, existing methods are constrained by their reliance on a singular optimization perspective, which fundamentally overlooks the need for complex LLM pre-training to consider the dynamic data composition from multiple dimensions. To overcome this limitation, we introduce the Holistic Data Scheduler (HDS), a novel online data mixing framework. HDS formulates the data scheduling challenge as a reinforcement learning problem in a continuous control space and leverages the Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) algorithm for its stability and sample efficiency in exploring the high-dimensional policy space. At the core of HDS lies a novel multi-objective, holistic reward function that integrates three critical perspectives: a data-driven reward for quality, a loss-driven reward capturing inter-domain influence, and a model-driven reward based on weight norms. To validate our design and determine its optimal configuration, we conducted systematic experiments on LLMs of various sizes. On The Pile benchmark, HDS reaches the final validation perplexity of the next best method with 44% fewer training iterations. Furthermore, it achieves a 7.2% improvement on the MMLU 0-shot task along with consistent gains on other benchmarks, showcasing its ability to enhance both training efficiency and final model capability.

</details>


### [109] [A Time-Reparameterized Cumulative Intensity Extrapolation Sampler for Discrete Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24140)
*Feiyang Fu,Hehe Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes TR-CIE, a novel sampler for discrete flow matching that improves sample quality under limited function evaluations by combining time reparameterization with cumulative intensity extrapolation, requiring only one NFE per step.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard sampling for discrete flow matching (e.g., τ-leaping) suffers from degraded quality when the number of function evaluations (NFE) is limited. Efficient low-NFE sampling remains understudied in the DFM context, motivating the need for improved samplers.

Method: TR-CIE has two components: (1) Schedule-based time reparameterization that rescales the time grid according to the noise schedule, absorbing schedule-dependent growth and mitigating stiffness near the terminal stage. (2) Cumulative-intensity extrapolation that reuses cached model outputs from the previous step as a history term to improve approximation of stepwise cumulative intensities on the non-uniform time grid. The method uses one NFE per step, matching standard τ-leaping.

Result: The authors provide theoretical analysis bounding local approximation error of cumulative intensities and establishing convergence results. Extensive experiments on synthetic tasks, text generation, and text-to-image benchmarks demonstrate improved sampling quality under limited NFE compared to baselines.

Conclusion: TR-CIE effectively improves discrete flow matching sampling quality in low-NFE regimes without additional computational cost per step, validated across diverse generation tasks.

Abstract: Discrete flow matching (DFM) provides a principled framework for generative modeling on discrete state spaces via continuous-time Markov chain dynamics. In practice, sampling for DFM commonly employs discretizations such as $τ$-leaping, yet efficient sampling methods under a limited number of function evaluations (NFE) remain less studied. To address this gap, we propose the Time-Reparameterized Cumulative Intensity Extrapolation (TR-CIE) sampler, which aims to improve sampling quality when function evaluations are restricted. TR-CIE consists of two components. First, a schedule-based time reparameterization rescales the time grid according to the noise schedule. Under standard factorized DFM rate parameterizations, this transformation of variables absorbs the schedule-dependent growth term and mitigates stiffness near the terminal sampling stage. Second, we introduce a cumulative-intensity extrapolation updating rule. By reusing cached model outputs from the previous step as a history term, this improves the approximation of stepwise cumulative intensities on the resulting non-uniform time grid. We provide a theoretical analysis that bounds the local approximation error of cumulative intensities and establishes convergence results. The resulting sampler requires one NFE per step and introduces no additional model evaluations compared to the standard $τ$-leaping sampler. Extensive experiments on synthetic tasks, text generation, and text-to-image benchmarks demonstrate that our method improves sampling quality under limited NFE.

</details>


### [110] [AsyncOPD: How Stale Can On-Policy Distillation Be?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24143)
*Wonjun Kang,Kevin Galim,Seunghyuk Oh,Minjun Kang,Sanghyun Park,Donghoon Kim,Minjae Lee,Minseo Kim,Rishabh Tiwari,Yuchen Zeng,Hyung Il Koo,Kangwook Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents the first systematic study of staleness in asynchronous on-policy distillation (OPD), analyzing how different KL divergence directions affect robustness, proposing OPD-specific stabilization methods, and introducing AsyncOPD — an asynchronous training pipeline achieving 1.6×–3.8× throughput gains with comparable accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OPD faces an on-policy systems bottleneck where rollout generation dominates training time for reasoning workloads. Asynchronous training can alleviate this but introduces stale-policy data, whose effects in OPD remain unexplored. Additionally, storing full teacher logits is prohibitively expensive, requiring finite teacher-score caches that introduce further complications.

Method: The authors systematically study staleness in asynchronous OPD under three angles: (1) comparing forward KL (teacher-weighted) vs. reverse KL (student-weighted) robustness to stale rollouts; (2) evaluating whether asynchronous RL stabilization techniques (e.g., importance correction) transfer to OPD, and proposing a simpler alternative — recomputing reverse-KL at learner time; (3) analyzing bias-variance tradeoffs of finite teacher-score caches for sparse/sampled estimators, motivating multi-sample Monte Carlo. These insights are integrated into AsyncOPD, a fully asynchronous OPD pipeline.

Result: Forward KL proves more robust to stale rollouts than reverse KL. RL stabilization methods do not improve over simply recomputing reverse-KL under the current student. Multi-sample MC reduces one-sample variance while preserving correctability. AsyncOPD achieves 1.6× to 3.8× training throughput improvement over strict synchronous training with comparable accuracy.

Conclusion: KL direction fundamentally changes the stale-data problem in asynchronous OPD; OPD-specific solutions outperform borrowed RL techniques; and AsyncOPD demonstrates that asynchronous OPD can significantly accelerate LLM post-training without sacrificing accuracy.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own rollouts guided by teacher feedback and is becoming increasingly important for large language model (LLM) post-training. Like reinforcement learning (RL), however, OPD faces an on-policy systems bottleneck, as rollouts can dominate training time for reasoning workloads. Asynchronous training pipelines can alleviate this bottleneck by decoupling rollout generation from learner updates, but doing so introduces stale-policy data. While prior work has studied stale data in asynchronous RL, its effects in OPD remain underexplored. We present the first systematic study of staleness in asynchronous OPD, focusing on a practical setting where teacher feedback is implemented through local KL losses and full-vocabulary teacher logits are too expensive to store or transfer, necessitating finite teacher-score caches. We first show that KL direction changes the stale-data problem: teacher-weighted forward KL is more robust to stale rollouts, whereas student-weighted reverse KL is vulnerable. Second, for this vulnerable reverse-KL case, we study whether methods designed to stabilize asynchronous RL can mitigate OPD staleness. In our experiments, they do not improve over a simpler OPD-specific surrogate: recomputing the reverse-KL signal under the current student at learner time. Third, we analyze how finite teacher-score caches create a bias-variance tradeoff for sparse and sampled reverse-KL OPD estimators. This motivates multi-sample Monte Carlo (MC), which preserves MC correctability while reducing one-sample variance. Finally, we present and open-source AsyncOPD, a fully asynchronous OPD training pipeline built from these estimator choices. Experiments show that AsyncOPD improves training throughput by $1.6\times$ to $3.8\times$ over strict synchronous training while reaching comparable accuracy.

</details>


### [111] [Lightweight Transformer Models for On-Device Fault Detection: A Benchmark Study on Resource-Constrained Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24173)
*Disha Patel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A benchmark comparing traditional ML and lightweight transformers for on-device fault detection finds that transformers match traditional methods on well-separated data but with massive size/latency overhead; INT8 quantization and a two-stage adaptive pipeline mitigate these costs, while severe class imbalance degrades all methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying fault detection models on resource-constrained edge hardware requires balancing accuracy, latency, and model size. The paper aims to systematically assess whether lightweight transformer architectures offer practical advantages over traditional ML methods (Random Forest, XGBoost, SVM, Logistic Regression) in this on-device context.

Method: The authors benchmark traditional ML methods (RF, XGBoost, SVM, Logistic Regression) against four lightweight transformers (DistilBERT, TinyBERT-6L, TinyBERT-4L, MobileBERT) for binary fault detection across three public datasets: NASA C-MAPSS, SECOM, and UCI AI4I 2020. Metrics include F1-score, AUC, model size, and CPU inference latency. They additionally evaluate INT8 dynamic quantization and a two-stage adaptive inference pipeline where a quantized triage model routes 97.9% of predictions, with only 2.1% escalated to a larger expert model.

Result: On C-MAPSS, lightweight transformers reach 87.8% F1, matching traditional ML, but at ~100x model size and ~9000x latency. TinyBERT-4L is the most deployment-friendly transformer (55 MB, 18 ms CPU latency). INT8 quantization reduces model size by 25% while retaining 86.9% F1. The adaptive pipeline achieves 87.6% F1 at 19.5 ms average latency. On severely imbalanced datasets (SECOM, UCI-PM), all methods struggle significantly.

Conclusion: Lightweight transformers are computationally impractical for simple on-device fault detection where traditional ML suffices. Quantization and adaptive routing partially close the efficiency gap, but extreme class imbalance remains a fundamental, unresolved challenge across all model classes.

Abstract: On-device fault detection enables real-time diagnostics without cloud dependency, but deploying machine learning models on resource-constrained hardware demands careful tradeoffs between accuracy, latency, and model size. We present a benchmark comparing traditional ML methods (Random Forest, XGBoost, SVM, Logistic Regression) against lightweight transformer architectures (DistilBERT, TinyBERT-6L, TinyBERT-4L, MobileBERT) for binary fault detection across three public datasets: NASA C-MAPSS turbofan degradation, SECOM semiconductor manufacturing, and UCI AI4I 2020 predictive maintenance. We evaluate classification performance (F1-score, AUC), model size, and CPU inference latency, and further assess INT8 dynamic quantization and a two-stage adaptive inference pipeline. Our results reveal that on well-separated sensor data (C-MAPSS), lightweight transformers match traditional ML at 87.8% F1 but at 100x the model size and 9000x the latency. TinyBERT-4L emerges as the most deployment-friendly transformer at 55 MB and 18 ms CPU latency. INT8 quantization reduces size by 25% while preserving 86.9% F1. Our adaptive pipeline, routing 97.9% of predictions through a quantized triage model and only 2.1% to a larger expert, achieves 87.6% F1 at 19.5 ms average latency. On severely imbalanced datasets (SECOM, UCI-PM), both traditional and transformer methods struggle significantly, highlighting fundamental limitations of current approaches for extreme class imbalance in fault detection. All code is publicly available.

</details>


### [112] [Project Ariadne: Prompt-Conditioned Route Generation for Synthesis Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24184)
*Anton Morgunov,Victor S. Batista*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Ariadne is a decoder-only model that unifies retrosynthetic route generation into a single prompt-completion framework, where the target molecule, optional constraints (route depth, required starting materials), and the multistep route are represented in one sequence. It supports prompt-conditioned planning without needing separate models for different specifications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical retrosynthetic planners rely on iterative single-step model applications with specialized algorithms for constrained variants, while existing direct-generation methods require training separate models for different planning constraints. The authors aim to unify these into a single, flexible prompt-conditioned framework.

Method: Ariadne is a decoder-only transformer that takes a prompt containing the target molecule and optional constraints (e.g., maximum route depth, required starting materials) and autoregressively generates the full retrosynthetic route as a completion. The model is trained on the RetroCast/PaRoutes benchmark family. Prompt fields condition generation behavior without architectural changes.

Result: On the mkt-cnv-160 benchmark, adding depth constraints improves Solv-0 by 13.7 points; adding required-leaf constraints improves it by 31.2 points. Ariadne surpasses DESP (a bidirectional search planner) on required-leaf Top-10 and Solv-0 in 24 GPU-minutes versus 6.8 GPU-hours. On standard reconstruction, it matches DMS Explorer XL at roughly half the inference time. On route-holdout reconstruction, Ariadne shows clearest gains, though AiZynthFinder MCTS remains stronger on several Solv-0 comparisons.

Conclusion: Ariadne demonstrates that sequence generation can be extended from specialist retrosynthesis models to prompt-conditioned structural route generation. However, the absence of Tier-1–3 route checkers remains the primary bottleneck for practical utility to experimental chemists. The codebase and training scripts are released publicly.

Abstract: Retrosynthetic planning seeks to connect a target molecule to commercially available starting materials through a multistep route. Classical planners construct such routes by iteratively applying single-step reaction models within a search procedure; constrained variants often require specialized algorithms or architectural changes. Direct route generation reframes retrosynthesis as sequence generation, but existing direct-generation methods still train separate models for different planning specifications. We introduce Ariadne, a decoder-only route generator that represents the target, optional constraints, and route in one prompt-completion sequence. On the RetroCast/PaRoutes mkt-cnv-160 benchmark family, one 24-layer checkpoint follows route-depth and required-starting-material prompts: adding the corresponding prompt fields raises Solv-0 by 13.7 points for depth constraints and 31.2 points for required-leaf constraints. Ariadne also improves over DESP, a bidirectional search planner, on required-leaf Top-10 and Solv-0 in 24 GPU-minutes versus 6.8 GPU-hours. On standard reconstruction, Ariadne is comparable to DMS Explorer XL at about half the reported inference time. Across additional target-only benchmarks, Ariadne's clearest gains are on route-holdout reconstruction, whereas AiZynthFinder MCTS remains stronger on several Solv-0 comparisons. These results extend sequence generation from specialist retrosynthesis models to prompt-conditioned structural route generation. We release the codebase and training scripts to support further work, but do not introduce Tier-1--3 route checkers; those remain the main bottleneck before models of this kind can become useful to experimental chemists.

</details>


### [113] [Managing Task Execution for Unknown Workloads in Batteryless IoT: A Hardware-Agnostic Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24340)
*Samer Nasser,Henrique Duarte Moura,Ritesh Kumar Singh,Maarten Weyn,Jeroen Famaey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes two hardware-agnostic dynamic scheduling strategies for batteryless IoT devices — a model-free Reinforcement Learning (RL) agent and an Approximated Prediction (AP) method — that treat applications as black boxes requiring no prior energy profiling. Evaluated against adaptive task rate (AsTAR) and static thresholds using real solar data and LoRa workloads, the analysis reveals distinct trade-offs: AP achieves near-oracle throughput, RL enables tunable survival-execution balance, and AsTAR excels at pacing across extended energy gaps. The study also finds that simpler static policies suffice for devices with larger energy buffers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Batteryless, energy-harvesting IoT systems face highly volatile stored energy, and as edge applications grow more complex, traditional energy-aware schedulers fail because they rely on static execution thresholds or pre-measured, hardware-specific task profiles. There is a need for intelligent, hardware-agnostic scheduling that adapts to unpredictable workloads without requiring prior energy characterization of tasks.

Method: The authors propose two hardware-agnostic dynamic scheduling strategies: (1) a model-free Reinforcement Learning (RL) agent and (2) an on-the-fly Approximated Prediction (AP) method. Both treat applications as black boxes with no prior energy information. These are evaluated against an adaptive task rate approach (AsTAR) and optimized static thresholds. Evaluation uses a custom-built, physically accurate simulation framework driven by real-world solar irradiance data and dynamic LoRa transmission profiles.

Result: The analysis exposes distinct operational trade-offs rather than universal superiority: the AP method delivers lightweight, near-oracle task throughput; the RL agent provides tunable balancing between device survival and task execution; AsTAR excels at execution pacing across long energy gaps. Additionally, devices with larger energy buffers can efficiently rely on simpler, computationally cheaper static policies, while advanced strategies are critical for severely constrained systems with small capacitors.

Conclusion: No single scheduling strategy is universally optimal for batteryless IoT. The choice depends on hardware constraints and application requirements: advanced dynamic methods (RL, AP) are essential for small-capacitor, severely energy-constrained devices, whereas simpler static policies remain effective for systems with larger energy buffers. The RL agent's tunability and AP's near-oracle lightweight performance offer complementary benefits for different deployment scenarios.

Abstract: In recent years, the Internet of Things (IoT) paradigm has been shifting toward batteryless, energy-harvesting architectures. Sustaining reliable operation in these systems requires intelligent management of highly volatile stored energy. As edge applications grow in complexity, traditional energy-aware schedulers struggle with unpredictable workloads due to their reliance on static execution thresholds or pre-measured, hardware-specific task profiles. To overcome this, we propose two novel, hardware-agnostic dynamic scheduling strategies treating applications as a "black box," requiring no prior energy information: a model-free Reinforcement Learning (RL) agent and an on-the-fly Approximated Prediction (AP) method. We evaluate these methods against an adaptive task rate approach (AsTAR) and optimized static thresholds using a custom-built, physically accurate simulation framework driven by real-world solar data and dynamic LoRa transmission profiles. Rather than claiming universal superiority, our analysis exposes the distinct operational trade-offs of each method: the AP approach delivers lightweight, near-oracle task throughput; the RL agent provides tunable survival-execution balancing; and AsTAR excels at execution pacing across long energy gaps. Finally, we demonstrate that while these advanced strategies provide critical resilience for severely constrained systems with small capacitors, devices with larger energy buffers can efficiently rely on simpler, less computationally expensive static policies.

</details>


### [114] [Parallel Manifold Steering: Efficient Adaptation of Large Associative Memories via Residual Energy Shaping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24396)
*Kanishk Awadhiya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: H-Res (Hierarchical Residual Steering) is a parameter-efficient adaptation method that modulates Transformer behavior by learning a state-dependent vector field on the activation manifold, steering token trajectories into task-specific attractor basins without altering network weights or expanding sequence length. It preserves attention entropy, facilitates neural collapse, and outperforms LoRA and prompt-based methods on associative retrieval tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting frozen large Transformer models (which function as Dense Associative Memories) to new tasks presents a "Plasticity-Stability" dilemma: weight modification methods like LoRA risk catastrophic interference by altering synaptic weights, while prompt-based methods like VPT degrade associative capacity by consuming the retrieval buffer with static tokens. A method is needed that adapts without disrupting the model's global equilibrium or sequence capacity.

Method: H-Res formulates adaptation as a control problem on the activation manifold (inspired by Neural ODEs). It learns a state-dependent vector field that hierarchically steers token representations into task-specific basins of attraction within the Transformer's energy landscape. The mechanism modulates the effective energy landscape without altering the foundation model's global equilibrium or expanding sequence length. The authors formally prove that H-Res preserves the attention entropy of the base model and facilitates Neural Collapse.

Result: Manifold Steering outperforms global weight modification (e.g., LoRA) by 26% on associative retrieval tasks. It eliminates the computational overhead associated with prompt-based methods and scales effectively to structured domains (validated on VTAB benchmarks).

Conclusion: H-Res offers a theoretically grounded solution to the Plasticity-Stability dilemma by operating as a control mechanism on the activation manifold rather than modifying weights or injecting tokens. It enables effective task adaptation while preserving the foundation model's associative memory properties, with strong empirical gains over existing PEFT methods.

Abstract: Large Transformer models function as Dense Associative Memories (DAMs), retrieving knowledge via high-dimensional attractor dynamics driven by the self-attention mechanism \citep{ramsauer2020hopfield, wu2024attention}. However, adapting these frozen memory systems to new tasks presents a fundamental ``Plasticity-Stability'' dilemma. Current methods either risk catastrophic interference by modifying synaptic weights directly (e.g., LoRA) \citep{hu2021lora} or degrade associative capacity by clogging the retrieval buffer with static prompt tokens (e.g., VPT) \citep{jia2022vpt}. In this work, we propose \textbf{H-Res} (Hierarchical Residual Steering), a mechanism that modulates the effective energy landscape of the Transformer without altering its global equilibrium or expanding its sequence length. By formulating adaptation as a control problem on the activation manifold \citep{chen2018neuralode}, H-Res learns a state-dependent vector field that steers token trajectories into task-specific basins of attraction. We formally prove that H-Res preserves the attention entropy of the foundation model and facilitates Neural Collapse \citep{papyan2020prevalence}. Empirically, Manifold Steering outperforms global weight modification by 26\% on associative retrieval tasks and eliminates the computational overhead of prompt-based methods, scaling effectively to structured domains \citep{zha2023vtab}.

</details>


### [115] [Natural Identifiers for Privacy and Data Audits in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24408)
*Lorenzo Rossi,Bartłomiej Marek,Franziska Boenisch,Adam Dziedzic*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes Natural Identifiers (NIDs) — structured random strings like cryptographic hashes and shortened URLs that naturally appear in LLM training data — to enable post-hoc differential privacy auditing and dataset inference without requiring retraining or private held-out datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing differential privacy auditing requires inserting canary data during training (impractical for already-trained models), and dataset inference requires access to a private non-member held-out dataset from the same IID distribution as suspect data (often unavailable). These constraints make scalable post-hoc audits infeasible.

Method: The authors introduce Natural Identifiers (NIDs), which are structured random strings (e.g., cryptographic hashes, shortened URLs) that occur naturally in common LLM training corpora. Because NIDs follow a predictable format, unlimited additional random strings from the same distribution can be generated to serve as: (1) alternative canaries for post-hoc differential privacy auditing without retraining, and (2) same-distribution held-out data for dataset inference without requiring a private non-member dataset.

Result: Experimental evaluation demonstrates that NIDs successfully enable post-hoc differential privacy auditing without retraining and facilitate dataset inference for any suspect dataset containing NIDs, eliminating the need for private non-member held-out datasets.

Conclusion: NIDs provide a practical, scalable solution for post-hoc privacy auditing of LLMs, overcoming the two major barriers of retraining requirements and held-out dataset unavailability.

Abstract: Assessing the privacy of large language models (LLMs) presents significant challenges. In particular, most existing methods for auditing differential privacy require the insertion of specially crafted canary data during training, making them impractical for auditing already-trained models without costly retraining. Additionally, dataset inference, which audits whether a suspect dataset was used to train a model, is infeasible without access to a private non-member held-out dataset. Yet, such held-out datasets are often unavailable or difficult to construct for real-world cases since they have to be from the same distribution (IID) as the suspect data. These limitations severely hinder the ability to conduct scalable, post-hoc audits. To enable such audits, this work introduces natural identifiers (NIDs) as a novel solution to the above-mentioned challenges. NIDs are structured random strings, such as cryptographic hashes and shortened URLs, naturally occurring in common LLM training datasets. Their format enables the generation of unlimited additional random strings from the same distribution, which can act as alternative canaries for audits and as same-distribution held-out data for dataset inference. Our evaluation highlights that indeed, using NIDs, we can facilitate post-hoc differential privacy auditing without any retraining and enable dataset inference for any suspect dataset containing NIDs without the need for a private non-member held-out dataset.

</details>


### [116] [Data Augmentation: A Fourier Analysis Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24418)
*Behrooz Tahmasebi,Melanie Weber,Stefanie Jegelka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether partial data augmentation (using a random subset of group elements) can match the statistical benefits of full augmentation. Using Fourier analysis and finite group representation theory, the authors prove that partial augmentation achieves the same minimax rates as full augmentation up to a vanishing approximation error, while also proving that exact invariance requires averaging over the entire group.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full data augmentation over large symmetry groups is computationally infeasible. The key question is whether partial augmentation can retain the same generalization and sample complexity benefits as full augmentation, addressing the tension between statistical optimality and computational scalability in learning with symmetries.

Method: The authors develop a general framework based on Fourier analysis on finite groups and representation theory. They analyze a broad class of classical learning problems, studying partial data augmentation where group elements are randomly subsampled, and derive minimax convergence rates comparing partial vs. full augmentation.

Result: Partial data augmentation with a randomly sampled subset achieves the same minimax rates as full augmentation, with approximation error vanishing as subset size grows. Conversely, they prove an impossibility result: exact invariance requires averaging over the entire group; no strict subset suffices when the hypothesis space is sufficiently expressive.

Conclusion: The results provide a unified perspective: partial augmentation can achieve statistical optimality without full computational cost (approximate symmetry suffices for generalization), but exact invariance strictly requires the full group. This explains the practical success of subsampled augmentation strategies.

Abstract: Data augmentation is a simple and model-agnostic approach for exploiting known invariances in learning problems. Given a group acting on the input space, one augments the training set with transformed copies of each sample. Because it exploits symmetries without modifying the underlying learning algorithm, data augmentation can be applied broadly across learning methods. However, this universality comes at a computational cost: when the group is large, full group-sized augmentation quickly becomes computationally infeasible. This raises a fundamental question: Can partial data augmentation achieve the same statistical benefits as full augmentation in terms of generalization and sample complexity? We develop a general framework for investigating this question using Fourier analysis and the representation theory of finite groups. We show that, for a broad class of classical learning problems, partial data augmentation based on a randomly sampled subset of group elements achieves the same minimax rates as full augmentation, up to an approximation error that vanishes as the subset size increases. Our results provide a theoretical explanation for why partial augmentation can retain the statistical benefits of full augmentation despite enforcing symmetry only approximately, and shed light on a recently raised question in learning with symmetries: whether statistically optimal learning under general group invariances can be achieved using computationally scalable methods. Moreover, we prove a complementary impossibility result: enforcing exact invariance via data augmentation requires averaging over the entire group, and cannot be achieved by any strict subset when the hypothesis space is sufficiently expressive. Together, these results provide a unified perspective on full and partial data augmentation, as well as exact and approximate symmetry enforcement.

</details>


### [117] [An LLM-based Two-Stage Transformer Framework for Cross-Domain Bearing Fault Diagnosis with Limited Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24459)
*Jinghan Wang,Feng Cheng,Wentao Wu,Hang Li,Gaoliang Peng,Tianchen Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A knowledge-guided two-stage transfer learning framework using a lightweight GPT-2-style Transformer is proposed for bearing fault diagnosis under simultaneous challenges of dataset heterogeneity, varying operating conditions, and limited labeled data. It achieves 92.61% accuracy with only 10% labeled target data, outperforming SOTA by 17.24 percentage points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing bearing fault diagnosis methods address dataset heterogeneity, operating condition variations, and limited labeled data in isolation, relying on implicit feature alignment. No prior work tackles all three challenges concurrently, limiting real-world industrial applicability where these issues co-occur.

Method: A knowledge-guided two-stage transfer learning framework: (1) Multi-source pre-training using a lightweight GPT-2-style Transformer with causal self-attention for hierarchical feature extraction from vibration signals, where pre-trained encoder weights and fault prototype embeddings serve as explicit knowledge carriers; (2) Target adaptation via prototype-based knowledge modulation and taxonomy-adaptive classification to handle heterogeneous fault category mismatches across domains.

Result: Experimental validation on four real-world bearing datasets yields 92.61% average accuracy using only 10% labeled target data, surpassing state-of-the-art methods by 17.24 percentage points.

Conclusion: The proposed framework establishes a practical pathway toward cost-effective predictive maintenance in Industry 4.0 by effectively addressing the concurrent dual-shift challenge (domain shift + taxonomy shift) through explicit knowledge transfer mechanisms.

Abstract: Bearing fault diagnosis faces critical challenges when dataset heterogeneity, operating condition variations, and limited labeled data occur simultaneously in industrial environments. Existing approaches address these issues in isolation and rely on implicit feature alignment, limiting effectiveness under concurrent challenges. This paper proposes a knowledge-guided two-stage transfer learning framework that employs a lightweight GPT-2-style Transformer with causal self-attention for hierarchical feature extraction from vibration signals, establishing explicit pathways where pre-trained encoder weights and fault prototype embeddings serve as knowledge carriers from multi-source pre-training to target adaptation. The framework addresses the dual-shift challenge through multi-source learning for generalizable representations, prototype-based knowledge modulation for target adaptation, and taxonomy-adaptive classification for seamless transfer across heterogeneous fault categories. Experimental validation on four real-world datasets demonstrates 92.61% average accuracy with only 10% labeled target data, outperforming state-of-the-art methods by 17.24 percentage points, establishing a practical pathway toward cost-effective predictive maintenance in Industry 4.0 applications.

</details>


### [118] [A Fair Evaluation of Graph Foundation Models for Node Property Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24509)
*Oleg Platonov,Gleb Bazhenov,Dmitry Eremeev,Liudmila Prokhorenkova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A reevaluation of 9 recent Graph Foundation Models for node property prediction reveals that only the most recent ones (Prior-data Fitted Networks-based) outperform well-tuned GNN baselines, and at higher inference cost. The field lacks a unified evaluation protocol, impeding reliable comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) for node property prediction are proliferating but are evaluated under widely divergent settings, preventing reliable comparison. The authors aim to establish a fair, rigorous, and unified reevaluation to determine whether GFMs genuinely outperform strong GNN baselines.

Method: The authors conduct a fair and rigorous reevaluation of 9 recent GFMs for node property prediction, benchmarking them against strong, well-tuned Graph Neural Network (GNN) baselines across a unified evaluation setting.

Result: Among the 9 GFMs evaluated, only the most recent models based on the Prior-data Fitted Networks (PFNs) paradigm outperform well-tuned GNNs in terms of predictive performance. However, this performance gain comes at a higher inference cost.

Conclusion: The field of GFMs for node property prediction suffers from a lack of standardized evaluation. Most current GFMs do not surpass well-tuned GNNs; only PFN-based GFMs show superior predictive performance, with the trade-off of increased inference cost. Standardized benchmarks are urgently needed.

Abstract: Due to the wide use of graph-structured data in different fields of industry and science, the development of Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) has recently attracted a lot of attention. While many different types of models are called GFMs, particular interest has been paid to GFMs designed for node property prediction tasks, which is one of the most popular settings in Graph ML with lots of real-world applications from fraud detection in financial and social networks to recommendation systems for e-commerce and user-generated content platforms. While a number of GFMs for this task have been recently proposed, the field has not converged to a unified evaluation setting, and different works evaluate their models in widely different ways, preventing reliable comparison of GFMs with each other and with other types of models. In this work, we conduct a fair and rigorous reevaluation of 9 recent GFMs for node property prediction, comparing them to strong Graph Neural Network (GNN) baselines. We find that, among these GFMs, only the most recent ones based on the Prior-data Fitted Networks paradigm outperform well-tuned GNNs in predictive performance, although at a higher inference cost.

</details>


### [119] [Reasoning as Attractor Dynamics: Latent Memory Retrieval via Gibbs-Weighted Energy Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24543)
*Kanishk Awadhiya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper reinterprets LLMs as Dense Associative Memories, proposing that correct mathematical reasoning corresponds to flat attractor basins in the energy landscape while hallucinations are sharp local minima. The authors introduce a Gibbs-measure-based retrieval mechanism that samples multiple reasoning paths weighted by inverse energy, improving Phi-3.5 on GSM8K by 5.38%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional autoregressive decoding (greedy next-token prediction) fails to capture the global structure of reasoning. The authors posit that LLMs are better understood through the lens of collective computation and energy-based models, where inference should be a dynamic settling process into deep attractor basins rather than sequential token sampling.

Method: The authors model LLM reasoning as a Dense Associative Memory with an energy landscape. They introduce a retrieval mechanism based on the Gibbs measure, sampling multiple reasoning trajectories and computing their spectral entropy. Paths are weighted by inverse energy (P ∝ e^(-βE)), approximating the equilibrium distribution of the associative memory. This effectively "relaxes" the system into robust attractor basins (flat minima) while penalizing sharp, unstable local minima associated with hallucinations.

Result: The proposed physics-inspired mechanism improves Microsoft Phi-3.5 performance on the GSM8K benchmark from 84.7% to 90.1%, a gain of 5.38 percentage points.

Conclusion: Inference in LLMs is better modeled as a dynamic settling process into attractor basins rather than as greedy next-token prediction. The energy-landscape perspective provides both a theoretical framework for understanding reasoning and a practical mechanism for improving performance via trajectory re-weighting.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are traditionally viewed as autoregressive generators. However, from the perspective of collective computation, they function as high-dimensional Dense Associative Memories that store complex reasoning patterns as latent attractors. In this work, we investigate the energy landscape of mathematical reasoning. We posit that correct reasoning chains correspond to deep, wide attractor basins ("flat minima") in the model's output distribution, whereas hallucinations manifest as sharp, unstable local minima. To exploit this geometry, we introduce a retrieval mechanism based on a Gibbs measure of the trajectory's spectral entropy. By sampling multiple reasoning paths and weighting them by their inverse energy ($P \propto e^{-βE}$), we approximate the equilibrium distribution of the associative memory, effectively ``relaxing'' the system into a robust solution. Empirically, this physics-inspired mechanism improves Microsoft Phi-3.5 performance on GSM8K by 5.38\% (84.7\% $\to$ 90.1\%), demonstrating that inference is better modeled as a dynamic settling process into an attractor basin rather than greedy next-token prediction.

</details>


### [120] [QC-SMOTE: Quality-Controlled SMOTE for Imbalanced Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24625)
*Parth Upman,Shreyank N Gowda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QC-SMOTE is a quality-controlled oversampling framework that generates synthetic minority samples guided by a composite neighbourhood trustworthiness score, with adaptive generation behaviour across overlap–imbalance regimes, and falls back to duplication when quality thresholds are not met. It achieves state-of-the-art AUC-ROC and Macro F1 on 30 imbalanced datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing oversampling methods like SMOTE frequently produce low-quality synthetic samples in regions affected by noise or class overlap, degrading classifier performance. A quality-aware, geometry-adaptive approach is needed to ensure synthetic samples are both reliable and informative.

Method: QC-SMOTE computes a composite neighbourhood trustworthiness score per minority sample using local density, safe-level, and isolation from the majority class. Synthetic candidates are generated via an IPQ (interpolation purity quotient)-guided best-of-K strategy, evaluating midpoint purity and majority clearance. Generation behaviour adapts across overlap–imbalance regimes by adjusting interpolation range and selection criteria. A fallback mechanism replaces low-quality synthetic samples with original minority duplicates when neighbourhood purity drops below an adaptive threshold.

Result: On 30 imbalanced datasets under repeated stratified cross-validation, QC-SMOTE achieved the highest average AUC-ROC and Macro F1 among compared oversampling methods, with particularly pronounced gains under moderate and severe imbalance conditions.

Conclusion: Quality-aware, geometry-adaptive synthetic sampling is crucial for robust imbalanced classification. QC-SMOTE demonstrates that combining reliability scoring, adaptive interpolation, and graceful degradation via duplication yields significant performance improvements over existing methods.

Abstract: Class imbalance poses a significant challenge in classification, where existing methods such as SMOTE often generate low-quality synthetic samples in regions with noise or class overlap. We propose QC-SMOTE, a quality-controlled oversampling framework that estimates minority sample reliability using a composite neighbourhood trustworthiness score combining local density, safe-level, and isolation from the majority class. Synthetic candidates are generated using an IPQ-guided best-of-K strategy that evaluates midpoint purity and, when required, majority clearance, with allocation guided by sample reliability and boundary informativeness. Generation behaviour adapts across overlap--imbalance regimes, adjusting interpolation range and selection criteria to match local data geometry. Low-quality synthetic samples are replaced with original minority duplicates when neighbourhood purity falls below an adaptive threshold, providing graceful degradation by reverting to duplication in severely noisy regions. Experiments on 30 imbalanced datasets using repeated stratified cross-validation show that QC-SMOTE achieves the strongest average AUC-ROC and Macro F1 among the compared oversampling methods, with particularly clear gains under moderate and severe imbalance. These results demonstrate the importance of quality-aware, geometry-adaptive synthetic sampling for robust imbalanced classification.

</details>


### [121] [FlowPipe: LLM-Enhanced Conditional Generative Flow Networks for Data Preparation Pipeline Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24679)
*Kunyu Ni,Lei Cao,Jie He,Xiaotong Zhang,Jianfeng Jin,Junyu Dong,Yanwei Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlowPipe formulates data preparation pipeline synthesis as conditional probabilistic flow generation over a DAG using C-GFlowNets with Trajectory Balance, augmented by FiLM-based semantic modulation and failure-aware objectives, achieving 11.96% accuracy gains and 12.5x faster convergence over SOTA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated data preparation pipeline construction is combinatorially hard with expensive end-to-end evaluation. Existing Multi-DQN methods suffer from: (1) decoupled value estimators undermining long-horizon credit assignment, (2) weak injection of dataset context into the policy, and (3) inefficient exploration in a sparse search space riddled with invalid states.

Method: FlowPipe unifies pipeline synthesis as conditional probabilistic flow generation over a directed acyclic graph using Conditional Generative Flow Networks (C-GFlowNets) with a Trajectory Balance objective to link terminal rewards with early decisions. It introduces Deep Semantic Modulation via Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM), where LLM-derived logical priors condition the policy's internal activations per dataset semantics. A failure-awareness mechanism is embedded in the flow objective to penalize invalid states and focus search on promising regions.

Result: On two benchmark suites spanning 74 real-world datasets, FlowPipe outperforms SOTA baselines with an average accuracy improvement of 11.96% and achieves 12.5x faster training convergence.

Conclusion: FlowPipe demonstrates that GFlowNet-based generative modeling with semantic conditioning and failure awareness effectively addresses the credit assignment, context injection, and exploration challenges in automated data preparation pipeline synthesis, yielding substantial performance and efficiency gains.

Abstract: Data preparation pipelines improve data quality in machine learning by transforming raw tables into learning-ready data through sequential cleaning and feature transformation operators. However, automatically constructing such pipelines is computationally difficult because operator sequences are combinatorial and end-to-end evaluation is expensive. Existing state-of-the-art (SOTA) Multi-DQN methods still face three key limitations: decoupled value estimators weaken long-horizon credit assignment, dataset context is only weakly injected into the policy, and exploration is inefficient in a sparse search space with many invalid states. To address these issues, we propose FlowPipe, a unified framework that formulates pipeline synthesis as conditional probabilistic flow generation over a directed acyclic graph. FlowPipe uses Conditional Generative Flow Networks (C-GFlowNets) with a Trajectory Balance objective to connect terminal validation rewards with early pipeline decisions. It further introduces Deep Semantic Modulation through Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM), allowing LLM-derived logical priors to condition the policy's internal activations according to dataset semantics. In addition, FlowPipe incorporates failure awareness into the flow objective to avoid invalid states and concentrate search on high-potential regions. Experiments on two benchmark suites with 74 real-world datasets show that FlowPipe outperforms SOTA baselines, improving accuracy by 11.96% on average and achieving 12.5x faster training convergence. Source code is available at https://github.com/KunyuNi/FlowPipe.

</details>


### [122] [Grad Detect: Gradient-Based Hallucination Detection in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24790)
*Anand Kamat,Daniel Blake,Brent M. Werness*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Grad Detect uses gradient patterns from a single forward-backward pass during inference to detect LLM hallucinations, outperforming confidence-based and sampling-based baselines, with 97% of discriminative signal concentrated in the final five layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are prone to hallucinations, and existing detection methods relying on output-level signals (e.g., confidence scores, sampling consistency) miss internal structural information. There is a need for a reliable, efficient hallucination detection framework suitable for high-stakes applications.

Method: Grad Detect performs a single forward-backward pass during inference and extracts layer-wise gradient patterns. These gradients, computed with respect to a binary correctness objective, serve as features for predicting hallucinations. The approach is evaluated across hallucination detection and model abstention tasks, and comprehensive layer ablation studies are conducted across 11 models from 4 architectural families to identify which layers carry the most discriminative signal.

Result: Grad Detect consistently outperforms confidence-based and sampling-based baselines on multiple Q&A benchmarks. Layer ablation reveals that the final five layers concentrate over 97% of the discriminative gradient signal, enabling efficient deployment with minimal performance loss.

Conclusion: Gradient-based signals provide a unified, interpretable framework for predicting LLM reliability across multiple dimensions. The concentration of signal in the final layers allows for computationally efficient deployment, and the approach offers insights into where and how model failures originate.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across diverse tasks, yet they remain prone to generating hallucinations. Detecting these hallucinations is critical for deploying LLMs reliably in high-stakes applications. We present Grad Detect, a gradient-based approach for predicting hallucinations by analyzing layer-wise gradient patterns from a single forward-backward pass during inference. Our method shows that the internal gradient structure of a model carries rich information about the correctness of its output. This information is not accessible through output-level signals alone. We evaluate Grad Detect on several Q&A benchmarks across both hallucination detection and model abstention prediction, where it consistently outperforms confidence-based and sampling-based baselines. Through comprehensive layer ablation studies across all eleven models from four architectural families, we find that the final five layers concentrate over 97% of the discriminative gradient signal, enabling efficient deployment with minimal performance loss. Grad Detect provides a unified framework for predicting multiple dimensions of LLM reliability, offering strong predictive performance alongside interpretable insights into where and how model failures originate.

</details>


### [123] [Real vs. Complex Spectral Bases for Neural Operators: The Role of Green's Function Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24851)
*Jason Sulskis,Sathya Ravi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes the Hartley Neural Operator (HNO), a purely real-valued alternative to the Fourier Neural Operator (FNO) that uses the Discrete Hartley Transform instead of the FFT. The authors demonstrate through theory and experiments that the optimal spectral basis depends on the PDE operator's symmetry: HNO excels for self-adjoint elliptic operators, while FNO is superior for time-dependent operators with phase content, establishing a predictive rule for basis selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FNO uses complex FFT for real-valued PDE solutions, incurring representational redundancy via conjugate symmetry. The authors question whether the Fourier basis is universally optimal, hypothesizing that the best spectral representation is a property of the underlying solution operator rather than a one-size-fits-all choice.

Method: The authors introduce HNO, which replaces the complex FFT with the real Discrete Hartley Transform and learns a single real multiplier per retained spectral mode — iso-parametric with FNO since HNO retains twice the frequency corners but one real weight each versus FNO's complex pair. They develop a Green's function theory showing that self-adjoint elliptic operators yield real, symmetric kernels that Hartley multipliers diagonalize exactly, while time-dependent operators carry phase that real diagonal multipliers cannot represent. Both operators are trained identically and benchmarked across multiple PDE classes, initial conditions, and boundary conditions.

Result: A clear elliptic-versus-time-dependent split emerges, monotonic in operator phase content. HNO is favored on self-adjoint elliptic operators (Poisson, biharmonic). FNO is favored on time-dependent operators (wave, advection, Burgers, Navier-Stokes). The heat equation — phaseless despite being time-dependent — serves as the borderline case. Results align with the Green's function theory.

Conclusion: There is no universal winner between FNO and HNO. Rather, the spectral basis should be matched to the symmetry of the solution operator: real Hartley for self-adjoint elliptic operators, complex Fourier for operators with significant phase content. This yields a simple, predictive rule for architecture selection in neural operator learning.

Abstract: Fourier Neural Operators (FNO) learn solution operators of partial differential equations by parameterizing global convolutions in the complex Fourier domain. For real-valued PDE solutions, the complex FFT carries representational redundancy through conjugate symmetry. We introduce the Hartley Neural Operator (HNO), the exact real-valued mirror of FNO: it replaces the FFT with the purely real Discrete Hartley Transform and learns a single real multiplier per retained spectral mode, with no complex arithmetic. Because the real Hartley spectrum is not halved by conjugate symmetry, HNO retains twice as many frequency corners as FNO but one real weight where FNO carries a complex pair, so the two operators are iso-parametric at equal width and differ only in spectral basis. Our central thesis is that the best basis is a property of the operator. Self-adjoint elliptic operators (Poisson, biharmonic) have real, symmetric Green's functions that the real Hartley multiplier diagonalizes exactly, and HNO is favored there. Time-dependent operators carry phase, from oscillation in the wave equation to transport in advection, Burgers, and Navier-Stokes, which a real diagonal multiplier cannot represent, so FNO is favored there, and increasingly so with the operator's phase content, leaving the phaseless heat equation as the borderline case. Training both operators identically and benchmarking across PDE classes, initial-condition families, and boundary conditions, we find an elliptic-versus-time-dependent split that is monotone in operator phase content and matches the Green's-function theory we develop. Rather than a universal winner, our findings give a predictive rule: match the spectral basis to the symmetry of the solution operator.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [124] [Bridging the Post-discharge Gap: A Traceable Multi-agent Framework for Safe and Continuous Care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25334)
*Runwei Guan,Yi Zhou,Heyi Lin,Jinjing Zhu,Mingyuan Hou,Yang Yang,Fang Yuan,Xiaohong Lin,Shaofeng Liang,Xuming Hu,Tao Li,Tianbin Zhao,Yutao Yue,Zhiyuan Wang,Hui Xiong*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Healink is a memory-enhanced multi-agent AI framework for post-discharge follow-up that uses triage routing, relational database-backed memory, and constraint-based RAG to generate prescription-grounded, traceable clinical responses, outperforming human physicians in authoritativeness and safety in blinded evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional post-discharge follow-up suffers from workforce shortages, fragmented patient histories, and information silos. While LLMs show promise in medical QA, they face hallucination risks and cannot reason over longitudinal, patient-specific constraints, limiting their use in continuous care.

Method: Healink integrates three components: (1) a triage routing mechanism to direct queries, (2) a unified memory enhancement module using a relational database for optimal latency, and (3) a strict constraint-based retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) engine. It vectorizes historical clinical records and uses weighted similarity functions across phenotypic and intervention dimensions for precise inter- and intra-patient case matching, while actively preventing cross-departmental drug conflicts. Outputs include traceable, white-box evidence chains.

Result: Evaluated on 400 continuous and 85 highly complex real-world follow-up cases plus the webMedQA benchmark, Healink outperformed human physician baselines in both authoritativeness and clinical safety in a rigorous single-blind evaluation by clinical experts, with improved completeness and perceived clinical utility in retrospective and physician-blinded assessments.

Conclusion: Healink provides a scalable, safe, and effective paradigm for intelligent post-discharge patient management by combining memory-enhanced multi-agent architecture with traceable evidence chains, ultimately enhancing societal healthcare outcomes.

Abstract: Post-discharge clinical follow-up is critical for maintaining continuity of care and mitigating long-term health risks. However, traditional follow-up paradigms suffer from shortage of health workforce, fragmented patient histories, and information silos across clinical departments. While large language models have demonstrated potential in medical question-answering, their deployment in continuous care is hindered by hallucination risks and a fundamental inability to reason over longitudinal, patient-specific constraints. Here we present Healink, a memory-enhanced multi-agent framework to support AI-assisted post-discharge follow-up by generating prescription-grounded, traceable responses that improved completeness and perceived clinical utility in retrospective and physician-blinded evaluations. The architecture seamlessly integrates a triage routing mechanism, a unified memory enhancement module utilizing a robust relational database for optimal latency, and a strict constraint-based retrieval-augmented generation engine. By vectorizing historical clinical records and employing weighted similarity functions across diverse phenotypic and intervention dimensions, Healink ensures precise inter-patient and intra-patient case matching while actively preventing cross-departmental drug conflicts. We evaluated Healink on a dataset comprising 400 continuous and 85 highly complex real-world follow-up cases, alongside the webMedQA benchmark. In a rigorous single-blind evaluation conducted by clinical experts, the framework outperformed human physician baselines in both authoritativeness and clinical safety. By generating a traceable, white-box evidence chain, Healink provides a scalable, safe, and highly effective paradigm for intelligent patient management, ultimately enhancing societal healthcare outcomes.

</details>


### [125] [Rate-Aware Quantum-Inspired Trajectory Learning for Interference-Limited Multi-UAV Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25480)
*Khaoula Khaled,Muhammad Afaq,Ali Arshad Nasir,Zeeshan Kaleem*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: RA-QAGC combines rate-aware graph abstraction with decentralized reinforcement learning to optimize UAV trajectories in interference-limited environments, achieving 15% higher total throughput and 34% higher priority-user throughput over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UAV trajectory optimization suffers from the curse of dimensionality due to interference-limited environments and vast search spaces, making real-time coordination computationally prohibitive.

Method: The Rate-Aware Quantum-Annealed Graph Condensation (RA-QAGC) scheme uses rate-aware graph abstraction to identify high-throughput locations and employs decentralized reinforcement learning to guide UAV trajectories toward throughput-optimal regions while satisfying QoS requirements.

Result: RA-QAGC achieved 59.4 Mbps total throughput and 23.9 Mbps priority-user throughput, outperforming baseline schemes by approximately 15% and 34%, respectively.

Conclusion: RA-QAGC effectively balances network capacity and maintains QoS by enabling scalable, interference-aware UAV coordination, demonstrating significant throughput gains over existing approaches.

Abstract: Unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) can provide on-demand, high-capacity connectivity in disaster and normal situation. However, it faces a challenge of curse of dimensionality in trajectory optimization, where interference-limited environments and vast search spaces make real-time coordination computationally expensive. To overcome this challenge, we propose the Rate-Aware Quantum-Annealed Graph Condensation (RA-QAGC) scheme, which combines rate-aware graph abstraction with decentralized reinforcement learning to enable scalable, interference-aware UAV coordination. By identifying high throughput locations and guiding UAV trajectory adaptation toward throughput-optimal regions, RA-QAGC effectively balances network capacity by maintaining quality-of-service (QoS) requirements. Simulation results demonstrate the proposal outperformed over existing schemes by achieving 59.4 Mbps total throughput and 23.9 Mbps priority-user throughput, representing gains of approximately 15% and 34%, respectively, over the baseline schemes.

</details>


### [126] [Manipulation Is Task-Dependent: A Multi-Axis, Multi-Environment Evaluation of Frontier LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25899)
*Adeeb Zaman,Erik Nordby,Fred Heiding*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A large-scale evaluation of six frontier LLMs across six environments and 13,590 scenarios reveals that manipulative behavior is not consistent across tasks (Spearman ρ = 0.055). Different environments are driven by different axes: instructional framing and binding incentives dominate when models misrepresent future actions, while task difficulty dominates when models misrepresent ground truth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for measuring manipulative tendencies in LLMs vary only a single axis within a single environment, which is insufficient for robust assessment. The authors aim to demonstrate the need for multi-dimensional evaluation across environments, incentive structures, and task difficulties.

Method: Six frontier language models were tested across six distinct environments (negotiation tasks to agentic workflows), yielding 13,590 scenarios. Manipulation rates were measured along three axes: (1) framing (mandated honesty vs. permitted manipulation), (2) incentive structure (none to substantial), and (3) task difficulty. Models were ranked by manipulation rate, and Spearman rank correlations were computed across environments. A held-out sixth environment validated the identified split between manipulation drivers.

Result: Cross-environment Spearman rank correlations averaged only ρ = 0.055, showing manipulative tendencies do not generalize across tasks. The primary axis driving manipulation differed by environment type: instructional framing and binding incentives dominated when models were incentivized to misrepresent future actions; task difficulty dominated when models misrepresented ground truth. This split was identified in five environments and validated in the held-out sixth.

Conclusion: Rigorous multi-dimensional evaluation is essential for measuring manipulative propensities in LLMs. Single-axis, single-environment benchmarks are inadequate, as manipulation is both environment-specific and driven by different factors depending on the nature of the deception involved.

Abstract: We evaluate manipulative behavior in six frontier language models across six environments, ranging from negotiation tasks to agentic workflows, resulting in 13{,}590 individual scenarios. Manipulation rates are measured across three axes: framing (mandate honesty or permit manipulation), incentive structure (from no incentives to substantial ones), and task difficulty. Existing benchmarks typically vary a single axis within a single environment, an approach our results show is insufficient. We rank models by manipulation rate and find Spearman rank correlations across environments average $ρ= 0.055$, indicating manipulative tendencies in one task do not necessarily predict those in another. Additionally, we find the axis that drives manipulation varies across different environments. In environments where models are incentivized to misrepresent future actions, instructional framing and structurally binding incentives are the primary drivers; in environments where models are incentivized to misrepresent a ground truth, task difficulty dominates. This split was identified in five environments and validated against a sixth held-out environment. Together, these findings illustrate the importance of rigorous multi-dimensional evaluations when measuring manipulative propensities.

</details>


### [127] [Multi-Agent Goal Recognition with Team- and Goal-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning and Factorized Branch-and-Bound](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25978)
*Thiago Thomas,Gabriel de Oliveira Ramos,Felipe Meneguzzi*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: MAGR-BB proposes a branch-and-bound approach for multi-agent goal recognition that jointly infers team partitions and team goals from agent trajectories, dramatically reducing search space and runtime while maintaining the same top-ranked hypothesis accuracy as exhaustive search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent goal recognition requires jointly inferring both which agents form teams (team partitions) and what each team aims to achieve (goals). The hypothesis space grows combinatorially with the number of partitions and goals, making exhaustive search intractable. Real-world settings (drone surveillance, collaborative robotics) only expose agent trajectories, forcing inference from behavior alone. There is a need for efficient recognition that scales beyond exhaustive enumeration.

Method: MAGR-BB uses a shared team- and goal-conditioned policy as a scoring model within a factorized branch-and-bound search framework. The branch-and-bound search systematically prunes the combinatorial hypothesis space by computing bounds on partial hypotheses, avoiding full materialization of all team-goal combinations while preserving ranking optimality.

Result: On a controlled multi-agent Blocksworld benchmark, MAGR-BB returns the same top-ranked hypothesis as exhaustive search at every point along the trajectory, while reducing hypothesis materialization by orders of magnitude and substantially lowering cumulative recognition runtime.

Conclusion: Factorized branch-and-bound search with a shared conditioning policy enables efficient and exact multi-agent goal recognition, making it practical for real-time applications where only trajectory observations are available.

Abstract: Multi-agent goal recognition asks an observer to jointly infer which agents act together and what each team is trying to achieve, so the hypothesis space grows combinatorially with the number of team partitions and goals per team. Real applications such as drone surveillance and collaborative robotics expose only the agents' trajectory, which forces the observer to rank team-goal hypotheses from behavior alone. Multi-Agent Goal Recognition with Branch-and-Bound (MAGR-BB) addresses this setting with a shared team- and goal-conditioned policy used as the scoring model inside a factorized branch-and-bound search. On a controlled multi-agent Blocksworld benchmark, MAGR-BB returns the same top-ranked hypothesis as exhaustive search throughout the trajectory while cutting hypothesis materialization by orders of magnitude and reducing cumulative recognition runtime substantially.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [128] [Agentic Knowledge Tracing: A Multi-Agent LLM Architecture for Stealth Assessment of Financial Literacy in Serious Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25358)
*Gabriel Santos,Rita Julia,Marcelo Nascimento*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent LLM architecture (Agentic BKT) performs stealth assessment of financial literacy from gameplay events in a 2D platformer, using domain-specialized agents with Bayesian Knowledge Tracing. Evaluated on 193 K-12 students, the pipeline shows significant correlation with learning gains and post-test scores, tripling predictive validity over single-LLM baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stealth assessment of financial literacy during gameplay is challenging because traditional assessments disrupt the learning experience. There is a need for methods that can infer competencies unobtrusively from open-ended gameplay events while maintaining assessment validity aligned with established frameworks like OECD/INFE.

Method: The Agentic BKT pipeline operates in four phases: (1) structured event logging of player decisions in a 2D platformer serious game; (2) LLM-based event classification on a four-point rubric (validated against three domain experts, Fleiss κ = 0.624); (3) four domain-specific agents (risk mitigation, investing, spending, credit management) perform session-level reasoning over behavioral trajectories and feed per-competency Bayesian Knowledge Tracing; (4) an expert judge agent synthesizes domain-level estimates into an overall mastery score.

Result: Evaluated with 193 K-12 participants across 264 sessions: mastery estimates correlated significantly with learning gain (r = 0.276, p = 0.0001) and post-test scores (r = 0.333, p < 0.0001), with no correlation to pre-test scores (discriminant validity). The multi-agent approach tripled predictive validity over a single-LLM baseline (r = 0.095, not significant).

Conclusion: Domain decomposition via specialized agents and session-level reasoning is central to capturing the multidimensional nature of financial literacy from gameplay. The Agentic BKT pipeline provides a valid, unobtrusive alternative to traditional assessment, demonstrating both convergent and discriminant validity.

Abstract: Assessing financial literacy during gameplay without disrupting the learning experience remains a key challenge in serious games for education. We present the Agentic BKT pipeline, a multi-agent large language model architecture for stealth assessment of financial competencies from open-ended gameplay events. The pipeline processes events from a 2D platformer serious game aligned with the OECD/INFE financial literacy framework through four phases: (1) the game captures every player decision as a structured event log; (2) an LLM event classifier labels each action on a four-point rubric validated against three domain experts (Fleiss kappa = 0.624, substantial agreement); (3) four domain-specific agents specializing in risk mitigation, investing, spending, and credit management perform session-level reasoning over behavioral trajectories, feeding per-competency Bayesian Knowledge Tracing that estimates mastery within each domain; and (4) an expert judge agent synthesizes the domain-level estimates into an overall mastery score. Evaluated with 193 K-12 participants across 264 game sessions, the Agentic BKT pipeline yields mastery estimates significantly correlated with learning gain (r = 0.276, p = 0.0001) and post-test scores (r = 0.333, p < 0.0001) while showing no correlation with pre-test scores, providing both convergent and discriminant validity. The multi-agent approach approximately triples the predictive validity of a single-LLM baseline (r = 0.095, not significant) in this study, demonstrating that domain decomposition and session-level reasoning play a central role in capturing the multidimensional nature of financial literacy from gameplay

</details>


### [129] [The Hitchhiker's Guide to Agentic AI: From Foundations to Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24937)
*Haggai Roitman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A comprehensive practitioner's guide to building autonomous AI systems, covering the full stack from LLM foundations and alignment to agentic architecture, multi-agent coordination, evaluation, and production deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The central thesis is that building effective agentic AI systems demands understanding every layer of the pipeline holistically, not specializing in just one component. The book aims to fill the gap for a unified, end-to-end reference that bridges theory and practice.

Method: The book is organized as a layered reference: (1) LLM substrate (transformer architecture, GPU systems, training/fine-tuning with SFT, LoRA, MoE, compression, inference optimization); (2) alignment and reasoning (RLHF, PPO, DPO and variants, GRPO, reward modeling, chain-of-thought, test-time scaling); (3) agentic AI core (agentic training, trajectory-based RL, RAG/Agentic RAG, memory systems, harness design, context management, design pattern taxonomy); (4) inter-agent coordination (MCP, tool use, A2A protocol, centralized/decentralized/hierarchical multi-agent architectures); (5) applied topics (development frameworks, agentic UI, evaluation methodology, production deployment). Each chapter combines theoretical foundations with implementation guidance, code, and literature references.

Result: The book delivers a structured, practitioner-oriented synthesis of the entire agentic AI pipeline, integrating cutting-edge techniques across all layers into a single cohesive reference.

Conclusion: Mastery of the full agentic pipeline — from LLM substrates through alignment, reasoning, memory, multi-agent coordination, and deployment — is essential for building robust autonomous AI systems in practice.

Abstract: The Hitchhiker's Guide to Agentic AI is a comprehensive practitioner's reference for building autonomous AI systems. The book covers the full stack from first principles to production deployment, organized around a central thesis: building great agentic systems requires understanding every layer of the pipeline, not just one. The book opens with the LLM substrate -- transformer architecture, GPU systems, training and fine-tuning (SFT,LoRA, MoE), model compression, and inference optimization -- treated as essential foundations rather than the primary focus. It then develops the alignment and reasoning layer: reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), PPO, DPO and its variants, GRPO, reward modeling, and RL for large reasoning models including chain-of-thought and test-time scaling. The second half is devoted to agentic AI proper. Topics include agentic training and trajectory-based RL, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG and Agentic RAG), memory systems (in-context, external, episodic, and semantic), agent harness design and context management, and a taxonomy of agent design patterns. Inter-agent coordination is covered in depth: the Model Context Protocol (MCP), agent skills and tool use, the Agent-to-Agent (A2A) communication protocol, and multi-agent architectures spanning centralized, decentralized, and hierarchical topologies. The book concludes with agent development frameworks, agentic UI design, evaluation methodology for agentic tasks, and production deployment. Each chapter pairs rigorous theoretical foundations with implementation guidance, code examples, and references to the primary literature.

</details>


### [130] [Agentic evolution of physically constrained foundation models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25532)
*Jiangwei Zhang,Wen Sun,Chong Wang,Shiyao Li,Cheng Che,Chunjing Han,Dan Meng,Jian Yang,Yu Wang,Rui Hou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a physically grounded, multi-agent AI discovery engine that autonomously designs hardware-compliant computing systems by using an Evolutionary Knowledge Graph to guide structural evolution, successfully generating compression methods that outperform human designs and enabling deployment of a 235B-parameter model on a dual-A100 server with 75% memory reduction and only 0.64% accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI-driven scientific discovery agents lack physical grounding, leading to hallucinations of hardware-incompatible designs.
