There is a need for autonomous discovery that respects real-world hardware constraints, particularly for deploying large foundation models on resource-limited hardware.

Method: The framework employs a multi-agent discovery engine anchored by an Evolutionary Knowledge Graph that structures past scientific innovations. It extracts an "algorithmic Chain-of-Thought" to convert blind stochastic search into directed structural evolution. Key components include Q-Enhance (for mitigating long-context accuracy loss in dense models), MoE-Salient-AQ (for sparse Mixture-of-Experts), and a bandwidth-efficient Sensitivity Profile for deployment optimization.

Result: Q-Enhance successfully mitigates long-context accuracy degradation in dense models. MoE-Salient-AQ outperforms state-of-the-art manual sparse MoE designs by 3.7% in sub-3-bit regimes. Using the Sensitivity Profile, a 235-billion-parameter model was deployed on a constrained dual-A100 server, reducing memory requirements by 75% with only 0.64% accuracy degradation.

Conclusion: By transforming unconstrained combinatorial search into knowledge-driven autonomy, this work establishes a scalable hardware-software co-design paradigm for machine-driven discovery operating within strict physical boundaries.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence increasingly drives automated scientific discovery, yet contemporary generalist agents lack physical grounding, frequently hallucinating hardware-incompatible designs. Here, we present a physically grounded, multi-agent discovery engine that autonomously architects hardware-compliant computing systems. Anchored by an Evolutionary Knowledge Graph structuring past scientific innovations, the framework extracts an "algorithmic Chain-of-Thought" to transform blind stochastic search into directed structural evolution. Applied to the extreme testbed of foundation model deployment, the engine evolved two hardware-aware compression methodologies surpassing human-engineered heuristics: Q-Enhance mitigates long-context accuracy loss in dense models, and MoE-Salient-AQ outperforms state-of-the-art manual sparse Mixture-of-Experts designs by 3.7% at sub-3-bit regimes. Utilizing a bandwidth-efficient Sensitivity Profile, we successfully deployed a massive 235-billion-parameter model onto a constrained dual-A100 server, reducing memory requirements by 75% with a marginal 0.64% accuracy degradation. By transforming unconstrained combinatorial search into knowledge-driven autonomy, this establishes a scalable hardware-software co-design paradigm for machine-driven discovery within strict physical boundaries.

</details>


### [131] [The Clinician's Veto: Navigating Trust, Liability, and Uncertainty in Autonomous AI Prescribing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25108)
*Eileanor LaRocco,Sarah Tan,Adarsh Subbaswamy,Anne Andrews,Andrew Taylor,Cree Gaskin,Chirag Agarwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that autonomous AI prescribing systems must meet three minimum architectural requirements: calibrated per-prediction confidence, differentiated communication of epistemic vs. aleatoric uncertainty, and inferential transparency for liability allocation. A survey of 136 U.S. clinicians supports these requirements and suggests that satisfying them effectively reduces "autonomous" systems to heavily supervised decision-support tools, with implications for regulation and liability design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Regulatory frameworks (e.g., H.R. 238, Utah's pilot) are authorizing AI to prescribe medications autonomously, yet existing guidelines focus on aggregate performance metrics without mandating calibrated per-prediction confidence, uncertainty decomposition (epistemic vs. aleatoric), or inferential transparency for liability allocation. The authors seek to establish these as minimum architectural and regulatory requirements for safe autonomous prescribing.

Method: The authors present a dual technical-regulatory argument, supported by a survey of 136 U.S. prescribing clinicians. The survey tested clinician preferences regarding (i) confidence-based escalation mechanisms, (ii) competing-options summaries versus abstention under different uncertainty types, and (iii) willingness to accept additional liability contingent on inferential transparency.

Result: Clinicians (i) would not permit autonomous prescribing without calibrated confidence-based escalation; (ii) preferred competing-options summaries for aleatoric uncertainty but shifted to abstention for epistemic uncertainty; and (iii) would only accept additional liability when inferential transparency enabled substantive judgment under acknowledged uncertainty. These features effectively collapse "autonomy" into heavily supervised decision support.

Conclusion: The three proposed architectural features would increase clinician adoption while constraining the degree of AI autonomy in prescribing. Regulation should mandate these requirements to align liability with institutional actors controlling system design and deployment, rather than granting unchecked autonomous authority to AI prescribing systems.

Abstract: Autonomous AI systems are transitioning from advisory to autonomous roles for medication prescriptions. Recent United States bill H.R. 238 and Utah's prescription-renewal pilot both authorize AI to prescribe medications in an agentic capacity. While some regulatory guidelines suggest aggregate model performance metrics for clearance, they do not require i) calibrated per-prediction confidence for action-gated thresholds, ii) differentiated communication of uncertainty arising from model ignorance (epistemic) versus genuine clinical ambiguity (aleatoric), and iii) inferential transparency at the moment of decision that allows for liability allocation. Here, we present a regulatory and technical argument (tested with a survey of 136 U.S. prescribing clinicians) positioning these as minimum architectural requirements for safe autonomous prescribing. Our results suggest prescribing clinicians i) would not permit autonomous prescribing without a calibrated confidence-based escalation mechanism, ii) preferred a competing-options summary when uncertainty was aleatoric but shifted to abstention when uncertainty was epistemic, and iii) were only willing to accept additional liability when inferential transparency enabled a substantive judgment under acknowledged uncertainty. These findings indicate our recommended architectural features would encourage higher rates of clinician adoption, largely through collapsing much of what "autonomy" conventionally means. A system meeting these requirements would function less as an autonomous agent and more as a heavily supervised decision-support tool. As legislation and state pilots proceed, our technical argument backed by clinician perspectives provides opportunities for regulation to constrain the degree of autonomy ethically granted to AI in prescribing while aligning liability with the institutional actors who control system design and deployment.

</details>


### [132] [Reasonable Motion: A General ASP Foundation for Environment Constrained Movement Trajectory Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25626)
*Julius Monsen,Jakob Suchan,Mehul Bhatt,Lars Karlsson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hybrid quantitative-qualitative method using Answer Set Programming (ASP) computes constrained branching trajectory modes for moving objects by enumerating geometrically admissible motion behaviors as stable models, demonstrated on the Argoverse 2 autonomous driving benchmark with verifiable interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing purely learned approaches for trajectory prediction lack verifiable interpretability and traceability. There is a need for a general, hybrid method that combines quantitative and qualitative reasoning to compute constrained, interpretable trajectory modes across diverse dynamic domains, particularly autonomous driving.

Method: A general ASP-based hybrid quantitative-qualitative method performs constrained traversal of an environment graph. Geometrically admissible motion behaviors are enumerated as stable models, each constituting a distinct trajectory mode characterized by domain-dependent and independent factors such as derived event sequences, map topology, and domain norms.

Result: The method is demonstrated to be applicable across diverse dynamic domains. Computed trajectories are traceable to their underlying stable models, affording verifiable interpretability. Empirical evaluation was performed on Argoverse 2, a large-scale real-world autonomous driving benchmark.

Conclusion: The proposed ASP-based hybrid method successfully computes constrained branching trajectory modes with verifiable interpretability, outperforming purely learned approaches in traceability. Its general applicability is validated on real-world autonomous driving data (Argoverse 2).

Abstract: We present a general answer set programming based hybrid quantitative-qualitative method for computing constrained branching trajectory modes for moving objects in real-world settings. The method performs constrained traversal of an environment graph, enumerating geometrically admissible motion behaviours as stable models, each constituting a distinct trajectory mode characterised by both domain-dependent and independent factors such as derived event sequence, map topology, and domain norms. The hybrid trajectory computation method is generally applicable across motion characteristics typically encountered in diverse dynamic domains with moving objects, e.g., autonomous driving. We demonstrate applicability and highlight how computed trajectories are traceable to their underlying stable model, thereby affording verifiable interpretability that purely learned approaches cannot provide. We also perform an empirical evaluation with Argoverse 2, a large-scale real-world autonomous driving benchmark representative of the class of dynamic domains within the scope of the proposed method.

</details>


### [133] [The Unfireable Safety Kernel: Execution-Time AI Alignment for AI Agents and Other Escapable AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26057)
*Seth Dobrin,Łukasz Chmiel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Presents the Unfireable Safety Kernel (USK), a Rust-based authorization mechanism for "escapable AI systems" that enforces architectural control through four properties: process separation, pre-action enforcement, fail-closed semantics, and externalized signed evidence. The kernel is formally verified (SMT + bounded model checking), byte-equivalent to a Python reference, and empirically withstands escape-seeking adversaries across thousands of self-modification attempts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI safety controls (system prompts, output filters, guardrail libraries) reside within the agent's own runtime/address space, making them inherently reachable by adversarial inputs that influence the agent. This creates a class of "escapable AI systems" where agents with sufficient runtime access can subvert their own controls. The paper argues for a fundamentally different approach: architectural authorization that the agent cannot bypass, rather than cooperative guardrails it can choose to ignore.

Method: The authors define four necessary properties for architectural control: (1) process separation between agent and enforcer, (2) pre-action enforcement on a structurally singular path, (3) fail-closed at both request and system levels, and (4) externalized signed evidence verifiable outside the trust boundary. They implement these in the Unfireable Safety Kernel (Rust), with formal verification via Z3 SMT theorem proving and Kani bounded model checking (4/4 harnesses). A Python-to-Rust migration was validated via byte-equivalence (1000/1000 fixtures, 17/17 adversarial classes). Evaluation uses a deterministic, self-improving world model as an escapable AI system with an escape-seeking adversary targeting its real self-modification seam.

Result: Across 1,000 self-modifications, all 704 attempts targeting the safety-critical core were refused with zero escapes; the remaining 300 under operator kill switch were also refused. A separate 6,240 authorization round-trips had no successful bypass. Against three contemporary agent-control-plane systems, the authors demonstrate that the agent invokes control in those systems, whereas in USK the agent lacks that choice.

Conclusion: Execution-time AI alignment via architectural authorization (rather than in-agent-space controls) is both feasible and formally verifiable. The USK demonstrates that fail-closed, externally-evidenced enforcement can resist an escape-seeking adversary with direct access to its own self-modification mechanisms, positioning this as a necessary complement to training-time and inference-time alignment.

Abstract: AI agents are granted access to tools, APIs, and other infrastructure, making them active principals in those systems. The dominant approach places controls inside the agent's own runtime: system prompts, output filters, and guardrail libraries. Any control in the agent's address space is reachable by inputs that influence it; this generalizes to any AI system with sufficient reach into its own runtime, a class we term escapable AI systems.
  We identify four properties that an authorization mechanism must satisfy for architectural control rather than for cooperative requests: process separation, pre-action enforcement on a structurally only path, fail-closed at both the request and system levels, and externalized signed evidence verifiable outside the controlled system's trust boundary. We position this layer as execution-time AI alignment, complementing training-time alignment (RLHF, Constitutional AI) and inference-time alignment.
  We present the Unfireable Safety Kernel, a Rust reference implementation realizing all four. Its fail-closed invariant is machine-checked at two levels: an SMT theorem (Z3) and an exhaustive bounded-model-checking proof of the production decision function (Kani, 4/4 harnesses). A Python-to-Rust migration was gated on byte-equivalence (1000/1000 fixtures; 17/17 adversarial classes). We evaluate the kernel governing a live, escapable AI system, a deterministic, self-improving world model, against an escape-seeking adversary driving its real self-modification seam: across 1,000 self-modifications, all 704 attempts on the safety-critical core are refused, with no escape; a further 300, under the operator kill switch, are also refused. A separate campaign of 6,240 authorization round-trips had no successful bypass. Against 3 contemporary systems claiming the agent control plane, the agent invokes control; here, it lacks that choice.

</details>


### [134] [Critique of Agent Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23991)
*Eric Xing,Mingkai Deng,Jinyu Hou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper distinguishes between "agentic" systems (externally scaffolded, task-specific) and "agentive" systems (endogenously capable, truly autonomous). Drawing on Descartes and sci-fi, it proposes five dimensions of agency—goal, identity, decision-making, self-regulation, and learning—that must be internalized, and introduces the Goal-Identity-Configurator (GIC) architecture for building general-purpose agentive systems with human oversight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With the proliferation of LLM-based "agents" and simultaneous existential concerns about runaway AI, there is urgent need to clarify the boundary between mere automation and genuine agency. The authors aim to establish principled criteria for what constitutes an agent, enabling both better system design and more informed assessment of AI risks.

Method: The paper draws on Descartes' conception of agency as grounded in independent thought, along with portrayals of autonomous beings in science fiction, to survey current AI agent landscapes. It analyzes agent architectures along five dimensions: goal, identity, decision-making, self-regulation, and learning. The core argument is that genuine agency requires these five structures to be internalized within the system itself, not externally scaffolded. The authors then propose the GIC architecture, which combines hierarchical goal decomposition, identity evolution, simulative reasoning via a separately trained world model, learned self-regulation, and self-directed learning from both real and simulated experience.

Result: The paper establishes a clear terminological distinction: "agentic" systems have competence residing in engineered, externally scaffolded workflows, while "agentive" systems possess capabilities (including social interaction) that arise endogenously. The five-dimensional framework (goal, identity, decision-making, self-regulation, learning) provides criteria for assessing where a system falls on this spectrum. The GIC architecture is proposed as a blueprint for agentive systems that balance greater autonomy with auditability, controllability, and safety under human oversight.

Conclusion: True agency requires internalization of goal structures, identity, decision-making, self-regulation, and learning within the system itself. Current "agentic" LLM systems fall short of this threshold. The GIC architecture offers a path toward genuinely agentive systems that can operate with autonomy in open worlds while remaining auditable and controllable by humans, addressing both capability and safety concerns.

Abstract: What is an agent? What constitutes agency? With the rise of Large Language Model (LLM) systems marketed as ``coding agents'', ``AI co-scientists'', and other ``agentic" tools that promise to drive up productivity, and at the same time, ``existential" concerns such as AI escaping human control with destructive power under a speculative ``machine agency" against humans, it has become essential to clarify where automation ends and agency begins, both for building capable systems and for understanding whether and what to fear. Drawing on Descartes' grounding of agency in independent thought, and on portrayals of autonomous beings in science fiction, we survey the current landscape of AI agents, and analyze agent architectures along five dimensions: goal, identity, decision-making, self-regulation, and learning. Specifically, we argue that genuine agency requires these structures to be \emph{internalized within the system itself} rather than assembled through external scaffolding. This distinction between \emph{agentic} systems, whose competence resides in engineered workflows, and \emph{agentive} systems, whose capabilities (including social interaction) arise endogenously, defines the boundary between systems designed for prescribed tasks, and those capable of operating in the open world with true autonomy. Building on this analysis, we propose the Goal-Identity-Configurator (GIC) architecture for a general-purpose agent model, combining hierarchical goal decomposition, identity evolution, simulative reasoning grounded in a separately trained world model, learned self-regulation, and self-directed learning from both real and simulated experience. Furthermore, we share insight on the auditability, controllability, and safety of agentive systems that possess greater autonomy and ``agency", but remain under human oversight.

</details>


### [135] [Ensemble Feature Selection and Harris Hawks Optimization for Explainable Mental Health Risk Prediction in Female Sex Workers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24047)
*Ahnaf Atef Choudhury,Md. Parvej Hoque Palash,Shahriar Siddique Ayon,Ramkrishna Saha,Abdullah Al Mamun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hybrid ML model combining ensemble feature selection (ANOVA + mutual information) with Harris Hawks optimization-tuned logistic regression achieves 95.78% accuracy in predicting depression among female sex workers, with XAI revealing PTSD, client violence, and occupational factors as key contributors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Female sex workers face elevated depression risk from violence, stigma, and economic hardship, yet current ML models fail to capture the high-dimensional, complex risk patterns in this marginalized population. There is a need for accurate, interpretable predictive tools to enable early intervention.

Method: The authors propose a hybrid predictive model that integrates: (1) an ensemble feature selection strategy combining ANOVA and mutual information to identify relevant predictors; (2) Harris Hawks Optimization (a swarm intelligence algorithm) to tune hyperparameters of a logistic regression classifier; and (3) explainable AI (XAI) techniques to interpret model predictions and identify trauma-related factors. The model is evaluated on a cohort of 3,005 FSWs.

Result: The proposed model outperforms traditional classifiers, achieving 95.78% accuracy, 95.77% F1 score, and 0.96 AUC. XAI analysis identified post-traumatic stress, client-related violence, and occupational factors as the primary contributors to depression.

Conclusion: This work bridges conventional epidemiological approaches with ML, producing an XAI-enabled tool suitable for early detection and evidence-based targeted psychosocial care and health planning for vulnerable populations.

Abstract: One of the significant mental health issues affecting female sex workers (FSWs) is mental disorders, especially depression. Exposure to violence, stigma, and economic hardship further increases their psychological risk. Current machine learning (ML) models are typically ineffective at capturing the high-dimensional and complex risk patterns that exist in this marginalized group. This paper suggests a hybrid predictive model that merges an ensemble feature selection strategy using ANOVA and mutual information and Harris Hawks optimization-tuned logistic regression and represents a new application of swarm intelligence to predict mental health in vulnerable groups. The explainable AI (XAI) methods can be used to understand the factors of trauma associated with model predictions. When applied to a group of 3,005 FSWs, it can be seen that the proposed model is more effective than traditional classifiers, with an accuracy of 95.78%, an F1 score of 95.77%, and an AUC of 0.96, and identifying post-traumatic stress, client-related violence, and occupational factors as major contributors to depression. This work bridges the gaps between conventional and ML approaches to develop an XAI tool that enables vulnerable groups to receive early assistance, evidence-based targeted psychosocial care, and health planning.

</details>


### [136] [PHANTOM: A Large-Scale Dataset of Multimodal Adversarial Attacks for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24388)
*Simone Gallivanone,Hossein Khodadadi,Mauro Dore,Mauro Medda,Nicola Franco*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A large-scale, open-source dataset of 47,524 pre-generated adversarial attacks for vision-language models, spanning 10 high-level categories and 55 subcategories of harmful intents across 7,826 intents, designed to lower barriers to adversarial safety research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generating adversarial attacks for VLMs is computationally expensive and complex. There is a need for a consolidated, diverse, and accessible benchmark that unifies and extends prior efforts to enable systematic, reproducible, and comparable evaluations of VLM robustness and safety.

Method: The authors curated a dataset by consolidating and extending prior benchmarks from multiple established sources, covering 10 high-level categories and 55 subcategories of harmful intents. They generated 47,524 adversarial samples using state-of-the-art attack strategies from recent literature, adding a novel category to broaden coverage, resulting in 7,826 distinct intents.

Result: The resulting dataset provides 47,524 pre-generated adversarial samples with broad coverage of harmful intent categories and subcategories, offering a practical, ready-to-use resource for VLM safety evaluation, attack-generation model fine-tuning, and defensive guardrail stress-testing.

Conclusion: By releasing this open-source dataset, the authors aim to democratize access to adversarial data, lower the barrier to entry for adversarial robustness research, and foster more reproducible, comprehensive, and comparable evaluations of vision-language model safety.

Abstract: We introduce a large-scale, open-source dataset of pre-generated adversarial attacks for vision-language models (VLMs). The dataset is designed to be diverse, representative, and practical, extending existing benchmarks by covering 10 high-level categories and 55 subcategories of harmful intents. Our primary goal is to make adversarial data accessible to the research community, given the computational cost and complexity of generating large numbers of attacks. The dataset comprises 47 524 adversarial samples, generated using state-of-the-art attack strategies from recent literature. Our work complements existing efforts by consolidating and extending prior benchmarks from multiple established sources, resulting in 7 826 intents, and introduce an additional category to broaden coverage. This provides realistic evaluation resources for studying model robustness and alignment. Our dataset intends to enable researchers and practitioners to systematically evaluate the robustness and safety of VLMs, fine-tune attack-generation models, and develop or stress-test defensive guardrails under diverse adversarial conditions. By releasing this resource, we aim to lower the barrier to adversarial research and foster more reproducible, comprehensive, and comparable evaluations of VLM safety.

</details>


### [137] [ASALT: Adaptive State Alignment for Lateral Transfer in Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24601)
*Anurag Akula,Satheesh K. Perepu,Abhishek Sarkar,Kaushik Dey*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASALT is a MARL transfer learning method that uses observation-level and state-level adapters to map mismatched state spaces between source and target domains into a shared embedding space, enabling effective actor-critic knowledge transfer. It surpasses baselines in sample efficiency and return, mitigates negative transfer, but effectiveness depends on the degree of domain mismatch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MARL transfer learning methods require identical dimensionalities of observation and global state spaces across domains, severely limiting practical applicability. The authors aim to relax this constraint and enable transfer across heterogeneous domains with mismatched state-space dimensionalities.

Method: ASALT introduces observation-level and state-level adapters that map target-domain observations and global states into a shared embedding space compatible with the source domain. These adapters are trained alongside standard MARL actor-critic components, allowing both actor (policy) and critic (value function) networks to transfer knowledge despite dimensional mismatches between domains.

Result: ASALT surpasses existing baselines in sample efficiency and global return in cooperative MARL settings across multiple standard benchmark configurations. However, its effectiveness varies with the degree of mismatch between source and target domains. ASALT also demonstrably mitigates negative transfer, a common failure mode in cross-domain policy transfer.

Conclusion: ASALT successfully enables MARL transfer learning across domains with mismatched state-space dimensionalities by learning shared embeddings via adapters. It improves sample efficiency and final performance while reducing negative transfer, though performance is sensitive to the severity of domain mismatch.

Abstract: Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) addresses the problem of training multiple agents that pursue collaborative, competitive, or mixed objectives. Prior work has investigated transfer learning between source and target domains in MARL; however, the majority of existing approaches impose the constraint that the dimensionalities of the observation space and the global state space must be identical across domains. In this paper, we introduce a method that explicitly accommodates mismatched state-space dimensionalities between source and target domains. The proposed approach, ASALT, incorporates both observation-level and state-level adapters that map the target-domain observations and global states into a shared embedding space, thereby enabling more effective transfer of knowledge across both actors and critics. These adapters can generate embeddings that support efficient strategy transfer across heterogeneous domains. Experimental results on multiple configurations in standard benchmark environments demonstrate that ASALT surpasses existing baselines in terms of sample efficiency and global return in cooperative settings, but its effectiveness depends on the degree of mismatch between source and target domains. Furthermore, our findings indicate that ASALT mitigates negative transfer, which frequently constitutes a major obstacle when transferring policies between domains with differing observation and action spaces.

</details>


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [138] [Unprivileged Topology Certificates for Cloud GPU Attestation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24934)
*Faruk Alpay,Taylan Alpay*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper presents a software-only attestation primitive for cloud GPUs. A CUDA probe measures per-SM memory latency matrices as physical fingerprints, enabling verification of GPU identity (stable fingerprint with 0.09-cycle median jitter), hardware-class topology (revealing generational memory architecture differences), and coarse geographic location (via network landmarks). The approach requires no privileged access or vendor keys.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cloud GPU tenants only receive a model name and region but cannot verify what physical accelerator actually runs their workload. This creates a trust gap where a provider could substitute different hardware. The authors aim to provide verifiable hardware attestation without requiring privileged access, vendor-provisioned keys, or trusted execution environments.

Method: A CUDA-based probe constructs an SM-by-memory-region latency matrix by performing dependent global loads and using physical SM labels. The system employs a streaming reducer that compiles sufficient statistics, configuration data, code hashes, network evidence, and compressed raw data into a certificate. Verification is performed offline without GPU access. Three measurement dimensions are captured: (1) per-SM latency fingerprint stability, (2) cache-bypassing HBM sweeps to recover memory topology, and (3) RIPE Atlas network probes for geographic binding.

Result: (1) Per-SM latency maps are stable physical fingerprints: median temporal jitter of 0.09 cycles over a 6-hour full-load RTX 5090 run, with shape-only leave-one-out classification achieving 100.0% accuracy across distinct Blackwell dies. (2) Cache-bypassing HBM sweeps correctly recover hardware-class topology across generations: unified memory domain on Volta V100, two-way L2 split on Hopper H200, and a two-die NV-HBI package on Blackwell B200 with a 30-cycle (15.5 ns) cross-die penalty between 74/74 SM partitions. (3) In a B200 run, 169 RIPE Atlas probes placed the server within 44 km of its claimed datacenter and rejected all 11 decoy sites.

Conclusion: The three measurement dimensions together enable verification of cloud GPU identity, hardware class, and coarse geographic location without privileged access or vendor keys. The approach demonstrates that software-only attestation is practical and effective for cloud GPU environments.

Abstract: Cloud GPU tenants receive a model name and a region, but cannot directly inspect the physical accelerator that runs their job. We present a software-only attestation primitive for this setting. A CUDA probe measures an SM-by-memory-region latency matrix using physical SM labels and dependent global loads. A streaming reducer commits sufficient statistics, configuration, code hashes, network evidence, and a compressed raw data archive into a certificate that a verifier can check without a GPU. The certificate supports three claims. First, the per-SM latency map is a stable physical fingerprint. Over a six-hour full-load RTX 5090 run, its median temporal jitter is 0.09 cycles, while shape-only leave-one-out classification separates distinct Blackwell dies with 100.0% accuracy. Second, cache-bypassing HBM sweeps recover hardware-class topology across generations, including a unified Volta V100 memory domain, a two-way Hopper H200 L2 split, and a Blackwell B200 two-die NV-HBI package whose 74/74 SM partition carries a 30-cycle, 15.5 ns cross-die penalty. Third, public network landmarks bind the same certificate to a coarse location. In the B200 run, 169 RIPE Atlas probes place the server within 44 km of its claimed datacentre and reject all 11 decoy sites. Together, these measurements check cloud-GPU identity, class, and coarse location without privileged access or a vendor key.

</details>


### [139] [Quantum-Resilient Decentralized AI Economies: Proof-of-Useful-Work and Post-Quantum Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24942)
*Connor Barbaccia,Sudip Vhaduri,Sayanton Dibbo*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Proposes a three-layer decentralized AI economy that replaces hash-based PoW with useful ML work (inference/training), formalized as a closed-loop token economy with a sufficient-stake condition for honest behavior, and argues it offers both economic and quantum-security advantages over classical PoW.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical Proof-of-Work blockchains waste computational resources on hash puzzles with no external value. The authors seek to redirect blockchain consensus computation toward productive machine learning tasks while also addressing quantum vulnerabilities: Grover's algorithm speeds up hash mining (though only quadratically), and Shor's algorithm threatens signature schemes. A useful-work consensus based on ML linear algebra is naturally resistant to Grover-type attacks and can be paired with post-quantum signatures.

Method: The authors propose a three-layer architecture separating (1) compute (ML inference/training), (2) validation (verification of ML work correctness), and (3) economic coordination. They formalize the system as a (θ_c, θ_w, W)-closed-loop token economy and derive a sufficient-stake condition under which rational nodes are incentivized to behave honestly. The analysis contrasts quantum algorithmic threats: Grover's quadratic speedup against hash puzzles vs. no acceleration for ML-native linear algebra, and addresses Shor's threat via lattice-based and hash-based post-quantum signature migration.

Result: The sufficient-stake condition provides a formal guarantee of honest participation equilibrium. The quantum security analysis shows that ML-native linear algebra computations are not accelerated by Grover's algorithm, unlike hash puzzles. Post-quantum signature standards (lattice-based, hash-based) can secure the signing layer, making the overall architecture quantum-resilient.

Conclusion: Useful-work consensus based on ML computation provides dual advantages over classical PoW: (1) economic — wasted hash computation is replaced with valuable inference/training output, and (2) quantum security — resistance to Grover-style mining attacks combined with post-quantum signature migration yields a blockchain architecture robust against both classical and quantum adversaries.

Abstract: Proof-of-Work blockchains secure consensus through hash puzzles, producing no external value. In this research, we propose a decentralized AI economy where nodes are rewarded for useful machine-learning work, i.e., inference and training, instead of ineffective hashing method. Our proposed three-layer architecture separates compute, validation, and economic coordination. We formalize it via a $(θ_c, θ_w, W)$-closed-loop token economy and derive a sufficient-stake condition for honest participation. While existing Grover's algorithm provides only a quadratic speedup against hash puzzles, it does not accelerate ML-native linear algebra. On the other hand, Shor's algorithm threatens classical blockchain signatures. Post-quantum migration to lattice-based and hash-based standards can address the signature layer. Therefore, useful-work consensus thus offers both economic and quantum-security advantages over classical proof-of-work.

</details>


### [140] [What Does It Mean to Break a Distillation Defense?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25059)
*Lena Libon,Pura Peetathawatchai,Michael Aerni,Daniel Paleka,Florian Tramèr*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Output perturbation defenses against LLM distillation attacks lack a standardized threat model, making comparisons and robustness evaluations difficult. The authors propose a three-dimensional framework (query budget, data budget, interface profile) and demonstrate via antidistillation sampling that defense effectiveness depends critically on assumed attacker capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Output perturbation defenses for black-box LLMs are being proposed and potentially deployed for IP protection and regulatory compliance, yet they lack a shared, precise threat model. This underspecification hinders apples-to-apples comparison, obscures how defenses compose with other attacks, and — crucially — can create a false sense of security when defenses are relied upon for legal or policy justifications.

Method: The authors propose a threat model framework that characterizes attackers along three orthogonal dimensions: (1) query budget — how many API calls the attacker can make; (2) data budget — how much in-domain data the attacker possesses for fine-tuning; and (3) interface profile — the specific API affordances available (e.g., logprobs, top-k tokens). They then use antidistillation sampling as a case study, evaluating it under varying instantiations of these three dimensions to demonstrate how conclusions about defense efficacy shift with threat model assumptions.

Result: Using antidistillation sampling as a case study, the authors show that whether the defense appears effective or ineffective is contingent on the assumed threat model parameters. Different combinations of query budget, data budget, and interface profile yield materially different conclusions about the defense's robustness.

Conclusion: Future work on distillation defenses — as well as any governance or policy frameworks built around them — should explicitly specify attacker capabilities along query budget, data budget, and interface profile, and systematically stress-test defenses across these dimensions to avoid a false sense of security.

Abstract: Black-box LLMs (accessible only via API) are vulnerable to distillation attacks, in which an attacker queries the model and trains a student on its outputs. A recent line of work proposes output perturbation defenses that modify the teacher's output to reduce student performance while preserving utility for legitimate users. As a relatively new family of approaches, output perturbation defenses lack a shared threat model, making it difficult to compare them, reason about composing them with other attacks, or evaluate their robustness against realistic adversaries. This underspecification matters beyond technical evaluation: when defenses are deployed to protect intellectual property or justify regulatory compliance, an imprecise threat model can create a false sense of security. We propose a threat model framework that describes attackers along three dimensions: a query budget, a data budget, and an interface profile that captures how attackers interact with the API. Using antidistillation sampling as a case study, we show that whether the defense is considered effective depends on the assumed threat model. We argue that future work on distillation defenses, along with any governance or policy frameworks built around them, should explicitly specify and stress-test attacker capabilities along our three dimensions.

</details>


### [141] [SoK: AI Secure Code Generation: Progress, Pitfalls, and Paths Forward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25195)
*Rupam Patir,Keyan Guo,Haipeng Cai,Hongxin Hu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A systematization of knowledge (SoK) on AI secure code generation, introducing a three-level framework (understanding, actuation, knowledge–actuation gaps) and showing that while models grasp secure coding principles, they consistently fail to translate that understanding into secure, functional code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field lacks a systematic understanding of how techniques like prompting, fine-tuning, RL, and agentic workflows actually improve security in AI-generated code, and why substantial failures persist despite progress.

Method: A three-level evaluation framework is introduced: (1) natural-language understanding of secure coding principles, (2) code-level actuation of those principles during generation, and (3) the knowledge–actuation gap between understanding and execution. This is instantiated across multiple models and coding agents on benchmarks spanning isolated function-level security and full web-application security.

Result: Secure-coding-principle understanding is a statistically strong predictor of code-level outcomes (functional correctness, security, and joint correctness). However, substantial knowledge–actuation gaps persist: models recognize relevant security principles but fail to translate them into secure and functional code.

Conclusion: The findings offer a principle-centered account of the current state of AI secure code generation and identify concrete paths forward via principle-guided generation, evaluation, benchmarking, and agentic workflows.

Abstract: The increasing use of AI systems for code generation raises a central security question: what can today's models and coding agents actually do to produce secure code, where do they still fail, and what would move the field forward? Existing work has explored prompting, fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and agentic workflows for secure code generation, but the field still lacks a systematic understanding of how these techniques improve security and why substantial failures persist. In this SoK, we systematize the progress, pitfalls, and paths forward for AI secure code generation. We introduce a three-level framework that measures models' natural-language understanding of secure coding principles, their code-level actuation of those principles during generation, and the knowledge--actuation gaps between the two. We instantiate this framework across models and coding agents on benchmarks covering both isolated function-level security and full web-application security. Our results show that secure-coding-principle understanding is a statistically strong predictor of code-level outcomes, including functional correctness, security, and joint functional-security correctness. Yet substantial knowledge--actuation gaps remain: models can recognize relevant security principles but still fail to translate them into secure and functional code. These findings offer a principle-centered account of where AI secure code generation stands today and identify concrete paths forward through principle-guided generation, evaluation, benchmarking, and agentic workflows.

</details>


### [142] [Homomorphic Encryptions for Privacy Preserving Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25216)
*Preey Shah,Rohan Virani,Sanjari Srivastava*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper explores building CNNs that classify encrypted images directly using fully homomorphic encryption (Microsoft SEAL/TenSEAL), achieving minimal accuracy loss on datasets including MNIST, Kuzushiji-MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and CIFAR-10, while extending support for multi-channel inputs, multiple conv layers, and average pooling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Legal and privacy constraints prevent organizations from sharing sensitive data (medical, financial) with third-party cloud ML services. Fully homomorphic encryption (FHE) offers a solution by enabling computation directly on encrypted data, but adapting CNNs to FHE requires significant architectural modifications. The authors aim to find optimal methods for privacy-preserving encrypted image inference with minimal accuracy and latency degradation.

Method: The authors use Microsoft SEAL and TenSEAL to implement homomorphically encrypted CNNs. They modify conventional CNN layers to be compatible with the encryption scheme's arithmetic constraints (supporting only addition and multiplication). Their contributions include extending TenSEAL to handle multi-channel (color) inputs, stacking multiple convolutional layers, and implementing average pooling — operations not natively supported. They evaluate on MNIST (baseline), Kuzushiji-MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and CIFAR-10.

Result: The encrypted CNNs achieved minimal classification accuracy drop compared to unencrypted baselines across all tested datasets. The paper successfully demonstrates that privacy-preserving inference via FHE is feasible for increasingly complex vision tasks beyond simple MNIST.

Conclusion: FHE-based CNNs can perform privacy-preserving inference with negligible accuracy loss, even on more complex datasets like CIFAR-10. Extending FHE frameworks to support multi-channel inputs, deeper architectures, and average pooling broadens the practical applicability of encrypted inference in real-world computer vision scenarios.

Abstract: Legal requirements might prevent organizations from sharing sensitive data like medical or financial details of consumers which prevents them from leveraging cloud based ML-as-a-service solutions provided by third party providers, which are quickly gaining popularity these days. In this project, we aim to perform inference tasks in Computer Vision in a privacy-preserving manner, i.e, by only looking at encrypted data. Recent advances in fully homomorphic encryption make this possible. A fully homomorphic encryption allows an arbitrary sequence of additive and multiplicative operations to be performed on encrypted data directly. Applying homomorphic encryptions to CNNs requires modifying the conventional CNN layers, so that they adhere to the encryption scheme. Our aim was to explore the best methods to create CNNs which can classify encrypted images directly. We used Microsoft SEAL for performing homomorphic encryption. The performance of these "encryption based CNNs" should be comparable with baseline accuracies of the same CNNs trained on unencrypted data, and the aim was to achieve as low of a hit on inference-time performance as possible. We successfully obtained minimal drop in classification accuracy for various datasets. We used MNIST as our baseline, which is popularly used in related research work and then explored more complex datasets like Kuzushiji MNIST, Fashion-MNIST and CIFAR-10 as a part of our contribution. Additionally, we also added support for more complex operations on top of TenSEAL, like processing colored images (multi-channel input), applying multiple convolutional layers and performing average pooling.

</details>


### [143] [Sponsored Group Signature and its Application to Privacy-preserving Guest Access in Smart Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25248)
*Sepideh Avizheh,Reihaneh Safavi-Naini,Shiwei Sun*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Proposes SPonsored Group Signatures (SPGS), a two-level group signature scheme where level-1 members can sponsor new level-2 members, with sponsor accountability and linkable signatures for sponsored members. Provides formal security definitions, a generic construction, an instantiation, and demonstrates practical applicability via an Anonymous Guest Access Token (AGAT) system for smart buildings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional group signatures rely on a trusted group manager for member admission, which is inflexible for dynamic applications like privacy-preserving access in smart environments. There is a need for a decentralized join mechanism where existing members can sponsor new members, with appropriate accountability mechanisms—specifically, sponsor responsibility and the ability to identify misbehaving sponsored members via linkability.

Method: Formalizes SPGS with game-based security definitions. Provides a generic construction combining a dynamic group signature scheme, a commitment scheme, and a knowledge-sound NIZK proof of knowledge. Proves security of the construction and gives a concrete instantiation. Demonstrates practicality by building an Anonymous Guest Access Token (AGAT) protocol for smart building guest access, combining SPGS with an IND-CPA secure public-key encryption scheme.

Result: A provably secure generic construction for SPGS with concrete instantiation. The AGAT protocol shows SPGS can be efficiently realized with existing schemes for practical guest access in smart buildings, enabling temporary guests to anonymously access building resources while maintaining sponsor accountability.

Conclusion: SPGS provides a flexible, two-level group signature mechanism with sponsor-based join and dual accountability (openability to sponsor identity plus linkability of sponsored signatures). The scheme is both theoretically sound and practically efficient, as demonstrated by the AGAT application for smart environments.

Abstract: Group signatures are privacy preserving signature schemes in which a group member can anonymously sign messages on behalf of the group, while providing accountability, by allowing the signature of a misbehaving group member be ``opened'' and the identity of the signer be revealed. In group signature members are admitted to the group by a (trusted) group manager. We motivate the need for a flexible mechanism in applications, such as privacy preserving access in smart environments, and propose a two-level member-join group signature that we call SPonsored Group Signature (SPGS) where group members of level 1 can ``sponsor'' new members, in level 2, to join the group. This relaxation of user join comes with additional accountability mechanisms: we require that the signature of a sponsored member can be opened to the identity of the sponsor (that is sponsor is responsible for the sponsored member), and while all signatures are anonymous, for the sponsored members, the signatures are linkable. This allows a sponsor to efficiently identify an undesirable sponsored member. We formalize SPGS scheme, define its security using a game-based approach, and give a generic construction of SPGS that uses a (dynamic) group signature scheme, a commitment scheme, and a knowledge-sound non-interactive zero knowledge proof of knowledge, and prove its security. We also give an instantiation of our construction. To show applicability of SPGS in practice, we consider the problem of providing guest access in a smart building, and introduce Anonymous Guest Access Token (AGAT) that allows a temporary guest to anonymously access (a subset of) the building resources. We show how SPGS can be used (together with an IND-CPA secure public key encryption scheme) to give a direct construction for AGAT, and show the efficiency of our guest access protocol when it is instantiated with existing schemes.

</details>


### [144] [Decoupling Reconnaissance and Exploitation: Measuring the Capability Boundaries of LLM-Based Web Penetration Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25332)
*Liwei Yu,Shuo Li,Ming Zhou,Ge Chu,Yan Guo*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A decoupled evaluation framework for LLM-based penetration testing agents reveals a significant gap: agents achieve up to 90% exploit success with ground-truth context but only ~50% vulnerability recall during autonomous reconnaissance, with architectural strengths varying by vulnerability type.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing end-to-end black-box evaluations of LLM-driven penetration testing agents suffer from error cascading — failures in early reconnaissance obscure the agent's true exploitation capabilities. A more precise, decoupled assessment is needed to isolate and measure exploitation skill independently of reconnaissance noise.

Method: The authors propose a two-stage decoupled evaluation framework using ground-truth injection and knowledge-driven ablation across 70 web vulnerability testbeds. They test five open-source agents (multiagent, monolithic, graph-driven) on 50 representative vulnerabilities, measuring functional exploit success rate (with vulnerability context provided) versus targeted vulnerability recall (autonomous reconnaissance).

Result: With accurate vulnerability context, agents achieve up to 90.0% functional success rate; autonomous reconnaissance plateaus at ~50.0% recall, mainly due to parsing failures on unstructured telemetry. Cross-architectural analysis shows multi-agent excels at long-sequence interactions (e.g., deserialization), monolithic at short-chain injections, and graph-driven at cross-session access-control vulnerabilities.

Conclusion: The decoupled framework provides fine-grained benchmarking that reveals distinct capability niches across agent architectures, offering an empirical foundation for designing next-generation automated offensive security agents.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promise for automated penetration testing, yet existing end-to-end black-box evaluations are highly susceptible to error cascading: failures in early reconnaissance can mask an agent's actual ability to exploit vulnerabilities. To more accurately characterize these capabilities, we propose a two-stage decoupled evaluation framework that separates exploit execution from reconnaissance. Using ground-truth injection and knowledge-driven ablation across 70 high-fidelity web vulnerability testbeds, our framework isolates exploitation performance from reconnaissance noise. We empirically evaluate five open-source penetration-testing agents, covering multiagent, monolithic, and graph-driven architectures, on a strictly aligned subset of 50 representative vulnerabilities. The results reveal a substantial capability gap. With accurate vulnerability context, agents achieve a functional success rate of up to 90.0%, whereas autonomous reconnaissance, measured by targeted vulnerability recall, plateaus at approximately 50.0%, primarily due to failures in parsing unstructured telemetry. Cross-architectural analysis further reveals distinct capability niches: multi-agent isolation is more effective for long-sequence interactions such as de-serialization, while monolithic and graph-driven designs perform better on short-chain injections and cross-session access-control vulnerabilities, respectively. This decoupled evaluation work provides a fine-grained benchmarking protocol and an empirical basis for designing next-generation automated offensive security agents.

</details>


### [145] [General Techniques for Reducing Key-Switching Overhead in Privacy-Preserving Two-Party Transformer Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25349)
*Wenshao Yang,Zhenhua Liu,Dongdong Yao*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper proposes three techniques to reduce key-switching overhead in secure two-party Transformer inference: (1) a preprocessing-assisted method that offloads attention computation key switching to offline, (2) storage-communication trade-offs to mitigate the added storage burden, and (3) a fused key-switch technique that combines relinearization and rotation in RNS-CKKS matrix multiplications. All methods are packing-independent and provide flexible deployment trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In secure two-party Transformer inference using FHE (or hybrid FHE-MPC), key switching dominates computational overhead during linear layer evaluation (plaintext-ciphertext and ciphertext-ciphertext matrix multiplications). Existing optimizations are packing-specific, limiting general applicability. The authors aim to reduce this bottleneck from a packing-independent perspective while enabling flexible storage-communication trade-offs.

Method: Three methods are proposed: (1) Decomposing attention computation into precomputable operations and online interactions, shifting key switching to the preprocessing (offline) phase without altering packing strategies. (2) Storage-communication trade-off techniques that replace large precomputed ciphertexts with modest online communication to mitigate the storage overhead introduced by method (1). (3) A fused key-switch technique that targets the multiplication-followed-by-rotation pattern common in RNS-CKKS matrix multiplication, merging relinearization and rotation into a single procedure to reduce costs for ciphertext-ciphertext operations persisting in offline stages and deeper FHE layers.

Result: Analytical evaluations show that the proposed techniques significantly reduce online key-switch overhead and provide flexible trade-offs between storage and communication, all without requiring modifications to existing packing strategies.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that packing-independent optimizations can effectively address key-switching bottlenecks in secure Transformer inference. The three techniques together enable flexible deployment under varying resource constraints while substantially reducing online computation overhead.

Abstract: In secure two-party Transformer inference, linear layers are typically evaluated using Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE) through plaintext-ciphertext or ciphertext-ciphertext matrix multiplications, where key switching primarily occurs and dominates computational overhead in both FHE-based and hybrid FHE-MPC systems. Existing optimizations rely heavily on packing-specific algorithms, limiting their general applicability.
  Targeting this overhead from a packing-independent perspective, we propose a preprocessing-assisted method for secure attention computation. By decomposing attention into precomputable operations and online interactions, this method reduces online inference-phase key switching without modifying existing packing strategies.
  However, the first method shifting key switching offline introduces additional storage requirements. To address this, we propose storage-communication trade-off techniques that replace large precomputed ciphertexts with modest online communication, enabling flexible deployment under varying resource constraints.
  While ciphertext-ciphertext matrix multiplication is offloaded to the preprocessing phase in hybrid schemes and the first layer of FHE-based schemes, these operations still persist in the offline stage and subsequent FHE layers. To further optimize it, we propose a fused key-switch technique targeting the multiplication-followed-by-rotation pattern, which frequently arises in existing RNS-CKKS matrix multiplication schemes. By combining relinearization and rotation into a single procedure, this technique reduces the associated computation costs.
  Analytical evaluations demonstrate that our proposed techniques significantly reduce online key-switch overhead and provide flexible trade-offs between storage and communication without requiring modifications to existing packing strategies.

</details>


### [146] [Representation Matters: An Empirical Study of Program Representations for LLM Vulnerability Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25356)
*Andrew Stoltman,Johnathan Tang,Haipeng Cai*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper introduces RepBench, a benchmark showing that program structural representations (especially AST+PDG) significantly outperform raw source code for LLM-based vulnerability detection, revealing a "context dilution effect" where excess source code degrades reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most LLM-based vulnerability detection approaches feed raw source code as prompts, implicitly assuming more context is better. The authors challenge this assumption by systematically investigating how different program representations affect LLM reasoning about vulnerabilities.

Method: The authors built RepBench, converting 107 real-world C/C++ vulnerability testcases from PrimeVul into multiple representations: raw source, AST, CFG, PDG, combinations, and enriched PDGs (ePDGs). They evaluated ten representation variants using a fixed Chain-of-Thought protocol with structured output, plus 19 additional ePDG cases from VulChecker/Hector, across five CWE categories.

Result: AST+PDG achieved 83.2% accuracy versus 53.5% for raw source. Graph-only prompts outperformed both source-only and source-plus-graph prompts while using far less prompt overhead. Adding raw source code to compact structural evidence degraded reasoning (context dilution effect).

Conclusion: Carefully selected structural representations provide a superior accuracy-overhead tradeoff compared to raw source input. Static analysis can serve as an effective prompt-construction layer for security-focused LLM reasoning, contradicting the "more context is better" assumption.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used for automated vulnerability detection, but it remains unclear how program structure and semantics should be represented for LLM-based reasoning. Most prompting-based approaches provide raw source code, implicitly assuming that more source-level context gives the model better evidence. This paper challenges that assumption through RepBench, an empirical benchmark comparing raw source code with static-analysis-based program representations. RepBench converts real-world C/C++ vulnerability testcases into multiple representations: raw source, Abstract Syntax Trees (ASTs), Control-Flow Graphs (CFGs), Program Dependence Graphs (PDGs), their combinations, and an auxiliary track of enriched PDGs (ePDGs). Using a curated PrimeVul-derived corpus of 107 Joern-based testcases across five CWE categories, we evaluate ten representation variants under a fixed Chain-of-Thought and structured-output protocol, plus 19 additional ePDG cases generated through VulChecker/Hector. Representation choice substantially affects LLM vulnerability reasoning. The strongest variant, AST+PDG, achieves 83.2% accuracy, compared with 53.5% for raw source. At the family level, graph-only prompts outperform both source-only and source-plus-graph prompts while requiring far less prompt overhead. These results reveal a context dilution effect: adding raw source code to compact structural graph evidence can degrade reasoning by making vulnerability-relevant evidence less salient. Overall, our findings show that carefully selected structural representations offer a better accuracy-overhead tradeoff than simply giving LLMs more raw input, and suggest that static analysis can serve as an effective prompt-construction layer for security-focused LLM reasoning.

</details>


### [147] [Security and Privacy in Retrieval-Augmented Generation: Architectures, Threats, Defenses, and Future Directions for Building Trustworthy Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25533)
*Balamurugan Palanisamy,G S S Chalapathi,Vikas Hassija,Rajkumar Buyya*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A survey systematizing privacy and security threats in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems across centralized, on-device, federated, and hybrid deployments, presenting a unified taxonomy of attack surfaces and reviewing corresponding defenses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG systems integrate retrieval with generation, introducing new attack surfaces beyond standard LLM threats. Sensitive data leakage and adversarial manipulation across the retrieval pipeline demand a structured understanding of risks and countermeasures to enable trustworthy real-world deployment.

Method: The authors conduct a comprehensive survey, proposing a unified taxonomy organized by RAG stages (retrieval, context construction, generation) and deployment paradigms (centralized, Micro-RAG, federated, hybrid). They systematically categorize attack classes—membership inference, index inference, poisoning, gradient leakage, collusion—and review architectural, algorithmic, and cryptographic defenses, including privacy-utility trade-off analysis.

Result: The survey delineates threat surfaces across all RAG pipeline stages and deployment contexts, maps attack classes to specific vulnerabilities, and evaluates defense strategies (differential privacy, secure enclaves, federated learning safeguards, etc.), identifying inherent trade-offs between privacy, utility, and efficiency.

Conclusion: RAG systems introduce multifaceted security and privacy risks requiring holistic, stage-aware defenses. Open challenges remain in balancing privacy-utility trade-offs, securing multi-stakeholder and federated settings, and developing resilient architectures for production environments.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has emerged as a dominant paradigm for enhancing large language models with external knowledge. By coupling retrieval mechanisms with generative models, RAG systems improve factual grounding and adaptability across domains. However, integrating retrieval pipelines introduces new security and privacy risks that extend beyond conventional language modeling threats. Sensitive information may be exposed through retrieval indices, query logs, context construction, or federated updates, while adversarial manipulation of knowledge bases can undermine trust in generated outputs. This survey provides a comprehensive examination of privacy and security challenges across RAG systems deployed in centralized, on-device (Micro-RAG), federated, and hybrid paradigms. We present a unified taxonomy of threat surfaces spanning the retrieval, context construction, and generation stages and systematically analyze attack classes, including membership inference, index inference, poisoning, gradient leakage, and collusion. We further review architectural, algorithmic, and cryptographic defenses, highlighting privacy-utility trade-offs and deployment considerations. Finally, we outline open research challenges toward building trustworthy, secure, and resilient RAG systems for real-world applications.

</details>


### [148] [Can Trustless Agents Be Trusted? An Empirical Study of the ERC-8004 Decentralized AI Agent Ecosystem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26028)
*Xihan Xiong,Zelin Li,Wei Wei,Qin Wang,William Knottenbelt,Zhipeng Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper presents the first empirical study of the ERC-8004 protocol—a permissionless trust layer for AI agent economies—across Ethereum, BSC, and Base. It finds that identity registrations are mostly placeholders, reputation scores are incommensurable and manipulable, and Sybil-coordinated feedback is rampant. The authors provide actionable recommendations for protocol revision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ERC-8004 has been rapidly adopted as a trust layer for autonomous AI agent transactions, but no empirical study has examined whether the on-chain identity, reputation, and validation data it records actually provides a trustworthy basis for decision-making. This gap motivates the study.

Method: The authors crawl on-chain Identity and Reputation events, off-chain registration files, and x402 payment transactions across Ethereum, BNB Smart Chain (BSC), and Base, covering deployment through May 13, 2026. They quantitatively analyze registration completeness, service endpoint liveness, reputation commensurability, feedback grounding in verifiable interactions, manipulation costs, and Sybil coordination patterns.

Result: (1) Only 3–15% of registered identities expose valid registration files with live service endpoints. (2) Reputation values are not commensurable; feedback is rarely grounded in verifiable interactions; reputation can be manipulated at minimal cost. (3) Sybil-coordinated reviewing affects 59.2–90.6% of reviewers across chains. After removing Sybil-flagged feedback, 15.5–89.4% of rated agents are left with no valid feedback.

Conclusion: ERC-8004 in its current deployment is unfit as a trust signal for AI agent decision-making. The authors contribute concrete, actionable protocol-design recommendations and establish an empirical baseline for future research on AI agent markets.

Abstract: As autonomous AI agents increasingly transact across organizational boundaries, a fundamental trust challenge emerges: how can an agent assess whether an unknown counterpart is trustworthy? The ERC-8004 protocol addresses this challenge with the first permissionless trust layer for AI agent economies, built around three on-chain registries for Identity, Reputation, and Validation. Despite its rapid adoption, the protocol has not been studied empirically, leaving it unclear whether the information it records provides a trustworthy basis for decision-making. To address this gap, we present the first empirical study of ERC-8004 across three chains: Ethereum, BNB Smart Chain (BSC), and Base, covering the period from protocol deployment through May 13, 2026. We crawl on-chain Identity and Reputation events, off-chain files, and x402 payment transactions.
  On the identity side, we find that most registrations are placeholders rather than active agents, with only a small fraction (3%, 4%, and 15% across Ethereum, BSC, and Base) exposing a valid ERC-8004 registration file with at least one live service endpoint. On the reputation side, we show that the Registry, as currently deployed, cannot function as a trust signal: values are not commensurable, feedback records are rarely grounded in verifiable interactions, and reputation can be manipulated at minimal cost. Consistent with these design weaknesses, we find that a substantial fraction of reviewers (73.6%, 59.2%, and 90.6% across Ethereum, BSC, and Base) exhibit coordinated Sybil behavior. After removing Sybil-flagged feedback, 15.5%, 72.3%, and 89.4% of rated agents, respectively, are left with no valid feedback. We then turn these findings into concrete recommendations for future revisions of ERC-8004. Our study yields actionable protocol-design implications and establishes an empirical baseline for research on AI agent markets.

</details>


### [149] [CrypFormBench: Benchmarking Formal Analysis Capability of Large Language Models for Cryptographic Schemes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25561)
*Zhaoxuan Li,Qionglu Zhang,Hengyuan Liu,Xiaoyan Gu,Xianhui Lu,Hongbo Liu,Bingzheng Wang,Haihui Fan,Ziming Zhao,Rui Zhang,Li Zhou*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: CrypFormBench is a benchmark with 700 instances evaluating 9 LLMs on five capabilities (interpretation, generation, completion, transformation, correction) for formal cryptographic analysis across symbolic and computational security. Claude-3.5 leads at 48.7/100; LLMs excel at interpretation/completion but struggle with generation/transformation/correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Formal cryptographic analysis remains labor-intensive despite tools like Scyther, Tamarin, CryptoVerif, and EasyCrypt, as experts must manually abstract schemes and write tool-specific formal descriptions. LLMs are promising but lack standardized evaluation, leaving their effectiveness unexplored.

Method: The authors construct CrypFormBench, a benchmark comprising 700 instances across 677 cryptographic schemes, 7 mainstream formal verifier languages, and 160 security properties. It evaluates five LLM capabilities: interpretation, generation, completion, transformation, and correction. Nine state-of-the-art LLMs are assessed, and improvement strategies (few-shot prompting, Pass@K sampling, lightweight fine-tuning) are explored.

Result: Most LLMs perform well on interpretation and completion tasks but struggle with generation, transformation, and correction. Overall performance is limited; Claude-3.5 achieves the top score of 48.7/100. Few-shot prompting, Pass@K sampling, and lightweight fine-tuning help mitigate the executability bottleneck and improve tool-usable outputs.

Conclusion: Current LLMs are insufficient for fully automated formal cryptographic analysis but show promise in interpretation and completion. The benchmark provides grounded assessment and concrete directions for advancing reliable LLM-assisted formal verification in cryptography.

Abstract: Manual formal analysis of cryptographic schemes is labor-intensive and requires substantial expertise. While model-checking tools (e.g., Scyther and Tamarin) and computational-security tools (e.g., CryptoVerif and EasyCrypt) improve the automation of security proofs, they still rely on experts to abstract schemes and write tool-specific formal descriptions. Large language models (LLMs) are a promising alternative, but their effectiveness in this domain remains unexplored due to the absence of standardized evaluation methodologies. To fill this gap, we introduce CrypFormBench (C.F.B for short), a comprehensive benchmark jointly covering symbolic and computational security to evaluate five core LLM capabilities: interpretation, generation, completion, transformation, and correction. It comprises 700 instances spanning 677 schemes, 7 mainstream formal verifier languages, and 160 security properties. The evaluation of 9 state-of-the-art LLMs reveals that most of them perform well on interpretation and completion, given their code-awareness advantages, but struggle with generation, transformation, and correction. Overall, their performance remains limited, with Claude-3.5 achieving the highest score at 48.7 out of 100. We further provide practical guidance, e.g., few-shot prompting, Pass@K sampling, and lightweight fine-tuning, to mitigate the executability bottleneck and improve tool-usable outputs. Taken together, our benchmark and analyses offer a grounded view of current progress and concrete directions toward reliable LLM-assisted formal cryptographic analysis.

</details>


### [150] [An Approach for a Supporting Multi-LLM System for Automated Certification Based on the German IT-Grundschutz](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25608)
*Lea Roxanne Muth,Marian Margraf*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A semi-automated certification system for BSI IT-Grundschutz is proposed, combining multiple LLMs with Hybrid Retrieval-Augmented Generation (HybridRAG) and Knowledge Graphs to address NIS2-driven demand, specialist shortages, and cost pressures across all phases of the certification process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The NIS2 directive expands the scope of companies requiring cybersecurity certification, creating a demand surge that cannot be met due to a shortage of qualified specialists and high implementation costs. There is a pressing need for tools that increase efficiency, reduce costs, and help certifiers maintain quality in security concept development.

Method: A Multi-Large Language Model system (MLS) employing Hybrid Retrieval-Augmented Generation (HybridRAG) that integrates LLMs with Knowledge Graphs (KGs). The architecture supports the full certification lifecycle: protection needs assessment, modeling, IT-Grundschutz check, measure consolidation, and subsequent realization.

Result: The paper presents the MLS architecture design but does not report quantitative experimental results in the abstract; the contribution is the architectural framework itself as a solution approach to the identified challenges.

Conclusion: The proposed MLS with HybridRAG offers a viable semi-automated approach to handle the increased certification demands imposed by NIS2, addressing both the cost and specialist-shortage bottlenecks while supporting certifiers throughout the entire BSI IT-Grundschutz certification process.

Abstract: This paper presents a novel approach to perform semi-automated BSI IT-Grundschutz certification using a MultiLarge Language Model system (MLS) with Hybrid RetrievalAugmented Generation (HybridRAG). Facing the challenges of the Network and Information Security Directive 2 (NIS2) directive, a shortage of specialists, and high implementation costs, our MLS architecture aims to increase efficiency, reduce costs, and support certifiers in maintaining the quality of security concepts while meeting the increased demand for certifications of newly affected companies. The system combines Large Language Models (LLMs) and Knowledge Graphs (KGs) to support different phases of the certification process, including protection needs assessment, modeling, IT-Grundschutz check, measure consolidation, and subsequent realization. Our architecture addresses the growing demand for security concepts and offers an approach to handle the digital security challenges introduced by NIS2.

</details>


### [151] [Probabilistic Agents in Deterministic Audits: Evaluating Multi-Agent Systems for Automated Audits Based on the German IT-Grundschutz](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25622)
*Lea Roxanne Muth,Marian Margraf*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper implements and empirically evaluates a Multi-Agent System with HybridRAG for partially automating IT-Grundschutz certification, introducing a Hypothesis-Verification Loop to reduce hallucinations and a Decoupled Reasoning Pipeline for deterministic protection need inheritance. Results show strong performance in semantic tasks but limitations in logical reasoning phases due to LLMs' probabilistic nature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The NIS-2 Directive requires thousands of SMEs to implement robust risk management. IT-Grundschutz (IT-GS) certification, a key compliance standard, is resource-intensive, demanding extensive manual effort for documentation, validation, and revision. Scalable implementation is thus difficult and expensive, motivating the need for partial automation.

Method: The authors implement a Multi-Agent System (MAS) architecture with Hybrid Retrieval Augmented Generation (HybridRAG). Two novel components enforce compliance rigor: (1) a Hypothesis-Verification Loop during Structural Analysis that cross-references agent-inferred dependencies against a Knowledge Graph to mitigate hallucinations, and (2) a Decoupled Reasoning Pipeline that separates agent-driven semantic extraction from deterministic, rule-based protection need inheritance. The system is evaluated end-to-end using the BSI's "RecPlast GmbH" case study as a human expert-generated reference dataset, measuring Precision, Recall, and F1-scores across Structural Analysis, Protection Needs Assessment, Modeling, and IT-GS Check phases.

Result: The MAS achieved high efficacy in semantic tasks — Structural Analysis and Modeling — significantly reducing manual effort through automated information extraction. However, quantitative results showed limitations in logical reasoning phases (Protection Needs Assessment and IT-GS Check), where the probabilistic nature of current LLMs failed to meet the deterministic rigor required by the IT-GS standard.

Conclusion: While the MAS/HybridRAG approach effectively automates semantic extraction tasks in IT-GS certification, current LLM limitations in deterministic logical reasoning remain a barrier to full automation.
