The decoupled architecture provides a pragmatic path forward by combining agent-driven semantics with rule-based reasoning where compliance rigor is non-negotiable.

Abstract: The NIS-2 Directive mandates robust Risk Management from thousands of small and medium enterprises. To ensure compliance, companies rely on established standards such as the German IT-Grundschutz (IT-GS) of the Federal Office for Information Security. However, IT-GS certification is resource-intensive and requires a high level of manual effort for documentation, validation, and revision, making scalable implementation difficult and expensive.
  Building upon our previous conceptual framework, this paper presents the technical implementation and empirical evaluation of a Multi-Agent System (MAS) architecture combined with Hybrid Retrieval Augmented Generation (HybridRAG) for the partial automation of IT-GS certification. We introduce two novel technical contributions to the MAS architecture to enforce the compliance rigor. The Hypothesis-Verification Loop in the Structural Analysis (SA) phase that cross-references agent-inferred dependencies against the Knowledge Graph to reduce hallucinations, and a Decoupled Reasoning Pipeline that separates agent-driven semantic extraction from the deterministic protection need inheritance. We utilize the BSI's "RecPlast GmbH" case study as a human expert-generated reference data set for end-to-end evaluation of the architecture and to quantify Precision, Recall, and F1-scores. The performance of the system is investigated across the phases of SA, Protection Needs Assessment (PNA), Modeling, and IT-GS Check.
  The empirical results reveal noticeable differences throughout the different steps of IT-GS. While the MAS demonstrates high efficacy in semantic tasks (SA and Modeling), significantly reducing manual effort through automated information extraction, quantitative results reveal limitations in logical reasoning phases (PNA and IT-GS Check) as the probabilistic nature of current LLMs struggles to meet the deterministic rigor required by IT-GS.

</details>


### [152] [Taxonomy of Risks on Automated Fact-Checking Systems Considering its Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25645)
*Jun Yajima,Tatsuya Oka,Takao Okubo*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper categorizes 32 specific risks associated with automated fact-checking systems using a three-stage propagation model (risk factors → hazardous situations → harm), then applies these categories as guide words to assess risks in the DEFAME system, demonstrating that this approach uncovers risks missed by conventional methods like STRIDE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated fact-checking systems using AI/LLMs are increasingly needed to combat fake news on SNS, but they introduce risks of incorrect judgments that can spread misinformation or cause defamation. A systematic risk categorization is needed as a first step toward safe deployment of such systems.

Method: The authors propose a three-stage risk propagation framework (risk factors, hazardous situations, harm) to categorize risks in automated fact-checking systems. They identify 32 specific risks through this categorization. These categorized risks are then used as analytical cues (guide words) to perform a risk assessment of the DEFAME fact-checking system, with results compared against the STRIDE methodology.

Result: The categorization yielded 32 specific risks in automated fact-checking systems. The risk assessment of DEFAME using these guide words successfully identified risks that the conventional STRIDE IT security risk assessment method could not derive.

Conclusion: The proposed risk categorization framework provides more comprehensive coverage than STRIDE for automated fact-checking systems, serving as an effective foundation for enabling the safe use of such systems in practice.

Abstract: In recent years, the posting of fake news including disinformation and misinformation on social networking services (SNS) has become a social problem. To combat this fake news, fact-checking that is the process of assessing the veracity of posts on SNS has become increasingly important. While fact-checking is currently performed by fact-checking organizations, it is difficult to fact-check all posts on SNS. Therefore, the use of automated fact-checking systems is effective. Recent automated fact-checking systems utilize artificial intelligence and large language models, so there are risks of incorrect judgments and posting incorrect results on social media which can lead to the spread of misinformation or to engage in defamation. In this paper, as a first step toward enabling the safe use of automated fact-checking systems, we categorize the specific risks on automated fact-checking systems. In this categorizing, we consider a three-stage risk propagation: risk factors, hazardous situations, and harm. Our analysis revealed that 32 specific risks exist in automated fact-checking systems. In this paper, we utilize the categorized risks as analytical cues (guide words) to present the risk assessment of the automated fact-checking system DEFAME. This assessment result indicates that risks that cannot be derived using STRIDE, a conventional IT security risk assessment method can be derived using our guide words.

</details>


### [153] [Tracing Target Answers in Poisoned Retrieval Corpora via Token Influence Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25721)
*Yan-Lun Chen,Pin-Yu Chen,Chia-Mu Yu,Ying-Dar Lin,Yu-Sung Wu,Wei-Bin Lee*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: TRACE is a lightweight framework for detecting corpus poisoning attacks in RAG systems by tracing token-level influence from retrieved documents to model outputs, eliminating the need for auxiliary classifiers or additional LLM verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG systems are vulnerable to corpus poisoning where malicious documents manipulate model outputs, but existing detection methods impose heavy computational costs through auxiliary classifiers or secondary LLM calls.

Method: TRACE performs two-stage detection: (1) discovering recurrent high-influence keywords across retrieved documents via token influence attribution, and (2) conducting secondary verification to confirm these keywords' causal influence on model predictions.

Result: Experiments across three QA benchmarks and six LLMs show strong detection performance, and the method successfully uncovers attacker-specified target answers.

Conclusion: TRACE provides an effective, computationally efficient alternative for RAG poisoning detection by leveraging token-level influence tracing, avoiding the overhead of prior approaches while maintaining robust detection and interpretability.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are vulnerable to corpus poisoning attacks that manipulate model outputs through malicious retrieved documents. Existing detection methods typically rely on auxiliary classifiers or additional LLM-based verification, introducing substantial computational overhead. We present TRACE, a lightweight detection framework that identifies poisoning attacks by tracing answer-related tokens through token influence attribution. TRACE first discovers recurrent high-influence keywords across retrieved documents and then performs a secondary verification to confirm their influence on model predictions. Experiments on three QA benchmarks and six LLMs demonstrate strong detection performance while simultaneously uncovering attacker-specified target answers.

</details>


### [154] [Shoot the Honey, Cloak the Player: Towards Zero-Runtime-Overhead Proactive Defense and Detection for Visual Game Cheating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25734)
*Jianing Wang,Chuqi Zhang,Yuancheng Jiang,Adil Ahmad,Shanqing Guo*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: AimTrap is an end-to-end defense against visual aimbots in FPS games, combining real-time protection via Adversarial Camouflage Textures (hiding real players) and Adversarial Honeypot Textures (luring aimbots onto fake targets) with post-game detection, achieving up to 96.9% defense success with negligible runtime overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual aimbots bypass memory-based anti-cheat by operating solely on rendered frames. Existing defenses are inadequate: post-hoc detectors cannot preserve match integrity in real time and are vulnerable to human-mimicking aimbots, while proactive runtime defenses suffer from lack of accountability, high overhead, or invasive system integration. An end-to-end solution combining real-time protection with reliable post-game detection is needed.

Method: AimTrap employs two adversarial texture mechanisms: (1) Adversarial Camouflage Textures (ACT) that conceal real players from aimbot targeting, and (2) Adversarial Honeypot Textures (AHT) that attract aimbots to fake targets, generating evidence of cheating. The system uses differentiable rendering with Expectation over Renderings for robust 3D texture synthesis, secure texture management, and a honeypot-interaction trajectory analysis pipeline for accurate cheating attribution.

Result: In real-game evaluation against a state-of-the-art visual aimbot, ACT achieved 85.1% defense success and AHT achieved 96.9%. AimTrap demonstrated extremely low false-positive rates compared to prior baselines and incurred negligible runtime overhead.

Conclusion: AimTrap delivers a practical and effective end-to-end defense against visual aimbots, successfully combining real-time protection with post-game accountability at minimal cost.

Abstract: Visual aimbots have emerged as a serious cheating threat in first-person shooter (FPS) games, as they evade existing anti-cheat defenses by operating only on rendered frames rather than game memory. However, existing defenses fail to provide an end-to-end solution: post-hoc behavior detectors cannot protect match integrity in real time and are increasingly fragile against human-mimicking aimbots, while proactive runtime defenses often lack accountability, incur substantial overhead, or require intrusive system integration.
  We present AimTrap, the first end-to-end defense against visual aimbots that combines real-time protection with post-game detection using two adversarial texture mechanisms. Adversarial Camouflage Textures (ACT) hide real players from aimbots, while Adversarial Honeypot Textures (AHT) lure aimbots into locking onto fake targets, yielding strong evidence of cheating. To make this practical, AimTrap integrates differentiable rendering with Expectation over Renderings for robust 3D texture synthesis, secure texture management, and a novel honeypot-interaction trajectory analysis pipeline for accurate cheating attribution.
  In real-game evaluation against a state-of-the-art visual aimbot, ACT achieves 85.1% defense success, AHT achieves 96.9%. Compared with prior baselines, AimTrap attains extremely low false-positive rates, while incurring negligible runtime overhead. These results show that AimTrap provides a practical and effective end-to-end defense against visual aimbots.

</details>


### [155] [RAS: Measuring LLM Safety Through Refusal Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25750)
*Chang-Chieh Huang,Yan-Lun Chen,Chia-Mu Yu,Wei-Bin Lee*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: SafeVec is a white-box LLM safety evaluation method that scores models by measuring internal representation alignment with refusal directions, avoiding the cost and brittleness of output-level judge-based evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Output-level safety evaluation (judging generated answers) is expensive, sensitive to judge choice, and tied to static question banks. A faster, more principled white-box approach using internal representations is needed.

Method: SafeVec extracts layer-wise refusal directions from a safety-aligned reference model, identifies stable layer windows where safe/unsafe behaviors are linearly separable, and computes the Refusal Alignment Score (RAS, 0–100) by measuring how well a target model's hidden states align with these refusal directions under unsafe and jailbreak prompts.

Result: Across Llama, Gemma, and Qwen families, RAS reliably separates aligned models from uncensored/abliterated variants, correlates with output-level attack success rate, and is substantially faster than judge-based evaluation.

Conclusion: Refusal alignment in hidden representations provides a compact, efficient, and reliable signal for white-box LLM safety evaluation.

Abstract: Safety evaluation of large language models (LLMs) is commonly performed by querying models with unsafe or jailbreak prompts and judging whether their outputs violate a safety policy. Although useful, output-level evaluation is expensive, sensitive to judge choice, and easily tied to fixed question banks. We propose **SafeVec**, a white-box evaluation procedure that measures safety from internal representations rather than generated answers. **SafeVec** first extracts layer-wise refusal directions from a safety-aligned reference model, then selects stable layer windows where safe and unsafe behaviors are separable, and finally scores a target model by measuring whether its hidden states align with these refusal directions under unsafe and jailbreak prompts. The resulting metric, **RAS** (**R**efusal **A**lignment **S**core), maps representation-level refusal alignment to a calibrated 0-100 safety score. Across `Llama`, `Gemma`, and `Qwen` model families, RAS separates aligned models from uncensored and abliterated variants, tracks output-level attack success rate, and is substantially faster than judge-based evaluation. These results suggest that refusal alignment provides a compact and efficient signal for white-box LLM safety evaluation.

</details>


### [156] [Color Matters: Trigger Color Affects Success in Federated Backdoor Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25858)
*Kavindu Herath,Joshua C. Zhao,Saurabh Bagchi*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper investigates how trigger color (black vs. white) in semantic backdoor attacks (using accessories like masks/sunglasses) affects attack success rates in federated learning, finding that white triggers work better when targeting the "blond" class and black triggers when targeting the "black" class, even under robust aggregation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning is vulnerable to backdoor attacks, but existing work largely overlooks how the color of semantic triggers (natural visual accessories) affects attack effectiveness. The authors aim to systematically study whether trigger color matters independently of trigger semantics, placement, and poisoning budget.

Method: The authors propose a semantics-driven backdoor framework where malicious clients apply natural accessories (masks, sunglasses) as triggers in either black or white variants to source-class images, relabeling them to a target class. They evaluate under two objectives: (1) a standard poisoning loss and (2) the SABLE objective, which combines clean classification loss, triggered target loss, feature-separation loss in the penultimate representation space, and a regularization term to keep malicious updates close to the global model. Experiments are conducted on a four-class CelebA hair-color task under both standard FedAvg and robust aggregation.

Result: Trigger color significantly impacts attack success rate even with identical semantics, placement, and poisoning budget. White triggers are more effective when targeting the "blond" class; black triggers perform better when targeting the "black" class. This trend persists under robust aggregation, confirming that trigger color is a meaningful factor in semantic backdoor persistence and evaluation.

Conclusion: Trigger color is an important and previously underappreciated factor in the design and evaluation of semantic backdoor attacks in federated learning. Future work on backdoor attacks and defenses should account for trigger color as a relevant dimension.

Abstract: Federated learning is vulnerable to backdoor attacks in which malicious clients inject poisoned updates while preserving benign-task performance. In this paper, we study a semantics-driven backdoor mechanism in which attackers use natural visual accessories as triggers and manipulate only the trigger color while keeping the attack pipeline fixed. Our framework considers semantic trigger objects such as masks and sunglasses, instantiated in black and white variants, and evaluates their effect in a controlled federated learning setting. Malicious clients construct poisoned samples by applying a trigger to source-class images and relabeling them to an attacker-chosen target class, while benign clients train only on clean data. We analyze this mechanism under both a standard poisoning objective and a stronger SABLE-based objective that combines clean classification loss, triggered target loss, feature-separation loss in the penultimate representation space, and regularization to keep malicious updates close to the global model. This design enables the attack to remain effective while reducing excessive update drift. Experiments on a four-class CelebA hair-color task show that trigger color significantly changes attack success rate even when trigger semantics, placement, and poisoning budget are unchanged. White triggers are more effective for attacks targeting the blond class, whereas black triggers perform better for attacks targeting the black class. The same trend persists under robust aggregation, showing that trigger color is a meaningful factor in the operation, persistence, and evaluation of semantic backdoor mechanisms in federated learning.

</details>


### [157] [A Tattered Cloak of Invisibility: Measuring Anonymity Loss in Railgun on Ethereum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25926)
*Kanan Huseynov,Ali Shahzaib,István András Seres,János Tapolcai*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper investigates how user behavior and transaction patterns leak privacy in cryptocurrency mixers (specifically Railgun on Ethereum), even when the underlying cryptography is sound. Using five heuristics, the authors link 17.65% of withdrawals to deposits and demonstrate a median anonymity loss of 3.42 bits via knapsack analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Blockchain-based financial systems expose transactions and balances publicly, and while cryptographic privacy solutions (mixers, anonymity pools) exist, real-world anonymity depends not only on protocol security but also on user behavior. The authors aim to quantify this behavioral leakage, which has been understudied relative to cryptographic attacks.

Method: The authors analyze Railgun on Ethereum using five heuristics: (1) characteristic timing patterns between deposits and withdrawals, (2) address reuse, (3) transaction graph proximity from prior public activity, (4) amount fingerprints preserving distinctive digit patterns, and (5) knapsack-type combinatorial matches where deposit and withdrawal sums align. They compute linking probabilities and apply a knapsack solver to quantify anonymity loss in bits.

Result: The five heuristics uniquely linked 17.65% of Railgun withdrawal transactions to their corresponding deposit transactions. The knapsack solver produced a median anonymity loss of 3.42 bits for withdrawals, demonstrating substantial privacy degradation from behavioral patterns alone.

Conclusion: Even cryptographically strong privacy systems suffer significant anonymity loss due to user behavior. The findings highlight practical privacy limits of mixers and anonymity pools, and suggest the need for safer usage practices and improved design principles to mitigate behavioral leakage.

Abstract: From a user's perspective, perhaps the most significant difference between traditional banking services and widely used blockchain-based financial systems is that, in the latter, transactions and, either directly or indirectly, account balances and transaction histories are publicly observable. Therefore, a growing number of cryptographic solutions have been proposed to add a privacy layer to such systems. However, the privacy that users actually obtain does not depend solely on the security of the underlying cryptographic protocol: user behavior, transaction amount patterns, and timing decisions can substantially reduce anonymity.
  In this work, we study behavioral leakage in cryptocurrency mixers, focusing on Railgun on Ethereum. We aim to heuristically estimate the probability that a given deposit and withdrawal transaction belong to the same user. We consider five sources of leakage: characteristic timing patterns, address reuse, proximity in the transaction graph induced by prior public transactions, amount fingerprints that preserve distinctive digit patterns across transaction values, and knapsack type matches in which groups of transaction amounts add up in revealing ways. Our results show that even cryptographically strong privacy systems may suffer substantial anonymity loss due to user behavior and transaction patterns. Our five heuristics are able to uniquely link 17.65% of Railgun withdraw transactions to deposit transactions. We also applied a knapsack solver algorithm that was able to produce a 3.42 bit median anonymity loss for withdraw transactions. This work contributes to a better understanding of the practical privacy limits of mixers and anonymity pools, and points toward safer usage practices and design principles.

</details>


### [158] [Do (Not) Tell Me About My Insecurities: Assessing the Status Quo of Coordinated Vulnerability Disclosure in Germany Amid New EU Cybersecurity Regulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25950)
*Sebastian Neef,Cenk Schlunke,Anne Hennig*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A longitudinal study tracking the adoption of Coordinated Vulnerability Disclosure (CVD) programs and security.txt files among Germany's 40 DAX-listed companies, showing a sharp increase from 50% in 2023 to over 90% in 2025. Survey responses (20% response rate) indicate legal mandates like NIS2 and CRA drive adoption, while resource constraints and inconsistent report quality remain key challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In an interconnected digital landscape, effective vulnerability disclosure mechanisms are critical for preventing data breaches. Despite growing recognition of CVD programs as a security best practice, empirical data on adoption rates, drivers, and practical challenges — particularly among major corporations in a leading European economy — is scarce. The authors aim to fill this gap through a systematic, longitudinal investigation of DAX companies.

Method: The study employs a mixed-methods longitudinal design spanning 2023 to 2025, targeting all 40 companies on Germany's DAX stock market index. Data collection combines: (1) continuous monitoring of publicly available CVD program information and security.txt files, and (2) two waves of structured questionnaires distributed via email and postal mail (2023 and 2025), yielding responses from 20% of the sampled companies.

Result: CVD program adoption among DAX companies surged from 50% (2023) to over 90% (2025). Ten new CVD programs and 25 new security.txt files were launched during this period. Survey responses identify regulatory drivers (notably NIS2 and CRA) as primary adoption motivators, while lack of human resources and variable quality of incoming vulnerability reports emerge as the principal drawbacks.

Conclusion: This is the first study to systematically survey DAX companies on CVD practices. The findings serve dual purposes: encouraging broader adoption of security programs among SMEs and other firms by documenting leading companies' experiences, and equipping policymakers with empirical evidence on practical barriers and industry realities to inform future regulation.

Abstract: In our increasingly interconnected world, good IT security practices are necessary to prevent vulnerabilities and data breaches. Providing security contacts, e.g., via Coordinated Vulnerability Disclosure (CVD) programs or security.txt files, is an important practice for businesses to facilitate vulnerability reporting by external parties. As part of a longitudinal study, we analyzed the adoption of, as well as the challenges and experiences with, CVD programs among the 40 companies listed on Germany's DAX (the country's primary stock market index). In addition to monitoring publicly available information about their CVD programs, we sent out questionnaires via email and postal mail in 2023 and 2025, and received answers from 20\% of the companies. The adoption rates show a significant increase from 50\% (2023) to over 90\% (2025), with ten new CVD programs and 25 new security.txt files now available. The survey answers reveal that, for example, legal obligations (e.g., NIS2 and CRA) drive the adoption of CVD practices, but a lack of (human) resources and varying report quality are considered drawbacks. As the first study to survey 40 German stock market index (DAX) companies on their CVD practices, our results can help foster the adoption and understanding of security programs among SMEs and other companies, and provide policymakers with insights into practical challenges and industry experiences.

</details>


### [159] [BlowLive: Blow-Based Multi-Factor Biometrics with Liveness Detection and Revocability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25998)
*Eyasu Getahun Chekole,Howard Halim,Daniël Reijsbergen,Jianying Zhou*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: BlowLive is a multi-factor biometric framework combining blow-acoustic signals with facial biometrics, using fuzzy-extractor-based key derivation and Doppler shift liveness detection to achieve high accuracy, strong spoofing resistance, and template revocability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional biometric systems suffer from four key weaknesses: vulnerability to spoofing attacks due to poor liveness detection, privacy leakage from stored biometric templates, accuracy degradation from intra-user variability, and the inherent non-revocability of physiological biometrics over long-term use.

Method: BlowLive integrates blow-acoustic behavioral signals with facial physiological biometrics in a multi-factor framework. It employs advanced spectral feature extraction and multimodal fusion, a fuzzy-extractor scheme that derives stable cryptographic keys from noisy biometric inputs for authentication (avoiding raw template storage), and a novel Doppler shift-based liveness detection mechanism to counter playback, synthetic, and deepfake attacks. The system was implemented and evaluated on data from 50 participants.

Result: BlowLive achieved 99.56% authentication accuracy for blow-acoustics, 100% for facial and fusion modalities, and 99.42% accuracy for liveness detection. The framework also demonstrated strong template protection, revocability, non-invasiveness, and high usability.

Conclusion: BlowLive effectively addresses the core limitations of conventional biometric systems by combining behavioral and physiological modalities with cryptographic key derivation and Doppler-based liveness detection, offering a practical, secure, and accurate multi-factor biometric authentication solution.

Abstract: Biometric authentication systems are increasingly deployed in security-critical applications, yet existing physiological and behavioral biometrics suffer from fundamental limitations: 1) they are vulnerable to spoofing attacks due to unreliable liveness detection, 2) biometric templates may leak privacy-sensitive information 3) intra-user variability results in accuracy degradation, and 4) it is difficult to revoke physiological biometrics and safeguard them over long-term use. To address these challenges, we propose BlowLive, a robust multi-factor biometric (MFB) framework that integrates blow-acoustic signals and facial biometrics as complementary behavioral and physiological modalities. BlowLive incorporates advanced spectral feature extraction and multimodal fusion techniques, achieving high authentication accuracy even for behavioral modalities. Instead of relying on conventional biometric approaches that utilize raw biometric templates for authentication, the proposed framework adopts a fuzzy-extractor-based biometric authentication scheme, wherein stable cryptographic keys are derived from inherently noisy biometric inputs and subsequently used for authentication. To defend against playback, synthetic, and deepfake attacks, BlowLive further integrates a novel Doppler shift-based liveness detection mechanism. We implement the complete BlowLive framework and experimentally evaluate its effectiveness using biometric data collected from 50 participants. The experimental results demonstrate high authentication accuracy (99.56% for blow-acoustics and 100% for facial and fusion modalities), robust liveness detection (99.42% accuracy), strong template protection and revocability, non-invasiveness, and high usability.

</details>


### [160] [Privacy Vulnerabilities of Attention Layers in Tabular Foundation Models and Protection of High-Risk Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26021)
*Tânia Carvalho,Maxime Cordy*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Tabular foundation models leak membership information through attention patterns during in-context learning. The authors propose AMIA, a shadow-model-free attack exploiting transformer attention concentrations, and an inference-time k-anonymity-based defense that cuts membership leakage by ~50% with minimal utility loss. Fine-tuning further amplifies memorization and privacy risk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tabular foundation models are widely assumed to be privacy-safe due to pre-training on synthetic data, but their in-context learning mechanism—where sensitive records are supplied as labelled examples at inference—creates an under-explored attack surface. The authors aim to expose and quantify this vulnerability, demonstrating that attention patterns leak membership information beyond what classical confidence-based attacks can capture.

Method: The paper introduces AMIA (Attention-based Membership Inference Attack), a shadow-model-free attack that exploits the concentration of transformer attention patterns to infer membership of context examples. For defense, the authors propose an inference-time mechanism inspired by k-anonymity: it reduces the uniqueness of context-key representations without adding noise or retraining, selectively applied to high-risk queries identified via AMIA scores.

Result: AMIA outperforms classical confidence-based membership inference attacks by an average gain of 7.7%, particularly in low false-positive regimes. The k-anonymity defense reduces membership leakage from AMIA by ~50% and from confidence-based attacks by ~25%, while incurring only 3.9% predictive performance degradation. Fine-tuning is shown to amplify memorization: samples with increased prediction confidence post-fine-tuning become more susceptible to MIAs.

Conclusion: In-context learning in tabular foundation models introduces a concrete privacy vulnerability via attention leakage. AMIA demonstrates this risk robustly, and the proposed lightweight k-anonymity defense offers a practical mitigation without retraining. Fine-tuning exacerbates the problem, suggesting that privacy auditing must extend beyond training data to include inference-time context and fine-tuning dynamics.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models are commonly assumed to present limited privacy concerns as they are often pre-trained on large collections of synthetic data. However, these models leverage in-context learning, where sensitive records may be provided directly at inference time as labelled context examples. In this paper, we demonstrate that predictions generated via the attention mechanism leak sufficient information to enable effective Membership Inference Attacks (MIAs). To highlight this vulnerability, we propose AMIA (Attention-based Membership Inference Attack), a shadow-model-free attack that exploits the concentration of transformer attention patterns. Our results show that attention mechanisms reveal strong membership signals, which exceed classical confidence-based attacks, achieving an average gain of 7.7\%, specially in low false-positive regimes. To mitigate this risk, we introduce an inference-time defence inspired by $k$-anonymity principles. This approach reduces the uniqueness of context-key representations without introducing random noise or retraining the model. By targeting only high-risk queries identified through AMIA scores, the defence substantially reduces membership leakage of this attack by an average of 50\% and 25\% against confidence-based attacks, while preserving predictive utility with only 3.9\% performance degradation. Beyond showing that context examples are vulnerable, we further demonstrate that fine-tuning introduces an additional source of privacy risk. In particular, samples whose prediction confidence increases after fine-tuning become more susceptible to MIAs, indicating that fine-tuning can amplify memorisation and expose sensitive training information through confidence shifts.

</details>
